USMLE Family MedicineShelf Exam High-Yield Topics

Categorized via Standardized Taxonomy

Forms Included: 2A, 3A, 4A, 5A

▶ Behavioral Health Major Depressive Disorder Without Psychotic Features 7 questions
Source: 3A #18
A 20-year-old college student comes to the student health service because of insomnia, nausea, indigestion, and periodic anorexia over the past year. She has had a 4.5-kg (10-lb) weight loss over the past month. She worries about her physical problems and feels that her friends are avoiding her because she is not worthy of their friendship. She states that her grades have fallen, and she has dropped out of her sorority. She denies suicidal ideation but states that she cries daily for no apparent reason. She is 163 cm (5 ft 4 in) tall and weighs 45 kg (99 lb); BMI is 17 kg/m². Her pulse is 68/min, and respirations are 16/min. Examination and laboratory studies show normal findings. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Adjustment disorder with depressed mood B) Anorexia nervosa C) Dysthymic disorder D) Major depressive disorder E) Somatization disorder
Correct Answer: D) Major depressive disorder
This 20-year-old patient presents with a constellation of depressive symptoms meeting criteria for major depressive disorder (MDD). She exhibits at least five of the nine DSM-5 criteria including: depressed mood (cries daily), anhedonia (loss of interest in activities, dropped out of sorority), feelings of worthlessness, sleep disturbance (insomnia), appetite changes with significant weight loss (4.5 kg in one month), difficulty concentrating (grades fallen), and neurovegetative symptoms. These symptoms have persisted for one year without an identifiable organic cause. MDD requires at least five criteria present for at least 2 weeks. Adjustment disorder with depressed mood (Choice A) presents after an identifiable psychosocial stressor and typically lasts less than 6 months. This patient's symptoms have been present for one year. Anorexia nervosa (Choice B) is characterized by intense fear of gaining weight, decreased self-worth related to body weight, and restrictive eating with binge-purge behaviors. While this patient has weight loss and BMI of 17 kg/m², the clinical presentation is dominated by depressive symptoms rather than the characteristic body image disturbance and eating disorder behaviors of anorexia nervosa. The appetite changes and weight loss are secondary to depression. Dysthymic disorder/Persistent Depressive Disorder (Choice C) involves mild depressive symptoms persisting for at least 2 years without meeting full MDD criteria. This patient's symptoms are more severe and meet full MDD criteria rather than the milder, chronic presentation of dysthymia. Somatization disorder/Somatic Symptom Disorder (Choice E) is characterized by preoccupation with somatic symptoms that disrupt daily life, with excessive time and energy devoted to health concerns. While this patient has some physical complaints, the primary presentation is mood-related with secondary physical symptoms, not primary somatic preoccupation. Treatment for MDD involves neurotransmitter modulation using selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) such as sertraline, or agents blocking both serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake including serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors and tricyclic antidepressants.
Source: 3A #23
A 77-year-old man is brought to the office by his daughter because of a 1-month history of progressive difficulty with memory. The patient moved to the USA from a small island in Micronesia 3 months ago to live with his daughter after his wife died. His daughter says that his memory was excellent 3 months ago but that it has declined noticeably during the past month. The patient has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. On mental status examination, the patient answers simple questions slowly but appropriately. He defers to his daughter to answer more complicated questions. He has a flat mood and affect. He recalls zero of three objects after 5 minutes. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease B) Dementia, Alzheimer type C) Pseudodementia D) Vascular dementia E) Vitamin B1 (thiamine) deficiency
Correct Answer: C) Pseudodementia
This patient is experiencing pseudodementia, which describes the cognitive deficits associated with major depressive disorder in elderly patients. Key clinical features supporting this diagnosis include: (1) recent significant loss (wife's death), (2) recent emigration from home country, (3) relatively rapid onset of cognitive decline over 1 month, (4) flat mood and affect on examination, and (5) poor cooperation and effort on cognitive testing. Pseudodementia is distinguished from true dementia by the preponderance of mood symptoms rather than neurologic symptoms, absence of findings like word-finding difficulty, dyspraxia, or nocturnal worsening of cognition, and typically responds well to antidepressant treatment including SSRIs. Creutzfeldt-Jakob disease (Choice A) presents with rapidly progressive dementia accompanied by myoclonus and cerebellar manifestations (ataxia, nystagmus), with death typically occurring within 1 year. This patient lacks these neurologic findings. Dementia, Alzheimer type (Choice B) presents with gradual and progressive cognitive decline beginning with short-term memory impairment and visuospatial decline, progressing to apraxia, language abnormalities, and dysexecutive syndrome. The 1-month rapid onset is inconsistent with Alzheimer dementia. Vascular dementia (Choice D) features stepwise cognitive decline following cerebral infarctions. This patient demonstrates no focal neurological deficits indicative of current or prior cerebral infarction. Vitamin B1 (thiamine) deficiency (Choice E) is seen in chronic alcohol abusers and presents with Wernicke encephalopathy, characterized by the triad of acutely altered mental status, ophthalmoplegia with nystagmus, and ataxia. This patient has no history of alcohol abuse and lacks these neurologic findings. Educational Objective: Pseudodementia refers to cognitive deficits associated with major depression in elderly patients. It typically presents with rapid onset, mood symptoms predominating over neurologic findings, and responds well to antidepressant treatment including SSRIs.
Source: 5A #25
An elderly woman underwent a DEXA scan within the past year demonstrating stable bone density with a T-score of -2.3. She is still active in her community but lives alone. BMI is 21 kg/m². She has never smoked cigarettes and does not drink alcoholic beverages. Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination discloses no abnormalities. In addition to refilling her prescription for alendronate, which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Obtain depression screening B) Obtain serum thyroid-stimulating hormone concentration C) Order mammography D) Order mental status examination E) Schedule a follow-up DEXA scan now
Correct Answer: A) Obtain depression screening
Depression screening is a routine preventive health measure recommended for all adults, particularly elderly adults who are underscreened for depression. Since depression is relatively prevalent in older adults and easily treatable, routine depression screening should occur as standard preventive care. Patients older than age 65 years should also be counseled about a healthy lifestyle, including regular aerobic exercise, a diet heavy in healthy fats, and smoking cessation. Choice B (Obtain serum thyroid-stimulating hormone concentration) is not a routine screening test in older patients. TSH derangements typically present with physical examination findings such as skin or hair changes, and this patient's physical examination disclosed no abnormalities. Choice C (Order mammography) is unlikely to be beneficial in patients with a life expectancy of less than 5 years. This patient's advanced age makes breast cancer screening unlikely to be helpful. Choice D (Order mental status examination) is incorrect because any physician can perform a mental status examination without a formal order. Physicians should regularly monitor their patients' mental status for behavior, affect, mood, and cognitive changes during routine visits. Choice E (Schedule a follow-up DEXA scan now) is not necessary. DEXA scans should be repeated every 2 years for patients with osteopenia or osteoporosis. This patient had a relatively recent DEXA scan within the past year with stable results.
Source: 5A #28
A 70-year-old man with type 2 diabetes mellitus and hypertension comes to the physician for a routine examination. Six months ago following a myocardial infarction, he underwent coronary artery bypass grafting that involved harvesting of the saphenous vein. He says he feels well. His medications are metformin, metoprolol, and lisinopril. Before his operation, he walked 3 miles daily; he has not resumed walking because he says he lacks the desire. On further questioning, he says he has not resumed any social activities because he feels tired on most days and does not want to leave his house. His blood pressure is 140/80 mm Hg; other vital signs are within normal limits. On examination, there is lower extremity edema bilaterally, which is worse on the left. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in diagnosis? A) Administration of the Geriatric Depression Scale B) Determination of ankle brachial indices C) Determination of erythrocyte sedimentation rate D) Doppler ultrasonography of the lower extremities E) Echocardiography
Correct Answer: A) Administration of the Geriatric Depression Scale
This patient is likely to meet criteria for major depressive disorder (MDD). Symptoms of MDD include 2 or more weeks of at least five of the following: depressed mood, anhedonia (decreased interest in exercising or socializing), guilt or worthlessness, difficulty concentrating, psychomotor slowing or agitation, suicidal thoughts, and neurovegetative symptoms (decreased energy, sleep disturbance, appetite disturbance). These symptoms disrupt everyday functioning. This patient demonstrates anhedonia and decreased energy despite his recovery from surgery several months ago. Notably, some patients—particularly men, those over age 65, and African American patients—may not initially report depressed mood and may present with primarily somatic symptoms. The Geriatric Depression Scale is an appropriate screening tool for this patient population. Treatment of MDD should include psychotherapy and antidepressant medication. Determination of ankle brachial indices (Choice B) would assist in diagnosing peripheral artery disease (PAD), which commonly presents with extremity pain with activity and weak pulses. Edema is uncommon in PAD, and this patient demonstrates a constellation of symptoms consistent with MDD. Determination of erythrocyte sedimentation rate (Choice C) would assess for systemic inflammation. Though commonly measured in patients with fatigue and focal inflammatory symptoms (e.g., joint pain), this patient's fatigue is likely caused by MDD given multiple suggestive symptoms. He lacks focal symptoms other than pedal edema, a known complication of coronary artery bypass surgery with saphenous vein harvesting. Doppler ultrasonography of the lower extremities (Choice D) assists in diagnosing deep vein thrombosis (DVT), which presents with unilateral lower extremity edema, skin pigmentation changes, pain, vein dilation, and venous ulcer formation. This patient shows bilateral pedal edema and lacks other typical DVT signs. Fatigue and anhedonia are not common DVT symptoms. Echocardiography (Choice E) evaluates for systolic or diastolic dysfunction, intravascular volume status, and pericardial effusion, commonly used to assess heart failure severity. Though bilateral pedal edema can indicate heart failure exacerbation, this patient feels physically well and does not report heart failure symptoms such as dyspnea or orthopnea. His bilateral pedal edema is likely secondary to coronary artery bypass surgery, as saphenous vein harvesting can lead to pedal edema. Additionally, anhedonia is more consistent with MDD than congestive heart failure.
Source: 2A #10
An 82-year-old woman comes to the physician because of difficulty sleeping at night for 6 months. She says she watches television or listens to talk radio at night because she cannot sleep. She has daytime fatigue and takes frequent naps. Her husband died 3 years ago, and she lost her pet dog of 11 years 8 months ago. She has had a 5.4-kg (12-lb) weight loss since her last examination 6 months ago. She is 173 cm (5 ft 8 in) tall and weighs 59 kg (130 lb); BMI is 20 kg/m². Her temperature is 37.5°C (99.5°F), pulse is 86/min, respirations are 10/min, and blood pressure is 150/84 mm Hg. She appears disheveled and has cracked, chapped lips. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. On mental status examination, her eye contact is poor. She answers questions with delayed, short responses and has difficulty finding the right words. She describes her activities during the past few days vaguely after being asked for specific details. When asked to draw the face of a clock and indicate 1:30 on the drawing, she requires 5 minutes to complete the clock face. She must be reminded that she was told to indicate 1:30, which she then proceeds to do correctly. She can recall two of three objects after 5 minutes. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Emphasize the importance of good sleep hygiene B) Recommend grief counseling C) Suggest she get a new household pet D) Begin donepezil therapy E) Begin sertraline therapy
Correct Answer: E) Begin sertraline therapy
This elderly patient presents with a major depressive episode characterized by difficulty sleeping, weight loss, psychomotor retardation, energy changes, poor concentration, and cognitive decline. Major depressive disorder requires at least five of nine DSM-5 criteria (including depressed mood or anhedonia plus additional symptoms such as guilt, energy changes, difficulty concentrating, appetite/activity changes, psychomotor retardation, sleep disturbances, and suicidality) for at least 2 weeks without an organic cause. The cognitive decline observed (clock-drawing test difficulty, word-finding problems, impaired recall) is secondary to her mood disorder rather than primary dementia. Treatment involves pharmacotherapy with selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) such as sertraline, which increase synaptic serotonin availability. Choice A (sleep hygiene) addresses only one symptom and ignores the broader depressive syndrome. Choice B (grief counseling) would be appropriate for uncomplicated grief, which typically lasts less than 6 months and does not meet full major depressive disorder criteria; this patient's symptoms have persisted 8 months after pet loss and 3 years after husband's death, indicating major depression rather than acute grief. Choice C (new pet) would be an overwhelming responsibility and does not address acute depressive symptoms. Choice D (donepezil) is a cholinesterase inhibitor used for Alzheimer dementia to slow cognitive decline, but this patient's cognitive impairment is secondary to depression, not primary dementia, and would improve with treatment of the underlying mood disorder.
Source: 2A #11
A 16-year-old girl is brought to the physician by her parents because she has become increasingly withdrawn during the past 5 months. She quit the swim team and no longer enjoys spending time with friends. Her parents report that she was previously an honor roll student, but her grades have dropped to Cs. When questioned about her behavior, she states that she is tired and her stomach hurts. She sleeps 10 to 12 hours each night and takes a nap after school most days. She has had a 5.4-kg (12-lb) weight loss during this time that she attributes to decreased appetite. She reports that she is not concerned about her weight loss and is not intentionally dieting. She often has nausea after eating. She says she is not depressed or upset about anything, but she becomes tearful when discussing her physical symptoms saying, "Maybe I have mono or something. I am just so tired." She has not had fever, sore throat, cough, or diarrhea. Menses occur at regular 28-day intervals. Her last menstrual period was 1 week ago. She reports that she drank alcohol once at a party. She does not use illicit drugs. She has been sexually active with one male partner for the past 6 months, and they usually use condoms. She appears tired. She is 165 cm (5 ft 5 in) tall and weighs 55 kg (122 lb); BMI is 20 kg/m². Her temperature is 37.2°C (98.9°F), pulse is 92/min, respirations are 15/min, and blood pressure is 108/70 mm Hg. Examination shows no lymphadenopathy. Abdominal examination shows no abnormalities. Her hematocrit is 39%, and leukocyte count is 7000/mm³. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Assessment for suicide risk B) Serologic testing for Epstein-Barr virus C) Thyroid function tests D) Urine pregnancy test E) Endoscopy
Correct Answer: A) Assessment for suicide risk
This adolescent patient presents with classic features of major depressive disorder, including sleep dysfunction (sleeping 10-12 hours nightly), weight loss (5.4 kg), decreased energy, anhedonia (loss of interest in swim team and friends), and social withdrawal over 5 months. Major depressive disorder requires at least five of nine DSM-5 criteria for at least 2 weeks without an organic cause. The criteria include sadness or anhedonia (at least one required), plus guilt, energy changes, difficulty concentrating, appetite/activity changes, psychomotor retardation, sleep disturbances, or suicidality. All patients with major depressive disorder must be assessed for suicide risk, as depression significantly increases suicidality risk. Risk factors include male sex, young adult or elderly age, depression, alcohol/drug use, lack of support, prior suicide attempts, and organized plans or stated intent. Suicide represents the greatest immediate risk in depressed patients; moderate to high-risk patients require immediate medical and psychiatric intervention with potential hospitalization. Treatment involves neurotransmitter modulation through SSRIs (e.g., sertraline) or agents blocking serotonin and norepinephrine reuptake (SNRIs, tricyclic antidepressants), combined with therapy. Incorrect answers: Serologic testing for Epstein-Barr virus (B) is unnecessary; while infectious mononucleosis causes fatigue, it typically presents with pharyngitis, fever, and lymphadenopathy—none present here. Thyroid function tests (C) would evaluate hypothyroidism/hyperthyroidism, which can cause fatigue but present with additional symptoms (cold intolerance, menorrhagia, hair loss, weight gain, constipation) absent in this patient. Urine pregnancy test (D), while necessary before prescribing teratogenic medications, is unlikely positive given her menstrual period 1 week ago; however, it is not the most appropriate immediate next step. Endoscopy (E) is unnecessary; her weight loss results from decreased appetite secondary to depression, not from malignant, inflammatory, or structural gastrointestinal pathology. Educational Objective: Major depressive disorder is characterized by sadness or anhedonia (at least one required) plus energy changes, difficulty concentrating, appetite/activity changes, psychomotor retardation, sleep disturbances, guilt, and/or suicidality. All patients with major depressive disorder require suicide risk assessment, as depression substantially increases suicidality risk.
Source: 2A #21
A 27-year-old man comes to the physician because of sadness, loss of appetite, and difficulty falling asleep since he and his wife separated 5 weeks ago. His energy level has decreased, and he no longer enjoys spending time with friends. He does not believe that his wife and he will be able to work through their conflicts but wishes they could. He has no history of serious illness. His sister has major depressive disorder well controlled with sertraline. The patient drinks one to two glasses of wine nightly. He does not use illicit drugs. He is 178 cm (5 ft 10 in) tall and weighs 104 kg (230 lb); BMI is 33 kg/m². Physical examination shows no abnormalities. On mental status examination, his mood is sad, and he is tearful. His thought process is logical. There is no evidence of suicidal ideation or hallucinations. Which of the following is the most appropriate treatment? A) Cognitive behavioral therapy B) Hypnotherapy C) Psychoanalysis D) Relaxation therapy E) Systematic desensitization
Correct Answer: A) Cognitive behavioral therapy
This patient meets criteria for major depressive disorder, requiring at least five of nine DSM-5 criteria for at least 2 weeks without an organic cause. His symptoms include sadness, anhedonia (loss of enjoyment with friends), decreased energy, sleep disturbance, and appetite loss. Cognitive behavioral therapy (CBT) is the most appropriate psychosocial treatment for major depressive disorder. CBT focuses on challenging harmful thoughts, beliefs, attitudes, and behaviors while developing coping strategies. In addition to therapy, many patients also require antidepressant medication, such as a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor (SSRI), particularly given his family history of major depressive disorder. All patients with depression should be evaluated for suicide risk, though this patient denies suicidal ideation. Other psychotherapeutic approaches have not demonstrated equivalent efficacy for major depressive disorder: hypnotherapy (B) lacks evidence for depression treatment; psychoanalysis (C) is lengthy and not first-line; relaxation therapy (D) is supportive but not primary treatment; and systematic desensitization (E) is specifically used for phobic disorders with severe, persistent fear of specific objects or situations, not depression.
▶ Cardiovascular System Congestive Heart Failure 3 questions
Source: 3A #1
An 80-year-old man comes to the office for a routine follow-up examination. He has been hospitalized four times during the past year for management of congestive heart failure. He follows a 2 g/d sodium-restricted diet. His blood pressure is 150/100 mm Hg; other vital signs are within normal limits. The lungs are clear to auscultation. On cardiac examination, no murmurs or gallops are heard. Serum studies show a potassium concentration of 3.8 mEq/L, total cholesterol concentration of 260 mg/dl, and triglyceride concentration of 300 mg/dl. These findings have been consistent during the past year. Which of the following is most likely to decrease this patient's risk for future hospitalization? A) Eating a diet restricted to 500 mg of sodium daily B) Eating a diet rich in potassium C) Improving control of blood pressure D) Improving control of cholesterol concentration E) Improving control of triglyceride concentration
Correct Answer: C) Improving control of blood pressure
The general management for chronic congestive heart failure involves treatment of the underlying cause (such as ischemic heart disease, valvular disease, reversible causes of cardiomyopathy), control of associated conditions (such as hypertension, diabetes mellitus, thyroid dysfunction, hyperlipidemia), and alleviation of symptoms. For this patient, improving control of blood pressure is most likely to decrease the risk for future hospitalizations. Hypertension is an important modifiable risk factor in patients with heart failure. Increased afterload puts additional strain on the left ventricle, leading to further cardiomyocyte structural remodeling and progression of ventricular failure. Maintaining a lower blood pressure improves cardiac output (along with symptoms of heart failure) and slows the rate of ventricular decline. Choice A (500 mg sodium diet) is not practical or efficacious for most patients in the outpatient setting. Patients should be counseled to avoid excessive salt intake to reduce intravascular volume retention, as this patient is currently doing with a 2 g/d diet. Choice B (potassium-rich diet) is unlikely to reduce the risk for future hospitalization in a patient with otherwise functional kidneys. Hypokalemia is a complication of diuretic therapy and may require potassium supplements, dietary modifications, or addition of a potassium-sparing diuretic. This patient's current potassium concentration of 3.8 mEq/L is within expected limits. Choices D and E (cholesterol and triglyceride control) are important for reducing the long-term risk for ischemic cardiovascular disease that could worsen the patient's heart failure, but improving control of blood pressure is more likely to help in the short term and reduce immediate hospitalization risk.
Source: 2A #41
Over the past 4 months, a 66-year-old woman has had progressive shortness of breath on exertion. For 2 weeks, she has had two-pillow orthopnea and paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea not associated with neck, chest, or arm pain. She has a history of untreated borderline hypertension. Her pulse is 96/min, respirations are 18/min, and blood pressure is 150/94 mm Hg. Examination shows 3-cm jugular venous distention. Bilateral inspiratory crackles are heard most prominently at both lung bases. There is a laterally displaced apical impulse and an S3 gallop without murmur. There is mild pitting edema bilaterally. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in diagnosis? A) Measurement of creatinine clearance B) Serial measurement of serum cardiac enzyme activities C) Measurement of left ventricular ejection fraction D) Thallium stress test E) Coronary artery angiography
Correct Answer: C) Measurement of left ventricular ejection fraction
The patient presents with classic congestive heart failure (CHF) characterized by progressive dyspnea on exertion, orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, jugular venous distention, bilateral pulmonary crackles, laterally displaced apical impulse, S3 gallop, and peripheral pitting edema. These findings indicate a volume-overloaded state. Chronic untreated hypertension is a significant risk factor for left ventricular hypertrophy, diastolic dysfunction, and CHF. The most appropriate next diagnostic step is measurement of left ventricular ejection fraction via echocardiography to establish the diagnosis and determine whether the patient has reduced or preserved ejection fraction, which guides subsequent management. Choice A (creatinine clearance) should be performed to assess for chronic kidney disease from hypertension, but cardiac etiology is more likely and should be evaluated first. Choice B (cardiac enzymes) would be indicated only if acute coronary syndrome were suspected, which is not suggested by the clinical presentation lacking chest pain or referred pain. Choices D (thallium stress test) and E (coronary angiography) may be considered after echocardiography to evaluate for ischemic heart disease contributing to heart failure, but the diagnosis must first be established by measuring ejection fraction. Educational Objective: Congestive heart failure classically presents with orthopnea, dyspnea on exertion, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, jugular venous distention, peripheral pitting edema, pulmonary crackles, and S3 or S4 gallop. Diagnostic evaluation should include echocardiography to assess for reduced or preserved ejection fraction.
Source: 4A #18
A 62-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 2-month history of shortness of breath with exertion. He also has had paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea and orthopnea. He has type 2 diabetes mellitus and coronary artery disease; he had a myocardial infarction 3 years ago. Current medications include simvastatin and aspirin. His pulse is 100/min, respirations are 12/min, and blood pressure is 100/60 mm Hg. Crackles are heard on auscultation. There is an S3. A chest x-ray shows cardiomegaly, cephalization of blood flow, and Kerley B lines. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacotherapy to improve this patient's chances of survival? A) Angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitor B) Calcium-channel blocking agent C) Digitalis D) Diuretic E) Nitrates
Correct Answer: A) Angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) inhibitor
This patient presents with classic signs and symptoms of heart failure with reduced ejection fraction (HFrEF): dyspnea on exertion, orthopnea, paroxysmal nocturnal dyspnea, bibasilar crackles, S3 gallop, tachycardia, hypotension, and chest x-ray findings of cardiomegaly, pulmonary edema (cephalization of blood flow), and Kerley B lines. The history of myocardial infarction 3 years ago is a significant risk factor for ischemic cardiomyopathy. Initial guideline-recommended therapy for HFrEF is a combination of a beta-adrenergic blocking agent, a renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (RAAS) inhibitor, and a diuretic. ACE inhibitors, beta-blockers, aldosterone receptor blockers (ARBs), and mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists have been proven to reduce mortality in patients with HFrEF. The question specifically asks for the most appropriate pharmacotherapy to improve survival, making ACE inhibitors the correct choice as they are first-line agents with mortality benefit. Incorrect choices: B) Calcium-channel blocking agents (diltiazem, verapamil) reduce cardiac contractility and cardiac output, potentially exacerbating heart failure and should be avoided. C) Digitalis (digoxin) improves symptoms through increased contractility but has not been shown to reduce mortality. D) Diuretics provide symptomatic relief by reducing preload but do not improve survival. E) Nitrates are second-line agents used only when patients cannot tolerate ACE inhibitors or ARBs; this patient has not yet had a trial of first-line therapy. Educational Objective: Primary pharmacotherapy for HFrEF includes a combination of beta-adrenergic blocking agents, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, and diuretics. Only beta-blockers, ACE inhibitors/ARBs, and mineralocorticoid receptor antagonists have demonstrated mortality reduction in HFrEF.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Celiac disease 3 questions
Source: 3A #19
A 32-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 3-month history of diarrhea and rash on her knees. During this time, she also has had a 3.2-kg (7-lb) weight loss despite a normal appetite. She says that 1 to 2 hours after almost every meal, she has severe "rumbling and gurgling noises in my belly" and diarrhea containing partially undigested food. After the diarrhea begins, she notices that small, itchy, painless "red spots" appear on her knees. She has not had joint pain. She has not traveled recently. She is 165 cm (5 ft 5 in) tall and weighs 52 kg (115 lb); BMI is 19 kg/m². Her temperature is 36.2°C (97.1°F), pulse is 78/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 112/62 mm Hg. Examination shows mildly pale conjunctivae. There are four areas of 5-mm, erythematous vesiculopapular lesions over the knees. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. Laboratory studies show: Hemoglobin 10.8 g/dL, Mean corpuscular volume 79 µm³, Erythrocyte sedimentation rate 16 mm/h, Serum ferritin 10 ng/mL. Test of the stool for occult blood is negative. Which of the following is the most likely mechanism of these clinical findings? A) Absence of gastric intrinsic factor B) Granulomatous inflammation restricted to the intestinal mucosa C) Granulomatous inflammation through the intestinal mucosa D) Lactase deficiency E) Presence of anti-tissue transglutaminase antibody
Correct Answer: E) Presence of anti-tissue transglutaminase antibody
Celiac disease is a gluten-sensitive enteropathy mediated through immune intolerance of the protein gliadin, commonly found in wheat products. This results in inflammatory infiltration of the proximal small bowel, leading to characteristic intraepithelial lymphocytosis, villous atrophy, and crypt hyperplasia. Malabsorption of substances in the proximal small bowel ensues, presenting symptomatically as chronic diarrhea, steatorrhea, and nutritional deficiencies. Patients with celiac disease may develop iron deficiency anemia, evidenced here by decreased hemoglobin (10.8 g/dL), microcytosis (MCV 79 µm³), and low serum ferritin (10 ng/mL). Dermatitis herpetiformis is a vesiculobullous disorder characterized by small, tense vesicles located on extensor sites (elbows, knees, and buttocks), which are extremely pruritic. Dermatitis herpetiformis is associated with IgA deposition along the epidermal basement membrane and is strongly associated with celiac disease. The diagnosis of celiac disease is confirmed by detection of serum anti-transglutaminase antibodies or by small bowel biopsy demonstrating villous atrophy with intraepithelial lymphocytes and crypt hyperplasia. Incorrect answers: (A) Absence of gastric intrinsic factor occurs in pernicious anemia, leading to vitamin B12 malabsorption and macrocytic, megaloblastic anemia, not microcytic anemia or dermatitis herpetiformis. (B) Granulomatous inflammation restricted to the intestinal mucosa is characteristic of ulcerative colitis, which presents with bloody diarrhea, not vesiculopapular rash. (C) Granulomatous inflammation extending through the intestinal mucosa to the serosa is characteristic of Crohn disease, which presents with bloody diarrhea and abdominal pain, not dermatitis herpetiformis. (D) Lactase deficiency presents with abdominal cramping and diarrhea after lactose ingestion but is not associated with vesiculopapular rash or iron deficiency anemia. Educational Objective: Celiac disease is a gluten-sensitive enteropathy mediated through immune intolerance of gliadin. Patients present with malabsorption, steatorrhea, nutritional deficiencies, iron deficiency anemia, and may develop dermatitis herpetiformis. Diagnosis is confirmed by serum anti-transglutaminase antibodies or small bowel biopsy.
Source: 5A #17
A 31-year-old woman comes to the office because of a 2-month history of progressive fatigue and lack of energy. During this time, she also has had a 2.3-kg (5-lb) weight loss despite no changes in appetite or exercise. She has a 2-year history of frequent diarrhea. She has not had any abdominal pain but does have flatulence, which is worse when she runs. She is an avid runner and is preparing for her sixth marathon. Her medications are an oral contraceptive and over-the-counter loperamide as needed. She traveled to Peru 8 months ago. She is 168 cm (5 ft 6 in) tall and weighs 66 kg (145 lb); BMI is 23 kg/m². Her temperature is 37.2°C (99.0°F), pulse is 60/min, respirations are 12/min, and blood pressure is 110/64 mm Hg. Examination shows no abnormalities. Laboratory studies show: Hemoglobin 11 g/dL, Hematocrit 33%, Mean corpuscular volume 72 μm³, Leukocyte count 6700/mm³, Serum ferritin 30 ng/mL. Results of a basic metabolic profile are within the reference ranges. Which of the following is the most likely mechanism of this patient's diarrhea? A) Amyloid infiltration of the bowel B) Bacterial invasion of the bowel wall C) Enzyme deficiency D) Intestinal hypermotility E) Villous atrophy F) Vitamin deficiency
Correct Answer: E) Villous atrophy
Villous atrophy in the small bowel is a characteristic finding of celiac disease, a gluten-sensitive enteropathy mediated through immune intolerance of the protein gliadin found in wheat products. This results in inflammatory infiltration of the proximal small bowel, leading to characteristic intraepithelial lymphocytosis, villous atrophy, and crypt hyperplasia. Malabsorption of substances in the proximal small bowel ensues, presenting symptomatically as chronic diarrhea, weight loss, steatorrhea, and nutritional deficiencies. Iron deficiency is a common cause of anemia in celiac disease, presenting with microcytosis (mean corpuscular volume <80 μm³) and decreased serum ferritin, as seen in this patient. Abnormal small bowel absorption results in abnormal serum D-xylose (indicating reduced mucosal surface area) and elevated fecal fat (indicating lipid malabsorption). Amyloid infiltration (Choice A) occurs with amyloidosis, typically seen in older patients with multiple myeloma and kidney/cardiac involvement, not in this younger healthy woman. Bacterial invasion (Choice B) causes acute gastrointestinal illness with bloody diarrhea and toxic presentation, not the chronic nonbloody diarrhea seen here. Enzyme deficiency (Choice C) such as pancreatic insufficiency or lactase deficiency would present with a history of pancreatitis or specific food triggers, neither present here. Intestinal hypermotility (Choice D) in irritable bowel syndrome does not cause malabsorption or nutritional deficiencies. While celiac disease causes vitamin deficiency (Choice F), vitamin deficiency itself does not explain the gastrointestinal symptoms; the underlying villous atrophy is the primary mechanism. Educational Objective: Celiac disease is a gluten-sensitive enteropathy resulting in inflammatory infiltration of the proximal small bowel with characteristic intraepithelial lymphocytosis, villous atrophy, and crypt hyperplasia, leading to malabsorption presenting as chronic diarrhea, weight loss, steatorrhea, and nutritional deficiencies.
Source: 2A #35
A 37-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 3-month history of generalized fatigue. She has noticed a decreased energy level, which is most pronounced at the end of the day. She has a 3-year history of abdominal bloating and intermittent diarrhea, especially after eating pasta. Her appetite has not changed, and she has had no weight loss. Menses occur at regular 28-day intervals with light flow for 2 days. She is 168 cm (5 ft 6 in) tall and weighs 70 kg (155 lb); BMI is 25 kg/m². Her pulse is 80/min, and blood pressure is 110/80 mm Hg. Examination shows moderately pale oral mucosa. The abdomen is distended and tympanic; there is no tenderness or organomegaly. Rectal examination shows no abnormalities. Test of the stool for occult blood is negative. Laboratory studies show: Hematocrit 30%, Mean corpuscular volume 78 µm³, Leukocyte count 6400/mm³, Platelet count 450,000/mm³. Which of the following is most likely to confirm the underlying cause of her symptoms? A) Measurement of serum folic acid concentration B) Schilling test C) Serum Helicobacter pylori antibody assay D) CT scan of the pancreas E) Colonoscopy F) Assay for anti-transglutaminase antibodies
Correct Answer: F) Assay for anti-transglutaminase antibodies
Celiac disease is a gluten-sensitive enteropathy mediated through immune intolerance of gliadin, commonly found in wheat products such as pasta. This results in inflammatory infiltration of the proximal small bowel, leading to intraepithelial lymphocytosis, villous atrophy, and crypt hyperplasia. Malabsorption of substances in the proximal small bowel ensues, presenting as chronic diarrhea, steatorrhea, and nutritional deficiencies. Patients with celiac disease may develop iron deficiency anemia, evidenced by decreased hematocrit and microcytosis (MCV 78 µm³). The diagnosis is confirmed by detection of serum anti-transglutaminase antibodies or small bowel biopsy demonstrating villous atrophy with intraepithelial lymphocytes and crypt hyperplasia. Treatment includes avoidance of gluten-containing foods and reversal of nutritional deficiencies. Incorrect choices: (A) Serum folic acid measurement is useful for macrocytic anemia; celiac disease patients do not typically develop folate deficiency. (B) The Schilling test evaluates vitamin B12 absorption in pernicious anemia causing macrocytic anemia, not the microcytic anemia present here. (C) Helicobacter pylori antibody assay evaluates gastric ulcers; while these cause microcytic anemia from chronic blood loss, the patient's history of bloating and diarrhea after gluten ingestion is more suggestive of celiac disease. (D) CT pancreas evaluates pancreatitis or pancreatic cancer; while pancreatitis causes malabsorption and diarrhea, this patient lacks abdominal pain or steatorrhea. (E) Colonoscopy evaluates microcytic/normocytic anemia from chronic blood loss in colon adenocarcinoma; the patient's gluten-related symptoms are more suggestive of celiac disease. Educational Objective: Patients with celiac disease may develop iron deficiency anemia. Diagnosis is confirmed by serum anti-transglutaminase antibodies or small bowel biopsy showing villous atrophy with intraepithelial lymphocytes and crypt hyperplasia.
▶ Endocrine System Diabetes mellitus, type 2 3 questions
Source: 3A #37
A 45-year-old man with hypertension and sleep apnea comes to the office for a follow-up visit 4 weeks after an evaluation for polydipsia and polyuria disclosed a nonfasting serum glucose concentration of 220 mg/dL, an LDL-cholesterol concentration of 200 mg/dL, and hemoglobin A1c of 8.5%. His only medication is lisinopril. The patient was advised to begin using a non-invasive positive pressure airway machine three months ago but does not use the machine nightly as recommended. During the initial evaluation, the patient was counseled on lifestyle modifications including weight loss, diet, and exercise. He has smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for the past 30 years and works as a stockbroker. He is 178 cm (5 ft 10 in) tall and weighs 120 kg (265 lb); BMI is 38 kg/m². His blood pressure is 150/92 mm Hg; the remainder of vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. In addition to initiating metformin therapy, which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation for this patient? A) Adjustment of medication to achieve target blood pressure B) Adjustment of medication to achieve target hemoglobin A1c C) Examination of the feet in the office every 3 to 4 months D) Ophthalmologic examination every 3 years
Correct Answer: A) Adjustment of medication to achieve target blood pressure
This patient has newly diagnosed type 2 diabetes mellitus and has been initiated on metformin therapy, which is first-line therapy for type 2 diabetes with normal kidney function. The goal blood pressure for patients with type 2 diabetes is less than 130/80 mm Hg, as this is correlated with reduction in cardiovascular morbidity and mortality. This patient's blood pressure of 150/92 mm Hg is above goal. He is already on lisinopril, an ACE inhibitor, which is appropriate for type 2 diabetes with hypertension. The next step is to increase lisinopril or add a second agent such as a calcium channel blocker or thiazide diuretic to achieve target blood pressure. Choice B is incorrect because adjusting medication for hemoglobin A1c would be premature; the patient was diagnosed only 1 month ago, and A1c reflects 3-month glucose average and should be rechecked in 2 months. Choice C is incorrect because dedicated foot examination with monofilament testing is indicated once yearly, not every 3-4 months, though feet should be checked for onychomycosis or ulcers at every visit. Choice D is incorrect because patients with diabetes should have annual ophthalmologic examination for retinopathy screening, not every 3 years. Educational Objective: The goal blood pressure for patients with diabetes mellitus is less than 130/80 mm Hg. Medication classes for blood pressure management include ACE inhibitors, angiotensin receptor blockers, thiazide diuretics, and calcium channel blockers.
Source: 5A #36
A 28-year-old woman, who was diagnosed with type 2 diabetes mellitus 1 month ago, comes to the office as a new patient. She recently moved to the area. The diagnosis was made based on a 2-week history of excessive urination and a nonfasting serum glucose concentration of 225 mg/dL. She is currently asymptomatic. She has no other history of serious illness. Her only medication is metformin. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows no abnormalities. Today, her hemoglobin A1c is 6.1%, and nonfasting serum glucose concentration is 135 mg/dL. Which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation for this patient to prevent disease-related morbidity? A) Annual eye examination B) Exercise stress test C) Herpes zoster vaccination D) Monthly determination of hemoglobin A1c E) Pneumococcal vaccination
Correct Answer: A) Annual eye examination
According to 2023 United States Preventive Services Task Force guidelines, patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus require comprehensive annual screening and preventive care to prevent disease-related morbidity. Annual eye examination is the most appropriate recommendation for this patient to detect diabetic retinopathy early. Other annual recommendations include foot examination, major depressive disorder screening, weight management counseling, and tobacco cessation counseling. Adults with type 2 diabetes should also be screened for high blood pressure at every medical visit, and annual urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio testing and serum creatinine measurement should be performed to detect early-stage kidney disease. Lipid profile screening should be done at least every 5 years. Exercise stress test (Choice B) is not appropriate for this asymptomatic patient with no signs or symptoms of cardiovascular disease, no chest pain, and no ECG findings suggestive of coronary artery disease, despite the increased cardiovascular risk in diabetes. Herpes zoster vaccination (Choice C) is recommended for immunocompetent individuals over age 50. This 28-year-old patient with no history of HIV, cancer, or autoimmune disease does not yet meet criteria for this vaccination. Monthly determination of hemoglobin A1c (Choice D) is not appropriate. Hemoglobin A1c measures average glycosylation of red blood cells over 90-120 days, providing a reliable estimate of average blood glucose over the preceding three months. Routine screening should occur every 3 months, not monthly. Pneumococcal vaccination (Choice E) is recommended for patients over age 65 or those who are asplenic. This patient does not meet these criteria.
Source: 4A #7
A 45-year-old man, who was diagnosed with type 2 diabetes mellitus 5 years ago, comes to the office for a visit. His diabetes mellitus has been well controlled. At his last visit 3 months ago, he had a complete metabolic panel, serum lipid studies, and screening of the urine for microalbumin. In addition to measuring this patient's hemoglobin A1c, which of the following is the most appropriate next step in evaluation at today's visit? A) Foot examination B) Fasting serum glucose concentration C) Liver function tests D) Serum lipid studies E) Urine microalbumin concentration
Correct Answer: A) Foot examination
The United States Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) recommends various screening tests and interventions for patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus. Adults with type 2 diabetes mellitus should be screened for high blood pressure at every medical visit. Lipid profile screening should be done at least every 5 years to measure total cholesterol, low-density lipoprotein cholesterol, high-density lipoprotein cholesterol, and triglycerides. An annual urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio test and blood test to measure serum creatinine should be performed to detect early-stage kidney damage. Comprehensive eye examination, foot examination, depression screening, weight management counseling, and tobacco cessation counseling are also recommended annually. Given that this patient has had all the above studies except for a foot examination at the visit 3 months prior, this is the most appropriate step at this time. Fasting serum glucose concentration (Choice B) is not necessary as hemoglobin A1c measurement provides sufficient information about blood glucose control over the last 3 months. Liver function tests (Choice C) are not routinely evaluated in patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus unless there is reason to suspect liver damage. Serum lipid studies (Choice D) and urine microalbumin concentration (Choice E) are not necessary as the USPSTF recommends these once yearly, and this patient just had these tests 3 months prior with results within reference ranges. Educational Objective: Patients with type 2 diabetes mellitus should receive routine screening of hemoglobin A1c every 3 months, blood pressure screening at every visit, lipid profile screening every 5 years, and annual kidney function testing, eye examination, and foot examination based on the most recent United States Preventive Services Task Force guidelines.
▶ Respiratory System Asthma 3 questions
Source: 5A #44
A 9-year-old girl is brought to the physician because of daily expiratory wheezing for 2 weeks. She has also had an occasional cough during exercise and at night. She has had asthma for 2 years treated with inhaled albuterol. Examination shows bilateral expiratory wheezes. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in pharmacotherapy? A) Inhaled corticosteroids B) Inhaled cromolyn sodium C) Oral leukotriene inhibitor D) Oral prednisone E) No additional pharmacotherapy indicated
Correct Answer: A) Inhaled corticosteroids
Asthma is characterized by reversible obstruction of the bronchi secondary to hyperreactivity and airway inflammation, presenting with wheezing, dry cough, and dyspnea. Severity is graded based on frequency of daytime and nighttime symptomatic episodes. Intermittent asthma (symptoms <2 days/week and <2 nights/month) requires only as-needed short-acting bronchodilators. Mild persistent asthma (symptoms ≥2 days/week or ≥2 nights/month) requires the addition of daily inhaled low-dose corticosteroids (e.g., fluticasone). Moderate persistent asthma requires inhaled long-acting β2-agonists (e.g., salmeterol) added to low-dose inhaled corticosteroids. Severe persistent asthma requires high-dose corticosteroids. This patient exhibits daily wheezing and nighttime cough, consistent with mild persistent asthma, making inhaled corticosteroids the most appropriate next step. Inhaled cromolyn sodium (Choice B) prevents mast-cell degranulation but is not first-line for mild persistent asthma. Oral leukotriene inhibitors (Choice C) such as montelukast prevent smooth muscle contraction and airway edema but are less effective than inhaled corticosteroids for mild persistent asthma. Oral prednisone (Choice D) is reserved for acute exacerbations or severe persistent asthma, not long-term management of mild disease. No additional pharmacotherapy (Choice E) is inappropriate; mild persistent asthma requires controller agents in addition to as-needed short-acting bronchodilators.
Source: 2A #6
A 19-year-old college student comes to the physician because of a 3-month history of cough that occurs only in midafternoon and evening and is not associated with any other symptoms. The cough seems to be somewhat worse in cold weather but does not wake her from sleep or prevent her from performing routine activities. She has no personal or family history of medical disease. She has never been hospitalized. Examination and an x-ray of the chest show no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Asthma B) Cocaine use C) Granulomatosis with polyangiitis D) Sarcoidosis E) Tuberculosis F) Vocal cord polyp
Correct Answer: A) Asthma
Asthma, particularly cough variant asthma, is the most likely diagnosis in this patient. Asthma often presents with episodic wheezing and shortness of breath with or without a cough, but it can also present with an isolated cough. The patient's presentation of a chronic, episodic cough without systemic symptoms or radiographic abnormalities is classic for cough variant asthma. Patients should be assessed for other atopic symptoms such as allergic rhinitis or eczema as these conditions commonly occur in conjunction with asthma. Other causes of chronic cough include gastroesophageal reflux disease and rhinosinusitis. Diagnosis is made through spirometry with measurement of the one-second forced-expiratory volume (FEV1) and forced vital capacity (FVC). Since asthma is a reversible and episodic condition, spirometry is frequently normal unless the patient is exhibiting symptoms at the time of testing. Therefore, most patients undergo a methacholine challenge test. Methacholine is a potent stimulator of bronchoconstriction, and as asthma is defined by airway hyperreactivity, patients with asthma experience bronchospasm at lower doses of methacholine than patients without asthma. An improvement in FEV1 by at least 12% and 200 mL with bronchodilators is diagnostic of asthma. Treatment depends on severity but may include short-acting bronchodilators and/or inhaled corticosteroids. Cocaine use (Choice B) does not generally cause a cough. Chronic use can result in nasal congestion and erosions of the nasal septum, but cough is not frequently encountered apart from cases of smoked cocaine, which is a less common form of use. Sarcoidosis (Choice D) may present with a cough, but chest radiographs commonly demonstrate bilateral hilar adenopathy with or without upper-lobe predominant coarse interstitial infiltrates, which are absent in this case. Tuberculosis (Choice E) is unlikely given her normal chest x-ray. Additionally, she has no explicit risk factors such as immigration from an endemic country or exposure to the homeless or prison population. Vocal cord polyp (Choice F) is more likely to cause voice hoarseness, not a chronic cough. Granulomatosis with polyangiitis (Choice C) is a pulmonary-renal syndrome presenting with cough with scant hemoptysis and kidney dysfunction (nephritic syndrome), with concomitant nasal congestion frequently seen. Educational Objective: Asthma may present with an isolated cough in the absence of wheezing or shortness of breath. Diagnosis is made by bronchial provocation with methacholine followed by a response to bronchodilators. Patients with mild intermittent asthma may be treated with short-acting bronchodilators, but patients with moderate or severe disease often require further treatment with inhaled corticosteroids and/or long-acting bronchodilators.
Source: 4A #35
A 9-year-old boy is brought to the physician by his mother because of a 1-month history of intermittent wheezing and mild shortness of breath. The mother reports that his symptoms occur once or twice weekly, occasionally before exercise, and never at night. When his symptoms occur, he must sit and rest. He has no history of serious illness or known allergies. He receives no medications. His older brother and younger sister have asthma. His parents do not smoke cigarettes. The patient's temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 92/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 90/50 mm Hg. Pulse oximetry on room air shows an oxygen saturation of 99%. Examination shows enlarged, pale nasal turbinates; mildly enlarged tonsils; and shotty cervical lymphadenopathy. Cardiopulmonary examination shows no abnormalities. Spirometry shows a peak expiratory flow rate of 200 L/min (88% of predicted). In addition to short-acting β-adrenergic agonist therapy, which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacotherapy? A) Leukotriene therapy B) Low-dose corticosteroid and long-acting β-adrenergic agonist therapy C) Low-dose corticosteroid therapy D) Medium-dose corticosteroid therapy E) No additional pharmacotherapy is indicated
Correct Answer: E) No additional pharmacotherapy is indicated
Asthma is characterized by reversible obstruction of the bronchi secondary to hyperreactivity and airway inflammation. Management is dictated by severity level: intermittent, mild persistent, moderate persistent, moderate-to-severe persistent, and severe persistent. Severity depends on the prevalence of exacerbations/symptoms, night awakenings, and impairment of daily functions. This patient has symptoms 1-2 times weekly, never has nighttime awakenings, and no reported limitations to daily life from symptoms. With a strong family history of asthma, intermittent asthma is the most likely diagnosis. Patients with intermittent asthma should be given a rescue inhaler of a short-acting β-adrenergic agonist (SABA) only; no other treatment is indicated at this time. Choice A (Leukotriene therapy) would be appropriate for mild persistent asthma (>2 episodes/week, 3-4 night awakenings monthly, SABA use >2 days weekly). Choice B (Low-dose corticosteroid and long-acting β-adrenergic agonist) would be appropriate for moderate persistent asthma (daily episodes, >1 night awakening weekly, daily SABA use). Choice C (Low-dose corticosteroid) would be appropriate for mild persistent asthma. Choice D (Medium-dose corticosteroid) would be appropriate for moderate persistent asthma. Educational Objective: Patients with intermittent asthma suffer from symptoms two or fewer times weekly, less than two nighttime awakenings from symptoms monthly, and no interference with daily activities. These patients should be treated with a SABA rescue inhaler only.
▶ Cardiovascular System Stable Angina Pectoris 2 questions
Source: 3A #2
A 47-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 4-month history of intermittent substernal chest pain with exertion. She describes the pain as pressure-like and says it is relieved with rest. She does not have shortness of breath, nausea, or sweating during the episodes. She says the episodes do not seem to be occurring more frequently or with less exertion. She has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. She does not smoke. Her pulse is 90/min, and blood pressure is 150/90 mm Hg. Cardiac examination shows no abnormalities. An ECG shows no abnormalities. The patient is reluctant to undergo cardiac catheterization. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) 24-Hour ambulatory ECG monitoring B) Measurement of serum creatine kinase activity and troponin I concentration C) Echocardiography D) Exercise stress test E) MUGA scan F) Aspirin and metoprolol therapy
Correct Answer: D) Exercise stress test
The patient presents with classic stable angina pectoris: substernal pressure-like chest pain occurring with exertion and relieved by rest, without associated dyspnea, nausea, or diaphoresis. The symptoms are stable and not worsening. The exercise stress test is the most appropriate next diagnostic step for risk stratification in patients with suspected obstructive coronary artery disease (CAD). Angina results from a mismatch between myocardial oxygen demand during stress and oxygen supply limited by coronary artery blockages. While coronary catheterization is the gold standard for diagnosis, stress testing is typically performed first for risk stratification. Various stress testing modalities exist (exercise treadmill, exercise nuclear, pharmacologic), with exercise stress testing being appropriate for this patient who is capable of exercising. Choice A (24-hour ambulatory ECG monitoring) is indicated for suspected arrhythmias with palpitations, not for angina evaluation. Choice B (serum creatine kinase and troponin I) is indicated for acute coronary syndrome presenting with chest pain unrelieved by rest and ECG changes (ST elevation/depression), not chronic stable angina. Choice C (Echocardiography) is reserved for suspected heart failure or valvular disease and is not the first-line test for angina evaluation. Choice E (MUGA scan) assesses ejection fraction and wall motion abnormalities suggestive of prior myocardial infarction but is not used for initial angina evaluation. Choice F (Aspirin and metoprolol therapy) would be appropriate after stress testing confirms obstructive CAD, but initiating therapy before diagnostic confirmation is premature. All patients with obstructive CAD should receive aspirin and a statin, with beta-blockers, calcium channel blockers, and nitrates used for symptom management. Educational Objective: Patients with symptoms consistent with angina pectoris should undergo stress testing for further evaluation and risk stratification. Those capable of exercising should undergo either exercise treadmill stress testing or exercise nuclear stress testing.
Source: 4A #5
A 55-year-old woman comes to the office as a new patient because she was dissatisfied with her last physician. Four months ago, she had the onset of moderate chest pain. Cardiac stress scintigraphy showed a small area of reversible ischemia. The patient was diagnosed with stable angina; after beginning metoprolol therapy, her chest pain decreased from twice to once weekly. She has no other history of serious illness and takes no other medications. Her temperature is 36.9°C (98.4°F), pulse is 62/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 115/70 mm Hg. Examination shows no abnormalities. Her previous physician recommended that she undergo cardiac catheterization, but she declined after researching the procedure on the Internet. She says she has read that medication alone is an acceptable alternative to cardiac catheterization and that she is unwilling to accept the risks associated with the procedure. She agrees to add atorvastatin to her medication regimen. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Attempting to persuade the patient to undergo catheterization B) Referral to a cardiologist C) Repeat cardiac stress scintigraphy D) Stress echocardiography E) No further management is indicated at this time
Correct Answer: E) No further management is indicated at this time
This patient with stable angina that improved with medical management and a cardiac stress test demonstrating a small area of reversible ischemia does not warrant any further diagnostic testing at this time. The primary indications for cardiac catheterization in patients with stable angina include angina that is severe and interferes with the patient's daily living or a stress test that indicates a large area of affected and viable myocardium. In patients with mild stable angina, initial medical management is appropriate, and beta-adrenergic blocking agents are recommended as first-line medical therapy. Beta-adrenergic blocking agents reduce both pulse and cardiac contractility, thereby reducing cardiac oxygen demand and improving anginal symptoms. Additionally, all patients with stable angina should be started on a statin and an antiplatelet agent to reduce risk for disease progression and future adverse cardiac events. This patient appears to have received appropriate counseling about the catheterization and has a thorough and stable understanding of the risks and benefits associated with the procedure. Given appropriate counseling and decision-making capacity, her choice should be both documented and respected. Attempting to persuade the patient to undergo catheterization (Choice A) is not appropriate. This patient's angina is mild, does not interfere with her daily living, and has improved since starting medical therapy. Furthermore, her stress test indicated only a small area of reversible ischemia. Medical management alone is reasonable in this patient, and trying to persuade her to undergo the procedure contradicts her autonomy for something that may be medically unnecessary. Referral to a cardiologist (Choice B) is not currently necessary as this patient has refused cardiac catheterization. If her disease progresses to the point that cardiac catheterization is needed, a cardiology consult would be warranted. Repeat cardiac stress scintigraphy (Choice C) and stress echocardiography (Choice D) are not indicated in this situation. This patient has already undergone a positive stress test that showed a small area of reversible ischemia. Repeating a stress test would not provide any additional information that would alter the patient's management. If a period of time passes in which disease is likely to progress, or if the patient develops symptoms suggestive of worsening ischemia, evocative testing could be repeated. Educational Objective: Primary indications for cardiac catheterization in a patient with stable angina include angina that is severe and interferes with the patient's daily living, or a stress test that indicates a large area of affected and viable myocardium. In patients with mild stable angina, medical management alone is appropriate. Beta-adrenergic blocking agents are recommended as the initial therapy in patients with stable angina.
▶ Renal Urinary System Pyelonephritis 2 questions
Source: 3A #6
A 65-year-old man is brought to the physician because of a 4-day history of increasingly severe upper back pain, pain with urination, nausea, fever, and chills. During the past 2 days, he has been able to ingest only clear fluids. He has a 5-year history of benign prostatic hyperplasia and a 25-year history of hypertension. His medications are tamsulosin and lisinopril. He is alert and cooperative. His temperature is 38.5°C (101.3°F), pulse is 96/min and regular, respirations are 18/min, and blood pressure is 104/68 mm Hg. Examination shows dry mucous membranes. Abdominal examination shows no abnormalities. There is costovertebral angle tenderness bilaterally. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Intramuscular administration of an antibiotic followed by a course of oral antibiotic therapy B) Intravenous administration of antibiotics and CT scan of the abdomen C) Intravenous administration of fluids and antibiotics D) Oral antibiotic therapy and re-examination in 2 days E) Oral antibiotic therapy and re-examination in 1 week
Correct Answer: C) Intravenous administration of fluids and antibiotics
Acute pyelonephritis is a urinary tract infection that has spread to the kidneys, characterized by flank pain, fever, chills, nausea, vomiting, and costovertebral angle tenderness on physical examination. In men, benign prostatic hyperplasia contributes to urinary retention and incomplete bladder emptying, increasing the risk for urinary tract infections. If untreated, bacteria can travel up the ureter to infect the kidney. Urinalysis typically shows positive leukocyte esterase and nitrites with many WBCs and WBC casts on microscopy. Treatment includes antibiotics covering common pathogens, most commonly Escherichia coli and Gram-negative enteric flora. In patients able to tolerate oral medications and appearing well with stable vital signs, outpatient management with oral antibiotics is adequate. However, in patients unable to tolerate oral antibiotics, appearing unwell, or septic, intravenous fluids and antibiotics are indicated. This patient presents with signs of sepsis (fever, borderline hypotension at 104/68 mm Hg, tachycardia at 96/min) and has only tolerated clear fluids for 2 days, making him unable to tolerate oral antibiotics. He requires intravenous fluids for resuscitation and intravenous antibiotics. Choice A: Intramuscular administration is not first-line for pyelonephritis; patients receive either oral or intravenous antibiotics. Choice B: While intravenous antibiotics are indicated, a CT scan is unnecessary given the normal abdominal examination and clinical diagnosis of pyelonephritis; it is a costly and unnecessary test. Choices D and E: Oral antibiotic therapy is inappropriate in this patient who cannot tolerate oral intake and shows signs of sepsis requiring intravenous resuscitation.
Source: 5A #10
A 72-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 2-day history of moderate back pain, fever, chills, and nausea. During this time, he also has had a burning sensation with urination and urinary urgency. He has a 3-year history of benign prostatic hyperplasia characterized by incomplete voiding and intermittent urges to void during the night. His medications are tamsulosin and aspirin. He appears ill. His temperature is 39.1°C (102.3°F). Percussion of the back elicits moderate discomfort. Abdominal examination shows diffuse tenderness to palpation but no rebound or guarding. Urine dipstick testing shows 2+ blood, positive nitrites, and positive leukocyte esterase. Results of urine culture are pending. Which of the following is the most likely causal organism? A) Citrobacter freundii B) Escherichia coli C) Klebsiella pneumoniae D) Proteus mirabilis E) Staphylococcus saprophyticus
Correct Answer: B) Escherichia coli
Pyelonephritis occurs from retrograde spread of lower urinary tract infection. In men, benign prostatic hyperplasia and urinary outlet obstruction increase risk for urinary tract infections. Upper urinary tract infections present with systemic symptoms including rigors, nausea, vomiting, myalgias, arthralgias, fatigue, high fevers, tachycardia, leukocytosis, flank pain, and costovertebral angle tenderness on physical examination, as seen in this patient with discomfort on back percussion. Urinalysis in pyelonephritis shows greater than 10 leukocytes per high-power field and may show white blood cell casts. Gram-negative rods, especially Escherichia coli, are the most common pathogen. E. coli produces nitrites detectable on urinalysis, as demonstrated in this patient. Pyelonephritis is treated with broad-spectrum antibiotics. Citrobacter freundii (Choice A) causes hospital-acquired infections such as pneumonia, urinary tract infections, and sepsis. This patient has no risk factors for nosocomial infection and has community-acquired pyelonephritis, of which E. coli is most common. Klebsiella pneumoniae (Choice C) is an encapsulated gram-negative bacterium presenting with lobar pneumonia, especially in patients with diabetes or alcohol use disorder. It can cause nosocomial urinary tract infections. However, E. coli is the most common cause of community-acquired urinary tract infections, and this patient has no nosocomial risk factors. Proteus mirabilis (Choice D) is a nonlactose fermenting, oxidase-negative, gram-negative rod that frequently causes urinary tract disease. It is characterized by urease activity and hydrogen sulfide production on triple sugar iron agar. However, E. coli is more common than Proteus in causing urinary tract infections. Staphylococcus saprophyticus (Choice E) can cause community-acquired pyelonephritis and is a coagulase-negative staphylococcus that often causes urinary tract infections in women. However, S. saprophyticus is not a urease-splitting organism, so urinalysis would be unlikely to show positive nitrites. Positive nitrites, as in this patient, are more suggestive of E. coli. Educational Objective: Acute pyelonephritis typically presents with systemic symptoms such as fever, nausea, vomiting, and flank pain with associated costovertebral angle tenderness on physical examination. Pyelonephritis most commonly occurs from ascending urinary tract infections caused by Escherichia coli.
▶ Skin Subcutaneous Tissue Basal Cell Carcinoma 2 questions
Source: 3A #10
A 62-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a lesion on her right cheek; she has had the lesion for 1 year but it has gradually increased in size over the past 6 months. She has a history of heavy sun exposure. Examination shows a 12-mm lesion on the right cheek with a rolled, pearly border, telangiectasia, and a small central crust. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Basal cell epithelioma B) Keratoacanthoma C) Melanoma D) Metastatic cancer E) Squamous cell carcinoma
Correct Answer: A) Basal cell epithelioma
Basal cell carcinoma (epithelioma) is the most common form of skin cancer, derived from basal cells of the epidermis due to mutations in the sonic hedgehog pathway or p53 tumor suppressor. The primary risk factor is ultraviolet light exposure, though age, fair skin, and family history also contribute. Basal cell carcinomas typically present as pink, pearly papules or nodules with rolled borders and central ulceration, most commonly on sun-exposed areas of the head and neck. Subtypes include less invasive forms (superficial and nodular) and more invasive forms (infiltrative and morpheaform). The risk for metastasis is extremely low, though tumors can invade locally and cause significant tissue damage. Treatment requires surgical removal via excision or Mohs micrographic surgery. Keratoacanthoma (Choice B) is a subtype of squamous cell carcinoma with a unique crateriform or volcano-like appearance that arises very quickly over weeks and sometimes regresses spontaneously. Melanoma (Choice C) presents with asymmetry, irregular borders, variable coloration, diameter greater than 6 mm, and rapid evolution. Subtypes include superficial spreading, nodular, lentigo maligna, and acral lentiginous. Amelanotic melanoma lacks pigment and may initially resemble basal cell carcinoma but is less common. Metastatic cancer (Choice D) can present as a hard skin nodule with any cancer type. This patient's lesion is more likely basal cell carcinoma based on morphology and location in a distinctly sun-exposed area. Squamous cell carcinoma (Choice E) can often be differentiated from basal cell carcinoma by appearance alone. Squamous cell carcinoma tends to have a hyperkeratotic crust rather than a smooth, pearly surface, though pathologic diagnosis is needed for definitive differentiation. Educational Objective: Basal cell carcinoma is the most common form of skin cancer, typically presenting as pink, pearly papules or nodules with rolled borders, telangiectasias, and central ulceration. Treatment requires surgical removal via excision or Mohs micrographic surgery. Patients with a history of skin cancer should receive yearly skin cancer screening examinations.
Source: 5A #38
A 50-year-old man comes to the physician because of a nonitchy, painless, gradually enlarging lesion on his face that he first noticed 6 months ago. He has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows no other abnormalities. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Actinic keratosis B) Basal cell carcinoma C) Keratoacanthoma D) Seborrheic keratosis E) Squamous cell carcinoma
Correct Answer: B) Basal cell carcinoma
Basal cell carcinoma is the most common subtype of skin cancer and is associated with ultraviolet (UV) exposure. It clinically presents as a pearly papule with rolled borders, overlying telangiectasia, and may have focal erosion or ulceration. Patients often describe this lesion as 'the pimple that will not pop' or 'the wound that will not heal.' While basal cell carcinoma can be locally invasive, metastasis is rare. Clinical subtypes include superficial, nodular, pigmented, microcystic, and morpheaform types. Pathology shows islands of basaloid cells with peripheral palisading and retraction from the surrounding dermis. Treatment includes excision such as Mohs micrographic surgery, electrodessication and curettage, or topical imiquimod therapy. This patient's presentation of a gradually enlarging, painless lesion on sun-exposed skin is consistent with basal cell carcinoma. Actinic keratoses (Choice A) present as ill-defined, rough, scaly papules on sun-exposed sites and result from partial-thickness epidermal atypia. While associated with UV damage, they do not match this presentation. Keratoacanthoma (Choice C) is a variant of squamous cell carcinoma that presents as a rapidly enlarging, crateriform nodule with central hyperkeratotic core. Unlike this case, keratoacanthoma shows rapid growth and may involute spontaneously. Seborrheic keratosis (Choice D) is a common benign growth in middle-aged and elderly adults presenting as brown, sharply demarcated papules. These are not malignant and do not match the clinical presentation. Squamous cell carcinoma (Choice E) is the second most common skin cancer, presenting as a chronic, rough, eroded papule or nodule with overlying hyperkeratotic crust. While also associated with sun exposure, the clinical presentation described is more consistent with basal cell carcinoma. Educational Objective: Basal cell carcinoma is the most common form of skin cancer associated with chronic sun exposure. It clinically presents as a pearly papule with rolled borders and overlying telangiectasia. Although basal cell carcinoma rarely metastasizes, treatment is recommended as these tumors are locally destructive. Treatment options include excision, electrodessication and curettage, or topical imiquimod therapy.
▶ Behavioral Health Alcohol Use Disorder 2 questions
Source: 3A #12
A 26-year-old man comes to the office for a health maintenance examination. He describes drinking at least six beers nightly during the past 2 years. He has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. Vital signs are within normal limits and physical examination discloses no abnormalities. He is concerned about his health and is thinking about quitting drinking alcohol, but he is not sure if he is ready. Serum laboratory studies show: Alkaline phosphatase 65 U/L, AST 80 U/L, ALT 75 U/L. Which of the following strategies is most likely to motivate this patient to change his behavior? A) Acknowledge the discrepancy between his drinking and his desire to quit B) Convince the patient to accept the label that he is an alcoholic C) Describe the negative consequences of not following clinical advice D) Provide direction with a voice of authority E) Use humor whenever possible to lighten the mood
Correct Answer: A) Acknowledge the discrepancy between his drinking and his desire to quit
Motivational interviewing is a strategy in which the physician determines and then guides the patient to reflect on their willingness and motivation to change, and is helpful when discussing health behaviors with patients. By acknowledging the discrepancy between this patient's drinking and his desire to quit, the physician helps the patient realize the difference between his current behaviors and future goals. Including open-ended questions fosters discussion about the patient's motivations to change behavior—for example, asking what specific reasons he has for wanting to quit or what barriers prevent him from feeling ready. Even if the patient is not ready to stop a behavior, the physician should be direct and clear in explaining risks without making the patient feel guilty or defensive, while emphasizing willingness to partner with the patient when they decide to change. Convincing the patient to accept the label of 'alcoholic' (Choice B) will likely cause defensiveness or annoyance, reducing partnership effectiveness. Describing negative consequences (Choice C) and providing direction with authority (Choice D) are paternalistic strategies that may cause defensiveness, damage the physician-patient relationship, and deter future health behavior changes. Using humor (Choice E) may convey levity and the patient may misinterpret it as justification of his behavior. Educational Objective: Motivational interviewing is a useful strategy to help patients enact changes in health behaviors. The primary goals are to help the patient determine their readiness to change and identify steps to increase readiness to the point of actually making the change.
Source: 4A #16
A 37-year-old man with alcohol use disorder comes for an initial examination. He drinks approximately 12 cans of beer daily. Examination shows palmar erythema and multiple spider angiomas over the upper back and chest. The spleen is palpated 3 cm below the left costal margin. Abdominal ultrasound shows cirrhosis. Despite the physician's strong urging about drinking alcohol, he says he has no intention of abstaining. Which of the following is the most appropriate immunization for this patient? A) Acellular pertussis B) Haemophilus influenzae type b, conjugated C) Live Salmonella typhi D) Meningococcal E) Pneumococcal
Correct Answer: E) Pneumococcal
