Pneumococcal vaccine, available as either a 13-valent or 15-valent conjugate vaccine or as a 23-valent polysaccharide vaccine, protects against infection with Streptococcus pneumoniae. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention Advisory Committee on Immunization Practices recommends routine vaccination for protection against pneumococcal disease for infants, children, and adults 65 years of age or older. Pneumococcal disease is responsible for a large burden of morbidity and mortality. This patient should receive the pneumococcal 23-valent polysaccharide vaccine (PPSV23) because of his history of cirrhosis, which increases his risk for developing pneumococcal disease. Cirrhosis is an immunocompromising condition that increases susceptibility to encapsulated organisms including Streptococcus pneumoniae. Incorrect answers: Acellular pertussis (Choice A) vaccine protects against pertussis caused by Bordetella pertussis and is typically administered with tetanus and diphtheria vaccines. Cirrhosis does not confer increased risk for pertussis. Haemophilus influenzae type b, conjugated (Choice B) vaccine is indicated for asplenic patients at risk for infection with encapsulated bacteria, but cirrhosis alone does not confer additional risk for H. influenzae infection. Live Salmonella typhi (Choice C) is a live vaccine and would be contraindicated in an immunocompromised patient with cirrhosis. Meningococcal (Choice D) vaccine is not specifically indicated for cirrhosis. Educational Objective: Patients with cirrhosis have impaired immune function and are at increased risk for infection with encapsulated organisms, particularly Streptococcus pneumoniae. The pneumococcal 23-valent polysaccharide vaccine (PPSV23) is recommended for all patients with cirrhosis.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Tendinitis 2 questions
Source: 3A #13
A 29-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 3-week history of increasingly severe right shoulder pain. He says the pain begins as an ache at the front of his right shoulder and occasionally radiates down the front of his arm. He works as a lawyer and plays softball every weekend on his firm's team. He lifts weights at the gym. He sleeps well and is able to perform his usual work activities. Examination shows tenderness to palpation over the right bicipital groove. Flexion at the right elbow against resistance produces pain. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial step in management? A) 8-Day course of prednisone therapy B) MRI of the right shoulder C) Physical therapy D) Recommendation for rest from softball and weight lifting E) Sling immobilization of the right upper extremity for 3 days
Correct Answer: D) Recommendation for rest from softball and weight lifting
This patient presents with biceps tendinitis, characterized by anterior shoulder pain with tenderness over the bicipital groove and pain with elbow flexion against resistance. The biceps tendon consists of long and short heads; the long head originates from the glenoid edge and runs through the intertubercular groove of the humerus, making it more susceptible to injury from repetitive throwing, pulling, or lifting activities. Conservative management with activity modification and rest is the most appropriate initial intervention, allowing gradual reintroduction of activity. Choice A (Prednisone) is not standard treatment for biceps tendinitis as it does not address the underlying cause and carries potential side effects. Choice B (MRI) is premature and typically reserved for suspected tendon rupture or rotator cuff injuries prior to surgical intervention. Choice C (Physical therapy) may exacerbate symptoms during the acute phase; early aggressive therapy is inappropriate. Choice E (Sling immobilization for 3 days) is contraindicated as prolonged immobilization can lead to adhesions and tendon tightening, whereas early range of motion is generally appropriate for minor shoulder injuries. Educational Objective: Biceps tendinitis typically affects the long head of the biceps tendon as it traverses the intertubercular groove at the anterior shoulder. It results from repetitive throwing, pulling, or lifting and presents with anterior shoulder pain worse with supination and elbow flexion against resistance. Initial management focuses on rest and activity modification with gradual return to activity.
Source: 2A #33
A previously healthy 32-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 4-month history of constant pain on the outside of his right elbow at rest. The pain has not responded to use of nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs or a wrist splint. He is right-handed and recently began building an addition to his house, lifting heavy supplies. He is 175 cm (5 ft 9 in) tall and weighs 75 kg (165 lb); BMI is 24 kg/m². Examination shows severe tenderness of the bony right distal humerus 1 cm proximal to the radial head. Extension of the right wrist against resistance produces pain at the area of maximal tenderness. AP and lateral x-rays of the right elbow show no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Use of a forearm strap B) Oral vitamin B6 supplementation C) Oral corticosteroid therapy D) Intravenous corticosteroid therapy E) Surgical decompression
Correct Answer: A) Use of a forearm strap
The patient has lateral epicondylitis (tennis elbow), characterized by pain at the lateral epicondyle of the distal humerus with pain on wrist extension against resistance. The lateral epicondyle is the origin point of wrist extensor muscles. This condition commonly results from repetitive wrist extension under heavy load, as occurs with lifting heavy objects, particularly in individuals not conditioned for such activities. Initial treatment consists of activity modification and counterforce bracing (forearm strap/compression sleeve), which applies pressure distal to the origin of the wrist extensors to relieve pressure during wrist movement. Most patients improve with these conservative measures. Oral vitamin B6 supplementation is used for isoniazid-induced neuropathy, not tennis elbow. Oral and intravenous corticosteroids may provide temporary relief but are not typical first-line treatment and are not necessary when most patients respond to activity modification and bracing. Surgical decompression is reserved only for severe, refractory cases and would be premature at this stage of management.
▶ Cardiovascular System Hyperlipidemia 2 questions
Source: 3A #14
A 56-year-old man with hypertension comes to the office for a routine examination. He says he feels well. His only medication is lisinopril. He follows a low-cholesterol diet and walks 30 minutes daily. His temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), pulse is 75/min, respirations are 14/min, and blood pressure is 120/80 mm Hg. He is 183 cm (6 ft) tall and weighs 79 kg (175 lb); BMI is 24 kg/m². Examination shows no abnormalities. Laboratory studies show: Total cholesterol 194 mg/dL, HDL-cholesterol 35 mg/dL, LDL-cholesterol 135 mg/dL, Triglycerides 120 mg/dL. His estimated 10-year atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease risk is calculated at 8.3%. Addition of which of the following is the most appropriate next step in pharmacotherapy to prevent cardiovascular mortality in this patient? A) Atorvastatin B) Ezetimibe C) Gemfibrozil D) Niacin E) Omega-3 fish oil F) No change in pharmacotherapy is indicated
Correct Answer: A) Atorvastatin
The patient has increased total serum cholesterol (194 mg/dL) and LDL-cholesterol (135 mg/dL) with an estimated 10-year atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease (ASCVD) risk of 8.3%, which exceeds the 7.5% threshold for statin therapy initiation. Statin therapy (HMG-CoA reductase inhibitors such as atorvastatin) is strongly recommended for patients with an estimated 10-year ASCVD risk score greater than 7.5%, as it has been shown to significantly reduce cardiovascular mortality in this population. Before initiating therapy, secondary causes of hyperlipidemia should be evaluated (thyroid function, hemoglobin A1c, chemistry panel, and urinalysis). The patient should continue his low-cholesterol diet, daily physical activity, and maintain his lower-risk BMI. Ezetimibe (Choice B) inhibits cholesterol reabsorption in the small intestine and may be added as a second agent for suboptimally controlled hyperlipidemia, but statins have greater protective benefit as monotherapy. Gemfibrozil (Choice C), a fibrate, is effective for hypertriglyceridemia but is associated with increased LDL-cholesterol and is not typically used as monotherapy for ASCVD prevention. Niacin (Choice D) increases HDL-cholesterol and may be considered as a second agent but has less protective benefit than statins as monotherapy. Omega-3 fish oil (Choice E) may reduce inflammation and cholesterol but is used as a second-line agent with statins. No change in pharmacotherapy (Choice F) is inappropriate given the patient's ASCVD risk exceeding 7.5%.
Source: 5A #48
A 37-year-old woman, gravida 3, para 3, comes to the physician for a routine health maintenance examination. One year ago, her serum cholesterol concentration was 260 mg/dL, and she was started on a low-fat diet. She has no history of serious illness. Her Pap smears during the past 10 years have shown no abnormalities. Her last Pap smear was 1 year ago. Her mother has type 2 diabetes mellitus, and her father died of colon cancer at the age of 62 years. The patient does not smoke cigarettes or drink alcohol. She has been married for 15 years. She is 157 cm (5 ft 2 in) tall and weighs 77 kg (170 lb); BMI is 31 kg/m². Her pulse is 64/min, and blood pressure is 120/60 mm Hg. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Colonoscopy B) 5-Hour glucose tolerance test C) Mammography D) Measurement of serum cholesterol concentration E) Pap smear
Correct Answer: D) Measurement of serum cholesterol concentration
This 37-year-old woman with elevated serum cholesterol (260 mg/dL one year ago) who was started on a low-fat diet requires rechecking of her serum cholesterol concentration to assess response to lifestyle modifications. Increased serum concentrations of lipids, including low-density cholesterol, are associated with an increased risk for coronary vascular disease. The USPSTF recommends screening for hyperlipidemia in all men greater than age 35 years and in all women greater than age 45 years who are at an increased risk for coronary vascular disease. They also recommend initiating statin medication in persons age 40 to 75 years who are at an increased 10-year risk (>10%) of atherosclerotic cardiovascular disease. Other risk factors for coronary vascular disease include hypertension, type 2 diabetes mellitus, positive family history of coronary vascular disease, and smoking. This patient has multiple risk factors: elevated cholesterol, obesity (BMI 31), and family history of early coronary disease (father died of colon cancer at 62). Optimization of serum lipids with pharmacologic treatment and lifestyle modification can reduce the risk for coronary vascular disease and myocardial infarction. Statins remain first-line pharmacologic therapy for most patients with hyperlipidemia. This patient should have her serum cholesterol concentration rechecked after implementing lifestyle modifications 1 year ago, and if still increased, further interventions should be initiated. Colonoscopy (Choice A) is not currently indicated by age alone; USPSTF recommends routine colorectal cancer screening beginning at age 45 years. However, patients with family history of colon cancer should have first colonoscopy at age 40 or 10 years younger than family member's diagnosis age. This patient's father was 62, so screening at age 52 would be appropriate, not now. 5-Hour glucose tolerance test (Choice B) is not standard screening. USPSTF recommends screening for abnormal blood glucose or diabetes mellitus in patients age 35 to 70 years who are overweight or obese. Currently, fasting blood glucose or hemoglobin A1c measurements are more commonly used than glucose tolerance testing for initial screening. While this patient should be screened for diabetes given her obesity and family history, the 5-hour glucose tolerance test is not considered standard. Mammography (Choice C) is not indicated at this time. USPSTF recommends screening mammogram every 1 to 2 years for patients over age 50 years, with individualized decisions beginning at age 40 years. Pap smear (Choice E) is not indicated since the patient had one 1 year ago. USPSTF recommends Pap smear every 3 years in patients age 21 to 65 years or every 5 years in patients with normal Pap smear and negative human papillomavirus test.
▶ Skin Subcutaneous Tissue Dermatophytosis 2 questions
Source: 3A #16
A 64-year-old African American woman with type 2 diabetes mellitus comes to the office for a routine follow-up visit. The patient is concerned about increasingly dry skin on her feet. She takes metformin, atorvastatin, and enalapril. She has been trying to improve her diet and overall adherence to her diabetic treatment plan by following a carbohydrate counting plan. She is 163 cm (5 ft 4 in) tall and weighs 102 kg (225 lb); BMI is 39 kg/m². Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination of the feet shows linear, white, scaly areas with moist, raw skin between the toes. Which of the following is the most appropriate treatment for this patient's rash? A) Clotrimazole cream B) Hydrocortisone ointment C) Mupirocin cream D) Petrolatum-based ointment E) Thorough washing of the feet twice daily
Correct Answer: A) Clotrimazole cream
Tinea pedis is a common superficial cutaneous fungal infection caused by dermatophytes, most commonly Trichophyton rubrum. The most common presentations include a moccasin-pattern distribution of white scale around the edge of the sole or macerated patches between the toes, as seen in this patient. Comorbidities that decrease immune system function, such as diabetes mellitus, increase the risk for dermatophyte infection. Additionally, dermatophyte infections provide a portal of entry for bacterial infection, which in diabetic patients can lead to serious infections such as osteomyelitis. Diagnosis can be confirmed with potassium hydroxide preparation showing hyphae or fungal culture. First-line treatment of tinea pedis is with a topical antifungal, usually clotrimazole, terbinafine, or ketoconazole. Hydrocortisone (Choice B) is a topical corticosteroid that will worsen tinea pedis by inhibiting the inflammatory response necessary to eliminate the infection. Tinea treated with topical corticosteroids is often referred to as tinea incognito due to the severe and unusual morphologies these infections develop. Combined topical antifungal and corticosteroid products are not advised in treating tinea pedis or any dermatophyte infection. Mupirocin (Choice C) is a topical antibacterial agent inappropriate for fungal infections. Topical antibacterial therapy is appropriate for bacterial skin infections such as impetigo and folliculitis, not fungal infections. Petrolatum-based ointment (Choice D) and thorough washing of the feet twice daily (Choice E) will not treat the underlying dermatophyte infection causing tinea pedis. Educational Objective: Tinea pedis is caused by superficial dermatophyte infection of the skin, most often Trichophyton rubrum. Classic presentations include moccasin-distribution patches on the sole with scaly borders or macerated patches between the toes. Treatment is with topical antifungal agents such as clotrimazole or systemic antifungals if severe.
Source: 2A #26
A previously healthy 6-year-old boy comes to the physician because of a 1-week history of an itchy, nonpainful rash on his arm. His mother says that it began as a small red spot but has tripled in size since then. He has had no other symptoms. Which of the following is the most appropriate topical pharmacotherapy? A) Clindamycin B) Clotrimazole C) Hydrocortisone D) Permethrin E) Selenium sulfide
Correct Answer: B) Clotrimazole
Tinea corporis is a common superficial cutaneous fungal infection caused by dermatophytes, most commonly Trichophyton rubrum. It involves the body or extremities and presents as round, annular patches with scaly borders. Potassium hydroxide preparation will show hyphae, and fungal culture may confirm diagnosis. First-line treatment is with a topical antifungal, usually clotrimazole, terbinafine, or ketoconazole. Combined topical antifungal and corticosteroid products are not advised as corticosteroids worsen the disease by inhibiting the inflammatory response necessary to eliminate infection. Clindamycin (Choice A) is unnecessary as tinea corporis is a fungal infection, not bacterial. Topical antibacterial therapy is appropriate for bacterial infections like impetigo and folliculitis. Hydrocortisone (Choice C) is a topical corticosteroid that will worsen tinea corporis. Tinea treated with topical corticosteroids is referred to as tinea incognito due to severe and unusual morphologies that develop. Permethrin (Choice D) is an antiparasitic agent used for scabies infestations, which present with burrows and excoriated papules/nodules in web spaces of fingers, volar wrists, umbilicus, and genitalia—not annular scaly patches. Selenium sulfide (Choice E) is used in shampoos for seborrheic dermatitis caused by Malassezia yeast but is less effective against dermatophytes. Educational Objective: Tinea corporis is caused by superficial dermatophyte infection of the skin, most often Trichophyton rubrum, presenting as round annular patches with scaly borders. Treatment is with topical antifungals such as clotrimazole or systemic antifungals if widespread.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Irritable bowel syndrome 2 questions
Source: 3A #25
A 42-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 10-month history of intermittent diffuse abdominal pain. The pain has occurred more frequently during the past month and is not associated with any other symptoms or changes in stool color. She has not had diarrhea or loose stools during the past month. She has not noticed any precipitating factors but notes that she has four children and is always under stress. She has a 10-year history of constipation and often does not have a bowel movement for 4 or 5 days. She has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. Last year, she was evaluated for similar symptoms; colonoscopy and CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis showed no abnormalities. Examination today shows tenderness over the left lower abdomen with palpable fullness in the lower left quadrant. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in diagnosis? A) 72-Hour collection of stool for measurement of fecal fat B) Psychiatric assessment C) Measurement of serum carcinoembryonic antigen concentration D) Serum antigliadin antibody assay E) Schilling test F) Biopsy of the small bowel G) No further evaluation is indicated at this time
Correct Answer: G) No further evaluation is indicated at this time
Irritable bowel syndrome (IBS) is a functional gastrointestinal disorder characterized by intermittent, often cramping abdominal pain relieved by bowel movements, and altered bowel habits including diarrhea, constipation, and alternating episodes. Patients often report significant stress and may present with other stress-associated functional disorders such as fibromyalgia or chronic fatigue syndrome. This patient's presentation is classic for IBS: 10-month history of intermittent diffuse abdominal pain, chronic constipation (no bowel movements for 4-5 days), significant stress (four children), and prior negative colonoscopy and CT imaging ruling out structural abnormalities. IBS lacks alarm features such as onset after age 50, bloody diarrhea, weight loss, or nocturnal diarrhea that would warrant further investigation. Extensive diagnostic testing is not indicated for patients with signs and symptoms consistent with IBS. Most patients improve with reassurance, exercise, and dietary modifications; severe symptoms may benefit from pharmacotherapy for constipation or diarrhea. Incorrect choices: (A) 72-hour fecal fat collection is for malabsorption evaluation (pancreatic malignancy, gastrinoma, cystic fibrosis, celiac disease, small bowel bacterial overgrowth). (B) Psychiatric assessment may help IBS patients with mood disorders like generalized anxiety disorder or major depression but is not necessary for all IBS patients. (C) Serum carcinoembryonic antigen is a tumor marker with low sensitivity and specificity, inappropriate for initial screening. (D) Serum antigliadin antibody assay evaluates celiac disease, which presents with malabsorption, steatorrhea, nutritional deficiencies, and dermatitis herpetiformis. (E) Schilling test evaluates vitamin B12 absorption in suspected pernicious anemia with macrocytic anemia. (F) Small bowel biopsy may evaluate celiac disease, showing intraepithelial lymphocytosis, villous atrophy, and crypt hyperplasia.
Source: 2A #1
A 67-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 1-month history of diffuse abdominal cramps and worsening constipation. Her last bowel movement was 4 days ago, and the stool was hard. She has a long-standing history of constipation with typical frequency of one bowel movement every other day, but this pattern varies from two bowel movements daily to one bowel movement weekly. She has stopped using a laxative because of diarrhea resulting in fecal incontinence. Colonoscopy 3 years ago showed no abnormalities. She has major depressive disorder with anxiety treated with sertraline and lorazepam. She does not appear to be in distress. Her temperature is 37.2°C (99°F), pulse is 88/min, respirations are 18/min, and blood pressure is 152/84 mm Hg. The abdomen is soft, nondistended, and nontender. Bowel sounds are decreased. Rectal examination shows no masses or stool; test of the stool for occult blood is negative. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) X-rays of the abdomen B) CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis C) Discontinuation of lorazepam D) Discontinuation of sertraline E) Trial of acidophilus F) Trial of fiber supplementation G) Colonoscopy
Correct Answer: F) Trial of fiber supplementation
Irritable bowel syndrome (IBS) is diagnosed using Rome-IV criteria, which requires abdominal pain at least once weekly associated with (generally relieved by) defecation, along with a change in stool appearance or stool frequency (at least two of three criteria required). IBS is subdivided into IBS with constipation (IBS-C), with diarrhea (IBS-D), or mixed (IBS-M). IBS presents with intermittent abdominal pain relieved by bowel movements and associated alterations in bowel habits with intermittent episodes of diarrhea and/or constipation. Stress management may improve bowel habits in patients with IBS, which is often associated with concomitant anxiety. In this patient, fiber supplementation is the appropriate first-line management for constipation and helps improve regularity of bowel movements. Features suggestive of underlying organic pathology rather than IBS include occult blood or melena, nocturnal diarrhea, worsening abdominal pain, weight loss, or family history of inflammatory bowel disease or colon cancer—none of which are present here. X-rays of the abdomen (Choice A) show increased stool burden but are poorly sensitive and nonspecific for bowel pathology and would not change management. CT scan of the abdomen and pelvis (Choice B) is appropriate for acute abdominal pain with examination findings concerning for intra-abdominal pathology (fever, vomiting, focal tenderness, rigidity, rebound tenderness, peritonitis), not for chronic abdominal pain with benign examination. Discontinuation of lorazepam (Choice C) or sertraline (Choice D) may be harmful since IBS is associated with stress and anxiety. Trial of acidophilus (Choice E), a probiotic, can be helpful in IBS but dietary changes with increased fiber intake is the appropriate first step in this constipation-predominant patient. Colonoscopy (Choice G) would be unrevealing given normal colonoscopy 3 years ago and absence of red flag features suggesting organic pathology. Educational Objective: Irritable bowel syndrome is characterized by abdominal pain and a change in stool appearance or stool frequency. In patients with IBS and constipation, dietary changes such as hydration and fiber supplementation are the first steps in management.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Sprains, strains 2 questions
Source: 3A #31
A 17-year-old boy is brought to the physician shortly after injuring his right ankle during a high school football game. He says that his ankle "turned in" while he was trying to make a tackle. He was able to limp off the field and can bear weight on the ankle. Examination of the right ankle shows moderate swelling over the area distal to the lateral malleolus. The area is tender to palpation. There is no bony point tenderness. Joint testing shows no instability. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Apply ice and advise periodic leg elevation B) Apply topical capsaicin cream to the tender area C) Immobilize the ankle in a short-leg walking cast D) Inject a cortisone and lidocaine mixture into the tender area E) Order x-rays of the ankle and foot
Correct Answer: A) Apply ice and advise periodic leg elevation
Ankle sprain is a common sports injury in which a ligament is stretched or torn, particularly common among young athletes sustained while twisting a planted foot or landing a jump. Lateral ankle sprains of the anterior talofibular ligament are most common and occur when a plantar-flexed foot is inverted. Mild or moderate sprains involve stretching or mild tearing of a ligament, accompanied by pain, swelling, and tenderness over the affected ligament. Severe sprains involve complete tear with mechanical instability and loss of weight-bearing ability. This patient has a mild-to-moderate sprain evidenced by ability to bear weight, no instability on joint testing, and no bony point tenderness. Most patients with ankle sprains improve with conservative management including rest, application of ice, compression, and elevation of the affected joint (RICE protocol). The Ottawa Ankle Rules assess likelihood of fractures and need for x-rays based on focal tenderness at specific locations (posterior edge of lateral or medial malleolus, navicular bone, base of fifth metatarsal) and weight-bearing ability. This patient does not meet criteria for imaging. Topical capsaicin cream (Choice B) is useful for neuropathic pain in diabetic polyneuropathy or post-herpetic neuralgia, not acute sprain pain. Immobilization in short-leg walking cast (Choice C) is reserved for severe sprains; mild-to-moderate sprains may benefit from elastic wrap or stirrup but do not require casting. Cortisone and lidocaine injection (Choice D) is unnecessary for mild ankle sprains and steroids can impair healing of tendons and ligaments. X-rays (Choice E) are not indicated as patient can ambulate, has pain distal to lateral malleolus rather than overlying posterior edge, and is unlikely to have clinically significant fracture; application of Ottawa Ankle Rules helps reduce unnecessary imaging.
Source: 2A #8
A 23-year-old woman comes to the physician because of moderate pain and swelling of her right ankle after twisting it yesterday. She has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows moderate edema of the lateral aspect of the right ankle, ecchymosis below the lateral malleolus, and tenderness to palpation of the anterior aspect of the lateral malleolus. The posterior aspects of the lateral and medial malleoli are nontender. Neurovascular function of the ankle is intact. The patient can bear weight as she walks across the room but has been using a set of crutches. In addition to recommending 2-day ice pack application to the injury, which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Stress x-rays of the ankle to assess ligament integrity B) X-rays of the ankle to rule out fracture C) Use of a soft protective brace and early range of motion exercises D) Splinting the ankle and referral to an orthopaedist
Correct Answer: C) Use of a soft protective brace and early range of motion exercises
This patient most likely has an ankle sprain from an inversion injury to the lateral ankle. The Ottawa ankle rules guide imaging decisions for ankle injuries: x-rays are only required if there is pain with palpation of the posterior edge or tip of the medial or lateral malleolus, inability to bear weight for more than four steps, or midfoot pain with tenderness at the base of the fifth metatarsal or navicular bone. This patient has anterior pain of the lateral malleolus and can walk independently, so imaging is not indicated. Appropriate management involves a soft protective brace to stabilize the ankle followed by early mobilization when able. Stress x-rays (Choice A) assess ligament integrity but are unnecessary since the patient can bear weight, indicating intact ligaments and joint congruity. X-rays to rule out fracture (Choice B) are not required given the clinical presentation and ability to ambulate. Splinting and orthopedic referral (Choice D) would be appropriate only if fracture were suspected, but splinting impairs early mobilization and is counterproductive for ankle sprains. Early range of motion exercises promote healing and functional recovery in ankle sprains.
▶ Male Reproductive Benign Prostatic Hyperplasia 2 questions
Source: 3A #41
A 75-year-old man comes to the physician with his wife because of a 1-day history of severe lower abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, and an inability to urinate. Six months ago, the patient's wife began to notice that he was taking longer than usual to urinate and leaving drops of urine on the toilet bowl rim and floor. During the past 5 days, he has made frequent trips to the bathroom and often has needed to return shortly after voiding. He has type 2 diabetes mellitus, hypertension, and stage 2 chronic kidney disease. His medications are glipizide, glargine insulin, and amlodipine. Examination shows suprapubic tenderness. Laboratory studies show: Serum Na+ 140 mEq/L, K+ 4.1 mEq/L, Cl- 101 mEq/L, HCO3- 25 mEq/L, Urea nitrogen 23 mg/dL, Creatinine 3.4 mg/dL; Urine pH 6.3, Specific gravity 1.015, Glucose 1+, Bilirubin negative, Protein negative, Ketones 30 mg/dL, Nitrites negative, Leukocyte esterase 1+, Urobilinogen 0.2 mg/dL (N<2). Which of the following is the most likely underlying cause of this patient's current symptoms? A) Acute tubular necrosis B) Benign prostatic hyperplasia C) Dehydration D) Nephrolithiasis E) Progression of hypertensive nephropathy
Correct Answer: B) Benign prostatic hyperplasia
Benign prostatic hyperplasia (BPH) affects middle-aged and elderly male patients and occurs because of excess growth of tissue in the central zone of the prostate causing lower urinary tract obstruction. BPH presents with urinary dribbling and incontinence, difficulty starting or maintaining a urinary stream, nocturia, the need to frequently urinate, and incomplete voiding. These symptoms are typically insidious in onset, chronic, and progressive. The patient's 6-month history of urinary hesitancy, dribbling, and recent acute presentation with urinary retention, suprapubic tenderness, and elevated post-void residual (frequent returns to bathroom) are classic for BPH with acute decompensation. Chronic outlet obstruction leads to urinary retention and may cause hydronephrosis or urinary tract infection, which explains the leukocyte esterase and nitrites on urinalysis. The diagnosis of BPH is made by history, physical, and urodynamic testing, along with ruling out alternate diagnoses. Treatment is with supportive care, alpha-blockers (e.g., tamsulosin), 5-alpha-reductase inhibitors (e.g., finasteride), or surgery. Urinary tract infections associated with BPH are treated with antibiotics. Acute tubular necrosis (Choice A) typically occurs following an ischemic or nephrotoxic insult to the kidneys, resulting in necrosis of the tubular epithelium with granular, muddy brown casts on urinalysis, not the clinical presentation here. Dehydration (Choice C) may lead to kidney injury and increase the risk for urinary tract infection but does not present with chronic urinary frequency or urgency, which are suggestive of urinary tract outlet obstruction secondary to BPH. Nephrolithiasis (Choice D) presents with colicky flank pain radiating to the groin, gross hematuria, and difficulty voiding. This patient's chronic history of urinary frequency and hesitancy is more suggestive of BPH. Progression of hypertensive nephropathy (Choice E) would lead to a reduction in glomerular filtration rate and reduced urinary output. It would not lead to suprapubic tenderness, urinary hesitancy, or frequency. Educational Objective: Benign prostatic hyperplasia presents in middle-aged to elderly males with urinary dribbling and incontinence, hesitancy, urgency, difficulty starting or maintaining a urinary stream, and nocturia. Incomplete voiding and urinary retention increase the risk for urinary tract infection in patients with benign prostatic hyperplasia.
Source: 4A #3
An 82-year-old man presents with increased urinary frequency and decreased urinary stream. He had a transurethral resection of the prostate 11 years ago because of benign hyperplasia. He takes no medications. Physical examination shows a moderately enlarged prostate. His serum creatinine has increased from 1.3 mg/dL 10 months ago to 2.4 mg/dL today. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial step in management? A) Measurement of postvoid residual volume B) Urine cytology C) Intravenous pyelography D) Retrograde urethrography E) CT scan of the abdomen
Correct Answer: A) Measurement of postvoid residual volume
Benign prostatic hyperplasia (BPH) affects middle-aged and elderly male patients and occurs as a result of excess growth of tissue in the central zone of the prostate causing lower urinary tract obstruction. BPH presents with urinary dribbling and incontinence, difficulty starting or maintaining a urinary stream, nocturia, frequent urination, and incomplete voiding. These symptoms are typically insidious in onset, chronic, and progressive. Chronic outlet obstruction leads to urinary retention and may cause the formation of bladder diverticula or lead to hydronephrosis. Patients with urinary retention may develop urinary tract infection due to loss of hydrodynamic flushing of bacteria out of the urethra or acute kidney injury due to urinary outlet obstruction. This patient's acute kidney injury, as evidenced by the increase in serum creatinine from 1.3 to 2.4 mg/dL, is likely caused by urinary outlet obstruction secondary to BPH. Diagnosis is made by history, physical examination, and urodynamic testing, along with ruling out alternate diagnoses (e.g., prostatic malignancy). The first step in diagnosis of urinary outlet obstruction is measurement of postvoid residual volume. Treatment is with supportive care, alpha-blockers (e.g., tamsulosin), 5-alpha-reductase inhibitors (e.g., finasteride), or surgery. Urine cytology (Choice B) showing malignant cells is highly specific for bladder cancer, though this finding is not always present. Cystoscopy is the gold standard for the diagnosis of bladder cancer. This patient does not have any hematuria that would be suggestive of bladder cancer, and urinary outlet obstruction is most likely due to BPH. Intravenous pyelography (Choice C) involves the intravenous injection of radiopaque contrast to subsequently visualize the kidney collecting system, ureters, and bladder under fluoroscopy. This allows for the investigation of suspected urinary tract anatomic abnormalities, malignancies, and strictures. While intravenous pyelography may be helpful, it is costly and would not be the most appropriate next step, as BPH is the most likely cause of the urinary outlet obstruction, which can be illuminated by an increased postvoid residual volume. Retrograde urethrography (Choice D) involves the injection of contrast into the urethra to evaluate for the extravasation of contrast, indicating urethral discontinuity. It is used to diagnose urethral injuries, which can occur from straddle injuries (anterior urethra) or pelvic fractures (posterior urethra). It is not used to diagnose chronic urinary outlet obstruction, and this patient has not sustained any urethral trauma. CT scan of the abdomen (Choice E) may help diagnose other causes of urinary outlet obstruction such as nephrolithiasis or intra-abdominal tumors. This patient does not have any flank pain that would suggest nephrolithiasis or chronic abdominal pain that would suggest an intra-abdominal tumor. He has a known history of BPH, which is the most likely cause of urinary outlet obstruction. The first step in diagnosing this outlet obstruction is measurement of postvoid residual volume.
▶ Human Development Routine vaccinations in adolescence 2 questions
Source: 3A #42
A 17-year-old girl comes to the office for an annual well-child examination. Her last visit to the physician was 14 months ago, during which she received one dose of the quadrivalent human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine. She has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. During the past 2 years, she has been sexually active with one male partner; they use condoms consistently. Examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management regarding the HPV vaccine? A) Readministration of the first dose of the vaccine today with a recommendation that the patient receive a second dose in 2 months and a third dose in 6 months B) Administration of a second dose of the vaccine today with a recommendation that the patient receive a third dose in 4 months C) Administration of the last dose of the vaccine today D) No further management is indicated
Correct Answer: B) Administration of a second dose of the vaccine today with a recommendation that the patient receive a third dose in 4 months
The patient should receive the second dose of the HPV vaccine today with a third dose in four months. HPV is a common human pathogen that infects epithelial cells with multiple strains, both low- and high-risk for squamous cell carcinoma development, resulting in cervical cancer, condylomata acuminata, verruca vulgaris, plantar warts, and mucosal lesions of the oropharynx, respiratory epithelium, and larynx. HPV is primarily transmitted through genital contact. The quadrivalent vaccine covers four serotypes: HPV types 6, 11, 16, and 18. Routine vaccination is recommended at age 11-12 or as young as age 9. For patients receiving their first dose after age 15, three doses are recommended at months 0, 2, and 6. Doses may be administered after these minimum intervals, so this patient, who is 12 months late for her second dose, can still receive it today and the third dose in four months. Only two doses are needed if the first dose was given before age 15. Choice A is incorrect because readministration of the first dose is not necessary; she can still receive the second and third doses despite being over a year late, as doses only need readministration if given before minimum time intervals. Choice C is incorrect because administering only one more dose would leave the patient partially vaccinated; since her first dose was after age 15, she requires two additional doses. Choice D is incorrect because the patient is only partially vaccinated for HPV serotypes 6, 11, 16, and 18, and two additional doses are required to achieve maximum protection. Educational Objective: HPV vaccination is an important public health initiative to decrease the risk for HPV-driven cancers such as cervical cancer. The quadrivalent vaccine covers four serotypes and is given as a two-part or three-part series based on the patient's age at the first dose.
Source: 4A #9
A 13-year-old boy is brought to the office by his mother for a well-child examination. He has no current medications. Growth and development have been normal. His medical records indicate that vaccinations were up-to-date through age 9 years; he has not received any vaccinations since then. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. Administration of which of the following vaccines is most appropriate at this time? A) Hepatitis B B) Inactivated poliovirus C) Measles-mumps-rubella D) Meningococcal E) Pneumococcal F) Varicella
Correct Answer: D) Meningococcal
Meningococcal vaccine protects against infection by Neisseria meningitidis, which may lead to life-threatening meningitis or sepsis. Meningococcal disease is more prevalent in low- and middle-income countries but may occur in the United States in crowded conditions such as dormitories, barracks, or prisons. Meningococcal vaccine is available as a conjugate vaccine and a recombinant protein serogroup B vaccine. The conjugate vaccine is indicated for all children and is administered in two doses: the first at age 11 to 12 years and the second at age 16 years. The serogroup B vaccine is given as an additional dose, usually between age 16 and 18 years, particularly in teens who plan to attend an institution of higher education or enlist in the military. Certain teenage patients at particular risk for infection with N. meningitidis or other encapsulated organisms, such as patients with functional or anatomic asplenia or complement deficiency, should receive vaccination with serogroup B vaccine regardless of their plans to attend college or university. This 13-year-old patient is at the appropriate age for the first dose of meningococcal conjugate vaccine. Hepatitis B vaccine is typically administered in three doses within the first 6 months of life and this patient has likely already received it. Inactivated poliovirus vaccine is given in four doses by age 6 years, and this patient's vaccines are up-to-date through age 9 years. Measles-mumps-rubella vaccine is administered in two doses between ages 12-15 months and 4-6 years, which this patient has likely already received. Pneumococcal conjugate vaccine is given as four doses by age 15 months, which this patient has likely already received. Varicella vaccine is administered in two doses between ages 12-15 months and 4-6 years, which this patient has likely already received.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Septic arthritis 2 questions
Source: 3A #45
A previously healthy 7-year-old boy is brought to the physician because he started limping on his left leg 3 days ago and now refuses to bear weight on it. The mother says that he has "felt feverish" to touch. He has no history of recent trauma. He is at the 45th percentile for height and the 60th percentile for weight. His temperature is 38.9°C (102°F), pulse is 108/min, respirations are 14/min, and blood pressure is 110/72 mm Hg. The patient holds the left hip flexed, abducted, and externally rotated. Passive and active range of motion of the left hip is limited by pain. The left sacroiliac joint is nontender to palpation. Laboratory studies show a leukocyte count of 15,500/mm³, erythrocyte sedimentation rate of 110 mm/h, and a serum C-reactive protein concentration of 4.2 mg/L (N=0.08-3.1). Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Acute rheumatic fever B) Legg-Calve-Perthes disease C) Septic arthritis D) Slipped capital femoral epiphysis E) Toxic transient synovitis
Correct Answer: C) Septic arthritis
Septic arthritis results from bacterial infection of the synovial joint space, most commonly by Staphylococcus aureus. This patient presents with the classic triad of fever, refusal to bear weight, and limited range of motion of the affected joint (left hip held in flexion, abduction, and external rotation—the position of comfort that minimizes intra-articular pressure). Serum laboratory features include leukocytosis (15,500/mm³), markedly elevated erythrocyte sedimentation rate (110 mm/h), and elevated C-reactive protein (4.2 mg/L), all consistent with acute bacterial infection. Arthrocentesis is indicated and would characteristically disclose purulent synovial fluid with >50,000 leukocytes/mm³. Treatment includes operative management (arthrotomy or arthroscopic drainage) and empiric antibiotic coverage with vancomycin and a third-generation cephalosporin. Acute rheumatic fever (Choice A) presents with migratory polyarthralgia, pancarditis (pericarditis, myocarditis, or endocarditis), subcutaneous nodules, erythema marginatum, and Sydenham chorea following group A streptococcal infection, typically pharyngitis. This patient has monoarticular involvement without these systemic features. Legg-Calve-Perthes disease (Choice B) is idiopathic avascular necrosis of the femoral head occurring in younger boys aged 4-9 years with slow onset of hip pain worsened by activity. Critically, it does not present with fever or leukocytosis, distinguishing it from this acute presentation. Slipped capital femoral epiphysis (Choice D) typically occurs in overweight children aged 10-15 years and results from displacement of the femoral epiphysis relative to the femoral neck. While it can present with hip pain and limp, it is not associated with fever or significant inflammatory markers. Toxic transient synovitis (Choice E) is an inflammatory arthropathy presenting with joint pain, often the hip, frequently following viral infection in children. However, it typically presents with milder symptoms, lower inflammatory markers, and normal or only mildly elevated white blood cell counts. The markedly elevated inflammatory markers in this case favor septic arthritis. Educational Objective: Septic arthritis results from bacterial infection of the synovial joint space, most commonly by S. aureus. Patients typically present with fever, reduced range of motion of the affected joint, refusal to bear weight, joint swelling and erythema, leukocytosis, and increased serum inflammatory markers (elevated ESR and CRP). Arthrocentesis with synovial fluid analysis is diagnostic, and prompt operative drainage with empiric antibiotic therapy is essential to prevent permanent joint damage.
Source: 5A #21
A 47-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 1-week history of progressive pain and swelling of the left knee. She has pain with walking and at rest. There is no history of trauma. She has hypertension, type 1 diabetes mellitus, and asthma. Her current medications are insulin, a beta-adrenergic blocking agent, and a daily inhaled corticosteroid. She is 158 cm (5 ft 2 in) tall and weighs 136 kg (300 lb); BMI is 55 kg/m². Her temperature is 37.8°C (100.1°F); other vital signs are within normal limits. The knee is tender, swollen, warm, and erythematous. There is severe pain with passive and active range of motion. Plain x-rays of the knee show periarticular osteopenia; the joint space is narrowed medially. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Measurement of serum uric acid concentration B) MRI of the knee C) Aspiration of the knee joint D) Intra-articular injection of corticosteroids E) Naproxen therapy
Correct Answer: C) Aspiration of the knee joint
Septic arthritis classically presents with a painful, erythematous, swollen joint along with systemic signs and symptoms of illness such as fever, chills, myalgia, arthralgia, and nausea. Septic arthritis results from a bacterial infection of the synovial joint space, most commonly by Staphylococcus aureus. The affected joint discloses pain upon passive range of motion, swelling, and erythema on physical examination. Arthrocentesis is indicated for the evaluation of monoarticular erythema and swelling to evaluate for septic arthritis, which characteristically reveals purulent synovial fluid with greater than 50,000 leukocytes/mm³. Serum tests may also show leukocytosis and increased inflammatory markers such as C-reactive protein concentration and erythrocyte sedimentation rate. Treatment involves urgent intravenous antibiotics with patients often requiring surgical irrigation of the joint. In this case, the most likely diagnosis is septic arthritis because of the patient's presentation of increased temperature and erythematous joint with pain upon passive range of motion in the absence of reported trauma. Choice A (Measurement of serum uric acid concentration) could be reasonable in cases of suspected gout, an inflammatory arthropathy incited by hyperuricemia with deposition of uric acid crystals. Gout typically presents after consumption of a purine-rich meal or alcohol and microscopic examination of joint aspiration would show negatively birefringent needle-shaped crystals with a leukocyte count less than 50,000/mm³. Choice B (MRI of the knee) can be useful in cases of traumatic injuries affecting the ligament, tendon, and/or meniscus of the knee. However, this patient did not sustain any trauma, making septic arthritis the most likely diagnosis. Choice D (Intra-articular injection of corticosteroids) can be utilized in cases of knee osteoarthritis. In the setting of possible septic arthritis, intra-articular injection of corticosteroids is contraindicated. Choice E (Naproxen therapy) may be reasonable in acute gout or osteoarthritis, but in this case there is concern for septic arthritis requiring joint aspiration with subsequent analysis of joint fluid. Educational Objective: Septic arthritis presents with a painful, erythematous, swollen joint with pain on passive range of motion. Microscopic examination of joint aspiration will classically disclose purulent synovial fluid with a leukocyte count greater than 50,000/mm³. While awaiting culture results, intravenous antibiotics should be started urgently. Joint aspiration is the most appropriate next step in management for patients with progressive knee pain, increased temperature, and knee pain with passive range of motion in the absence of trauma.
▶ Endocrine System Hyperparathyroidism 2 questions
Source: 3A #46
A 67-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 2-month history of fatigue and increased urine output. She has hypertension treated with enalapril. Her blood pressure is 143/88 mm Hg. Examination shows no other abnormalities. Laboratory studies show: Serum Ca2+ 11.9 mg/dL, Phosphorus 2.1 mg/dL, Albumin 4.8 g/dL, Urine specific gravity 1.005. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in diagnosis? A) Glucose tolerance test B) Urine culture C) Measurement of serum parathyroid hormone concentration D) Measurement of serum vitamin D concentration E) Intravenous pyelography
Correct Answer: C) Measurement of serum parathyroid hormone concentration
This patient presents with hypercalcemia (11.9 mg/dL) and hypophosphatemia (2.1 mg/dL) along with symptoms of fatigue and polyuria, which are classic for primary hyperparathyroidism. Excessive production of parathyroid hormone (PTH) results in increased serum calcium concentration through increased osteoclast activity, intestinal absorption, and renal tubular reabsorption. Serum phosphorus concentration is decreased secondary to increased renal tubular excretion. Patients with laboratory findings concerning for hyperparathyroidism should undergo further evaluation with measurement of serum parathyroid hormone concentration. Once confirmed, imaging studies may localize a parathyroid adenoma, hyperplasia, or carcinoma. Glucose tolerance testing (Choice A) is useful for diabetes mellitus diagnosis, which may present with polyuria but does not cause hypercalcemia and hypophosphatemia. Urine culture (Choice B) is useful for urinary tract infection diagnosis, which may present with increased urination but is typically accompanied by dysuria, urgency, and suprapubic tenderness, and does not cause hypercalcemia and hypophosphatemia. Measurement of serum vitamin D concentration (Choice D) is useful for identifying hypervitaminosis D, which may cause hypercalcemia; however, vitamin D is associated with normal or increased phosphorus concentrations, in contrast to hyperparathyroidism where phosphorus is decreased. Intravenous pyelography (Choice E) visualizes the ureters and bladder to investigate suspected urinary tract anatomic abnormalities, malignancies, and strictures, which is not indicated by this clinical presentation.
Source: 5A #23
A 65-year-old woman comes to the office as a new patient to refill her prescriptions after relocating to the area. She reports feeling well. She has a history of hypothyroidism, bipolar disorder, and schizophrenia, which are well-controlled with lithium carbonate, nortriptyline, olanzapine, and levothyroxine. Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination discloses a flat affect; no additional abnormalities are noted. Laboratory results show: Hemoglobin A1c 5.0%, Serum Na+ 139 mEq/L, K+ 5.0 mEq/L, Ca2+ 11.9 mg/dL, Urea nitrogen 26 mg/dL, Creatinine 1.6 mg/dL, Albumin 4.6 g/dL, LDL-cholesterol 100 mg/dL, TSH 4.2 pU/mL, AST 25 U/L, ALT 22 U/L, and 25-hydroxyvitamin D levels. Results of HIV antibody and hepatitis C testing are negative. After reviewing the patient's results, a follow-up examination is scheduled for 3 days from today. In addition to measuring serum lithium concentration, which of the following tests is most appropriate to perform at this time? A) Bone density test B) Renal ultrasonography C) Serum parathyroid hormone concentration D) Urinary calcium concentration E) X-ray of the spine
Correct Answer: C) Serum parathyroid hormone concentration
This patient presents with hypercalcemia (Ca2+ 11.9 mg/dL), elevated creatinine (1.6 mg/dL), and elevated urea nitrogen (26 mg/dL), which are consistent with primary hyperparathyroidism. The patient also has psychiatric disturbances (bipolar disorder and schizophrenia), which can be associated with hypercalcemia. Primary hyperparathyroidism results from excessive production of parathyroid hormone, leading to increased serum calcium concentration through increased osteoclast activity, intestinal absorption, and renal tubular absorption. Serum phosphorus concentration is typically decreased secondary to increased excretion by the renal tubules. Patients with laboratory findings consistent with hyperparathyroidism, such as hypercalcemia, should undergo further evaluation with measurement of serum parathyroid hormone concentration. Once confirmed, imaging studies may localize a parathyroid adenoma, hyperplasia, or carcinoma. Bone density test (Choice A) is not the most appropriate next step. While this patient does meet screening criteria for osteoporosis given her age and sex, a DEXA scan will not provide information about the cause of her increased calcium concentration. Serum parathyroid hormone concentration should be assessed first. Renal ultrasonography (Choice B) is not necessary at this time. While it could provide information about kidney disease as a potential explanation for increased creatinine, measurement of serum parathyroid hormone concentration is more appropriate to assess the origin of hypercalcemia, with additional testing following if indicated. Urinary calcium concentration (Choice D) is not necessary currently. Patients with chronic kidney disease commonly have increased urinary calcium excretion, but this would not provide diagnostic information about the underlying cause of the condition. X-ray of the spine (Choice E) is not appropriate at this time. While patients with multiple myeloma can present with increased calcium, bone pain, anemia, and end-stage renal disease, it is more appropriate to assess parathyroid hormone concentration first to identify the source of hypercalcemia. Educational Objective: Patients with laboratory findings consistent with hyperparathyroidism, such as hypercalcemia, should undergo further evaluation with measurement of serum parathyroid hormone concentration. Common symptoms of hyperparathyroidism include nephrolithiasis, bone pain, abdominal pain, and psychiatric issues.
▶ Skin Subcutaneous Tissue Atopic Dermatitis 2 questions
Source: 3A #47
A 22-year-old woman comes to the physician in January because of a 6-week history of an itching, burning rash over her forearms and wrists. She has a history of similar rashes around this time of year but says that this rash is the worst she has ever experienced. Two weeks ago, she had an upper respiratory tract infection that resolved spontaneously. She has no other history of serious illness and takes no medications. A photograph of the right upper extremity is shown with findings also appearing over the volar aspect of the wrists. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Oral cyclosporine therapy B) Oral methotrexate therapy C) Topical ketoconazole cream D) Topical moisturizing cream E) Ultraviolet light therapy
Correct Answer: D) Topical moisturizing cream
Atopic dermatitis (AD), or eczema, is a chronic pruritic inflammatory skin disease common in children that may persist into adulthood. It is suggested by a personal or family history of atopic disease (e.g., asthma or allergic rhinitis). The rash is initially characterized by erythematous papules and vesicles with exudates and crusting and, with chronic scratching, becomes dry, scaly, and excoriated in a process called lichenification. AD patients tend to have generalized background dryness (xerosis). Gentle skin care and frequent use of topical moisturizing cream is the initial step in managing AD. Patients should be counseled to use fragrance-free products, avoid bathing in hot water, and apply emollient (moisturizing cream) daily. In patients whose eczema persists despite these interventions, a mid-potency topical corticosteroid such as triamcinolone acetonide 0.1% is appropriate. Oral cyclosporine (Choice A) and oral methotrexate (Choice B) are immunosuppressive agents used for severe AD, but are indicated only when conservative measures including topical corticosteroids have failed, body surface area involvement exceeds 10%, or dermatitis severely impacts daily functioning. In this case, eczema is limited to a small area and conservative management has not yet been attempted. Topical ketoconazole cream (Choice C) is an antifungal used for dermatophyte infections such as tinea corporis, but AD can be distinguished from tinea by its lack of annular scaly patches and classic distribution involving flexural surfaces. Ultraviolet light therapy (Choice E) is a systemic therapy for AD and psoriasis typically reserved for patients who have failed conservative measures but do not yet warrant immunosuppressants, due to inconvenience and cost of three times weekly office treatments.
Source: 2A #19
An 18-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a pruritic facial rash for 2 days. The rash began around the lips and then spread to the cheeks. She is sexually active and uses an oral contraceptive. She has a history of asthma treated with a β-adrenergic agonist inhaler. There is a family history of extrinsic asthma. She has had a chronic rash on her extremities for 13 years. The uninvolved skin is chronically dry and scaling. Examination shows a vesicular facial rash and eczematous lichenified plaques on the cheeks, neck, and flexor surfaces of the extremities. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Atopic dermatitis with eczema herpeticum B) Chronic contact dermatitis with superinfection C) Dermatitis herpetiformis D) Erythema multiforme with bullous formation E) Psoriasis with vesicular exacerbation
Correct Answer: A) Atopic dermatitis with eczema herpeticum
This patient's history and physical examination findings are most consistent with atopic dermatitis with eczema herpeticum. A background of atopic dermatitis is suggested by the patient's chronic and pruritic rash on the extremities, personal and family history of asthma, and the eczematous plaques. Patients with atopic dermatitis have a dysfunctional skin barrier, even in uninvolved areas of skin, along with changes to the innate immune system that increase their risk for secondary bacterial and viral infections. A high suspicion of herpes simplex virus (HSV) secondary infection, or eczema herpeticum, should be maintained in a patient with atopic dermatitis who develops a sudden onset of vesicles. The infection may be preceded by other signs of herpetic infections such as oral herpes labialis, herpetic whitlow, or genital herpes. Treatment in severe cases or in young children requires hospitalization and administration of intravenous acyclovir. Less severe cases can be managed on an outpatient basis with oral acyclovir. Management of the underlying atopic dermatitis should also be optimized. Choice B (Chronic contact dermatitis with superinfection) is less likely than HSV superinfection of atopic dermatitis. Patients with atopic dermatitis have a systemically diminished innate immunity and compromised skin barrier, making them particularly susceptible to secondary skin infections. In contrast, contact dermatitis is localized and is not accompanied by changes to the innate immune system; thus, patients are less likely to become secondarily infected. Choice C (Dermatitis herpetiformis) is a vesiculobullous disorder characterized by small, tense vesicles located on extensor sites (elbows, knees, and buttocks), which are extremely pruritic. Vesicles on the face are not typically seen. Dermatitis herpetiformis has a strong association with celiac disease, and the primary treatment is the avoidance of gluten. Choice D (Erythema multiforme with bullous formation) is characterized by targetoid lesions classically on the palms, soles, and oral mucosa. These findings are not seen in this patient's physical examination. Choice E (Psoriasis with vesicular exacerbation) is not a presentation of psoriasis. Pustules, but not vesicles, may be present in psoriasis. Psoriasis is characterized by overactivity of the innate and adaptive immune systems, and secondary infection is uncommon. Educational Objective: Patients with atopic dermatitis demonstrate an impaired skin barrier and changes to the innate immune system that increase their risk for secondary bacterial and viral infections. Eczema herpeticum is characterized by the sudden onset of vesicles or punched-out erosions in a patient with atopic dermatitis and requires systemic antiviral therapy.
▶ Blood Lymphoreticular System Anemia of chronic disease 2 questions
Source: 5A #12
A 9-month-old girl is brought to the physician for a well-child examination. Developmental milestones are appropriate for age. She is at the 50th percentile for length and 60th percentile for weight. Her temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 137/min, respirations are 32/min, and blood pressure is 86/42 mm Hg. Examination shows no abnormalities other than mild conjunctival pallor. Her hematocrit is 29%, and mean corpuscular volume is 65 m². Which of the following questions would be most helpful to ask the patient's parents to determine the cause of this child's anemia? A) Did she have jaundice at birth? B) Does she bruise easily? C) Has anyone in the family had their spleen removed? D) Has she passed any blood with her urine? E) What does she eat?
Correct Answer: E) What does she eat?
The correct answer is E. This 9-month-old presents with microcytic anemia (hematocrit 29%, MCV 65 fL) and mild conjunctival pallor, consistent with iron deficiency anemia. Neonates and infants fed exclusively with whole cow milk are at high risk for developing iron deficiency anemia. Both breast milk and cow milk contain low concentrations of iron, but the iron in breast milk has high bioavailability while cow milk proteins impair iron absorption in human infants. Additionally, cow milk is associated with increased occult enteric bleeding that contributes to iron loss during infancy. Iron deficiency anemia early in life can lead to significant neurocognitive and psychomotor impairments that persist even after treatment. Asking about diet is therefore the most helpful question to identify the underlying cause. Choice A (jaundice at birth) is not helpful because newborn physiologic jaundice occurs in nearly all newborns from increased RBC breakdown, immature liver metabolism, and enterohepatic circulation of bilirubin. It does not explain microcytic anemia in a 9-month-old. Choice B (bruising easily) would help identify bleeding issues from platelet or factor dysfunction, but there is no evidence of blood loss anemia (no petechiae, bleeding gums, or bloody stools), making diet assessment more appropriate. Choice C (family history of splenectomy) could suggest hereditary spherocytosis, but this condition typically causes normocytic anemia, not the microcytic anemia seen here. Choice D (hematuria) can have many causes in pediatrics (postinfectious nephritis, Henoch-Schönlein purpura, IgA nephropathy), but without recent illness or purpura history, dietary assessment is more helpful to rule out nutritional deficiencies. Educational Objective: Neonates and infants fed with only whole cow milk are at risk for developing iron deficiency anemia. Breast milk or iron-fortified formula is recommended for the first year of life.
Source: 2A #38
A 32-year-old woman is referred to the physician because of anemia noted on routine testing prior to blood donation. She felt well until recently when she began to experience decreased exercise tolerance. She has regular menstrual periods with no abnormal bleeding. Examination shows no abnormalities. Test of the stool for occult blood is negative. Laboratory studies show: Hematocrit 27%, Mean corpuscular volume 73 µm³, Leukocyte count 5200/mm³ with a normal differential. A blood smear is shown. Which of the following is the most appropriate initial step in management? A) Administration of folic acid B) Parenteral iron therapy C) Measurement of serum iron and ferritin concentrations D) Hemoglobin electrophoresis E) Bone marrow aspiration
Correct Answer: C) Measurement of serum iron and ferritin concentrations
Measurement of serum iron and ferritin concentrations should be performed next in this patient with microcytic anemia. The differential diagnosis of microcytic anemia includes alpha- or beta-thalassemia, anemia of chronic disease, sideroblastic anemia, and iron deficiency anemia among several other rare causes. In young female patients who are actively menstruating, iron deficiency should be suspected. Diagnosis is made by assessing the serum concentration of ferritin (the storage form of iron) and serum iron concentration, both of which will be decreased in iron deficiency anemia. Iron is required for the synthesis of heme, a necessary component of the hemoglobin molecule and erythrocytes. Erythrocytes become microcytic as a result of continuing erythrocyte division in order to reach adequate hemoglobin concentration; because hemoglobin stores are inadequate, cell division continues beyond normal and causes cells to be smaller than normal. Treatment should include oral iron supplementation, or intravenous infusions if severe. Administration of folic acid (Choice A) should be considered in women of childbearing age and patients with megaloblastic anemia from folate deficiency, but this patient has microcytosis, not macrocytosis; folate supplementation would be unlikely to correct her anemia. Parenteral iron therapy (Choice B) is a viable treatment option for severe iron deficiency anemia but should only be considered after diagnosis is confirmed. Serum iron and transferrin concentrations must be measured first. Hemoglobin electrophoresis (Choice D) diagnoses sickle cell disease, alpha- and beta-thalassemia major, and other hemoglobinopathies. Patients with iron deficiency anemia have normal electrophoresis. Additionally, this patient only recently became symptomatic, and hemoglobinopathies are inherited conditions that often present in infancy. Bone marrow aspiration (Choice E) is unnecessary and invasive at this time. This procedure is reserved for patients suspected of having anemia secondary to malignant bone marrow or myeloablative processes such as leukemia, myelodysplastic disorder, or aplastic anemia. Educational Objective: Iron deficiency anemia is occasionally encountered in menstruating women and presents with microcytic anemia. Diagnosis is made by demonstrating decreased serum iron and transferrin concentrations. Additional markers, including increased total iron binding capacity, can also be helpful in diagnosis.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Osteoporosis 2 questions
Source: 5A #15
A 57-year-old woman comes to the office because she is concerned about her risk for osteoporosis. Her 65-year-old sister recently sustained a hip fracture. One year ago, the patient sustained a fracture of her right wrist in a fall. She has no other history of serious illness. Menopause occurred 10 years ago. Her only medication is a daily multivitamin. She has smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 30 years. She has a sedentary lifestyle. She is 152 cm (5 ft) tall and weighs 50 kg (110 lb); BMI is 22 kg/m². Her pulse is 64/min, and blood pressure is 120/60 mm Hg. Examination shows no abnormalities. The physician advises her to stop smoking, begin calcium supplementation, and begin a weight-bearing exercise program. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Bisphosphonate therapy B) DEXA scan at the age of 65 years C) DEXA scan now D) Estrogen and progestin replacement therapy E) Follow-up examination in 1 year
Correct Answer: C) DEXA scan now
Osteoporosis and osteopenia are metabolic bone diseases characterized by diffusely low bone density. The most common risk factors are age, smoking, chronic inflammatory disease, corticosteroid use, and estrogen-depleting conditions. Such demineralization and low bone density is often asymptomatic but increases the risk for fractures, most commonly involving the hip, vertebrae, and distal radius. Screening for osteoporosis utilizing DEXA scan is recommended for all women either at age 65 years or earlier in those who have at least one risk factor. Common risk factors include family history of hip fracture, prior fragility fracture, smoking history, low BMI, or use of corticosteroids. This patient has multiple risk factors: family history of hip fracture (sister's fracture), prior fragility fracture (wrist fracture 1 year ago), and significant smoking history (1 pack daily for 30 years). Therefore, earlier screening with DEXA scan is appropriate now rather than waiting until age 65. Bisphosphonate therapy (Choice A) is indicated for patients with osteoporosis or osteopenia with a high FRAX score. Bisphosphonates inhibit osteoclasts, thereby decreasing bone resorption and increasing bone mineral density. However, this patient does not currently have a diagnosis of osteoporosis, as she has not received a DEXA scan to determine this, so bisphosphonates would not be appropriate at this time. DEXA scan at the age of 65 years (Choice B) is the appropriate time of screening for osteoporosis in female patients at average risk. However, in patients with a family history of fracture, prior fragility fractures, smoking history, low BMI, or long-term use of corticosteroids, earlier screening is appropriate. This patient's risk factors warrant earlier screening. Estrogen and progestin replacement therapy (Choice D) are not appropriate at this time. While they are potential treatments to help reduce bone loss in postmenopausal women, this patient with a history of smoking has significantly increased risk for hypercoagulability. Additionally, the patient does not currently have a formal diagnosis of osteoporosis, and initiation of pharmacotherapy would not be appropriate without diagnostic confirmation. Follow-up examination in 1 year (Choice E) is not appropriate. Given this patient's risk factors for osteoporosis, it is more appropriate to pursue diagnostic testing at present. Delaying testing only increases her risk for further fractures. First-line treatment and prevention of osteoporosis includes calcium and vitamin D supplementation, reduction of active osteoporotic risk factors such as smoking cessation, and weight-bearing exercises to slow deterioration of bone mineral density. Educational Objective: Osteoporosis is often asymptomatic but increases the risk for fractures, most commonly involving the hip, vertebrae, and distal radius. Women should undergo a DEXA scan for osteoporosis either at age 65 years or sooner if at least one risk factor is identified.
Source: 4A #17
A 66-year-old woman comes to the office for a routine health maintenance examination. Her last menstrual period was 10 years ago. She has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. Five years ago, a routine Pap smear showed no abnormalities and human papillomavirus testing was negative. A mammogram obtained 1 year ago showed no abnormalities. A colonoscopy done 8 years ago showed no abnormalities. She does not smoke cigarettes. She has been in a monogamous relationship for the past 40 years. Vital signs are within normal limits and physical examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following screening tests is most appropriate at this time? A) DEXA scan B) Measurement of serum CA-125 concentration C) Pap smear D) Test of the stool for occult blood E) Ultrasonography of the abdominal aorta
Correct Answer: A) DEXA scan
Osteoporosis is a metabolic bone disease characterized by diffusely low bone density. Evaluation for osteoporosis involves a DEXA scan and is defined as a bone mineral density less than 2.5 standard deviations compared to a young, normal population (T-score). The United States Preventive Services Task Force recommends, at the Grade B level, to screen women over age 65 years for osteoporosis, and under age 65 years when increased risk factors exist. The most common risk factors for the development of osteoporosis are age, smoking, chronic inflammatory disease, corticosteroid use, and estrogen-depleting conditions in women. Osteoporosis is often asymptomatic but increases the risk for fractures, most commonly involving the hip, vertebrae, and distal radius. Calcium and vitamin D supplementations are recommended for treatment and prevention of osteoporosis. Weight-bearing exercise is recommended to slow deterioration of bone mineral density. Bisphosphonates inhibit osteoclast activity and can be used to treat osteoporosis to reduce further loss of bone mineral density. For this 66-year-old patient, a DEXA scan is the most appropriate screening test at this time. Measurement of serum CA-125 concentration (Choice B) may be used to screen for ovarian cancer. However, because of low specificity and low positive predictive value, regular screening with CA-125 has not been shown to decrease mortality in cases of ovarian cancer. Pap smear (Choice C) is generally recommended as a routine screening for women age 21 to 65 years. This patient does not require routine screening because she is age 66 years and has a history of normal pap smear and negative human papillomavirus testing. Test of the stool for occult blood (Choice D) is not required for this patient who had a normal colonoscopy 8 years ago, as the common colonoscopy interval with normal findings is 10 years. Ultrasonography of the abdominal aorta (Choice E) is the recommended screening for abdominal aortic aneurysm for men and women age 65 to 75 years who have ever smoked. This patient denies smoking cigarettes.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Constipation 2 questions
Source: 5A #26
A 77-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 1-week history of decreased appetite, nausea, and occasional vomiting. She also reports fullness and discomfort in her abdomen and abdominal cramps that have awakened her on several occasions. Her last bowel movement was 5 days ago and was characterized by loose stool. Thirty years ago, she underwent hysterectomy for leiomyomata uteri. She has a 10-year history of intermittent urinary incontinence. Her only medication is oxybutynin. Colonoscopy 3 years ago showed no abnormalities. She appears frail. She is 157 cm (5 ft 2 in) tall and weighs 57 kg (125 lb); BMI is 23 kg/m². Her temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 80/min and regular, respirations are 14/min, and blood pressure is 130/85 mm Hg. Examination shows warm, dry skin. The lungs are clear to auscultation and percussion. Cardiac examination shows no murmurs. The abdomen is soft and diffusely tender; there is no rebound or organomegaly. Rectal examination shows firm stool in the rectal vault. Which of the following is the most likely cause of this patient's current symptoms? A) Adhesions B) Colon cancer C) Diverticulitis D) Fecal impaction E) Ischemic bowel F) Volvulus
Correct Answer: D) Fecal impaction
Loose stool in the setting of hard stool in the rectal vault and increased stool burden suggests overflow diarrhea in the setting of severe constipation. Chronic, severe constipation with fecal impaction in the rectum can occur in the setting of decreased mobility, opiate use, or anticholinergic (e.g., oxybutynin) use, which suppresses bowel peristalsis and increases the risk for constipation. This patient's episode of loose stool occurred because only liquid can pass adjacent to the impacted stool. The first-line treatment for severe constipation with fecal impaction is administration of an enema, which introduces fluid into the large bowel to expand the intestinal tract lumen, soften the impacted stool, and stimulate peristalsis. Modification of risk factors should also occur, such as increasing fiber in the diet, limiting the use of opiate medications, or introducing stool softeners or osmotic agents into the medication regimen. Adhesions (Choice A) are the most common cause of small bowel obstruction in patients with prior intra-abdominal surgery. Small bowel obstruction typically presents with nausea, vomiting, constipation, obstipation, abdominal pain, and abdominal distension. This patient has a soft abdomen without distension, making small bowel obstruction less likely. Firm stool in the rectal vault is more suggestive of fecal impaction. Colon cancer (Choice B) most commonly occurs in the rectosigmoid region. Lesions within the ascending colon typically result in slow bleeding leading to iron deficiency anemia. Cancers within the descending and rectosigmoid colon are more likely to be infiltrative, resulting in large bowel obstruction with thin-appearing stool. Loose stool with firm stool in the rectal vault is more suggestive of fecal impaction. In this patient with a normal colonoscopy 3 years ago, colon cancer severe enough to cause constipation is unlikely. Diverticulitis (Choice C) presents with left lower quadrant abdominal pain, sometimes accompanied by diarrhea, fever, and tenderness to palpation over the left lower quadrant. It is less likely to present with constipation. This patient does not have fever or focal tenderness, and has diffuse abdominal tenderness, making diverticulitis less likely. Ischemic bowel (Choice E), such as ischemic colitis, refers to inadequate blood supply to the colon, which can lead to bowel wall necrosis, loss of immune barriers, and bowel perforation. Ischemic colitis most commonly occurs in the setting of atherosclerosis with plaque rupture within a mesenteric vessel, systemic hypotension (e.g., shock), or emboli such as in atrial fibrillation. Typical symptoms include crampy abdominal pain, tenderness over the affected bowel, and hematochezia.
