This patient does not have focal tenderness, hematochezia, or risk factors for ischemic colitis, making this diagnosis less likely. Volvulus (Choice F), such as sigmoid volvulus, results from twisting of the sigmoid mesentery, which can lead to large bowel obstruction and potential rupture or intestinal necrosis if not treated. It typically shows a coffee-bean appearance on abdominal x-rays and generally presents in older patients with increasing abdominal distention, abdominal pain, constipation, and possible obstipation. This patient has a soft abdomen without distension and a sensation of fullness and discomfort, not severe abdominal pain, making sigmoid volvulus less likely. Educational Objective: Constipation and fecal impaction can present with severe constipation and overflow diarrhea, with liquid passing adjacent to hard impacted stool. First-line treatment includes administration of an enema, along with treatment of underlying causes.
Source: 4A #19
A 45-year-old man presents with a 5-year history of slowly worsening constipation. His bowel movements have become hard and have decreased in frequency. He reports incomplete evacuation following bowel movements. Previous colonoscopy 5 years ago showed no abnormalities. He takes a thiazide diuretic. Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination discloses a palpable colon with hard stool over the left abdomen; the remainder of the examination is within normal limits. In addition to increasing fluid intake, which of the following is the most appropriate initial pharmacotherapy? A) Lactulose B) Linaclotide C) Lubiprostone D) Magnesium citrate E) Psyllium
Correct Answer: E) Psyllium
Constipation is a common condition that can develop idiopathically or secondary to metabolic derangements, medication adverse effects, hypothyroidism, spinal cord injury, and pregnancy. Initial management of idiopathic constipation includes patient education, dietary changes such as increasing water and fiber intake, as well as bulking or non-bulking laxatives. Psyllium is a bulk-forming laxative that helps increase stool frequency and soften stool due to its fiber content. For a patient seeking first-time treatment without a clear secondary cause, psyllium is the most appropriate initial pharmacotherapy. Lactulose (Choice A) is an osmotic laxative that increases water content in the colon and stool and is also used for hepatic encephalopathy, but is not first-line for untreated idiopathic constipation. Linaclotide (Choice B) is used for chronic idiopathic constipation and IBS by increasing intestinal secretions through cyclic guanosine monophosphate induction, resulting in increased chloride and bicarbonate. However, it is reserved for cases not responding to initial therapy. Lubiprostone (Choice C) treats chronic idiopathic constipation and IBS by activating chloride channels to increase intestinal fluid secretion, but is not first-line initial therapy. Magnesium citrate (Choice D) is an osmotic laxative that increases intraluminal water content and colonic peristalsis. While reasonable for occasional use, it is not the most appropriate initial pharmacotherapy for previously untreated constipation. Educational Objective: For previously untreated idiopathic constipation, initial management includes patient education, dietary modifications (increased water and fiber), and bulk-forming laxatives such as psyllium, which increase stool frequency and soften stool through their fiber content.
▶ Biostatistics Epidemiology Population Health Sensitivity 2 questions
Source: 5A #29
Of 65 men with coronary artery disease, 38 had an abnormal exercise stress test. Which of the following characteristics of screening tests does this demonstrate? A) False-negative rate B) Likelihood ratio C) Predictive value of a positive test D) Sensitivity E) Specificity
Correct Answer: D) Sensitivity
Sensitivity is the ability of a test to detect a disease if it is present. It is calculated as TP/(TP + FN), where TP = true positives and FN = false negatives. In this vignette, 38 of 65 men with coronary artery disease had an abnormal exercise stress test, representing the proportion of true positives (those with disease who tested positive). This directly demonstrates sensitivity. High-sensitivity tests are useful for screening purposes where proving the absence of disease and limiting false-negative results are paramount. A negative result on a high-sensitivity test indicates a very low likelihood that disease is present. False-negative rate (Choice A) refers to the proportion of patients with negative test results who actually have the disease—the inverse of what is described. Likelihood ratio (Choice B) is the ratio of false results to true results (negative LR = FN/TN; positive LR = TP/FP), which is not what this vignette describes. Predictive value of a positive test (Choice C) defines the proportion of positive test results that are true positives, which is different from sensitivity—it describes the probability of disease given a positive test, whereas sensitivity describes the probability of a positive test given disease. Specificity (Choice E) is the proportion of people without disease who test negative (TN/(TN + FP)) and describes the false-positive rate; this vignette instead describes true positives with positive results, consistent with sensitivity.
Source: 2A #2
A previously healthy 4-year-old girl is brought to the physician because of a sore throat and temperatures to 38.8°C (101.8°F) over the past 24 hours. She attends preschool, and several other students have similar symptoms. She appears ill. Examination shows erythema of the tonsils with exudate. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. A rapid streptococcal antigen test is negative, but throat cultures grow Streptococcus pyogenes (group A). Which of the following characteristics of the rapid streptococcal antigen test best explains these results? A) Low likelihood ratio B) Low negative predictive value C) Low positive predictive value D) Low sensitivity E) Low specificity
Correct Answer: D) Low sensitivity
The rapid streptococcal antigen test demonstrates low sensitivity, which best explains the false-negative result in this patient with confirmed streptococcal pharyngitis. Sensitivity is the ability of a test to detect disease when it is present—a test with high sensitivity has a low likelihood of false negatives. A test with poor sensitivity, if negative, does not provide strong evidence that the patient does not have the disease. In this case, the rapid antigen test failed to detect the presence of Streptococcus pyogenes despite positive throat culture confirmation, exemplifying low sensitivity. However, the test remains clinically useful because if positive, it prevents the need for more expensive and time-consuming confirmatory testing. Likelihood ratios (Choice A) are ratios of true positive to false positive rates or false negative to true negative rates; the low sensitivity, not low likelihood ratio, best explains these results. Low negative predictive value (Choice B) indicates low probability that a negative test result truly means absence of disease, but this concept applies to predicted disease status rather than confirmed disease status. Low positive predictive value (Choice C) is not applicable here since the test was negative; positive predictive value is relevant for confirmatory testing. Low specificity (Choice E) describes false-positive errors, but this patient had a false-negative result, not a false positive. High specificity is required for disease confirmation, as exemplified by the throat culture. Educational Objective: High-sensitivity tests are required for effective disease screening to minimize false negatives. High-specificity tests are necessary for confirmation of disease to minimize false positives. Negative and positive predictive values are functions of sensitivity, specificity, and pretest probability of disease.
▶ Behavioral Health Postpartum Depression Screening 2 questions
Source: 5A #39
A 16-year-old girl is brought to the office by her mother because of a 3-week history of nausea without associated vomiting. The patient's mother waits outside the examination room during the evaluation. The patient has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. Since menarche at age 11 years, her menstrual periods have occurred at irregular 30- to 60-day intervals; her last menstrual period was 2 months ago. The patient reports that she has been dating her 18-year-old boyfriend for the past 5 months; they are sexually active and use condoms inconsistently. Temperature is 36.8°C (98.4°F), pulse is 88/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 98/62 mm Hg. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. Urine pregnancy test is positive. While discussing the results with the patient, she states that she does not know what to do. Which of the following is the most important question for the physician to ask the patient during the conversation? A) "Do you want me to contact child protective services?" B) "Have you considered placing the child up for adoption?" C) "How do you think your boyfriend will react to this news?" D) "Is this a situation we could discuss with your mother?" E) "Would you like a confidential referral to discuss abortion services?"
Correct Answer: D) "Is this a situation we could discuss with your mother?"
The correct answer is D. Parental consent is required for most medical care of minors (patients younger than 18 years). However, exceptions include medical care related to sexual health (e.g., pregnancy care, sexually transmitted infection, contraception), substance use, and life-threatening medical or psychiatric conditions. For these sensitive or time-sensitive issues, patient confidentiality and autonomy are favored over other ethical principles. This minor patient who is pregnant has challenging decisions to make. The physician's role is to discuss the options and facilitate informed and thoughtful decisions. She should be asked whether she wants her parents involved in the decision-making. If she expresses a preference to exclude her parents, the physician should honor her preference. Choice A is incorrect because "Do you want me to contact child protective services?" is not an appropriate question to ask a patient. As a mandated reporter, the physician is required by law to report any suspected child abuse, but there is no indication that the boyfriend or parents are abusing this patient or that this patient is a danger to the fetus. Choices B and E are incorrect because "Have you considered placing the child up for adoption?" and "Would you like a confidential referral to discuss abortion services?" are leading questions that presume one option is best. The decision of whether to deliver and raise a child is a personal decision. The physician should facilitate discussion of all options while abstaining from expressing an opinion. Choice C is incorrect because "How do you think your boyfriend will react to this news?" strays from the physician's role of facilitating informed decision-making about management options. Though the physician may encourage the patient to discuss the decision with her loved ones (including her boyfriend) so she can think through her decision, the boyfriend's reaction is irrelevant to the medical decision-making process. Additionally, this question may come off as judgmental. Educational Objective: Parental consent is not required for medical care related to sexual health (e.g., pregnancy, sexually transmitted infection, contraception). Physicians should ask minor patients about their preferences concerning disclosing these sensitive issues to their parents. If the patient expresses a preference to exclude parents, the physician should honor this preference.
Source: 4A #6
A 28-year-old woman presents with nausea and changes in her menstrual cycle. She reports using contraception inconsistently. Early symptoms include fatigue, nausea, breast tenderness, and urinary frequency. Which of the following is the most appropriate test to confirm the diagnosis? A) Measurement of serum estrogen:progesterone ratio B) Measurement of serum free thyroxine concentration C) Measurement of serum prolactin concentration D) Measurement of serum thyroid-stimulating hormone concentration E) Measurement of urine B-hCG concentration
Correct Answer: E) Measurement of urine B-hCG concentration
Clinicians should maintain a low threshold of suspicion for pregnancy in reproductive-age patients presenting with changes in their menstrual cycles, especially when those patients use contraception inconsistently. Early symptoms of pregnancy in addition to irregular menstruation include fatigue, nausea, breast tenderness, and urinary frequency. While amenorrhea is a more common sign of early pregnancy, light vaginal bleeding can occur with the implantation of a fertilized ovum and can be misinterpreted as a menstrual cycle. Evaluation of these patients includes measurement of B-hCG concentration in the urine or serum, with the concentration being increased in pregnant patients. Transvaginal ultrasonography can be used to confirm the diagnosis. The most appropriate test to confirm the diagnosis in this patient presenting with nausea and changes in her menstrual cycle is measurement of urine B-hCG concentration. Incorrect Answers: A) Determination of serum estrogen:progesterone ratio is not used in the diagnosis of pregnancy, although it may be used in the evaluation of hormone imbalance and to evaluate the adequacy of hormone replacement therapy or as a prognostic tool during in vitro fertilization cycles. B) Measurement of serum free thyroxine concentration may be used in the diagnosis of hypothyroidism and hyperthyroidism. In primary hypothyroidism, the free thyroxine concentration is decreased, whereas the thyroid-stimulating hormone is increased. These concentrations are reversed in primary hyperthyroidism. Hypothyroidism presents with weight gain, lethargy, fatigue, constipation, hair loss, and cold intolerance. In contrast, hyperthyroidism presents with heat intolerance or hot flashes, palpitations, weight loss, diaphoresis, and diarrhea. Both conditions can cause oligo- or amenorrhea. However, ruling out pregnancy with measurement of urine B-hCG concentration should be done before pursuing other causes of this patient's symptoms. C) Measurement of serum prolactin concentration may be increased in the setting of a prolactinoma, which is a functional adenoma that most commonly occurs in the pituitary gland. It can secrete high enough concentrations of prolactin to inhibit gonadotropin-releasing hormone, as well as follicle-stimulating hormone and luteinizing hormone. It can present with amenorrhea but is often associated with galactorrhea and would be unlikely to cause the nausea seen in this patient. D) Measurement of serum thyroid-stimulating hormone concentration may be used in the diagnosis of hypothyroidism and hyperthyroidism, but pregnancy should be ruled out first. Educational Objective: Measurement of urine B-hCG concentration should be the first step in diagnosis of a reproductive-age patient who presents with menstrual irregularities. Early symptoms of pregnancy in addition to irregular menstruation include fatigue, nausea, breast tenderness, and urinary frequency. Transvaginal ultrasonography may also aid in the diagnosis.
▶ Behavioral Health Tobacco Use Disorder 2 questions
Source: 2A #4
A healthy 14-year-old girl is brought for a routine health maintenance examination. On questioning, she says that she has been sexually active with her boyfriend of 6 months. She says that they use condoms consistently and she takes an oral contraceptive. She has smoked one pack of cigarettes weekly for 1 year and drinks beer and wine occasionally at weekend parties. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Inform her about the long-term health consequences of smoking B) Inquire about her attitudes toward smoking and health C) Schedule a meeting to speak to her parents about her smoking D) Prescribe a nicotine patch for smoking cessation E) Prescribe sustained-release bupropion for smoking cessation
Correct Answer: B) Inquire about her attitudes toward smoking and health
The most appropriate next step is to open a discussion about her cigarette use by inquiring about her attitudes toward smoking and health. By starting with an open-ended question, the physician gains insight into the patient's motivation for smoking and her understanding of smoking's health impact. This allows the physician to direct the conversation in a way most likely to promote change in this adolescent. The physician should be direct and clear in explaining risks without making the patient feel guilty or defensive. Motivational interviewing—determining the patient's willingness and motivation to change—is a helpful strategy. Choice A (informing about long-term health consequences) may cause defensiveness or annoyance, reducing partnership effectiveness. Teenagers are often less motivated by long-term consequences than immediate impacts on appearance, friends, relationships, and school performance. Choice C (scheduling a parental meeting) violates patient confidentiality obligations. Physicians must protect adolescent confidentiality except in cases of child abuse, suicidality, homicidal ideation, mental health care, or (in some cases) abortion consent. Discussing smoking with parents before the patient is inappropriate. Confidentiality laws vary by state. Choices D and E (nicotine patch or bupropion) may be warranted after discussing health risks and determining the patient's motivation to change. Prescribing cessation aids before assessing readiness to change is ineffective. Educational Objective: Physicians should initiate discussions with open-ended questions to gain insight into patients' mindsets and understanding of how behaviors impact health, particularly in adolescents.
Source: 4A #10
A 45-year-old man comes to the physician as a new patient. He has no other history of serious illness. He takes metformin and hydrochlorothiazide. He has smoked one pack of cigars daily. His blood pressure is 142/82 mm Hg. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. Laboratory studies from 1 month ago show: Hemoglobin A1c 7.8%, Serum Na+ 142 mEq/L, K+ 4.3 mEq/L, Cl- 109 mEq/L, Urea nitrogen 14 mg/dL, Glucose 135 mg/dL, Creatinine 0.7 mg/dL, Cholesterol 150 mg/dL, LDL-cholesterol 120 mg/dL, Triglycerides 142 mg/dL. Which of the following modifications would be most beneficial to this patient? A) Smoking cessation B) Adding lisinopril therapy to the medication regimen C) Adding pravastatin therapy to the medication regimen D) Increasing the dosage of hydrochlorothiazide E) Increasing the dosage of metformin
Correct Answer: A) Smoking cessation
Smoking is the strongest modifiable risk factor for coronary artery disease, the most common cause of mortality in adults. The Framingham Heart Study found that smoking is associated with approximately a two-fold increase in morbidity and mortality from ischemic heart disease. Smoking is also associated with various cancers, stroke, and chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, all associated with increased mortality rates. Smoking can exacerbate type 2 diabetes mellitus. Therefore, smoking cessation is the most beneficial behavior change for this patient. Patients should be advised that it is never too late to stop smoking, as cardiovascular risk and other smoking-related risks begin to decline immediately upon cessation. Motivational interviewing—a strategy in which the physician determines and guides the patient to reflect on their willingness and motivation to change—is helpful when discussing health behavior changes such as smoking cessation. Incorrect Answers: B) Adding lisinopril or D) Increasing hydrochlorothiazide would be unnecessary for this patient's mild hypertension. Though lisinopril is associated with decreased mortality in diabetic patients, addressing mild hypertension is unlikely to make a significant difference. Hypertension is a risk factor for coronary artery disease and stroke but is not as strongly correlated with coronary artery disease as smoking and is not commonly associated with cancer or COPD. C) Adding pravastatin would address hyperlipidemia, a risk factor for coronary artery disease and stroke, but hyperlipidemia is not as strong a risk factor as smoking and is not commonly associated with cancer or COPD. E) Increasing metformin would assist in managing type 2 diabetes mellitus. Diabetes is a risk factor for coronary artery disease and stroke but is not as strong a risk factor as smoking and is not commonly associated with cancer or COPD. Educational Objective: Smoking is the most significant modifiable risk factor for coronary artery disease and mortality in adults. It is additionally associated with various cancers, stroke, chronic obstructive pulmonary disease, and exacerbation of type 2 diabetes mellitus. Smoking cessation is the most beneficial behavior change for this patient. Motivational interviewing is a helpful strategy when discussing health behavior changes such as smoking cessation with patients.
▶ Human Development Routine vaccinations in older adulthood 2 questions
Source: 3A #32
A 61-year-old woman comes to the office in July for a routine examination. She has hypertension and major depressive disorder. Her medications are hydrochlorothiazide and citalopram. She does not smoke cigarettes. Her blood pressure is 122/78 mm Hg; other vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate vaccine at this time? A) Herpes zoster B) Human papillomavirus C) Influenza D) Meningococcal E) Pneumococcal
Correct Answer: A) Herpes zoster
Varicella zoster virus infection, also known as shingles or herpes zoster, commonly presents as a painful, unilateral, dermatomal erythematous patch with overlying clear to yellow, grouped vesicles. It is caused by varicella-zoster virus (human herpesvirus-3), transmitted via respiratory secretions and direct contact with infected skin lesions. It can be associated with neuropathic pain that may precede and outlast the rash, which is more common in elderly patients. The CDC recommends herpes zoster vaccine for people 60 years of age or older. It is a live virus vaccine indicated in this 61-year-old patient and may be given regardless of prior varicella zoster virus infection history, as reinfection can occur. Human papillomavirus (HPV) vaccine (Choice B) is sexually transmitted and associated with genital warts and premalignant/malignant lesions of cervical, anogenital, and oropharyngeal regions. HPV vaccines should be administered ideally before the teenage years when immune response is most robust and before sexual activity onset. Influenza vaccine (Choice C) is a respiratory viral illness that can be complicated by lower respiratory tract infection and bacterial superinfection, especially in elderly patients. Influenza is seasonal, typically affecting patients in winter and early spring months, making July an inappropriate time for vaccination. Meningococcal vaccine (Choice D) is indicated for all children, with additional serogroup B vaccine for patients over 10 years old at increased risk for meningococcal disease (e.g., dormitory, military living). Pneumococcal 23-valent polysaccharide vaccine (Choice E) is indicated for all adults over 65 years old and in patients with high-risk conditions such as chronic heart, kidney, and liver disease or chronic smokers. This 61-year-old patient without these risk factors does not meet criteria for pneumococcal vaccination at this time. Educational Objective: Varicella zoster virus infection presents as a painful, unilateral, dermatomal erythematous patch with overlying clear to yellow, grouped vesicles. Herpes zoster vaccine should be given to people 60 years of age or older.
Source: 5A #43
A 24-year-old primigravid woman at 10 weeks' gestation comes to the office for her first prenatal visit. She has no history of serious illness, and her only medication is a prenatal vitamin. Her childhood immunization history is unknown. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows a uterus consistent in size with a 10-week gestation. Which of the following is the most appropriate vaccine to administer at this time? A) Hepatitis B B) Human papillomavirus C) Measles-mumps-rubella D) Tetanus-diphtheria-acellular pertussis E) Varicella
Correct Answer: D) Tetanus-diphtheria-acellular pertussis
The tetanus-diphtheria-acellular pertussis (Tdap) vaccine is routinely administered during pregnancy, typically between weeks 27 and 36 of gestation, to optimize maternal antibody response and promote placental transfer of antibodies to the fetus. This protects infants against neonatal tetanus and pertussis, which are associated with significant mortality. Tdap vaccination should occur during each pregnancy and can be given at any time during pregnancy. Before infants receive their own DTaP vaccine at age 2 months, maternal Tdap antibodies provide critical protection. If a pregnant patient acquires a wound and has not yet received a routine Tdap vaccine during the current pregnancy, standard wound management measures should be adopted (e.g., Tdap vaccination in patients with minor wounds who have not received Tdap within the past 10 years). Hepatitis B vaccine is recommended during pregnancy for patients who have not previously completed the series. Because this patient's vaccine history is unknown, titers should be obtained to determine immunity; if not immune, the vaccine should be given, but it is not the most appropriate choice at this time. Human papillomavirus vaccine is not typically administered during pregnancy as it has not been studied for safety during pregnancy and is not part of recommended vaccinations for pregnant patients. Measles-mumps-rubella and varicella vaccines are live attenuated vaccines and should not be administered during pregnancy due to the theoretical risk that the vaccine virus may cause the infant to contract the disease (e.g., congenital rubella syndrome). All live attenuated vaccines must be avoided during pregnancy.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Radiculopathies 1 question
Source: 3A #33
A previously healthy 42-year-old man comes to the physician because of right low back pain that began 2 days ago after he cleaned his garage. The pain radiates to the right buttock and posterior aspect of the right lower extremity to below the knee. He rates the pain as an 8 on a 10-point scale. It is worse when he is sitting. He has not had any other neurologic symptoms. He is visibly in pain as he tries to climb onto the examination table. His temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 92/min, respirations are 12/min, and blood pressure is 142/88 mm Hg. There is straightening of the lumbar lordosis and tenderness to percussion over L4-5. Muscle strength is 5/5, and deep tendon reflexes and sensation are intact and symmetric. Straight-leg raise testing is positive on the right at 40 degrees with pain radiating down the right leg. Straight-leg raise testing is positive on the left at 45 degrees with pain radiating down the right leg. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Bed rest for 3 days B) Epidural corticosteroid injection C) MRI of the lumbar spine D) Normal activity as tolerated E) Transcutaneous electrical nerve stimulation F) Use of a lumbar support brace
Correct Answer: D) Normal activity as tolerated
Lumbar disc herniation typically occurs following chronic disc degeneration combined with a minor traumatic event such as bending and lifting, causing acute increase in intradiscal pressure. It presents with acute back pain and lumbosacral radiculopathy symptoms including extremity pain or weakness in a dermatomal and myotomal distribution. The diagnosis is clinical, made through history and examination maneuvers including the straight leg raise test. Lumbar imaging with MRI or CT is useful for patients with severe motor or sensory dysfunction but is not required for most patients with mild or moderate symptoms without objective neurologic deficit and no concern for spinal cord impingement. Lumbosacral radiculopathy secondary to lumbar disc herniation is typically self-limited, with most patients demonstrating spontaneous improvement with conservative management including analgesia, physical therapy, and normal activity as tolerated. Prolonged bed rest is not necessary and may worsen pain and prolong recovery. Bed rest (A) may worsen pain and prolong recovery; most patients resume normal activity as tolerated with symptomatic control. Epidural corticosteroid injection (B) may provide short-term pain reduction but is invasive with risks including paralysis, infection, and death. MRI (C) is useful for motor or sensory dysfunction but not required for mild or moderate symptoms. Transcutaneous electrical nerve stimulation (E) is sometimes used for chronic lower back pain but lacks demonstrated efficacy in randomized trials. Lumbar support brace (F) may benefit patients with compression fractures but is not typically necessary for disc herniation; prolonged use may lead to core abdominal muscle weakness or atrophy.
▶ Cardiovascular System Hypercholesterolemia 2 questions
Source: 2A #23
A 57-year-old woman comes to the physician for a routine health maintenance examination. She has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. She smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 20 years but stopped 10 years ago. She drinks one glass of wine daily. She does not exercise. She is employed as an office assistant. She is 168 cm (5 ft 6 in) tall and weighs 65 kg (143 lb); BMI is 23 kg/m². Her pulse is 80/min, respirations are 14/min, and blood pressure is 140/90 mm Hg. The lungs are clear to auscultation. Cardiovascular examination shows no abnormalities. Serum lipid studies show: Total cholesterol 250 mg/dL, HDL-cholesterol 35 mg/dL, LDL-cholesterol 179 mg/dL, Triglycerides 180 mg/dL. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Exercise program B) Weight loss program C) Glucose tolerance test D) 24-Hour urine collection for measurement of microalbumin E) Serum lipid studies in 1 year F) Atenolol therapy
Correct Answer: A) Exercise program
The patient presents with hypercholesterolemia (total cholesterol 250 mg/dL, LDL 179 mg/dL, low HDL 35 mg/dL, elevated triglycerides 180 mg/dL) and hypertension (140/90 mm Hg). Risk factors for hypercholesterolemia include family history, physical inactivity, obesity, diabetes mellitus, chronic kidney disease, nephrotic syndrome, thyroid dysfunction, and tobacco use. Hypercholesterolemia is associated with hypertension, cardiovascular disease, and increased complications of diabetes mellitus. Patients are typically asymptomatic, and diagnosis is made by routine screening. Management includes evaluating for secondary causes (thyroid function studies, hemoglobin A1c, chemistry panel, and urinalysis to assess kidney function) and counseling on dietary modifications, weight loss, and increased physical activity. Of these interventions, an exercise program is most appropriate for this patient because she has a normal BMI (23 kg/m²) and is sedentary. The patient's 10-year and lifetime cardiovascular risk scores should be determined using a validated tool, and lipid-lowering pharmacotherapy should be considered if the patient develops clinically significant cardiovascular disease, diabetes mellitus, or has repeat lipid panel with LDL-cholesterol greater than 190 mg/dL. Choice B (Weight loss program) is less beneficial as the patient is not overweight. Choice C (Glucose tolerance test) should be considered to evaluate for diabetes as a secondary cause, but this patient lacks significant diabetes risk factors. Choice D (24-hour urine microalbumin) is not indicated without chronic kidney disease or diabetes mellitus. Choice E (Serum lipid studies in 1 year) may assess response to management but lifestyle modifications should be implemented now. Choice F (Atenolol therapy) is not appropriate as initial management for hypercholesterolemia. Educational Objective: Hypercholesterolemia is commonly asymptomatic and increases risk for significant cardiovascular and cerebrovascular disease. Therapeutic options include dietary modifications, weight reduction, increased physical activity, and lipid-lowering pharmacotherapy.
Source: 2A #24
A 62-year-old man comes to the physician for a routine follow-up examination. He has type 2 diabetes mellitus and peripheral vascular disease. Current medications include metformin and glyburide. Dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are decreased bilaterally. Serum lipid studies show: Cholesterol, total (mg/dL): One Year Ago 190, Today 180 HDL-cholesterol (mg/dL): One Year Ago 39, Today 37 LDL-cholesterol (mg/dL): One Year Ago 139, Today 135 Triglycerides (mg/dL): One Year Ago 180, Today 140 Which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation? A) Repeat cholesterol testing in 3 months B) Dietary modification to decrease LDL-cholesterol concentration to less than 130 mg/dL C) Drinking wine with dinner to increase HDL-cholesterol concentration to greater than 40 mg/dL D) Lipid lowering therapy to decrease LDL-cholesterol concentration to less than 100 mg/dL E) No current treatment is indicated, and repeat cholesterol testing annually
Correct Answer: D) Lipid lowering therapy to decrease LDL-cholesterol concentration to less than 100 mg/dL
The patient presents with persistently elevated LDL-cholesterol (135 mg/dL) documented on two separate laboratory analyses one year apart, combined with multiple significant cardiovascular risk factors: age 62, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and clinically evident peripheral vascular disease (decreased dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses bilaterally). Risk factors for hypercholesterolemia include family history, physical inactivity, obesity, diabetes mellitus, chronic kidney disease, nephrotic syndrome, thyroid dysfunction, and tobacco use. Hypercholesterolemia is associated with hypertension, cardiovascular disease, and increased complications of diabetes mellitus. Patients are typically asymptomatic, and diagnosis is made by routine screening. While dietary modifications, weight loss, and increased physical activity should be counseled, the presence of multiple comorbidities and persistent hypercholesterolemia warrants initiation of lipid-lowering pharmacotherapy. Statin therapy is indicated to reduce LDL-cholesterol to less than 100 mg/dL, as this patient has clinically significant cardiovascular disease. Choice A is inappropriate because the patient has had consistently elevated LDL-cholesterol on two separate analyses a year apart, making repeat testing without intervention unnecessary. Choice B (dietary modification alone to <130 mg/dL) is insufficient given the patient's high-risk status and existing cardiovascular disease; more aggressive LDL lowering to <100 mg/dL is warranted. Choice C (wine consumption) is not evidence-based for this patient and does not address the primary problem of elevated LDL-cholesterol. Choice E (no treatment, annual testing) is inappropriate given the persistent elevation and significant cardiovascular risk profile. Educational Objective: Hypercholesterolemia is commonly asymptomatic and increases risk for significant cardiovascular and cerebrovascular disease. Therapeutic options include dietary modifications, weight reduction, increased physical activity, and lipid-lowering pharmacotherapy. Persistent hypercholesterolemia in the setting of multiple risk factors and comorbidities warrants the initiation of lipid-lowering pharmacotherapy.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Osteoarthritis 2 questions
Source: 2A #44
A 62-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 9-month history of pain and swelling of her finger joints. Her pain increases with activity. She has had progressive stiffness in her fingers during the past 2 years. She has mild hypertension controlled with diet. She takes no medications. Her blood pressure is 138/90 mm Hg. Examination shows an asymmetric distribution of enlarged bony proximal and distal interphalangeal joints; some of the joints are tender to palpation, and passive range of motion is limited by pain. Grip strength is normal. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. The most appropriate initial step in management is administration of which of the following? A) Acetaminophen B) Allopurinol C) Gold D) Methotrexate E) Prednisone
Correct Answer: A) Acetaminophen
Acetaminophen is the most appropriate first-line medication for this patient with osteoarthritis (OA). The clinical presentation is classic for OA: a 62-year-old elderly patient with pain and swelling of finger joints that worsens with activity, progressive stiffness, and asymmetric distribution of enlarged bony proximal and distal interphalangeal (PIP and DIP) joints. These are the most commonly affected joints in hand OA, along with the first carpometacarpal (CMC) joint. Physical examination findings often include Heberden or Bouchard nodes without active synovitis. The pain pattern is key to differentiation from inflammatory arthritis: OA pain worsens throughout the day with continued use, whereas inflammatory pain (as in rheumatoid arthritis) is worse in the morning and improves after about an hour of activity. First-line treatment of OA includes acetaminophen, with alternatives including oral NSAIDs (if no contraindications such as gastrointestinal bleeding or chronic kidney disease) or topical NSAID preparations. Allopurinol (Choice B) is used to prevent recurrent gout attacks by lowering serum urate concentration, but requires a history of multiple acute gout episodes, which this patient lacks. Gold (Choice C) was historically used for rheumatoid arthritis but is no longer recommended due to systemic toxicities. Methotrexate (Choice D) is a first-line treatment for rheumatoid arthritis, not OA, and this patient lacks features of inflammatory arthritis. Prednisone (Choice E) is used for acute gout episodes in patients unable to take NSAIDs or colchicine, has little effect on OA, and carries numerous adverse effects. Educational Objective: Osteoarthritis is a common condition in elderly patients causing joint pain. When present in the hands, the most commonly affected joints are the first CMC, DIPs, and PIPs. First-line therapy is acetaminophen, oral NSAIDs, or topical NSAIDs.
Source: 4A #11
A 72-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of a 6-year history of increasingly severe pain and progressively decreasing mobility of her left shoulder. She has congestive heart failure, coronary artery disease, kidney disease, and type 2 diabetes mellitus. Her medications are metoprolol, enalapril, and glipizide. Vital signs are within normal limits. Range of motion of the left shoulder is decreased and causes crepitus. Examination shows no other abnormalities. X-ray of the left shoulder shows joint space narrowing, osteophytes, subchondral cysts, and sclerotic changes. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Celecoxib therapy B) Glucosamine supplementation C) Ibuprofen therapy D) Physical therapy E) Recommendation for an anti-inflammatory diet
Correct Answer: D) Physical therapy
Osteoarthritis is a noninflammatory arthropathy that occurs secondary to deterioration of articular cartilage, typically affecting older patients in joints bearing greater weight or used in repetitive tasks, including the shoulders, knees, hips, and hands. It presents with chronic pain worse with movement or exertion and improved with rest, along with limited range of motion. Imaging findings include joint space narrowing, osteophytes, subchondral cysts, and sclerotic changes, as seen in this patient's shoulder x-ray. First-line treatment is conservative with physical therapy and oral pain medications as needed. Initial pharmacologic options include acetaminophen, though only a minority achieve meaningful relief with this alone. Additional options include nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory medications (NSAIDs), intra-articular injections (e.g., cortisone), and joint replacement surgery for severe or refractory cases. In this patient, NSAIDs are contraindicated due to her congestive heart failure, coronary artery disease, and kidney disease. NSAIDs have been shown to increase the risk for myocardial infarction and kidney injury, making them inappropriate despite their potential analgesic benefit. Celecoxib (Choice A) and Ibuprofen (Choice C) are NSAIDs that could be reasonable treatment options in addition to physical therapy for patients without cardiac or kidney disease, but are contraindicated in this patient. Glucosamine supplementation (Choice B) has shown conflicting results in clinical studies, with larger clinical trials finding negligible effects on joint pain, making it an unclear treatment option. Recommendation for an anti-inflammatory diet (Choice E) has not been shown to clearly improve joint pain in osteoarthritis cases. Physical therapy has been demonstrated to improve pain and functionality in osteoarthritis and is the most appropriate next step in management for this patient given her comorbidities and medication contraindications. Educational Objective: Osteoarthritis is a noninflammatory arthropathy secondary to articular cartilage deterioration. Conservative management includes physical therapy and oral analgesics such as acetaminophen and/or NSAIDs when there are no contraindications. In patients with significant cardiac and kidney disease, NSAIDs are contraindicated due to increased risk of myocardial infarction and kidney injury, making physical therapy the most appropriate initial management strategy.
▶ Renal Urinary System NSAIDs Nephrotoxicity 2 questions
Source: 3A #3
A 58-year-old man comes to the office because of a 5-day history of elevated home blood pressure readings. He has a 10-year history of hypertension. Since treatment with lisinopril was initiated 10 years ago, his previous home and office blood pressure measurements have been less than 140/90 mm Hg. Ten days ago, the patient's dentist initiated an acetaminophen, amoxicillin, and naproxen regimen for an abscessed tooth. The patient currently is asymptomatic. Blood pressure measured today in the office is 160/95 mm Hg; remainder of vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most likely explanation for this patient's findings? A) Antagonistic effects on cytochrome P450 B) Decreased prostaglandin synthesis C) Diminished absorption of lisinopril D) Excessive adrenergic stimulation E) Inflammation of the renal interstitium
Correct Answer: B) Decreased prostaglandin synthesis
The most likely explanation for this patient's recent hypertension is a drug-drug interaction between lisinopril and naproxen. Naproxen and other NSAIDs are inhibitors of cyclooxygenase (COX) enzymes, which have a primary role in the synthesis of prostaglandins and prostacyclins. These molecules regulate renal blood flow (RBF) by promoting renal afferent arteriole vasodilation. NSAID use can precipitate acute kidney injury (AKI) because of decreased prostaglandin synthesis, which reduces RBF. This causes worsening of hypertension, especially in patients taking ACE inhibitors such as lisinopril, as a result of autoregulation mechanisms increasing blood pressure as a means of increasing renal perfusion. Choice A (Antagonistic effects on cytochrome P450): While cytochrome P450 inhibition can account for severe medication interactions, this is not the mechanism of NSAID-ACE inhibitor interaction. Common CYP450 inhibitors include fluconazole, ciprofloxacin, and fluoxetine. Choice C (Diminished absorption of lisinopril): Although naproxen can decrease the gastric mucosal barrier, absorption of lisinopril is unlikely to be affected since most medications are absorbed in the small intestine. Choice D (Excessive adrenergic stimulation): Excessive adrenergic stimulation occurs with sympathomimetic drugs and pheochromocytoma, presenting with hypertension, tachycardia, tachypnea, diaphoresis, and dilated pupils. This patient shows no other signs of adrenergic excess. Choice E (Inflammation of the renal interstitium): Interstitial nephritis occurs secondary to medication use (NSAIDs, antibiotics, sulfas) and is characterized by decreased glomerular filtration rate, rising creatinine, eosinophilic infiltrates on biopsy, and urinalysis findings. Patients classically present with low-grade fever and diffuse rash, which this patient does not have. Educational Objective: NSAID use can precipitate acute kidney injury as a result of decreased prostaglandin synthesis, which would otherwise promote renal afferent arteriole vasodilation and renal blood flow. This can cause worsening of hypertension, especially in patients taking ACE inhibitors such as lisinopril.
Source: 2A #43
A 42-year-old woman comes to the physician for a follow-up examination. She has had persistent right heel pain since taking a 5-mile walk 3 weeks ago. She has been treated with naproxen, a heel cushion, and exercises. The pain has persisted, and she had the onset of foot swelling 4 days ago. She has well-controlled type 1 diabetes mellitus and hypertension. Her only other medications are benazepril and insulin. Examination shows 1+ edema bilaterally to the midcalf. Which of the following is the most likely cause of the edema? A) Decreased cardiac output B) Idiopathic anaphylactoid reaction C) Impaired renal excretion of sodium D) Localized soft-tissue swelling E) Naproxen-induced hepatic toxicity
Correct Answer: C) Impaired renal excretion of sodium
Naproxen is a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug (NSAID) that is nephrotoxic and can cause acute kidney injury, especially in patients with chronic kidney disease, volume depletion, or who are also taking diuretics or ACE inhibitors. This patient is taking benazepril (an ACE inhibitor) along with naproxen, which substantially increases the risk of acute kidney injury. NSAIDs reduce prostaglandin synthesis, impairing renal blood flow; combined with an ACE inhibitor, delivery of blood to the glomerulus may be substantially impaired. Acute kidney injury can manifest with decreased urine output, hypertension, and fluid overload with lower extremity edema. The kidney is the main site for sodium and volume regulation, and acute kidney injury results in impaired renal excretion of sodium. Sodium balance is a main driver of fluid balance. When sodium excretion is impaired, hypervolemia and fluid retention occur, leading to lower extremity edema and, if severe, pulmonary edema. Laboratory tests typically show increased blood urea nitrogen, potassium, and creatinine. Decreased cardiac output (Choice A) can cause lower extremity edema in congestive heart failure, but this patient has no history of congestive heart failure, myocardial infarction, myocarditis, or other causes of decreased cardiac output. Idiopathic anaphylactoid reaction (Choice B) presents with urticaria, shortness of breath, wheezing, vomiting, oropharyngeal or facial swelling, and hypotension—isolated lower extremity swelling would not be consistent with anaphylactoid reaction. Localized soft-tissue swelling (Choice D) can occur with deep venous thrombosis, cellulitis, or abscess and would be unlikely to present bilaterally; additionally, this describes an examination finding rather than an underlying cause. Naproxen-induced hepatic toxicity (Choice E) is rare and less common than nephrotoxicity; hepatotoxicity presents with cholestasis, hepatitis, and jaundice, not edema. Educational Objective: NSAIDs can cause acute kidney injury, especially in patients with chronic kidney disease, volume depletion, or who are taking diuretics or ACE inhibitors. The resultant impaired renal blood flow decreases excretion of sodium, leading to fluid retention and hypervolemia.
▶ Endocrine System Hyperaldosteronism 1 question
Source: 3A #4
A previously healthy 32-year-old woman comes to the physician because of mild headaches and weakness for 3 months. Her pulse is 80/min, and blood pressure is 150/105 mm Hg. Examination shows no other abnormalities. Serum studies show: Na+ 143 mEq/L, Cl- 98 mEq/L, K+ 2.4 mEq/L, HCO3- 33 mEq/L, Urea nitrogen 18 mg/dL, Creatinine 0.8 mg/dL. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Addison disease B) Adrenal adenoma C) Adrenogenital syndrome D) Cushing syndrome E) Hyperaldosteronism F) Pheochromocytoma
Correct Answer: E) Hyperaldosteronism
Hyperaldosteronism is the most likely diagnosis. It is a relatively common cause of secondary hypertension in young patients and presents with hypertension, hypokalemia, and metabolic alkalosis. Hyperaldosteronism can result from excess secretion from a unilateral adrenal adenoma but often results from bilateral adrenal hyperplasia. Aldosterone upregulates sodium channels in the cortical collecting tubule, resulting in increased sodium resorption. The transcellular shift of sodium causes the lumen to become electronegative, stimulating potassium secretion into the urine and resulting in urinary wasting of potassium. Metabolic alkalosis results from excess hydrogen ion secretion stimulated by hypokalemia and through direct effects of aldosterone on urine acidification. Diagnosis is made by measuring serum renin and aldosterone, demonstrating abnormally increased aldosterone and undetectable renin concentrations. Incorrect answers: Addison disease (Choice A) is immune-mediated adrenal insufficiency presenting with hypotension, hyponatremia, hyperkalemia, hypoglycemia, and dark skin pigmentation—opposite of this patient's findings. Adrenal adenoma (Choice B) can cause hyperaldosteronism but is less common than bilateral hyperplasia and often secretes other adrenal hormones. Adrenogenital syndrome (Choice C) usually refers to congenital adrenal hyperplasia from 21-hydroxylase deficiency, presenting at birth with ambiguous genitalia and salt-losing crises. Cushing syndrome (Choice D) presents with excess cortisol production causing weight gain, abdominal striae, hyperglycemia, and hypertension—not the hypokalemia and metabolic alkalosis seen here. Pheochromocytoma (Choice F) presents with episodic hypertension, tachycardia, and anxiety from excess metanephrines, not the chronic hypertension with electrolyte abnormalities in this case. Educational Objective: Hyperaldosteronism is a relatively common cause of secondary hypertension. Excess aldosterone production from an adrenal adenoma or bilateral adrenal hyperplasia causes hypertension from salt and water retention, along with hypokalemia and metabolic alkalosis.
▶ Multisystem Processes Disorders Lyme Disease 1 question
Source: 3A #5
A 15-year-old boy is brought to the office by his mother because of a 2-day history of moderate joint pain, fever, headaches, fatigue, and a blotch over his right shoulder blade. The blotch began as a small red papule and gradually spread. The patient says he thinks he was bitten by a tick 1 week ago. His temperature is 38.2°C (100.8°F); other vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows a 15-cm erythematous area over the right scapula. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in patient care? A) Serum titer of antibody to Borrelia burgdorferi B) Serum titer of Proteus vulgaris Ox-19 C) Skin biopsy to identify microorganisms D) Doxycycline therapy E) Penicillin therapy
Correct Answer: D) Doxycycline therapy
Lyme disease is a tick-borne disease caused by Borrelia burgdorferi, endemic in the Northeastern United States. In its early stages, it causes erythema chronicum migrans, an expanding rash with central ring-like clearing, often associated with flu-like illness and arthralgia. The clinical presentation in this case—known tick exposure 1 week prior, characteristic expanding erythematous rash, fever, arthralgia, and headache—is diagnostic of early Lyme disease. Doxycycline is the first-line treatment for early-stage Lyme disease. It acts by preventing the attachment of aminoacyl-tRNA to ribosomes. Side effects of tetracycline antibiotics include gastrointestinal distress, photosensitivity, and in children, discoloration of teeth and inhibition of bone growth, which contraindicates its use in pregnancy. Later stages of Lyme disease (characterized by carditis, atrioventricular heart block, bilateral facial nerve palsy, migratory arthralgias, and potentially encephalopathy and chronic arthritis) are treated with parenteral ceftriaxone. Choice A (Serum titer of antibody to Borrelia burgdorferi) is unnecessary. History and physical examination are adequate for diagnosis following known tick exposure with characteristic rash. Serum studies are often negative in early disease because antibodies have not yet developed. Choice B (Serum titer of Proteus vulgaris Ox-19) refers to the Weil-Felix agglutination test, which detects anti-Borrelia antibodies through cross-reactivity. This test was widely used in the 20th century but has poor sensitivity and specificity and has been largely supplanted by Western blot and enzyme-linked immunosorbent assay technologies. Choice C (Skin biopsy) is not necessary for Lyme disease diagnosis, which can be made clinically when erythema chronicum migrans is present. Choice E (Penicillin therapy) may be useful in patients intolerant of doxycycline or in pregnant women, but doxycycline is first-line for this immunocompetent, non-pregnant adolescent. Educational Objective: Early-stage Lyme disease presents with erythema chronicum migrans, arthralgia, and flu-like symptoms. Doxycycline is first-line treatment for early-stage Lyme disease.
▶ Behavioral Health Somatic Symptom Disorder 1 question
Source: 3A #7
A 36-year-old woman comes to the physician as a new patient for a follow-up examination because of ongoing "disabling" back pain. She rates her pain as a 10 on a 10-point scale. Physical examination and x-rays obtained during the past 3 years have shown no abnormalities. Back hygiene education, physical therapy, and acetaminophen and nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs have not provided adequate relief. She believes that "something terrible is going on" despite being told by an orthopaedic surgeon that there is no significant pathology. She reports that she has seen a psychiatrist a few times but found the visits unhelpful. She has no other history of serious illness and currently takes no medications. She appears anxious and expresses heightened concern about her inability to stop her pain. Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Application of a lidocaine patch B) Oral gabapentin therapy C) Oral oxycodone therapy D) Oral sertraline therapy E) Schedule frequent follow-up examinations
Correct Answer: E) Schedule frequent follow-up examinations
In somatic symptom disorder, the patient is preoccupied with one or more somatic symptoms such that these symptoms disrupt the patient's daily life. These symptoms may or may not originate from an underlying disease but are typically unconsciously produced or exacerbated by psychological factors such as stress. Patients with somatic symptom disorder persistently devote excessive time and energy to these symptoms or related health concerns (e.g., repeatedly going to the doctor). The disorder typically demonstrates a chronic and fluctuating course. Management of somatic symptom disorder centers around regular primary care follow-up that targets coping skills, provides reassurance, and avoids unnecessary tests. This patient has already undergone extensive evaluation with normal physical examination and imaging over 3 years, and has failed multiple therapeutic interventions including physical therapy, acetaminophen, and NSAIDs. Offering further therapeutic interventions (lidocaine patch, gabapentin, or oxycodone) would be counterproductive and less likely to be helpful in a patient with somatic symptom disorder, as they reinforce the focus on physical symptoms and may lead to medication dependence. Oral sertraline therapy has been shown to be effective in major depressive disorder, generalized anxiety disorder, panic disorder, and social anxiety disorder, but has not been shown to be effective in somatic symptom disorder. The most appropriate management is regular primary care follow-up that provides reassurance, avoids unnecessary testing, and helps the patient develop coping skills to manage her symptoms and psychological distress.
▶ Cardiovascular System Atrial Fibrillation 1 question
Source: 3A #8
A 78-year-old woman is brought to the office 2 hours after the sudden onset of shortness of breath, light-headedness, palpitations, and fatigue. She has a history of asymptomatic heart failure and hypertension. Her medications are lisinopril and aspirin. Her temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 108/min, respirations are 24/min, and blood pressure is 100/60 mm Hg. Pulse oximetry on room air shows an oxygen saturation of 92%. Bilateral basilar crackles are heard. An S3 also is heard on cardiac examination. There is edema of the lower extremities. No other abnormalities are noted. Echocardiography 2 months ago showed an ejection fraction of 35%. ECG obtained 2 months ago showed no abnormalities. ECG obtained today shows an irregular, narrow-complex arrhythmia lacking P-waves consistent with atrial fibrillation. Which of the following is the most likely explanation for these findings? A) Cerebral thromboembolism B) Decreased left ventricular filling C) Diastolic dysfunction D) Increased afterload E) Increased venous tone
Correct Answer: B) Decreased left ventricular filling
The ECG shows an irregular, narrow-complex arrhythmia lacking P-waves consistent with atrial fibrillation. Heart failure and atrial fibrillation share many common risk factors, and each predisposes to development of the other. Atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response (heart rate >100 beats/minute) causes acute decompensation of heart failure as a result of decreased left ventricular filling. In normal sinus rhythm, ideal stroke volume is achieved as diastolic filling time is sufficiently long to allow the ventricles to fill completely before contraction. In atrial fibrillation with rapid ventricular response, diastolic filling time is reduced, so the amount of blood in the ventricles prior to ventricular systole is less than would exist with sufficient filling time. This results in reduced stroke volume, decreased cardiac output, symptomatic heart failure, and potentially hypotension and hemodynamic instability in severe cases. Cerebral thromboembolism (Choice A) is a feared complication of atrial fibrillation but typically presents with acute confusion or focal neurologic deficits, not the acute heart failure decompensation seen here. Diastolic dysfunction (Choice C) alone does not likely explain the patient's current decompensated state. While diastolic dysfunction results from impaired relaxation and decreased compliance of the left ventricle, the patient's acute symptoms are more likely caused by poor ventricular filling from uncoordinated atrial contraction and tachycardia. Increased afterload (Choice D) is less likely to explain acute decompensation, especially in the absence of systemic hypertension or known aortic stenosis or coarctation of the aorta. Increased venous tone (Choice E) would result in increased venous return to the heart (preload) because of a greater pressure gradient between the venous system and the right atrium, leading to increased cardiac output. The patient displays signs and symptoms of insufficient cardiac output. Educational Objective: Atrial fibrillation is recognized on ECG as an irregular, narrow-complex arrhythmia lacking distinct P-waves. Uncoordinated atrial contractions and tachycardia result in diminished left ventricular filling during diastole, which decreases cardiac output.
▶ Human Development Polypharmacy in older adulthood 1 question
Source: 3A #9
A 77-year-old woman comes to the physician with her son for a follow-up examination. Two weeks ago, she was discharged from the hospital, where she was treated for chest pain and dyspnea and was diagnosed with left ventricular dysfunction. She is currently asymptomatic and is able to walk up three flights of stairs without symptoms. She has hypertension and hyperlipidemia. Before hospital admission, her medications were atenolol, simvastatin, aspirin, and calcium supplementation. On discharge, lisinopril and furosemide were added to the medication regimen. She lives alone. Her pulse is 68/min, and blood pressure is 132/90 mm Hg. Examination shows no abnormalities. The patient's son is concerned that his mother is confused by her medication regimen. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Ask the patient to bring in all current medications for review B) Contact the hospital pharmacy to reconfirm all pre- and postadmission medications and dosages C) Contact the patient's local pharmacy to confirm all preadmission medications and dosages D) Recommend purchase of a daily medication storage and dispensing device E) Review the medication list on the clinic chart
Correct Answer: A) Ask the patient to bring in all current medications for review
Polypharmacy is a common problem among elderly patients. Patients taking multiple medications are often confused about which medications are for which conditions and how to take each medication. When medications are discontinued or changed, patients may inadvertently continue taking incorrect medications or doses. Patients may also have multiple prescribing providers unaware of other medications being taken. Having the patient bring in all current medications for review yields the safest outcome. This allows the physician to confirm the patient has the correct medications and takes the correct dose at the correct time of day, relieving confusion and decreasing the risk for adverse effects from wrong medications or doses. Contacting the hospital pharmacy (Choice B) is only as accurate as hospital records, which may not include medications the patient is still taking at home but neglected to report. Contacting the local pharmacy (Choice C) leaves room for error as the patient may receive medications from multiple pharmacies or take over-the-counter medications. Recommending a medication storage device (Choice D) may be useful but only after verifying the accuracy of prescriptions and doses. Reviewing the medication list on the clinic chart (Choice E) is inadequate because it may be incomplete or inaccurate, and patients better recall medication counseling when shown the actual bottles, pill colors, sizes, and prescription labels. The most accurate way to complete medication reconciliation is to have the patient bring all medications to the visit, improving both accuracy and quality of medication counseling.
▶ Human Development Micturition patterns in infancy and childhood 1 question
Source: 5A #3
A 6-year-old girl is brought to the office by her mother for a well-child examination. The mother is concerned about nighttime bedwetting, reporting that although the patient has been toilet-trained for the past 2 years during the daytime, she has "never had a dry night." The patient has three siblings who the mother states were all dry through the night by age 5 years. The patient is otherwise healthy and vaccinations are up-to-date. Vital signs are within normal limits and physical examination shows no abnormalities. Fasting fingerstick blood glucose concentration is 82 mg/dL, and urinalysis is within the reference ranges. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Desmopressin therapy B) Fluid restriction after 2 pm C) Renal ultrasonography D) Urine culture E) Reassurance only
Correct Answer: E) Reassurance only
Enuresis is seen in approximately 15% of children and is defined as episodes of urinary incontinence in children age 5 years and older. This patient has primary monosymptomatic nocturnal enuresis, referring to nighttime bedwetting in children who have never been previously continent at night with no other lower urinary tract symptoms or history of bladder dysfunction. Nocturnal enuresis occurs as a result of a combination of factors, including maturational delay, genetic factors, disturbed sleep, and detrusor overactivity. Evaluation should include a thorough medical history and physical examination, as well as a urinalysis to screen for diabetic ketoacidosis, diabetes insipidus, and urinary tract infection. In almost all cases, monosymptomatic nocturnal enuresis resolves spontaneously. Primary management involves treatment of coexisting conditions, patient and parent education, and motivational therapy, including reassurance. This patient has normal vital signs, normal physical examination, normal blood glucose, and normal urinalysis, indicating no underlying pathologic cause. Desmopressin therapy (Choice A) is a secondary treatment option only recommended after 3 to 6 months of primary management without improvement. Fluid restriction after 2 pm (Choice B) is incorrect; limiting fluid intake in the evenings (after 5 pm) can be considered as part of initial management but should be done carefully with corresponding increase in morning fluid intake. Restricting fluids at 2 pm would lead to inadequate daily fluid intake. Renal ultrasonography (Choice C) and other imaging modalities are indicated only in patients with recurrent urinary tract infections or signs/symptoms of structural abnormalities; this patient has no such indication. Urine culture (Choice D) is not indicated unless there are white blood cells or nitrites on urinalysis; this patient's urinalysis is normal. The most appropriate next step is reassurance to the patient and family that this is a common, self-resolving condition.
▶ Cardiovascular System Myocarditis 1 question
Source: 3A #11
A 40-year-old man has had fatigue for 2 weeks and progressively severe shortness of breath over the past 24 hours. He had been well until 3 weeks ago when he developed fever, sore throat, and rhinitis. Examination shows distended neck veins and basilar crackles. S1 and S2 are normal; an S3 gallop is heard with no murmurs. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Acute myocardial infarction B) Acute rheumatic fever C) Hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy D) Pericarditis E) Viral myocarditis
Correct Answer: E) Viral myocarditis
Viral myocarditis is the most likely diagnosis. Myocarditis refers to inflammation of the cardiac myocytes, with notable viral causes including coxsackievirus B, parvovirus B19, human herpesvirus-6, adenovirus, and HIV. This patient presents with the classic presentation: a viral prodrome (fever, sore throat, rhinitis 3 weeks prior) followed by acute heart failure symptoms including fatigue, dyspnea, jugular venous distention (volume overload), and basilar crackles (pulmonary edema). The S3 gallop indicates ventricular dysfunction. Diagnostic studies typically show elevated cardiac biomarkers (troponin), though ECG and chest X-ray may be normal or show pulmonary edema/pleural effusions. Treatment is supportive, with diuresis and inotropic support as needed. Acute myocardial infarction (Choice A) is less likely in this otherwise healthy 40-year-old with acute heart failure preceded by infectious symptoms rather than typical ischemic presentation. Acute rheumatic fever (Choice B) is a delayed complication of untreated streptococcal pharyngitis occurring 1-5 weeks after infection. While the timeline matches, rheumatic fever is uncommon in adults and lacks supporting features such as migratory polyarthralgia, pancarditis, subcutaneous nodules, erythema marginatum, or Sydenham chorea. Hypertrophic obstructive cardiomyopathy (Choice C) classically presents with dyspnea, chest pain, syncope, or sudden cardiac death in young athletes with family history, not with acute heart failure in the setting of an infectious prodrome. Pericarditis (Choice D) can result from viral infection but typically presents with chest pain and a friction rub on auscultation. While it can cause pericardial effusion and tamponade, the clinical presentation here is more consistent with myocardial inflammation causing ventricular dysfunction. Educational Objective: Myocarditis refers to inflammation of the cardiac myocytes, which can result in acute heart failure from impaired ventricular function. Viral infections are the most common infectious cause, typically presenting with fever and viral illness symptoms preceding heart failure development.
▶ Social Sciences Advance care planning 1 question
Source: 3A #15
A 77-year-old man is brought to the physician as a new patient by his wife and adult children. He received the diagnosis of dementia, Alzheimer type, 1 year ago and is aware of his diagnosis. His family reports that he enjoys gardening and reading but is unable to manage his finances. He has no other serious medical illnesses and has not felt depressed. Vital signs are within normal limits. He is alert and oriented to person and place but not to time. His Mini-Mental State Examination score is 24/30. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. An advance directive has not been established. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in discussing advance care planning? A) Advise the patient's wife to discuss the issues with the patient, then inform the physician of the decisions made B) Advise the patient's wife to obtain legal guardianship through a court order C) Ask the patient's children to establish the goals of treatment D) Discuss the issues in private with the patient's wife E) Discuss the issues with the patient
Correct Answer: E) Discuss the issues with the patient
Patients with dementia face difficult challenges as their decision-making ability declines through disease progression. Advanced care planning for later stages of dementia should take place through discussion between the patient, their family, and their physician. Patients should be encouraged to participate in their own medical decision-making and exercise their capacity to the greatest possible extent. Patient participation allows expression of their own goals, beliefs, values, and preferences. Advanced care planning is a continual process and should be revisited routinely or as clinical condition changes. This patient is experiencing mild cognitive dysfunction and should be involved in his own care planning at this time. Choice A neglects the physician's responsibility to offer guidance and expertise; conversations regarding advance directives are difficult and may be ignored or postponed without physician prompting. Choice B is not necessary given the patient has only mild cognitive dysfunction and likely retains decision-making capacity; legal guardianship may be needed in later disease stages. Choices C and D are inappropriate because this patient likely retains capacity and should be allowed and encouraged to participate in his own medical decision-making. Additionally, these measures may violate patient confidentiality.
▶ Pregnancy Childbirth Puerperium Ectopic pregnancy 1 question
Source: 3A #17
A 19-year-old woman comes to the office because of a 4-hour history of severe right lower abdominal pain, heavy vaginal bleeding, lightheadedness, and dizziness. Her last menstrual period was 2 months ago. She has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. She has been sexually active with one male partner for 2 years; they use condoms inconsistently. Her temperature is 37.0°C (98.6°F), pulse is 112/min, and respirations are 18/min. While supine, her blood pressure is 92/58 mm Hg; while sitting, her blood pressure is 76/52 mm Hg. Abdominal examination shows moderate tenderness to palpation of the right lower quadrant but no rebound. Pelvic examination shows a small pool of blood in the vaginal vault, a uterine size at the upper limit of normal, and moderate tenderness to palpation of the right adnexa. No other abnormalities are noted. Urine β-hCG testing is positive. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Appendicitis B) Ectopic pregnancy C) Endometriosis D) Endometritis E) Threatened abortion
Correct Answer: B) Ectopic pregnancy
Ectopic pregnancy is an abnormal pregnancy in which the fertilized ovum implants in a non-endometrial location, most commonly the fallopian tube, but also on the ovary, within the peritoneal cavity, or elsewhere. It presents with vaginal bleeding and abdominopelvic pain. Complications include rupture, bleeding, and hemoperitoneum, leading to hemodynamic instability with tachycardia and postural hypotension (as evidenced by this patient's orthostatic blood pressure changes: supine 92/58 mm Hg versus sitting 76/52 mm Hg). When blood loss exceeds compensatory mechanisms, profound hypotension develops. Patients may have diffuse or localized abdominal tenderness with or without rebound tenderness. A ruptured ectopic pregnancy is a surgical emergency requiring laparoscopy with salpingectomy. Hemodynamically unstable patients require immediate fluid resuscitation, transfusion, and operative control. Appendicitis (Choice A) presents with loss of appetite, nausea, vomiting, right lower quadrant pain, fever, and leukocytosis. It does not explain vaginal bleeding or positive β-hCG. Endometriosis (Choice C) is characterized by endometrial tissue outside the uterus, presenting with abdominal and pelvic pain, dysmenorrhea, dyspareunia, and dyschezia. It does not cause hemodynamically significant bleeding. Endometritis (Choice D) is acute bacterial infection of the endometrium presenting with foul-smelling discharge, pelvic pain, fever, chills, and nausea, with uterine fundal tenderness on examination. This patient has adnexal rather than uterine tenderness, making ectopic pregnancy more likely. Threatened abortion (Choice E) presents with vaginal bleeding without cervical dilation or loss of products of conception in a pregnant patient. While this patient has positive β-hCG and vaginal bleeding, a ruptured ectopic pregnancy is more likely to cause significant hemodynamic instability than threatened abortion. Educational Objective: Ectopic pregnancy is a nonviable pregnancy with implantation outside the endometrium, most commonly in the fallopian tube. It presents with abdominopelvic pain and vaginal bleeding. Complications include rupture with hemoperitoneum and hemodynamic instability, warranting emergent surgical treatment.
▶ Respiratory System Squamous Cell Carcinoma of Bronchus and Lung 1 question
Source: 3A #20
A 55-year-old woman comes to the physician for a health maintenance examination. She has smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 35 years. Previous annual Pap smears have shown normal findings. It has been 5 years since her last mammography. She has never had a screening sigmoidoscopy or a test of the stool for occult blood. Her sister died of cervical cancer at the age of 50 years. This patient is at greatest risk for death from which of the following cancers? A) Breast B) Cervical C) Colon D) Lung E) Uterine
Correct Answer: D) Lung
The patient's 35-year history of smoking one pack of cigarettes daily puts her at greatest risk for death from lung cancer. Lung cancer is the second most common type of cancer and the leading cause of cancer-related deaths. Tobacco and second-hand smoke exposure are the strongest risk factors for lung cancer. Additional occupational risk factors include asbestos, arsenic, radon, and uranium exposure, as well as family history of lung cancer. Lung cancers are classically categorized as small cell or non-small cell lung cancers, with non-small cell types including adenocarcinoma, squamous cell carcinoma, and large cell carcinoma. Lung cancer may present with cough, hemoptysis, dyspnea, and chest pain. Preventative management involves counseling on smoking cessation and consideration of low-dose CT screening for patients aged 55-80 with significant smoking history. Breast cancer (Choice A) is the most common cause of cancer-related death in adult women, with risk factors including age, family history, genetic mutations (BRCA), obesity, prior thoracic radiation, and tobacco use. However, tobacco is more strongly associated with lung cancer risk. Cervical cancer (Choice B) risk factors include HPV infection (especially HPV-16 and HPV-18), multiple sexual partners, tobacco use, immunosuppression, and family history. This patient has had reassuring annual Pap smears without evidence of disease, and lung cancer is more common overall in patients with significant smoking history. Colon cancer (Choice C) incidence has decreased with effective screening implementation. Risk factors include age, hereditary disorders (familial adenomatous polyposis, Lynch syndrome), high-fat diets, inflammatory bowel disease, and tobacco use. While screening is recommended, lung cancer risk is greater in this patient. Uterine cancer (Choice E) risk factors include increased estrogen exposure, obesity, infertility/nulliparity, tamoxifen exposure, Lynch syndrome, and positive family history. This is not the primary cancer risk in this patient. Educational Objective: Lung cancer is the second most common type of cancer and the leading cause of cancer-related deaths. Tobacco and second-hand smoke exposure are the strongest risk factors.
▶ Respiratory System Foreign body aspiration (nose, pharynx, larynx, trachea) 1 question
Source: 3A #21
A 4-year-old boy is brought to the physician 5 minutes after choking on a piece of meat. On arrival, he is coughing frequently but is able to vocalize. He is not cyanotic. His pulse is 145/min. Breath sounds are loud and sonorous on auscultation of the chest. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Back blows B) Chest compressions C) Heimlich maneuver D) Mouth-to-mouth resuscitation E) No immediate intervention necessary
Correct Answer: E) No immediate intervention necessary
Foreign body aspiration can cause obstruction of the airway, leading to impaired oxygenation and ventilation. Children from 1 to 4 years old are at increased risk due to their ability to stand and mobilize independently, grasp and place small objects into their mouths, and immature swallowing mechanism. Foreign bodies can be located anywhere in the airway between the larynx and the bronchi. Children can present with severe respiratory distress, cyanosis, and stridor, or with a less severe presentation of partial airway obstruction, presenting with cough, tachypnea, decreased air entry on auscultation, or wheezing. Chest x-ray can show hyperinflation of one lung caused by air trapping and partial airway obstruction in which air passes via inhalation but not exhalation. Foreign bodies are typically in the right lung due to the more vertical anatomic position of the right main bronchus. Management of foreign body aspiration involves flexible or rigid bronchoscopy to remove the object. Object removal is necessary as retained foreign bodies can be complicated by airway inflammation and infection. In this patient with mild symptoms and no emergent airway compromise, no immediate intervention is necessary.
