Back blows and chest compressions are indicated only if an infant presents with complete airway obstruction with inability to speak or cough. In older patients with complete airway obstruction, a Heimlich maneuver can be attempted, involving abdominal thrusts to dislodge the foreign body. These maneuvers should be avoided in patients with partial obstruction as they may cause complete obstruction. Mouth-to-mouth resuscitation should not be attempted as it may cause movement of the foreign body and convert partial obstruction to complete airway obstruction. Educational Objective: Foreign body aspiration can cause airway obstruction leading to impaired oxygenation and ventilation. Patients with partial obstruction and mild symptoms of cough and tachypnea require no emergent intervention unless complete airway obstruction develops.
▶ Human Development Preventive and travel medicine in older adulthood 1 question
Source: 3A #22
A 72-year-old woman comes to the physician because the physician's office was notified of one of the patient's lapsed prescription refills 1 month ago. She has a 22-year history of hypertension, 10-year history of osteoarthritis, and 8-year history of congestive heart failure. Her medications are lisinopril, carvedilol, furosemide, and acetaminophen; the dosage schedule of some of these medications requires that she take them several times daily. She lives alone and has no relatives near her home. She appears alert and in no distress. She is dressed appropriately and maintains eye contact. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows mild osteoarthritic changes in the joints of the hands and knees. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. Assessment of which of the following would be most effective in determining this patient's ability to manage her medications? A) Gait and balance B) Home environment C) Instrumental activities of daily living D) Likelihood of depression E) Social support
Correct Answer: C) Instrumental activities of daily living
Instrumental activities of daily living (IADLs) are advanced skills requiring higher cognitive abilities than basic activities of daily living (ADLs). Examples include doing laundry, housework, shopping, preparing meals, taking medications, managing money, and driving. In contrast, basic ADLs include bathing, dressing, and feeding. For elderly adults to remain independent in their homes, they must be able to perform IADLs. In this patient, the examination suggests adequate basic ADLs (she is dressed appropriately), but there is no evidence of adequate IADL performance. Determining whether the patient possesses the skills necessary to refill and correctly take her medications is critical in ensuring compliance. This directly addresses the presenting problem of a missed prescription refill. Gait and balance (A) may be necessary to evaluate physical capabilities (e.g., in a recent fall), but is less relevant to a missed prescription refill. Home environment (B) and social support (E) are important health factors, but the knowledge that she lives alone with no nearby relatives is likely adequate; these are less directly contributing to the missed refill. Screening for major depressive disorder (D) is beneficial in elderly patients with health or behavioral changes, and pseudodementia from depression could impair IADLs, but evaluating instrumental activities of daily living is more directly important than assessing depression likelihood in this case. Educational Objective: Instrumental activities of daily living are necessary for elderly patients to live independently. In patients whose behavior deviates from baseline, review of these activities can identify underlying cognitive causes and functional limitations affecting medication management and compliance.
▶ Respiratory System Pulmonary Embolism 1 question
Source: 3A #24
A 35-year-old woman with hypertension comes to the physician because of moderate right-sided chest pain and cough productive of blood that began suddenly 2 hours ago. During this time, she also has had mild pain and swelling of her right calf. Her medications are hydrochlorothiazide and an oral contraceptive. She has smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 15 years. She is in mild respiratory distress. Her temperature is 37.4°C (99.3°F), pulse is 110/min and regular, respirations are 22/min, and blood pressure is 140/96 mm Hg. Pulse oximetry on room air shows an oxygen saturation of 92%. Cardiopulmonary examination shows no abnormalities. Examination of the right calf shows warmth, edema, and tenderness to palpation. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in diagnosis? A) Cardiac MRI B) CT angiography of the chest C) ECG D) PET scan E) Pulmonary function testing
Correct Answer: B) CT angiography of the chest
This patient presents with a classic constellation of findings suspicious for pulmonary embolism (PE): acute chest pain, hemoptysis, signs of deep venous thrombosis (unilateral calf swelling with warmth, edema, and tenderness), hypoxia (O2 sat 92%), and tachycardia (HR 110). Pulmonary thromboembolism is caused by occlusion of a pulmonary artery with impaired blood flow to a lung segment. Risk factors include immobility, DVT, obesity, recent surgery, pregnancy, oral contraceptive use, smoking, trauma, long bone fractures, and inherited thrombophilia. PE presents acutely with chest pain, shortness of breath, and hypoxia. Pulmonary emboli are often undetectable on chest X-ray, though large emboli with infarction may show wedge-shaped areas of infarction or Westermark sign (unilateral oligemia). CT angiography of the chest is the most appropriate next diagnostic step for PE evaluation. Additionally, oral contraceptive pills are contraindicated in women over age 35 who smoke due to increased thrombosis risk. Cardiac MRI (Choice A) is the gold standard for left ventricular hypertrophy diagnosis but is limited by availability, cost, and logistics as a first-line tool. This patient's symptoms are pulmonary in origin, making cardiac MRI inappropriate. ECG (Choice C) has modest sensitivity and specificity for PE. It may be normal or show sinus tachycardia. With right heart strain, it may demonstrate right axis deviation, S wave in lead I, Q wave in lead III, and inverted T wave in lead III (S1Q3T3 pattern). PET scan (Choice D) detects cancer or hypermetabolic lesions. While lung cancer can present with hemoptysis, it is less likely than PE in this young patient with clear signs of deep venous thrombosis. Pulmonary function testing (Choice E) is used for chronic restrictive diseases (idiopathic pulmonary fibrosis) or obstructive diseases (asthma, COPD). These are chronic processes, whereas this patient has acute PE symptoms. Educational Objective: Pulmonary embolism presents acutely with hypoxia, tachycardia, hemoptysis, chest pain, and syncope. CT angiography of the chest is the most appropriate diagnostic step in PE evaluation.
▶ Respiratory System Allergic Rhinitis 1 question
Source: 3A #26
A 32-year-old man comes to the physician for a routine examination in late winter. He has a history of allergic rhinitis every spring and routinely takes diphenhydramine 2 to 3 times daily when his symptoms begin. His symptoms have been more severe during the past 2 years; he also has had sinusitis during this time requiring prolonged use of antibiotics. He is otherwise healthy and takes no medications. Examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation to prevent a recurrence of his allergic symptoms this spring? A) Recommend minimizing outdoor activities B) Recommend continued use of over-the-counter antihistamines C) Recommend desensitization therapy D) Prescribe oral montelukast E) Prescribe oral prednisone F) Prescribe inhaled nasal fluticasone
Correct Answer: F) Prescribe inhaled nasal fluticasone
Allergic rhinitis is characterized by IgE-mediated inflammation (type I hypersensitivity) of the nasal mucosa. Patients commonly experience nasal or sinus congestion, rhinorrhea, sneezing, nasal pruritus, conjunctivitis, and tearing. Common inciting allergens include plant pollens, animal dander, dust, and mold, whose presence may be seasonally dependent. Patients with allergic rhinitis may display signs of atopic disease processes, such as asthma or eczema. First-line treatment is with inhaled nasal corticosteroids (e.g., fluticasone); other options include inhaled leukotriene receptor antagonists and oral antihistamines. Second-generation antihistamines, such as cetirizine, may be preferable for many patients because of their reduced central nervous system effects, such as somnolence. Removal or avoidance of the offending allergen is essential to prevent further exacerbations and improve symptoms, although this may be impractical for patients with seasonal allergies. Minimizing outdoor activities (Choice A) will have a detrimental impact on the patient's quality of life and is not necessary; pharmacotherapy usually provides sufficient alleviation of symptoms for patients to continue with normal activities. Continued use of over-the-counter antihistamines (Choice B) is appropriate as adjunctive therapy to inhaled corticosteroids, but second-generation antihistamines are often preferable to diphenhydramine because of their reduced central nervous system effects. Desensitization therapy (Choice C) may be performed in patients who require treatment with particular antibiotics but is not typically performed for patients with seasonal allergies. Oral montelukast (Choice D) is a leukotriene receptor antagonist that is useful for the treatment of allergies; however, it is less effective than inhaled corticosteroids and is associated with potentially severe side effects, including depression, insomnia, and increased suicidality. As a result of these side effects, the Food and Drug Administration advised restricting its use for allergic rhinitis in 2020. Oral prednisone (Choice E) is not usually required for the treatment of allergies and is associated with severe side effects, including altered mood, insomnia, weight gain, and immunosuppression. Educational Objective: Allergic rhinitis presents with nasal or sinus congestion, rhinorrhea, sneezing, nasal pruritus, conjunctivitis, and tearing, and is often caused by seasonal allergens. First-line treatment is with inhaled nasal corticosteroids.
▶ Endocrine System Hypothyroidism 1 question
Source: 3A #27
A 57-year-old woman comes to the physician for a follow-up examination. She was diagnosed with primary hypothyroidism 8 weeks ago. She is currently asymptomatic. Her only medication is levothyroxine. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows no abnormalities. Serum studies show a thyroid-stimulating hormone concentration of 9.5 µU/ml. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in pharmacotherapy? A) Add liothyronine to the medication regimen B) Continue the current levothyroxine dosage C) Decrease the levothyroxine dosage D) Discontinue levothyroxine E) Increase the levothyroxine dosage
Correct Answer: E) Increase the levothyroxine dosage
Primary hypothyroidism is characterized serologically by an increased TSH concentration and decreased T4 caused by decreased thyroid hormone production. Patients with hypothyroidism should be treated with levothyroxine regardless of symptom presence. This patient's elevated TSH (9.5 µU/ml, above normal range of 0.5-5.0 µU/ml) indicates inadequate levothyroxine dosing and unmet physiologic demand for thyroid hormone. The levothyroxine dose must be increased and titrated until TSH normalizes. Inadequate hypothyroidism treatment increases cardiovascular and liver disease risk. Choice A (liothyronine): Synthetic T3 is reserved for severe, refractory hypothyroidism with controversial efficacy; dose escalation of levothyroxine is the appropriate initial step. Choice B (continue current dose): Inappropriate given abnormally elevated TSH; inadequate treatment carries significant morbidity. Choice C (decrease dose): Inappropriate and would worsen hypothyroidism for the same reason as Choice B. Choice D (discontinue): Inappropriate as elevated TSH indicates undertreated, not overtreated, hypothyroidism. Levothyroxine is well-tolerated with minimal adverse effects; only inappropriately high doses produce hyperthyroid symptoms. Educational Objective: Patients with primary hypothyroidism should be treated with levothyroxine regardless of symptom presence. Therapy goals are to reduce hypothyroid symptoms and normalize serum TSH concentration through appropriate dose titration.
▶ Cardiovascular System Claudication 1 question
Source: 3A #28
A 68-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 1-month history of increasingly severe pain in his legs. He describes the pain as an ache that radiates to both calves. The pain becomes increasingly severe when he walks more than three blocks and resolves with rest. He has an 8-year history of low back pain after a fall from a ladder. His only medication is ibuprofen. He has smoked one-half pack of cigarettes daily for 45 years. He drinks two 12-oz beers daily. His temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 86/min, respirations are 12/min, and blood pressure is 138/92 mm Hg. Examination shows mild edema of the lower extremities. There is a small effusion over both knees but no erythema or tenderness to palpation. The shins are cool to the touch. Crossed straight-leg raising test is negative, and deep tendon reflexes are 2+ bilaterally. Pedal pulses are 1+ and equal bilaterally. Capillary refill time is 3 seconds in both feet. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in diagnosis? A) Arthrocentesis of both knees B) Determination of ankle brachial indices C) MRI of the lumbar spine D) Venous Doppler ultrasonography E) X-rays of both knees
Correct Answer: B) Determination of ankle brachial indices
This patient presents with classic claudication: exertional leg pain (calf aches worsening with walking >3 blocks) that resolves with rest. Physical examination findings support peripheral vascular disease (PVD): cool shins, diminished pedal pulses (1+), and prolonged capillary refill (3 seconds). The ankle brachial index (ABI) is the most appropriate next diagnostic step. ABI is a noninvasive, office-based test calculated by dividing systolic blood pressure at the ankle by systolic blood pressure at the brachial artery. An ABI <0.9 indicates occlusive arterial vascular disease with high sensitivity for clinically relevant PVD; 0.9-1.3 suggests normal arteries; >1.3 suggests calcified vessels requiring further evaluation. Once PVD is confirmed, patients should be started on statins and antiplatelet agents, with exercise programs to improve collateral flow. Choice A (arthrocentesis) is unnecessary as the small knee effusion is incidental and the pain pattern is vascular, not arthritic. Choice C (MRI lumbar spine) would be appropriate for neurogenic claudication, which presents with pain relief on forward flexion and worsening supine/standing; this patient lacks neurologic deficits. Choice D (venous Doppler) assesses for DVT, which typically presents with unilateral pain and swelling, not claudication; while it can identify arterial stenosis, ABI is quicker and office-based. Choice E (knee X-rays) is unhelpful for a vascular condition. Educational Objective: Claudication is defined by lower extremity pain and cramping with exercise that improves with rest, indicating underlying occlusive arterial disease. ABI measurement is the standard diagnostic approach for confirming PVD.
▶ Social Sciences Intimate partner violence 1 question
Source: 3A #29
A 35-year-old woman who has been a patient for several years comes to the office because a pregnancy test done at home was positive. The pregnancy is confirmed by physical examination. During the past 5 months, she had two visits to the emergency department for traumatic injuries, each of which she attributed to her own clumsiness. She is married and has four children. Reluctantly, she reports concern about her personal safety and, with great embarrassment, confesses that the emergency department visits were precipitated by her husband hitting and pushing her. She says that she is afraid he will hurt her if she leaves him or if he finds out she is pregnant again. He lost his job nearly 1 year ago. She says that he is abusive only when he has been drinking. She tearfully asks to be referred to someone who can terminate her pregnancy. Which of the following is the most accurate statement concerning the patient's situation? A) Her husband's behavior is a logical consequence of his job loss B) Her pregnancy increases her risk for abuse C) Hiding all liquor in the house will reduce her risk for abuse D) Marital therapy is usually helpful in reducing the risk for violence E) Termination of the pregnancy will increase her risk for abuse
Correct Answer: B) Her pregnancy increases her risk for abuse
Domestic abuse or intimate partner violence (IPV) may involve episodes of verbal, sexual, financial, emotional, and/or physical abuse. Risk factors for IPV are a combination of intersecting individual, relationship, community, and societal factors. Examples of risk factors for victims include female sex, alcohol use or drug use, unemployment, poverty, history of depression or mental illness, and relationships characterized by dominance and control or excessive jealousy. Risk factors for perpetrators include exposure to childhood violence, recent job loss or instability, and substance use. It has been shown that IPV often increases or begins during pregnancy, especially unintended pregnancies. IPV is often difficult to diagnose as a result of patients often concealing abusive relationships; signs may be subtle or absent. Inconsistent explanation of injuries, delay in seeking treatment, and frequent emergency department visits increase suspicion for IPV. Choice A is incorrect because IPV and physical abuse are never acceptable and would not be a logical consequence of job loss, though job loss as a stressor may precipitate episodes. Choice C is incorrect because while substance use increases the risk for IPV occurrence, it is not a direct cause of IPV, and hiding all liquor in the house would not necessarily reduce her risk for abuse. Choice D is incorrect because marital therapy is unlikely to reduce the risk for violence. Interventions for perpetrators are often minimally successful, and therapy has not been conclusively shown to reduce the risk for violence. Choice E is incorrect because termination of the pregnancy would be unlikely to increase her risk for abuse; rather, pregnancy increases the risk for domestic abuse. Educational Objective: Risk factors for IPV include recent job loss or instability and substance use. IPV often increases or begins during pregnancy, especially unintended pregnancies.
▶ Human Development Nutrition in infancy and childhood 1 question
Source: 3A #30
An 18-month-old girl is brought to the physician by her mother for a routine examination. She was born at 32 weeks' gestation by cesarean delivery because of maternal preeclampsia. Apgar scores were 5 and 6 at 1 and 5 minutes, respectively. She was in the neonatal intensive care unit for 2 days because of fetal lung immaturity; she progressed well and was discharged at 1 week of age. She was exclusively breast-fed until the age of 6 months, when pureed foods were added to her diet. She was weaned off breast milk at the age of 14 months; at that time, soft foods and whole milk, which she tolerates well, were added to her diet. She receives no medications. Results of a complete blood count and measurement of blood lead concentration at the age of 1 year were within the reference ranges. She appears healthy. She is at the 25th percentile for length, 5th percentile for weight, and 25th percentile for head circumference. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows no abnormalities. Screening for which of the following deficiencies is most appropriate at this time? A) Calcium B) Folic acid C) Iron D) Vitamin B12 (cobalamin) E) Vitamin D
Correct Answer: C) Iron
Iron is potentially deficient in this patient and screening is most appropriate. Premature infants are at increased risk for iron deficiency compared to term infants. In utero, fetuses develop iron stores primarily during the last trimester of gestation, so premature birth at 32 weeks reduces the time available for adequate iron store development. Additionally, preterm infants who spend time in the neonatal intensive care unit are at greater risk for iron depletion from frequent blood draws, which further reduce their already insufficient iron stores. Iron stores typically become insufficient within about 4 months without proper supplementation. Furthermore, breast milk contains little iron, so premature infants who are exclusively breast-fed are at even higher risk. All such patients should be screened for iron deficiency, with supplementation using oral iron for deficient patients. Calcium (Choice A) deficiency in otherwise healthy infants is rare and can be seen in DiGeorge syndrome. Folic acid (Choice B) deficiency is rare as folate is present in breast milk, and most mothers taking prenatal vitamins throughout pregnancy have adequate stores for breast-feeding infants. Vitamin B12 (cobalamin) (Choice D) stores in infants depend on sufficient maternal stores. Most women receiving prenatal care and taking prenatal vitamins are replete in vitamin B12. Vitamin D (Choice E) deficiency is uncommon as most infants who are exclusively breast-fed receive vitamin D supplements. Educational Objective: Preterm infants are at increased risk for iron deficiency due to less time in utero to develop adequate iron stores and because breast milk contains little iron. Preterm infants who are exclusively breast-fed are at even higher risk and should be screened for iron deficiency.
▶ Biostatistics Epidemiology Population Health Positive Predictive Value 1 question
Source: 3A #34
A large, multisite family medicine practice is planning a point-of-care ultrasonography screening program for abdominal aortic aneurysms in men age 65 to 75 years with a history of smoking. A pilot study of 100 patients in the practice group found point-of-care ultrasonography had the following results: Sensitivity 90%, Specificity 90%, Positive predictive value 36, Negative predictive value 99, Positive likelihood ratio 9.00, Negative likelihood ratio 0.11. The practice's prevalence of abdominal aneurysm was estimated at 6% for this age group and gender. If the actual practice prevalence of aortic aneurysm is 5%, which of the following would decrease? A) Negative likelihood ratio B) Negative predictive value C) Positive likelihood ratio D) Positive predictive value E) Sensitivity F) Specificity
Correct Answer: D) Positive predictive value
If the practice prevalence of abdominal aortic aneurysm is only 5%, not the estimated 6%, then the positive predictive value (PPV) of the point-of-care ultrasound will decrease. Pretest probability, or the baseline risk for having the condition of interest, is proportionate to the prevalence of disease in the population that will be tested. Pretest probability is important because it impacts the PPV and negative predictive value (NPV) of a test. PPV and NPV are statistical measures that define the proportion of positive test results that are true positives (positive tests in patients who have the condition) and true negatives (negative tests in patients without the condition). Both PPV and NPV vary with disease prevalence; PPV varies directly with prevalence whereas NPV varies inversely with prevalence. Therefore, the less prevalent a disease, the lower the PPV of a test used in that population and the higher the NPV of a test used in that population. In contrast, pretest probability does not impact the sensitivity or specificity of a test. Negative likelihood ratio (Choice A) is the ratio of false negatives to true negatives, calculated as (1-sensitivity)/specificity. Positive likelihood ratio (Choice C) is the ratio of true positives to false positives, calculated as sensitivity/(1-specificity). Because sensitivity and specificity are independent of pretest probability, a change in prevalence from 6% to 5% will not impact either positive or negative likelihood ratios. Negative predictive value (NPV) (Choice B) varies inversely with pretest probability rather than directly. Therefore, a decrease in prevalence will actually increase the NPV. Sensitivity (Choice E) and specificity (Choice F) do not differ with changes in pretest probability. Therefore, a change in the prevalence of the condition being tested will not change these parameters. Educational Objective: The pretest probability, which is proportionate to disease prevalence, impacts the positive predictive value (PPV) and negative predictive value (NPV) of a test. PPV is impacted directly and NPV inversely. In contrast, pretest probability does not impact the sensitivity or specificity of a test.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Adenovirus Conjunctivitis and Keratoconjunctivitis 1 question
Source: 3A #35
A previously healthy 15-year-old girl is brought to the physician because of a 2-day history of runny nose and nonproductive cough and a 1-day history of irritation and tearing of both eyes. The patient's temperature is 36.7°C (98°F); other vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows a small amount of crusty discharge over the eyelids. The conjunctivae are injected. There is no photophobia. Visual acuity is 20/20 bilaterally. There is a small amount of clear nasal discharge. The lungs are clear to auscultation. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Culture of the ocular discharge B) Oral doxycycline therapy C) Symptomatic treatment only D) Topical acyclovir therapy E) Topical sulfacetamide therapy
Correct Answer: C) Symptomatic treatment only
Viral conjunctivitis presents with bilateral follicular conjunctivitis, tearing, blurry vision, and irritation. Symptoms typically begin in one eye prior to spreading to the contralateral eye. Patients may also note a coincident or recent upper respiratory tract infection and may have symptoms of fever, rhinorrhea, and preauricular or submandibular lymphadenopathy. Adenoviruses are the most commonly implicated in the pathogenesis of viral conjunctivitis. The majority of patients improve with conservative symptomatic treatment only, including lubricating eye drops and cool compresses. Patients with epidemic keratoconjunctivitis (a severe form with photophobia, subepithelial corneal inflammatory infiltrates, and conjunctival pseudomembranes) benefit from topical steroids. Viral conjunctivitis is highly contagious, and patients should be counseled to stay home from work or school and to wash their hands frequently. Most patients do not require conjunctival swabs for PCR testing. Choice A (Culture of ocular discharge) is not typically required as it will not impact management for most patients given that viral conjunctivitis is self-limited. Culture may be helpful for bacterial conjunctivitis. Choice B (Oral doxycycline) is helpful for conjunctivitis caused by Chlamydia trachomatis, which may resemble viral conjunctivitis in the acute phase but typically presents a more chronic course. Choice D (Topical acyclovir) is used to treat herpetic viral conjunctivitis, which is typically accompanied by vesicular skin lesions and corneal dendritic lesions. Choice E (Topical sulfacetamide) is a sulfa antibiotic used to treat bacterial conjunctivitis, which is typically unilateral and presents with purulent discharge.
▶ Blood Lymphoreticular System Sickle cell disease 1 question
Source: 3A #36
A 4-year-old boy is brought to the office by his parents because of a 2-day history of increasingly severe right lower abdominal pain and nausea. He has not had fever, vomiting, or diarrhea. He receives no medications. He recently emigrated with his family from Libya to the USA. He appears to be in mild distress. His temperature is 37.2°C (99.0°F), pulse is 90/min and regular, respirations are 14/min, and blood pressure is 118/74 mm Hg. Abdominal examination shows tenderness to palpation of the right lower quadrant with no rebound, guarding, or masses. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. Laboratory studies show: Hemoglobin 8.2 g/dL (N=11.5-14.5), Mean corpuscular hemoglobin 20 pg/cell (N=25-31), Mean corpuscular volume 65 µm³ (N=76-90), Leukocyte count 8600/mm³ (with a normal differential), Urinalysis shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is most likely to confirm the underlying diagnosis? A) Abdominal ultrasonography B) Abdominal x-ray C) Blood smear D) Diagnostic laparoscopy E) Examination of the stool for ova and parasites
Correct Answer: C) Blood smear
A blood smear is most likely to confirm the underlying diagnosis of sickle cell disease (SCD). This patient presents with microcytic anemia (hemoglobin 8.2 g/dL, MCV 65 µm³, MCH 20 pg/cell) and acute right lower quadrant abdominal pain, which is concerning for a vaso-occlusive pain crisis secondary to SCD. The presence of sickle cells on the peripheral smear would strongly favor SCD as the cause of his pain. The absence of fever or leukocytosis lowers the probability of intra-abdominal infection. Patients with sickle cell crisis may develop pain secondary to vaso-occlusive events in nearly any part of the body, but acute abdominal pain and dactylitis are particularly common in children. Recent emigration from Libya, where sickle cell disease is more prevalent, supports this diagnosis. Abdominal ultrasonography (Choice A) can evaluate for intussusception and appendicitis but this patient's presentation, vital signs, and laboratory workup are not consistent with these diagnoses. Abdominal x-ray (Choice B) is used to evaluate for free air under the diaphragm or small bowel obstruction, but the patient's presentation does not fit these diagnoses. Diagnostic laparoscopy (Choice D) is an invasive and inappropriate procedure for this patient who does not have evidence of an acute abdomen. Examination of stool for ova and parasites (Choice E) is unnecessary. Parasitic infections typically cause chronic, indolent abdominal discomfort with diarrhea and malabsorption rather than acute abdominal pain. Treatment of sickle cell pain crisis focuses on aggressive pain management with opioid and non-opioid medications, reversing triggering causes (infection, acidosis, volume depletion), and fluid administration. Patients with frequent pain episodes should be considered for hydroxyurea therapy. Based on age, evaluation for vaccination with consideration of splenic function and prophylaxis against opportunistic infections is also warranted. Educational Objective: SCD should be considered in children presenting with acute abdominal pain and microcytic anemia without evidence of intra-abdominal infection. Diagnosis is readily made by peripheral blood smear examination, which demonstrates sickle cells.
▶ Behavioral Health Adjustment Disorder 1 question
Source: 3A #38
A popular 17-year-old high school student witnessed an argument between her present boyfriend and a previous boyfriend. She then ran away from home with her best friend and used excessive amounts of alcohol for 3 days. Throughout this period, she maintained her honors schoolwork and her after-school job. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Adjustment disorder B) Alcohol dependence C) Conduct disorder D) Panic disorder E) Separation anxiety disorder
Correct Answer: A) Adjustment disorder
Adjustment disorder presents with symptoms of depression, anxiety, or behavioral changes following an identifiable psychosocial stressor (such as divorce or illness) and typically lasts less than 6 months. While adjustment disorder generally causes significantly impaired functioning in daily life, this patient presents with acute behavioral change after a recent stressor (witnessing an argument between boyfriends) without significant impairment of functioning, as evidenced by maintained honors schoolwork and after-school job performance. Treatment involves cognitive behavioral therapy (focusing on challenging harmful thoughts, beliefs, attitudes, and behaviors while developing coping strategies) or selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors. Alcohol dependence (Choice B) is characterized by chronic alcohol intake with increased tolerance and withdrawal symptoms upon cessation. This patient's alcohol use appears acute and stressor-related, making withdrawal unlikely. Conduct disorder (Choice C) involves persistent emotional and behavioral problems including purposeful aggression toward people and animals, lying, and serious rule violations in children and adolescents. This patient does not display aggression and her behavior is acutely following a stressor. Panic disorder (Choice D) features recurrent unexpected panic attacks with behavioral changes to avoid triggers. Panic attacks involve acute fear or anxiety peaking within minutes with physical symptoms (palpitations, shortness of breath, chest pain, dizziness, choking sensation, gastrointestinal distress, paresthesias, sweating, chills, trembling, derealization, fear of dying, or fear of losing control). This patient shows no evidence of recurrent panic attacks. Separation anxiety disorder (Choice E) typically presents in young children aged 7-9 years with overwhelming anxiety about separation from home or attachment figures, potentially with school avoidance. This patient is 17 years old and does not present with separation anxiety symptoms.
▶ Multisystem Processes Disorders Infectious Mononucleosis 1 question
Source: 3A #39
A previously healthy 12-year-old boy is brought to the physician because of a 1-week history of sore throat and fatigue. His temperature is 38.1°C (100.5°F); other vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows enlarged tonsils and pharyngeal erythema and exudates. The anterior cervical lymph nodes are tender to palpation. A complete blood count shows leukocytosis and atypical lymphocytes. A rapid streptococcal test is negative. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacotherapy? A) Intramuscular ceftriaxone B) Oral acyclovir C) Oral amoxicillin D) Oral aspirin as needed for fever E) Viscous lidocaine gargle for pain
Correct Answer: E) Viscous lidocaine gargle for pain
Infectious mononucleosis is a viral illness caused most often by Epstein-Barr virus (EBV), transmitted through respiratory secretions and saliva, commonly acquired by teenagers and young adults. It presents with fatigue, malaise, fever, chills, and sore throat. Physical examination may disclose hepatosplenomegaly, posterior cervical lymphadenopathy, or pharyngitis with erythema and exudates. Laboratory evaluation demonstrates leukocytosis with atypical lymphocytes and occasionally increased transaminases. Diagnosis is made with the Monospot test, which detects heterophile antibodies through agglutination of sheep or horse erythrocytes. Treatment is supportive, and patients should avoid contact sports until splenomegaly resolves due to risk for splenic rupture. If amoxicillin is administered for misdiagnosed pharyngitis thought to be from S. pyogenes, patients may develop a characteristic generalized maculopapular rash. Therefore, viscous lidocaine gargle for throat pain is the most appropriate pharmacotherapy. Intramuscular ceftriaxone (Choice A) treats gonococcal pharyngitis, which presents with sore throat, erythema, exudates, and cervical lymphadenopathy but does not cause atypical lymphocytosis. Oral acyclovir (Choice B) treats herpes simplex virus infections. Infectious mononucleosis caused by EBV does not characteristically respond to acyclovir. Oral amoxicillin (Choice C) treats streptococcal pharyngitis. When given in infectious mononucleosis, it may cause a generalized maculopapular rash. This patient's negative rapid streptococcal test makes infectious mononucleosis more likely. Oral aspirin as needed for fever (Choice D) should be avoided in children and adolescents because of the risk for Reye syndrome, which presents with nausea, vomiting, altered mental status, seizures, and hepatomegaly. Acetaminophen or ibuprofen should be used instead. Educational Objective: Infectious mononucleosis presents with fatigue, malaise, fever, chills, and sore throat, most commonly caused by Epstein-Barr virus. Physical examination may disclose pharyngitis with erythema and exudates, posterior cervical lymphadenopathy, and hepatosplenomegaly. Treatment is supportive.
▶ Behavioral Health Suicidal Ideation 1 question
Source: 3A #40
A 13-year-old girl is brought to the office by her mother for a routine well-child examination. She began menstruating 1 year ago. She eats healthy food including vegetables. She plays tennis and soccer, sleeps well, and has a supportive network of friends. She is getting straight A's in school. The patient has a bright affect. Vital signs are within normal limits and physical examination shows no abnormalities. The physician provides counseling about accident prevention, drugs, safe sex, and use of helmets when riding a bicycle or scooter. Counseling about which of the following additional topics should be provided at this visit? A) Career planning and college preparedness B) Choking hazards and poisoning prevention C) Depression and suicide prevention D) Vitamin D and folic acid supplementation E) No additional counseling is necessary
Correct Answer: C) Depression and suicide prevention
All well-child examinations of adolescents should include screening for depression and discussion of suicide prevention. Suicide is the third leading cause of death in adolescents and is becoming increasingly prevalent in this age group. Risk factors associated with suicide include increased rates of substance use, major depressive disorder, access to firearms, and social and family challenges. A diagnosis of major depressive disorder is made when a patient experiences five or more of the following symptoms over a 2-week period or greater: depressed mood, anhedonia, change in appetite or weight, insomnia or hypersomnia, psychomotor agitation or retardation, fatigue, feelings of worthlessness, diminished ability to think or concentrate, and recurrent thoughts of death including suicidal ideation with or without a plan. Various screening tools can identify patients at risk for major depressive disorder. Suicide is the most serious and acute risk for patients with major depressive disorder, so physicians should ask detailed questions about patients' suicidal ideation and previous suicide attempts when there is any suggestion of suicidality. Career planning and college preparedness (Choice A) is not necessary at this time but may be addressed with older adolescents preparing to transition out of their childhood homes. Choking hazards and poisoning prevention (Choice B) are necessary during well-child examinations of infants or toddlers but are not likely to cause harm in an adolescent. Vitamin D and folic acid supplementation (Choice D) is not necessary in this patient with a healthy, well-rounded diet and lack of other medical comorbidities. No additional counseling is necessary (Choice E) is incorrect as all adolescent well-child examinations should include screening for depression and suicidality. Educational Objective: Suicide is the third most common cause of death in adolescents and has been rising in recent years. Physicians can play a critical role in protecting adolescent patients by screening for depression and suicidality at every adolescent visit.
▶ Behavioral Health Autism Spectrum Disorder 1 question
Source: 3A #43
A 20-month-old boy is brought to the office by his mother because she is concerned about his speech. She says he babbles various sounds but makes no words. He was born at term to a 26-year-old primigravid woman following an uncomplicated pregnancy and spontaneous vaginal delivery. Apgar scores were 8 and 9 at 1 and 5 minutes, respectively. He has no history of serious illness and receives no medications. The patient's grandmother assists with child care while the mother works part-time at a clothing store. The patient lives in a house constructed in 1938. He is at the 35th percentile for length and weight. Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. He is active and responds to aural and tactile stimuli. He has limited direct eye contact, makes babbling sounds, and does not say any words. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Autism spectrum disorder B) Down syndrome C) Failure to thrive D) Hearing deficit E) Lead poisoning
Correct Answer: A) Autism spectrum disorder
Autism spectrum disorder is a neurodevelopmental disorder characterized by restricted and repetitive patterns of behavior or activities, difficulties with social interaction, and difficulties with communication. Children with autism often display a lack of interest in others, failure in empathy, and challenging behavior. Clinical manifestations typically present within the first 2 years of life with delayed or regression of communication or social skills. Impaired social cognition includes impaired nonverbal communication such as limited eye contact and facial expressions. This patient demonstrates delayed speech (babbling without words), limited direct eye contact, and reduced social engagement—all consistent with autism spectrum disorder. Management is individualized and involves behavioral, developmental, social, and educational interventions to improve social functioning, communication skills, and academic functioning. Down syndrome (Choice B) results from trisomy 21 and presents with characteristic physical features including broad, flat facial features with prominent epicanthal folds and a single palmar crease. This patient has no such features. Failure to thrive (Choice C) is characterized by inadequate or abnormal weight gain and can result from inadequate caloric intake, malnutrition, inborn errors of metabolism, or hyperthyroidism. This patient is at the 35th percentile for length and weight, which is normal. Hearing deficit (Choice D) can lead to delays in speech, language, and cognitive development. However, this patient responds readily to aural stimuli, making hearing deficit unlikely. Additionally, children with hearing impairment typically have normal reciprocal social interactions and normal eye contact, which this patient lacks. Lead poisoning (Choice E) causes encephalopathy, cognitive dysfunction, peripheral neuropathy, hearing loss, renal tubular dysfunction, interstitial nephritis, vomiting, abdominal pain, constipation, and anemia. While the house was constructed in 1938 (potential lead exposure), the clinical presentation is more consistent with autism spectrum disorder, and lead poisoning would not typically present with the specific pattern of social and communication deficits seen here. Educational Objective: The clinical manifestations of autism spectrum disorder typically present within the first 2 years of life with delayed or regressing communication and/or social skills.
▶ Multisystem Processes Disorders Cytomegalovirus Infection 1 question
Source: 3A #44
A 37-year-old man comes to the office because of a 1-week history of visual disturbances, flashing lights, and blurred vision. He tested positive for HIV infection 8 years ago. He has not been taking his antiretroviral medications during the past 4 months, explaining, "I just got tired of the daily routine." His temperature is 37.1°C (98.8°F), pulse is 84/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 128/76 mm Hg. Physical examination discloses no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most likely infectious agent? A) Cryptococcus neoformans B) Cryptosporidium parvum C) Cytomegalovirus D) Rubella virus E) Toxoplasma gondii
Correct Answer: C) Cytomegalovirus
Cytomegalovirus (CMV), also known as human herpesvirus-5 (HHV-5), is an opportunistic infection commonly occurring in immunocompromised patients. It can cause a variety of presentations, including mononucleosis in immunocompetent patients, and retinitis, esophagitis, and pneumonia in immunocompromised patients. Retinitis occurs in HIV infection or AIDS when the CD4+ T lymphocyte count is low (often less than 50 cells/ml) and presents with blurred vision, blind spots, flashing lights, or floaters, most commonly unilateral. Symptoms are caused by CMV-induced retinal inflammation, necrosis, and edema, which can lead to retinal detachment and blindness if untreated. Treatments include intravitreal and systemic antiviral therapy. Cryptococcus neoformans (Choice A) is an encapsulated yeast and common opportunistic pathogen in immunocompromised patients, especially those with HIV infection or AIDS and CD4+ T lymphocyte counts less than 200 cells/ml. Infection can cause pulmonary cryptococcosis, meningitis, and encephalitis, not the visual symptoms described. Cryptosporidium parvum (Choice B) causes self-limited watery diarrhea in immunocompetent patients. In patients with HIV infection or other immunosuppression, it causes profound weight loss and dehydration, not visual disturbances. Rubella virus (Choice D) presents with fever, arthralgias, and lymphadenopathy (most often postauricular) with a confluent rash spreading from head to trunk to extremities. Congenital rubella can cause retinitis, but infection in adulthood does not. Toxoplasma gondii (Choice E) can cause pneumonia, chorioretinitis, hepatitis, myositis, and cerebral toxoplasmosis in immunocompromised patients. Chorioretinitis most commonly presents with decreased visual acuity and eye pain, rather than the flashing lights and visual disturbances seen in this patient. Educational Objective: CMV can cause a variety of infections in immunocompetent and immunocompromised patients. In patients with HIV infection or AIDS and a CD4+ T lymphocyte count less than 50 cells/ml, CMV can cause retinitis and retinal necrosis, which presents with blurred vision, blind spots, flashing lights, and/or floaters.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Hepatitis B 1 question
Source: 3A #48
A 25-year-old man comes to the physician for evaluation 2 days after his sexual partner was diagnosed with acute hepatitis B. Examination shows no abnormalities. Three days later, laboratory studies show: Hepatitis B surface antigen (negative), Antibody to hepatitis B surface antigen (negative), Antibody to hepatitis B core antigen (negative). In addition to condom use counseling, which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation to prevent hepatitis B in this patient? A) Hepatitis B immune globulin only B) Hepatitis B vaccine series only C) Hepatitis B immune globulin and hepatitis B vaccine series D) Corticosteroid therapy only E) Ganciclovir therapy only F) Azathioprine therapy only G) Azathioprine and corticosteroid therapy H) No preventative measures indicated
Correct Answer: C) Hepatitis B immune globulin and hepatitis B vaccine series
Hepatitis B is transmitted through blood, sexual contact, or perinatally and can lead to chronic infection, cirrhosis, and hepatocellular carcinoma. This patient has negative hepatitis B surface antigen (indicating no active infection), negative antibody to hepatitis B surface antigen (indicating no prior vaccination or immunity), and negative antibody to hepatitis B core antigen (indicating no previous infection). Therefore, he is completely susceptible to hepatitis B infection. The most appropriate management requires two components: (1) Hepatitis B immune globulin (HBIG) to provide immediate passive immunity and prevent infection from the current exposure, and (2) Hepatitis B vaccine series to provide active immunity and prevent future infections. HBIG alone (Choice A) would only address the current exposure but not provide long-term protection. Vaccine series alone (Choice B) would not prevent infection from the current exposure because it requires time to become effective. Corticosteroids (Choice D) are not indicated for hepatitis B prevention and have controversial, mixed efficacy in acute hepatitis B treatment. Ganciclovir (Choice E) is not appropriate for hepatitis B; antivirals used for hepatitis B include entecavir, tenofovir, lamivudine, telbivudine, and adefovir. Azathioprine alone (Choice F) or with corticosteroids (Choice G) is not used for hepatitis B prevention or treatment. No preventative measures (Choice H) is incorrect given the documented exposure and lack of immunity. Educational Objective: Patients without immunity to hepatitis B (absent anti-HBs) who have been exposed should receive both hepatitis B immune globulin for immediate passive protection against the current exposure and the hepatitis B vaccine series for long-term active immunity. Lack of immunity is evidenced by absent antibody to hepatitis B surface antigen, active infection by positive hepatitis B surface antigen, and past or chronic infection by positive antibody to hepatitis B core antigen.
▶ Gastrointestinal System GERD 1 question
Source: 3A #49
A 47-year-old woman comes to the physician because of nonproductive, "bothersome" cough almost every day for 2 months, especially at night. Her symptoms have not responded to a 4-week course of antihistamines and decongestants. She has not had fever, shortness of breath, or any other symptoms. She has hypothyroidism well controlled with levothyroxine. There is no family history of serious illness. She has smoked one-half pack of cigarettes daily for 25 years. Her temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 60/min and regular, respirations are 12/min, and blood pressure is 110/76 mm Hg. The oropharynx appears normal with no cobblestoning. Cardiopulmonary examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most likely cause of this patient's cough? A) Allergic rhinitis B) Chronic sinusitis C) Emphysema D) Gastroesophageal reflux disease E) Upper respiratory tract infection
Correct Answer: D) Gastroesophageal reflux disease
The most common causes of chronic cough are gastroesophageal reflux disease (GERD), asthma, and postnasal drip. GERD is most likely in this patient whose symptoms are exacerbated by supine positioning (worse at night), not associated with shortness of breath, and not responsive to antihistamines or decongestants. GERD is caused by decreased lower esophageal sphincter tone or increased intra-abdominal pressure that causes acidic gastric contents to reflux into the esophagus. It typically presents with burning epigastric and lower chest pain, a sour or metallic taste, and nausea, although it can present solely with a nocturnal cough, as in this patient. Treatment includes proton pump inhibitors or H2 receptor antagonists. Complications of untreated GERD include reflux esophagitis, esophageal strictures, Barrett esophagus, and esophageal adenocarcinoma. Allergic rhinitis and chronic sinusitis can both cause chronic cough worse at night from postnasal drainage, but the cough would be expected to improve with antihistamines and decongestants. Postnasal drip is commonly associated with cobblestoning of the oropharynx on physical examination, which is absent here. Emphysema occurs in patients with significant smoking history and presents with exertional dyspnea, cough, wheezing, and sputum production. This patient has only a 12.5 pack-year history and has no dyspnea or sputum production, making emphysema unlikely. Upper respiratory tract infection can cause a lingering cough that outlasts other infection symptoms, but the cough associated with acute upper respiratory tract infection most commonly improves after 3 to 4 weeks. This patient's symptoms have been present for over 2 months, making GERD more likely.
▶ Respiratory System Chronic Obstructive Pulmonary Disease 1 question
Source: 3A #50
A 66-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 1-year history of shortness of breath with exertion and progressive cough productive of small amounts of white sputum. He has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. He has smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 50 years. Vital signs are within normal limits. Pulse oximetry on room air shows a resting oxygen saturation of 91%; his oxygen saturation is 89% with ambulation. Pulmonary examination shows a prolonged expiratory phase. Prior spirometry shows a baseline FEV1 of 55% of predicted and an FEV1:FVC ratio of 60%. The FEV1 improved by 15% after administration of a bronchodilator. Which of the following is most likely to prevent worsening of this patient's underlying condition? A) Combined inhaled corticosteroid and long-acting β-adrenergic agonist therapy B) Long-acting anticholinergic therapy C) Long-term, low-dose oral corticosteroid therapy D) Oxygen therapy E) Smoking cessation
Correct Answer: E) Smoking cessation
Smoking cessation is the most likely intervention to prevent worsening of this patient's obstructive lung disease. This patient has chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD), diagnosed by spirometry when the FEV1:FVC ratio is less than 70%. An FEV1 of 55% indicates moderate obstruction. Long-term smokers are at risk for numerous lung diseases including COPD, which presents with dyspnea on exertion and productive cough. Treatment focuses on preventing further decline in respiratory function through smoking cessation in addition to medications such as inhaled antimuscarinics, short and long-acting β-adrenergic agonists, and corticosteroids. Choice A (Combined inhaled corticosteroid and long-acting β-adrenergic agonist therapy) is used as step-up therapy for patients who continue to have symptoms despite use of antimuscarinics or long-acting β-adrenergic agonists, but does not prevent worsening of the underlying disease. Choice B (Long-acting anticholinergic therapy) is a good initial pharmacologic choice for COPD patients, but the first step in treatment should be smoking cessation. Anticholinergic therapy does not prevent worsening of the underlying disease. Choice C (Long-term, low-dose oral corticosteroid therapy) is rarely indicated to treat COPD. Acute exacerbations are treated with short courses of high-dose prednisone, not long-term low-dose therapy. It does not prevent worsening of the underlying disease. Choice D (Oxygen therapy) is indicated for patients with COPD who have resting or ambulatory oxygen saturation less than 88%. This patient's saturation is 89% with ambulation, so he does not currently meet criteria for oxygen therapy. Educational Objective: Patients with COPD should be encouraged to stop smoking as this intervention is most likely to prevent further worsening of the underlying disease process. Additional pharmacotherapy is utilized to control symptoms and is added in tiers based on symptom control.
▶ Male Reproductive Breast Cancer 1 question
Source: 5A #1
A 52-year-old woman comes to the office for a health maintenance examination. She has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. Menopause occurred 1 year ago. She does not exercise. Her diet consists mainly of meat and potatoes with very few vegetables. She drinks one beer daily and four to five large cups of coffee daily. She had smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 20 years, but quit 10 years ago. She works as a mechanic. She has no family history of cancer, but wishes to know what she can do to reduce her risk for breast cancer. The patient appears well. She is 168 cm (5 ft 6 in) tall and weighs 95 kg (210 lb); BMI is 34 kg/m². Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is most likely to reduce this patient's risk for breast cancer? A) Abstinence from caffeine B) Alcohol cessation C) Exercising and reducing her caloric intake D) Increasing her intake of soy E) Raloxifene therapy
Correct Answer: C) Exercising and reducing her caloric intake
Exercising and reducing her caloric intake is the action most likely to reduce this patient's risk for breast cancer. Breast cancer is the most common nondermatologic malignancy in women and commonly presents with a breast mass, skin or nipple retraction, or axillary lymphadenopathy. Risk factors for breast cancer development include a family history of premenopausal breast cancer, genetic mutations (BRCA1 and BRCA2), early menarche, late menopause, and nulliparity. Several modifiable risk factors exist, including obesity, exogenous estrogen administration, alcohol and tobacco use, and lack of physical activity. Postmenopausal obesity specifically is associated with increased morbidity and mortality related to breast cancer. Abstinence from caffeine (Choice A) has not been associated with a decreased incidence of breast cancer and is not a modifiable risk factor for this patient. Alcohol cessation (Choice B) may decrease breast cancer risk, as moderate alcohol use has been associated with increased risk in a dose-dependent fashion. However, this patient's risk is more significantly increased due to obesity and lack of physical activity, making exercise and weight loss the more appropriate intervention. Increasing her intake of soy (Choice D) has not been associated with a protective effect against breast cancer development. Raloxifene therapy (Choice E) is an estrogen modulator most commonly used in the treatment of osteoporosis and prevention of postmenopausal breast cancer in patients at increased risk. While effective in decreasing breast cancer risk, it is associated with increased risk for thromboembolism. As this patient is at average risk for breast cancer with no history of premalignant lesions, exercise and weight loss would be more appropriate than pharmacotherapy at this time. Educational Objective: Breast cancer is the most common nondermatologic malignancy in women. Risk factors include family history of premenopausal breast cancer, genetic mutations (BRCA1 and BRCA2), early menarche, late menopause, and nulliparity. Modifiable risk factors include obesity, exogenous estrogen administration, alcohol and tobacco use, and lack of physical activity. Postmenopausal obesity specifically is associated with increased morbidity and mortality related to breast cancer.
▶ Biostatistics Epidemiology Population Health Primary Prevention 1 question
Source: 5A #2
A state-supported academic health and insurance system is contracted across a large state in the western United States to cover aspects of care for patients of all ages and income levels, including the Medicaid and Medicare populations. The system's goal is to provide the highest quality of care in the most efficient manner to all patients equally and to support the productivity of the population in that state. The organization already has many skilled providers within every specialty, but they also wish to prioritize key areas for proactive improvement. Which of the following strategies is most likely to be effective in achieving this goal? A) Eliminate prior authorization requirements and expand drug formularies to include more prescription options B) Establish a mobile clinic to bring specialty services directly to underserved areas of the state C) Expand insurance access for specialty, advanced imaging, and surgical services D) Expand telemedicine services E) Increase relative investment in primary care services
Correct Answer: E) Increase relative investment in primary care services
Quality improvement systematically improves and standardizes health care system performance. Primary care has been shown to improve population health and decrease health disparities. The vast majority of medical care is delivered through primary care, which commonly uses primary prevention strategies. Primary prevention aims to prevent disease before it occurs through interventions such as diet and exercise to prevent obesity and diabetes mellitus. Primary prevention strategies are effective across diverse subpopulations and represent an efficient means of increasing productivity by preventing disease across an entire population. Choice A (eliminating prior authorization and expanding formularies) would affect only patients requiring specialized medications, not the entire population. Choice B (mobile clinics) and Choice D (telemedicine) would improve health outcomes only for specific subpopulations such as rural areas or those without easy transportation access, not the entire population. Choice C (expanding insurance access for specialty services) may help a subpopulation with suboptimal insurance but would not improve population health overall. Additionally, specialty services provide tertiary prevention (preventing complications of existing disease) rather than primary prevention. Primary prevention applies to more of the population than tertiary prevention, as tertiary prevention only applies to subpopulations that already have specific diseases. Educational Objective: Primary care improves population health and decreases health disparities. Most medical care is delivered through primary care. Primary care affects population health through primary prevention strategies that encourage lifestyle changes such as diet and exercise—effective strategies across diverse subpopulations.
▶ Skin Subcutaneous Tissue Lipoma 1 question
Source: 5A #4
A 44-year-old man comes to the office because of a 5-year history of a lump on his upper back. Four months ago, the lump became tender for 1 week when he had been exercising at the gym more often, but the tenderness resolved spontaneously. He says the lump has not changed in size and he has not noticed any skin changes. The patient is worried that the lump could be cancerous. He has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. He is 183 cm (6 ft) tall and weighs 82 kg (180 lb); BMI is 24 kg/m². Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows a 4-cm nontender, moderately firm, mobile mass between the right scapula and the spine. No other masses are palpated. Ultrasonography of the lump shows a well-defined mass with a homogeneous echotexture. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Excisional biopsy B) Fine-needle biopsy C) MRI of the thoracic spine D) Repeat ultrasonography in 3 months E) Continued observation
Correct Answer: E) Continued observation
Lipomas represent benign tumors of mature adipocytes that can develop at any age. Clinically, lipomas present as solitary, painless, soft, mobile, subcutaneous tumors ranging from a few centimeters to over 10 cm in diameter. Pathologic examination shows mature adipocytes with small, uniform, eccentric nuclei. In this case, the clinical examination and ultrasonography findings are consistent with an uncomplicated lipoma: a well-defined, homogeneous mass that is mobile and nontender. For asymptomatic lipomas, clinical monitoring is the appropriate management. If patients seek definitive management secondary to symptoms or appearance, lipomas may be excised. Though generally benign and harmless, lipomas have a rare chance of transforming into malignant neoplasms (liposarcoma). Excisional biopsy (Choice A) is used to diagnose suspicious cutaneous neoplasms including melanoma and allows removal of an entire lesion with histologic diagnosis. However, the clinical and ultrasound findings are consistent with benign lipoma, which may be clinically monitored without biopsy. Fine-needle biopsy (Choice B) may be used for subcutaneous tumors including breast masses or thyroid nodules with ultrasound guidance. However, it is not recommended in the management of benign lipomas. MRI of the thoracic spine (Choice C) may be useful for evaluating bones, soft tissues, and neurovascular structures when there is concern for spinal stenosis, cord compression, or ill-defined subcutaneous tumors. In this case, ultrasonography and clinical examination are consistent with uncomplicated lipoma, so additional imaging is not required. Repeat ultrasonography in 3 months (Choice D) may be used to monitor indeterminate lesions where there is concern for malignancy such as liposarcoma. However, superficial lipomas are slow-growing tumors that may be monitored clinically without serial imaging. Educational Objective: Lipomas represent benign overgrowth of mature adipocytes that clinically present as asymptomatic, subcutaneous tumors. Benign lipomas may be diagnosed clinically and are slow-growing, allowing for clinical monitoring. In patients seeking definitive management, surgical excision is recommended.
▶ Respiratory System Peritonsillar Abscess 1 question
Source: 5A #5
A previously healthy 19-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 1-week history of sore throat, difficulty swallowing, and fever. He has a muffled voice. His temperature is 39.4°C (103°F). Which of the following would warrant the need for surgical intervention? A) Anterior cervical lymphadenopathy B) Lateral deviation of the uvula C) Petechiae over the soft palate D) Pharyngeal exudates E) Tenderness of the trachea
Correct Answer: B) Lateral deviation of the uvula
This patient presents with a peritonsillar abscess, a localized accumulation of pus between the palatine tonsil and pharyngeal muscles. This is a potentially life-threatening condition due to risk of airway compression. The classic presentation includes fever, sore throat, trismus, localized swelling and fluctuance overlying the tonsil, lateral deviation of the uvula, and signs of airway compromise such as muffled voice or drooling. The lateral deviation of the uvula indicates pharyngeal asymmetry from the abscess and is the key finding that warrants surgical intervention. Treatment involves needle aspiration or incision and drainage of the peritonsillar space combined with broad-spectrum antibiotics. Anterior cervical lymphadenopathy, petechiae over the soft palate, and pharyngeal exudates can all occur with simple bacterial or viral pharyngitis and do not indicate the presence of a structural compressive lesion requiring surgery. Tenderness of the trachea suggests tracheitis, which presents with stridor and requires airway management and antibiotics rather than surgical intervention.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Peptic ulcer 1 question
Source: 5A #6
A 37-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 6-week history of epigastric pain. The pain is most severe 1 hour after eating and is relieved by over-the-counter antacids. He has had no fever, nausea, or weight loss. He has hypertension treated with hydrochlorothiazide. He does not smoke and drinks one glass of wine with dinner daily. His pulse is 76/min, and blood pressure is 136/88 mm Hg. There is mild epigastric tenderness without rebound or guarding. The stool is brown, and test for occult blood is negative. His hemoglobin concentration is 13.8 g/dL. Liver function tests show no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in diagnosis? A) Esophageal pH monitoring B) Measurement of serum amylase activity C) Serum Helicobacter pylori antibody assay D) Abdominal ultrasonography E) Abdominal CT scan
Correct Answer: C) Serum Helicobacter pylori antibody assay
Peptic ulcer disease presents with abdominal pain related to food consumption (gastric ulcers worsen with eating; duodenal ulcers improve with eating). This patient's 6-week history of epigastric pain 1 hour after eating, relieved by antacids, is classic for peptic ulcer disease. The most common cause of peptic ulcer disease is infection with Helicobacter pylori, which accounts for nearly all duodenal ulcers. Serologic testing for H. pylori antibodies should be performed as the next diagnostic step. Eradication therapy using triple or quadruple combined therapy can eliminate the bacterium and permit healing with few complications if managed promptly. Choice A (Esophageal pH monitoring) is used for GERD diagnosis when clinical findings are inconclusive. GERD presents with chest discomfort (not epigastric), often worse with supine positioning and trigger foods. This patient's epigastric pain 1 hour after eating is more consistent with peptic ulcer disease. Choice B (Serum amylase activity) is useful for acute pancreatitis, which presents with severe epigastric pain radiating to the back, nausea, and emesis in patients with risk factors (gallstones, alcohol use disorder, hypertriglyceridemia, hypercalcemia). This patient has chronic pain without nausea, emesis, or risk factors. Choice D (Abdominal ultrasonography) is first-line for cholelithiasis or cholecystitis. While cholelithiasis can present with postprandial epigastric pain, acute cholecystitis typically presents with fever, right upper quadrant pain, and tenderness. Importantly, cholelithiasis would not be relieved by antacids. This patient has no history of gallstones or signs of acute cholecystitis. Choice E (Abdominal CT scan) is useful for complicated peptic ulcer disease (perforation, hemorrhage, stricture). Perforation presents with acute abdominal pain, peritonitis, and fever. This patient lacks these acute findings, making perforation unlikely. Educational Objective: Peptic ulcer disease classically presents with abdominal pain related to food consumption and is strongly associated with Helicobacter pylori infection.
▶ Endocrine System Hyperthyroidism 1 question
Source: 5A #7
A previously healthy 47-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 4-week history of palpitations and feeling hot, sweaty, and anxious. During the past 2 months, she has had a 3.2-kg (7-lb) weight loss despite a normal appetite. She has not had chest pain, shortness of breath, light-headedness, dizziness, or nausea. Her last menstrual period was 3 weeks ago. Menses previously occurred at regular 30-day intervals, but she notes that during the past year she "might have missed a few periods." She does not smoke cigarettes. She drinks one glass of wine monthly. She is 165 cm (5 ft 5 in) tall and weighs 61 kg (135 lb); BMI is 23 kg/m². Her pulse is 112/min and regular, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 138/88 mm Hg. Examination shows no other abnormalities. Her serum thyroid-stimulating hormone concentration is 0.1 pU/mL. Results of additional laboratory studies are pending. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Admission to the hospital B) Endometrial biopsy C) Fine-needle biopsy of the thyroid gland D) I-131 therapy E) Thyroid ultrasonography
Correct Answer: E) Thyroid ultrasonography
This patient presents with classic signs and symptoms of hyperthyroidism: tachycardia (pulse 112/min), elevated blood pressure, palpitations, anxiety, weight loss despite normal appetite, and hot flashes. The low TSH concentration (0.1 pU/mL) indicates primary hyperthyroidism, suggesting etiologies such as Graves disease or a hyperfunctioning thyroid nodule. The irregular menstrual periods can be attributed to the hyperthyroid state. Physical examination reveals no discrete thyroid mass. All patients with signs and symptoms of hyperthyroidism and low TSH should undergo thyroid ultrasonography along with testing for thyroid-stimulating antibodies. Ultrasonography-guided fine-needle aspiration biopsy can be performed simultaneously if risk factors for malignancy or concerning ultrasound features are identified. Subsequent management depends on TSH results, antibody detection, radioiodine uptake testing, nodule characteristics on ultrasound, and biopsy results. Admission to the hospital (Choice A) is not warranted. The patient has stable vital signs and no features mandating hospitalization. Outpatient testing is appropriate given her clinical stability. Hospitalization would be considered only if she develops signs of thyroid storm. Endometrial biopsy (Choice B) is inappropriate. The patient's irregular menstrual cycles result from her current hyperthyroidism, confirmed by the low TSH. Endometrial biopsy would be unnecessarily invasive and would not address the underlying pathology. Fine-needle biopsy of the thyroid gland (Choice C) is premature. No thyroid nodule has been identified on physical examination. Biopsy may become appropriate after thyroid ultrasound identifies a nodule with concerning features, but it is not the appropriate initial next step. I-131 therapy (Choice D) is premature. While radioactive iodine can destroy hyperfunctioning thyroid tissue in Graves disease or toxic nodules, the etiology of this patient's hyperthyroidism has not yet been established. Further investigation is necessary before definitive treatment. Educational Objective: Symptoms of thyrotoxicosis include diaphoresis, weight loss, anxiety, palpitations, and tremor. Evaluation should include measurement of serum TSH concentration along with ultrasound and/or antibody testing as appropriate based on clinical suspicion. In patients with low TSH concentration and no identifiable thyroid nodule on examination, thyroid ultrasonography should be performed as the next diagnostic step.
▶ Behavioral Health Generalized Anxiety Disorder 1 question
Source: 5A #8
A 25-year-old woman comes to the office because of a 6-month history of difficulty sleeping and "feeling worried and edgy most days." She has been a patient for the past 10 years with no history of serious illness. Her only medication is an oral contraceptive. She works as an emergency room technician but is applying to nursing school next year. Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. On mental status examination, she has an anxious mood and jittery affect. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Alprazolam therapy B) Diphenhydramine therapy C) Office-based counseling D) Pregabalin therapy E) Valerian root supplementation
Correct Answer: C) Office-based counseling
Generalized anxiety disorder (GAD) requires excessive worry about numerous aspects of daily life occurring on more days than not for at least 6 months, with inability to control symptoms and at least three additional symptoms including irritability, muscle tension, sleep problems, poor concentration, fatigue, and restlessness. This patient meets diagnostic criteria with a 6-month history of worry and sleep difficulty. Treatment depends on symptom severity. For mild GAD that does not significantly impair daily function, as in this patient who continues working as an emergency room technician, office-based counseling is the appropriate initial step. The patient's anxiety should be monitored over the next few months. If office-based counseling proves ineffective or if the patient develops more severe dysfunction, selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors (SSRIs) and/or cognitive-behavioral therapy should be initiated. Alprazolam (Choice A) is a benzodiazepine reserved for refractory anxiety disorders in patients without substance abuse history and is not appropriate for initial GAD treatment, particularly in mild cases. Diphenhydramine (Choice B) is a first-generation H1-receptor antagonist used for allergies, insomnia, and motion sickness. While sometimes used off-label for anxiety, it is not first-line treatment for GAD and should not precede counseling in mild cases. Pregabalin (Choice D) is an anticonvulsant that modulates calcium influx and inhibits excitatory neurotransmitter release. Although used for seizures, generalized and social anxiety disorders, and neuropathic pain, it is not first-line for GAD. SSRIs should be considered before pregabalin, and counseling should precede all medications in mild GAD. Valerian root (Choice E) is an herbal remedy with sedative and anxiolytic properties but has not demonstrated superiority over placebo in clinical trials and is not evidence-based for GAD treatment. Counseling should precede herbal supplementation in mild cases.
▶ Human Development Constitutional delay of growth 1 question
Source: 5A #9
A 14-year-old boy is brought to the physician because his parents are concerned that he is shorter than his classmates. He is at the 10th percentile for height and 25th percentile for weight. Both parents are at the 75th percentile for height. His diet consists of cereal, milk, bread, and fried food. The patient's temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 75/min, respirations are 12/min, and blood pressure is 110/65 mm Hg. Examination shows sparse facial hair and sparse, straight, slightly pigmented hair at the base of a slightly enlarged penis. The skin is warm and moist. The testes are approximately 3 cm long and are descended into the scrotum bilaterally. X-rays show a bone age of 12 years (+4 months). Which of the following is the most likely cause of these findings? A) Constitutional growth delay B) Familial short stature C) Hypothyroidism D) Nutritional deficiency E) Normal development
Correct Answer: A) Constitutional growth delay
Constitutional growth delay is a normal variant of growth that is independent of parental stature and presents with consistently decreased growth velocity and bone age younger than chronological age. This patient has parents at the 75th percentile for height (normal stature), making familial short stature unlikely. His bone age of 12 years is significantly less than his chronological age of 14 years, which is the hallmark finding of constitutional growth delay. Patients with this condition may experience delayed onset of puberty (evidenced by sparse facial hair and small testes at age 14) but typically reach normal adult height with more rapid growth velocity during puberty. Familial short stature (Choice B) presents in patients whose parents are both short, with normal growth velocity and bone age consistent with chronological age. This patient's parents have normal stature, making this unlikely. Hypothyroidism (Choice C) is associated with short stature but typically presents with additional symptoms such as fatigue, cold intolerance, weight gain, constipation, and dry skin. This patient lacks these features and has no history of chronic illness. Nutritional deficiency (Choice D) results in abnormally low weight with signs of malnutrition such as dry skin and hair loss. This patient has normal weight (25th percentile) and lacks physical examination signs of malnutrition. Normal development (Choice E) is associated with bone age consistent with chronological age. This patient is several standard deviations below expected height for his age, classifying him as having growth delay rather than normal variation. Educational Objective: Constitutional growth delay is a normal growth variant occurring in patients with parents of normal stature, presenting with short stature, delayed pubertal development, slow but consistent growth velocity, and bone age less than chronological age. These patients typically achieve normal development and stature once they reach puberty.
▶ Human Development Screening in infancy and childhood 1 question
Source: 5A #11
A 10-year-old boy is brought to the office for a well-child examination. He and his family emigrated from Bolivia to the USA 7 years ago. He has no history of serious illness and receives no medications. Immunizations are up-to-date. He lives in a house built in 1965. Development is appropriate for age. He is at the 68th percentile for height, 96th percentile for weight, and greater than 95th percentile for BMI. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows no other abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate screening at this time? A) Measurement of blood lead concentration B) Measurement of fasting serum glucose concentration C) Measurement of serum hepatitis B surface antibodies D) PPD skin test E) Urinalysis
