Correct Answer: B) Measurement of fasting serum glucose concentration
In pediatric patients, obesity is defined as a BMI at or above the 95th percentile for age and sex. This patient has a BMI greater than the 95th percentile, meeting criteria for obesity. Children with obesity are at significantly increased risk for developing comorbidities including hypertension and diabetes mellitus. Therefore, screening for these disorders is essential. Fasting serum glucose concentration is the appropriate screening test to assess for development of diabetes mellitus in this obese child. Choice A (blood lead concentration) is not appropriate. While lead exposure is a concern in older homes, this patient has no clinical signs or symptoms of lead poisoning (encephalopathy, cognitive dysfunction, peripheral neuropathy, hearing loss, renal dysfunction, gastrointestinal symptoms, or anemia). Choice C (hepatitis B surface antibodies) is not appropriate. The patient is up-to-date on immunizations and lacks significant risk factors for hepatitis B exposure (blood contact, sexual contact, or perinatal transmission). Choice D (PPD skin test) is not appropriate at this time. While tuberculosis screening may be considered given the patient's emigration from Bolivia, there are no signs or symptoms of pulmonary disease, and this is not more pertinent than screening for diabetes given the markedly increased BMI. Choice E (urinalysis) is not appropriate. While urinalysis can detect glucose in urine, it does not definitively screen for diabetes. Fasting serum glucose concentration is the more appropriate screening test. Educational Objective: In pediatric patients, obesity (BMI ≥95th percentile for age and sex) is associated with increased risk of hypertension and diabetes mellitus. Patients meeting obesity criteria should be screened for these comorbidities with fasting serum glucose concentration.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Synovitis 1 question
Source: 5A #13
A 4-year-old boy is brought to the office because of a 1-day history of moderate left hip pain. He walks with a limp. Ten days ago, he had a mild cough and runny nose that resolved spontaneously. He has not sustained any trauma. He has no other history of serious illness and receives no medications. He appears well. Vital signs are within normal limits. On examination, the left hip is in normal position. Palpation of the left hip joint produces no tenderness. Active and passive range of motion is limited by pain. Examination of all other joints shows no abnormalities. Results of laboratory studies are pending. Ultrasonography of the left hip joint shows a small amount of fluid. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Ceftriaxone therapy B) Ibuprofen therapy C) MRI of the left hip D) Vancomycin therapy E) X-ray of the left hip
Correct Answer: B) Ibuprofen therapy
The most likely diagnosis is transient synovitis of the hip, an inflammatory condition involving the synovial lining of weight-bearing joints. It classically presents in children recovering from a viral infection with pain on weight-bearing, antalgic gait, pain with range of motion, and normal vital signs. Antecedent viral infection occurs in up to 30% of cases. Diagnosis involves excluding emergent conditions such as septic arthritis, osteomyelitis, cellulitis, and fracture. Treatment is conservative and supportive, involving rest plus nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory medications like ibuprofen. In this case, the patient has recent viral illness, no history of trauma, no systemic symptoms, and ultrasonography showing only a small amount of fluid, making transient synovitis most likely. Ceftriaxone (Choice A) and vancomycin (Choice D) are appropriate for septic arthritis, which presents with painful, erythematous, swollen joints and purulent synovial fluid with leukocyte count >50,000/mm³—not present here. MRI (Choice C) is the most sensitive imaging for osteomyelitis but is not indicated without systemic symptoms suggesting bone infection. X-ray (Choice E) evaluates for fractures, which are unlikely without trauma history. The most appropriate next step is conservative management with ibuprofen therapy.
▶ Male Reproductive Varicocele 1 question
Source: 5A #14
A 14-year-old boy presents with a painless mass in the scrotum that he did not notice previously. Physical examination reveals a mass that decreases while supine and has a bag-of-worms sensation. The boy denies participation in school sports. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Epididymitis B) Hydrocele C) Inguinal hernia D) Leydig cell tumor E) Teratoma of the testis F) Torsion of the testis
Correct Answer: G) Varicocele
Varicoceles are caused by incompetence of the spermatic vein and are more common in the left scrotum. They may be asymptomatic or present with a dull, achy scrotal pain and heaviness. Physical examination characteristically shows a palpable, bag-of-worms-like mass that increases in size with standing and improves with lying down (as seen in this patient). The diagnosis is often clinical but can be aided by ultrasonography. Varicoceles are associated with infertility and testicular atrophy and may be managed symptomatically or treated with surgical correction. Epididymitis (Choice A) presents acutely with unilateral scrotal pain and a tender, swollen mass over the posterior aspect of the testis, typically caused by Chlamydia trachomatis or Neisseria gonorrhoeae in men under 35 years. This patient is asymptomatic with no risk factors for sexually transmitted infection. Hydrocele (Choice B) results from failure of the processus vaginalis to close, allowing peritoneal fluid to descend into the scrotum. It causes uniform scrotal enlargement with transillumination and anechoic fluid on ultrasound, not an isolated mass. Inguinal hernia (Choice C) occurs when solid abdominal contents herniate through a patent processus vaginalis. It presents with a mass that enlarges with increased abdominal pressure (cough, Valsalva), not one that improves in the supine position. Leydig cell tumor (Choice D) is a sex cord-stromal tumor presenting with precocious puberty in children or gynecomastia in young adults, with a firm testicular mass. This patient is asymptomatic. Teratoma of the testis (Choice E) is a germ cell tumor containing cell types from all three germ layers. It presents as a firm testicular mass, often with a surrounding reactive hydrocele, not consistent with this patient's findings. Torsion of the testis (Choice F) presents acutely with severe testicular pain, tenderness, scrotal swelling, and a negative cremasteric reflex. It does not present with a scrotal mass and requires urgent surgical intervention.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Otitis Externa 1 question
Source: 5A #16
A 15-year-old girl is brought to the physician because of left ear pain for 2 days. She competes in long-distance open-water swimming events and trains in the ocean several hours daily. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination of the left ear shows erythema and edema of the auditory canal and a small amount of white discharge. Manipulation of the left pinna produces pain; the left tympanic membrane appears normal. After treating this patient for her acute condition, recommendation of which of the following is the most appropriate next step to prevent recurrence of this condition? A) Training in chlorine-treated pools only B) Daily use of topical benzocaine in the ear canal C) Daily use of alcohol-acetic acid ear drops D) Daily use of carbamide peroxide ear drops E) Daily use of hydrocortisone ear drops
Correct Answer: C) Daily use of alcohol-acetic acid ear drops
Otitis externa, also known as 'swimmers' ear,' is inflammation of the external auditory canal associated with moisture that causes breakdown of cerumen and skin maceration. Disruption of the cerumen-skin barrier causes an imbalance of local pH and promotes overgrowth of microflora, triggering an inflammatory response. The most common pathogen is Pseudomonas aeruginosa. Clinically, patients experience tenderness upon tragal pressure or pain with manipulation of the auricle. Application of alcohol-acetic acid ear drops gently removes cerumen, desquamated skin, and purulent material, preventing inflammation and limiting bacterial superinfection likelihood. For moderate-to-severe cases, topical antibiotics or topical steroids are recommended; for malignant otitis externa, intravenous antibiotics are required. Choice A (chlorine-treated pools only) does not prevent recurrence since otitis externa is associated with moisture disrupting the cerumen-skin barrier, not the type of water. Choice B (topical benzocaine) is an ester anesthetic that relieves pain but does not prevent inflammation. Choice D (carbamide peroxide) may soften cerumen but can cause worsening pain and irritation in otitis externa patients and is not recommended for prevention. Choice E (hydrocortisone) is a topical steroid used for acute treatment to reduce inflammation, but long-term use causes cutaneous atrophy and does not prevent recurrence. Educational Objective: Otitis externa develops from cerumen breakdown and skin maceration leading to debris buildup, pH imbalance, and microflora overgrowth. Alcohol-acetic acid drops prevent recurrence by gently removing cerumen, desquamated skin, and purulent material that promote otitis externa development.
▶ Social Sciences Advance directives 1 question
Source: 5A #18
An 86-year-old man is admitted to the hospital because of a 4-day history of cough, diagnosed in the emergency department as pneumonia. The patient's medical record includes a signed and notarized living will dated 2 years ago that indicates his desire for no cardiopulmonary resuscitation or ventilation in the event of incapacitation, and administration of comfort measures only. Upon admission, the patient requests full resuscitation if his status should decline. The patient is oriented to person, place, and time. Initially, only oxygen via nasal cannula and antibiotics are administered, but overnight the patient's status worsens. In the morning, pulse oximetry on 40% oxygen administered via face mask shows an oxygen saturation of 85%. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Adhere to the living will and provide morphine for comfort B) Ask the patient why his wishes changed C) Contact the hospital ethics committee to provide guidance D) Contact the patient's family to clarify his preferences E) Provide ventilator support
Correct Answer: E) Provide ventilator support
Regardless of advance directives or prior code-status documentation, physicians must assess all patients' code status at each hospitalization, as the context differs. The patient or decision maker should be given the opportunity to update code status. This patient expresses a preference to be full code, signifying that life-prolonging care be administered while the patient is alive (respiratory support, including intubation) and after cardiac arrest (cardiopulmonary resuscitation). This preference supersedes his previously expressed preference for comfort care only. Advance directives are legal documents enabling patients to dictate end-of-life care preferences ahead of time, especially important when patients become incapacitated. However, capacitated patients can express wishes that supersede advance directive preferences, including code status. Since this patient is oriented and able to express a clear preference, the medical team should ventilate him, acting in accordance with his current wishes rather than his advance directive. Choice A violates the patient's autonomy. He is capacitated and expresses a preference to change code status to full code, which supersedes the advance directive; life-prolonging care should be administered. Choice B is not the most urgent next step. While asking why wishes changed would be appropriate during initial code status assessment to determine decision-making capacity, doing so now would delay needed respiratory support. Choice C may be indicated if current expressed wishes conflict with previously stated wishes and decisional capacity is impaired. However, this patient is capacitated and clear in his preference. Additionally, contacting the ethics committee would delay appropriate emergency care needed to sustain life. Choice D would be appropriate if the patient were incapacitated and lacked an advance directive. However, the patient is capacitated and clear in his preference to change code status, making family consultation unnecessary. Educational Objective: Capacitated patients can express wishes that supersede advance directive preferences, including code status. When a patient is oriented and able to express a clear preference, the medical team should act in accordance with the patient's current wishes rather than the advance directive.
▶ Immune System HIV Type 1 1 question
Source: 5A #19
A 24-year-old man comes to the office because of a 1-week history of fever, sore throat, and diarrhea. The patient was evaluated in an urgent care clinic 3 days ago; results of a rapid streptococcal test and heterophile antibody test for infectious mononucleosis were negative. He has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. The patient reports that he has had oral-receptive and anal-receptive sex with men and that his most recent sexual encounter was 2 weeks ago. He does not use condoms. Today, temperature is 38.5°C (101.3°F), pulse is 96/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 124/78 mm Hg. Physical examination shows three oral ulcerations and anterior cervical lymph nodes that are slightly tender to palpation. The remainder of the physical examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following studies is most likely to establish the diagnosis? A) Epstein-Barr virus DNA detection B) Epstein-Barr virus specific antibodies C) HIV antibody testing D) HIV viral load E) Throat culture for Neisseria gonorrhoeae F) Throat culture for streptococcal pharyngitis
Correct Answer: D) HIV viral load
This patient with lymphadenopathy, fever, diarrhea, and oral ulcerations most likely has acute HIV infection, known as acute retroviral syndrome. The presentation of primary HIV infection varies significantly between patients, with some experiencing a mild febrile illness while others experience a severe, mononucleosis-like viral syndrome. The incubation period is variable, lasting several weeks to months. During acute infection, there is high viral replication followed by widespread infection of CD4+ T-lymphocytes. Initial laboratory evaluation includes a fourth-generation HIV test (which tests for HIV p24 antigen and antibodies) combined with direct measurement of HIV viral load using reverse transcription polymerase chain reaction. It is common in early HIV infection for patients to have a negative fourth-generation test but a high viral load—this is known as the window period. The window period can be as many as 18-24 days for antibody testing. Viral testing generally has the shortest window period and is therefore the most reliable test to detect infection. Once diagnosis is made, antiretroviral therapy should be initiated immediately with a combination of at least three drugs from at least two different classes. Epstein-Barr virus DNA detection and specific antibodies are not appropriate currently. While the patient could have had a false-negative heterophile antibody test, his clinical picture is more consistent with acute retroviral syndrome. Acute mononucleosis would present with cervical lymphadenopathy but oral ulcerations would be uncommon, and splenomegaly would be expected on examination. HIV antibody testing is used in potential acute retroviral syndrome but may be falsely negative during the window period. While it can indicate previous exposure, it does not provide information about current severity of infection and is less accurate than assessing viral load. Throat culture for Neisseria gonorrhoeae is not the most appropriate next step. Gonococcal pharyngitis can present with fever and sore throat, but diarrhea and oral ulcerations are uncommon, making it unlikely given the patient's sexual history and examination findings. Throat culture for streptococcal pharyngitis is not warranted. The patient was previously tested and was negative. Streptococcal pharyngitis would present with erythematous, raised mucosa and tonsillar inflammation rather than oral ulcerations. Educational Objective: The presentation of primary HIV infection varies significantly between patients. HIV viral load should be obtained to assess for acute retroviral syndrome, particularly during the window period when antibody testing may be falsely negative.
▶ Behavioral Health Cannabis Use Disorder 1 question
Source: 5A #20
During a routine health maintenance examination, a 15-year-old girl says that she has used marijuana with friends on four occasions. She is healthy and doing well in school. She requests that her marijuana use be kept confidential. In addition to counseling her about the adverse effects of marijuana use, which of the following is the most appropriate intervention at this time? A) Referral to a psychologist for treatment of substance use disorder B) Scheduling a family conference C) Establishing regular follow-up visits to monitor for adverse consequences D) Screening for human papillomavirus infection E) Urine toxicology screening
Correct Answer: C) Establishing regular follow-up visits to monitor for adverse consequences
Parental consent is not required for medical care of minors related to substance use, sexual activities (sexually transmitted infection, contraception, pregnancy care), and life-threatening medical or psychiatric conditions. For these sensitive issues, patient confidentiality and autonomy are favored over other ethical principles. Respecting the patient's wishes about disclosure of medical information builds therapeutic alliance and increases the likelihood that patients will continue to share sensitive information in the future. The physician should respect this patient's wishes to keep her marijuana use confidential and establish regular follow-up visits to monitor for adverse consequences of marijuana use (cognitive impairment, psychosis) and development of a use disorder. Choice A (Referral to a psychologist) is premature because this patient does not show signs of a use disorder, which commonly include using a substance more than planned, failed attempts to cut back use, and negative psychosocial consequences. The physician should monitor this patient longitudinally for development of marijuana use disorder. Choice B (Family conference) would oppose the patient's wishes and violate the ethical principle of autonomy. Physicians do not need to inform parents about medical care related to substance use. Respecting the patient's wish to not disclose her marijuana use to her parents builds therapeutic alliance. Choice D (HPV screening) is not clinically indicated. HPV screening typically begins at age 25 years, and marijuana use does not necessarily indicate high-risk sexual activity. Choice E (Urine toxicology screening) may provide useful information about the full scope of substance use in the future. However, this patient appears to be sharing honestly about her current substance use. Performing urine toxicology screening as a first step is likely to rupture the therapeutic relationship and decrease the chance the patient candidly shares sensitive information and collaborates in treatment planning in the future.
▶ Respiratory System Obstructive sleep apnea 1 question
Source: 5A #22
A 55-year-old man comes to the physician with his wife because of a 6-month history of daytime drowsiness. His wife reports that he has had increased snoring, which is especially loud when he sleeps on his back, but he does not stop breathing for prolonged periods. The patient has a 15-year history of hypertension treated with hydrochlorothiazide. He is 175 cm (5 ft 9 in) tall and weighs 82 kg (180 lb); BMI is 27 kg/m². Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows an increased intensity of the pulmonic component of S2. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) ECG B) Echocardiography C) Methylphenidate therapy D) Modafinil therapy E) Polysomnography F) Sleep positioning therapy
Correct Answer: E) Polysomnography
This patient presents with classic features of obstructive sleep apnea (OSA): daytime drowsiness, loud snoring (especially positional), and an increased pulmonic S2 indicating pulmonary hypertension from chronic OSA. Risk factors include male sex, obesity (BMI 27), and hypertension. The loud pulmonic component of S2 reflects pulmonary hypertension secondary to untreated OSA, which can progress to right-sided heart failure if left untreated. Polysomnography (sleep study) is the gold standard diagnostic test for OSA and is the most appropriate next step. It objectively characterizes the severity of apneic episodes and guides treatment decisions. ECG (Choice A) is not appropriate as the initial diagnostic step for OSA, though it may show right ventricular hypertrophy. The patient has no acute cardiac symptoms. Echocardiography (Choice B) may be indicated after polysomnography to assess cardiac pressures and function, but it is not the first step in diagnosing OSA. The patient lacks signs of acute heart failure (no dyspnea, edema, or JVD). Methylphenidate (Choice C) and Modafinil (Choice D) are not first-line treatments for OSA-related daytime somnolence. These agents are reserved for narcolepsy or central sleep apnea and do not address the underlying airway obstruction. Sleep positioning therapy (Choice F) may be a helpful adjunct once OSA is diagnosed, but the severity must first be characterized by polysomnography to determine appropriate treatment intensity and modality. Management of OSA includes lifestyle modifications (weight loss, positional changes), CPAP therapy as first-line treatment, and second-line options such as mandibular advancement devices or surgery for obstructing lesions. Educational Objective: Obstructive sleep apnea is diagnosed by polysomnography. Risk factors include male sex, obesity, and upper airway abnormalities. Chronic untreated OSA causes pulmonary hypertension and can lead to right heart failure. First-line management is CPAP therapy combined with lifestyle modifications.
▶ Cardiovascular System Essential Hypertension 1 question
Source: 5A #24
An asymptomatic 42-year-old man with a 1-month history of essential hypertension comes for a follow-up examination. He has been moderately obese for 20 years and follows a high-calorie, high-fat diet. He drinks four to five cups of coffee daily. He has smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 20 years. He drinks one beer at dinner almost daily. He has a sedentary lifestyle. He is 175 cm (5 ft 9 in) tall and weighs 100 kg (220 lb); BMI is 33 kg/m². His pulse is 78/min, and blood pressure is 148/94 mm Hg. Examination shows no other abnormalities. Which of the following is the most effective method to decrease his blood pressure? A) Abstinence from alcohol B) Abstinence from coffee C) Smoking cessation D) Weight loss of 9 kg (20 lb) E) Biofeedback F) Vitamin E supplementation
Correct Answer: D) Weight loss of 9 kg (20 lb)
Weight loss of 9 kg (20 lb) is the most effective intervention to decrease this patient's blood pressure. On average, for patients who are overweight or obese, the loss of 0.45 kg (1 lb) of body weight correlates with a drop in mean blood pressure by approximately 1 mm Hg. Continued aerobic exercise at least several times per week will also help reduce blood pressure. Other helpful interventions include dietary salt restriction, although many patients with essential hypertension will eventually require pharmacologic therapy to reduce blood pressure below the current goal of 130/80 mm Hg. Abstinence from alcohol (Choice A) is unlikely to have a meaningful effect on blood pressure in this patient who only drinks one beer daily. Male patients with hypertension who drink three or more drinks per day should be advised to reduce intake to two or fewer drinks per day. Abstinence from coffee (Choice B) is unlikely to affect blood pressure significantly. The association between caffeine intake and long-term blood pressure is unclear, and regular coffee intake is not thought to contribute to primary hypertension development. While there may be a transient increase in blood pressure following caffeine consumption due to sympathomimetic effects, this is brief and temporal to caffeine's duration of action. Smoking cessation (Choice C) is unlikely to affect blood pressure in the long term. Smoking raises blood pressure transiently while smoking and for minutes afterward due to sympathetic nervous system activation, but the effect returns to baseline within 10-15 minutes. There is no clear association between smoking and chronic hypertension development. Biofeedback (Choice E) is a mind-body technique using visual and/or auditory feedback to gain control over involuntary bodily functions, but it is not a proven means of reducing chronic hypertension. Vitamin E supplementation (Choice F) is unlikely to significantly affect blood pressure. While some evidence suggests vitamin E may cause slight systolic blood pressure decrease in mild hypertension, no overall effect has been found on diastolic blood pressure or mean arterial pressure, and the effect is not as significant as weight loss. Educational Objective: Weight loss is an effective intervention for overweight or obese patients with essential hypertension. For every 0.45 kg (1 lb) of weight loss, patients can expect a decrease in blood pressure of approximately 1 mm Hg.
▶ Renal Urinary System Chlamydial Urinary Tract Infection 1 question
Source: 5A #27
A sexually active male patient under age 25 with a history of previous chlamydial infection presents after unprotected sexual intercourse with multiple partners. Which of the following should be recommended? A) Chlamydia trachomatis B) Herpes simplex virus, type 1 C) Herpes simplex virus, type 2 D) Human papillomavirus E) No further testing is indicated
Correct Answer: A) Chlamydia trachomatis
This patient requires testing for Chlamydia trachomatis given his history of previous infection and unprotected sexual intercourse with multiple partners. C. trachomatis is a sexually transmitted infection that classically presents in men with urethritis, dysuria, and urethral discharge, though it can be asymptomatic. The United States Preventive Services Task Force recommends testing for C. trachomatis and Neisseria gonorrhoeae in all sexually active patients under age 25 years. Complications of undiagnosed infection include epididymitis and reactive arthritis, as well as transmission to additional partners. Diagnosis is made with nucleic acid amplification testing, and treatment consists of azithromycin or doxycycline in combination with ceftriaxone (covering potential N. gonorrhoeae coinfection). Patients previously treated for C. trachomatis have high risk for reinfection and should be retested approximately 3 months after treatment. Herpes simplex virus type 1 (Choice B) typically presents with tender vesicles, ulceration, and crusting around the mouth, while type 2 (Choice C) presents similarly on the genitalia. Routine herpes testing is not meaningful as many patients have positive serology without active infection. Human papillomavirus (Choice D) causes cervical/vulvar and penile/anal carcinoma and condyloma acuminata but is not part of routine sexually transmitted infection diagnosis; many infections are transient and self-limited. No further testing (Choice E) is incorrect because this patient has previous sexually transmitted infection history and continues unprotected sexual intercourse, placing him at increased risk for reinfection with C. trachomatis or new infection with N. gonorrhoeae or HIV, necessitating testing to prevent disease sequelae and transmission. Educational Objective: Many sexually transmitted infections remain asymptomatic, making testing essential for all sexually active patients under age 25 years per USPSTF recommendations. Failure to diagnose increases risk for complications and disease transmission, particularly in high-risk patients with unprotected sex or multiple partners. Patients previously treated for sexually transmitted infections require retesting approximately 3 months after treatment due to high reinfection risk.
▶ Human Development Nutrition in adolescence 1 question
Source: 5A #30
A 16-year-old girl comes to the physician for a routine health maintenance examination. She says that she began following a vegan diet (no dairy or animal products) 2 months ago. She takes no medications or dietary supplements. She is 165 cm (5 ft 5 in) tall and weighs 50 kg (110 lb); BMI is normal. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. Supplementation with which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation for this patient? A) Calcium B) Folic acid C) Magnesium D) Manganese E) Vitamin A
Correct Answer: A) Calcium
In this patient who has recently converted to a vegan diet, it is most appropriate to begin supplementation with calcium. Patients who follow vegan diets are at increased risk for deficiencies of iron, calcium, zinc, protein, fatty acids, and vitamin B12 (cobalamin). Given that these patients do not have consistent intake of these essential vitamins and minerals, it is appropriate to begin supplementation even prior to the emergence of any symptoms or signs of deficiency. In these patients, the low intake of dietary calcium increases the total body concentration of parathyroid hormone as well as vitamin D. This causes a reuptake of calcium from the bones to maintain appropriate serum concentrations. Over time, this can lead to low bone density and subsequent pathologic fractures. Folic acid (Choice B) supplementation is not necessary at this time. Patients who follow vegan or vegetarian diets are not generally at risk for folate deficiency because high concentrations of folate can be found in green leafy vegetables, which the patient is regularly consuming. Magnesium (Choice C) supplementation is not necessary at this time. Patients who follow vegan or vegetarian diets are not generally at risk for magnesium deficiency because high concentrations of magnesium can be found in green leafy vegetables, beans, kale, spinach, bananas, and avocados. Manganese (Choice D) supplementation is not necessary in this patient. Patients who follow vegan or vegetarian diets are not generally at risk for manganese deficiency because many foods that are part of these diets are high in manganese, such as whole grains, quinoa, almonds, hazelnuts, chickpeas, spinach, and tofu. Vitamin A (Choice E) supplementation is not necessary in this patient. Patients who follow vegan or vegetarian diets are not generally at risk for Vitamin A deficiency because many foods that are part of these diets are high in Vitamin A, such as sweet potatoes, carrots, spinach, kale, squash, and cantaloupe. Educational Objective: Patients who follow vegan diets are at increased risk for deficiencies of iron, calcium, zinc, protein, fatty acids, and vitamin B12 (cobalamin). Supplementation should be initiated even if the patient has just recently converted to these diets to avoid deficiency.
▶ Respiratory System Community-Acquired Pneumonia 1 question
Source: 5A #31
A 38-year-old woman has had an 8-day history of malaise, sore throat, nasal congestion, and rhinitis and a 2-day history of temperatures to 40°C (104°F), shortness of breath, and cough productive of yellow mucus. She is alert and oriented. Her temperature is 39.7°C (103.4°F), pulse is 116/min, respirations are 24/min, and blood pressure is 108/72 mm Hg. Breath sounds are decreased at the left apex posteriorly and anteriorly with increased tactile fremitus over that area. Inspiratory and expiratory crackles and rhonchi are heard. There is a normal S1 and S2 and no cyanosis, clubbing, or pedal edema. Her leukocyte count is 16,000/mm³. An x-ray of the chest shows a left upper lobe consolidation. Which of the following is the most likely causal organism? A) Mycobacterium tuberculosis B) Mycoplasma pneumoniae C) Neisseria meningitidis D) Pneumocystis jiroveci (formerly P. carinii) E) Pseudomonas aeruginosa F) Respiratory syncytial virus G) Rhinovirus H) Streptococcus pneumoniae
Correct Answer: H) Streptococcus pneumoniae
Streptococcus pneumoniae is a gram-positive diplococcus and the most common bacterial cause of community-acquired pneumonia. Typical signs include fever, chills, productive cough, shortness of breath, and pleuritic chest pain. Vital signs show tachycardia and tachypnea. Physical examination findings include rhonchi, crackles, decreased breath sounds, and increased tactile fremitus, all present in this patient. Chest x-rays commonly show lobar consolidation. Management includes antibiotics, and depending on severity and risk factors, may require supplemental oxygen, mechanical respiratory support, and admission. Acute bacterial pneumonia may occur following a viral respiratory syndrome, as in this case. Mycobacterium tuberculosis presents with hilar adenopathy and calcified granulomas in primary infection, or apical opacities with night sweats, weight loss, and hemoptysis in reactivation—this patient lacks these features. Mycoplasma pneumoniae causes atypical pneumonia with alveolar and interstitial infiltrates, not the lobar infiltrate seen here. Neisseria meningitidis causes meningococcemia or meningitis with acute fever, altered mental status, myalgias, and petechial rash, not pneumonia. Pneumocystis jiroveci causes opportunistic pneumonia in immunocompromised patients with diffuse bilateral infiltrates; this patient is immunocompetent with lobar pneumonia. Pseudomonas aeruginosa is associated with hospital-acquired or ventilator-acquired pneumonia, not community-acquired pneumonia in non-hospitalized patients. Respiratory syncytial virus and rhinovirus cause atypical pneumonia with diffuse opacities or upper respiratory infection, not lobar consolidation. Educational Objective: Streptococcus pneumoniae is the most common cause of community-acquired pneumonia with typical presentation of fever, productive cough, and lobar consolidation on chest x-ray. Treatment includes antibiotics and supportive care measures.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Gout 1 question
Source: 5A #32
A 45-year-old man with gout has the sudden onset of extreme pain, swelling, and exquisite tenderness of the right knee; he cannot bear the pressure of wearing slacks. He has not recently taken any medication and previously had intractable diarrhea while taking colchicine. Which of the following is the most appropriate drug for treating this acute episode? A) Allopurinol B) Codeine C) Indomethacin D) Prednisone E) Probenecid
Correct Answer: C) Indomethacin
Gout is an acute, monoarticular arthropathy resulting from an intra-articular inflammatory reaction to precipitation of monosodium urate crystals. It typically presents with atraumatic joint pain, erythema, and swelling. The most common joint involved is the first metatarsophalangeal, but other common joints include the knees and elbows. Arthrocentesis shows a leukocyte count of 10,000-50,000/mm³ with needle-like, birefringent crystals. Treatment of an acute flare consists of nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs (NSAIDs) such as naproxen or indomethacin, colchicine, or corticosteroids. In this patient presenting with an acute gout flare and a history of intractable diarrhea with colchicine, indomethacin is the most appropriate initial pharmacotherapy. Allopurinol (Choice A) and probenecid (Choice E) are urate-lowering medications used for prevention of gout flares, not acute treatment. Allopurinol inhibits xanthine oxidase while probenecid decreases urate reabsorption. Codeine (Choice B) is an opiate used for pain management but is not first-line for acute gout. NSAIDs, colchicine, and corticosteroids are preferred for pain relief and management of acute gout flares. Prednisone (Choice D) is a corticosteroid that can be used for acute gout flares but is not the most recommended initial therapy due to its side effect profile. NSAIDs or colchicine are typically administered first, with corticosteroids reserved for patients who cannot tolerate these agents. Educational Objective: For acute gout flares, NSAIDs (such as indomethacin) are first-line therapy. In patients with previous intolerance to colchicine, indomethacin is the most appropriate initial pharmacotherapy for acute gout.
▶ Cardiovascular System Abdominal Aortic Aneurysm 1 question
Source: 5A #33
A 72-year-old African American man comes to the office because of a 2-day history of mild, vague abdominal pain. He has hypertension, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and osteoarthritis of the knees. His medications are lisinopril, metformin, and acetaminophen. He does not smoke cigarettes or use illicit drugs. He drinks eight glasses of wine weekly. He is a retired hotel desk clerk. He is 178 cm (5 ft 10 in) tall and weighs 79 kg (175 lb); BMI is 25 kg/m². His temperature is 37.1°C (98.8°F), pulse is 92/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 156/97 mm Hg. Abdominal examination shows a pulsatile midline mass 7 cm above the umbilicus. No other abnormalities are noted. Laboratory studies show a hemoglobin A1c of 8.4% and serum total cholesterol concentration of 260 mg/dL. Which of the following factors is most predictive of future complications of this patient's condition? A) Alcohol consumption B) Blood pressure C) Ethnicity D) Hemoglobin A1c measurement E) Serum total cholesterol concentration
Correct Answer: B) Blood pressure
This patient presents with a pulsatile abdominal mass consistent with an abdominal aortic aneurysm (AAA). AAAs most commonly occur in male smokers with a family or personal history of atherosclerotic disease, typically in the infrarenal abdominal aorta. While smoking is the modifiable risk factor most strongly associated with AAA development, expansion, and rupture, uncontrolled hypertension has also been shown to be associated with increased rates of expansion and rupture. Blood pressure control is therefore a critical factor in preventing future complications. Alcohol consumption (Choice A) has variable effects; moderate consumption may be protective while high levels (>2 drinks/day) may slightly increase risk, but this patient's moderate consumption would not predict complications. Ethnicity (Choice C) affects AAA prevalence but not rupture or expansion risk. Hemoglobin A1c (Choice D) is not predictive; diabetes is actually associated with lower risk for AAA development, expansion, and rupture. Serum total cholesterol (Choice E) contributes to AAA development but has no association with expansion and rupture. The USPSTF recommends screening for AAA in men age 65-75 years with a history of tobacco use. Smoking cessation provides the greatest risk reduction, but blood pressure control also significantly reduces the risk of complications in existing aneurysms.
▶ Renal Urinary System Urinary Incontinence 1 question
Source: 5A #34
A 32-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 3-month history of frequent loss of urine. During this time, she has had loss of urine at rest and with activity. She wears an absorbent pad each night; it is soaked by morning. She has difficulty initiating her urinary stream, which is weak. She has had six urinary tract infections during the past year. She has had type 1 diabetes mellitus since the age of 6 years. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. A cough stress test is positive. Her postvoid residual volume is 800 mL. Urinalysis shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Functional incontinence B) Overflow incontinence C) Stress incontinence D) Urge incontinence E) Urinary tract infection F) Vesicovaginal fistula
Correct Answer: B) Overflow incontinence
Overflow incontinence occurs when bladder emptying is impaired, eventually resulting in intravesical hydrostatic pressure overcoming internal and external urinary sphincter closing pressures, thereby causing leakage of urine. Common causes include neurologic insults (diabetic neuropathy, stroke, trauma, multiple sclerosis) resulting in bladder atony, and bladder outlet obstruction (benign prostatic hyperplasia, bladder mass). This patient's long history of type 1 diabetes mellitus increases her risk for diabetic neuropathy and overflow incontinence secondary to neurogenic bladder. Patients develop urinary retention with inability to void and may have loss of urine with Valsalva maneuver or increased abdominopelvic pressure. The diagnosis is established with urodynamic study showing increased postvoid residual volume, as in this patient with 800 mL. Treatment includes bethanechol (muscarinic agent promoting bladder contraction) or intermittent catheterization. Functional incontinence (Choice A) describes incontinence secondary to external factors or conditions creating inability to void timely or recognize the need to void (dementia, Parkinson disease, physical barriers, medication effects). This patient shows signs of primary incontinence (overflow) evidenced by increased postvoid residual volume. Stress incontinence (Choice C) is triggered by increased intra-abdominal pressure (lifting, sneezing, Valsalva). It results from urethral hypermobility caused by pelvic floor weakness/prolapse or intrinsic sphincter deficiency. This patient's normal physical examination and increased postvoid residual volume make stress incontinence unlikely. Stress incontinence would not produce elevated postvoid residual volume. Urge incontinence (Choice D) or overactive bladder is caused by detrusor muscle instability, presenting with urge to void followed by leakage. Patients have low postvoid residual volume, contrasting with this patient's 800 mL. Oxybutynin treats urge incontinence from overactive bladder. Urinary tract infection (Choice E) can present with overactive bladder symptoms (frequency, urgency, urge incontinence) and typically includes dysuria, suprapubic pain, and bacteria/white blood cells on urinalysis. This patient has no dysuria and normal urinalysis, making UTI unlikely. Vesicovaginal fistula (Choice F) results from gynecologic surgery, pelvic malignancy, or radiation therapy, presenting with painless urinary leakage into the vagina with urine pooling in the vaginal canal on examination. This patient's normal physical examination makes fistula unlikely.
▶ Female Reproductive Breast Cervical Cancer 1 question
Source: 5A #35
A 20-year-old woman comes to the office for a routine health maintenance examination. She says she feels well. She has no history of serious illness or sexually transmitted infection. Her only medication is an oral contraceptive, for which she requests a refill. She has had five lifetime sexual partners. During the past 4 months, she has been monogamous with one male partner. She has never used condoms. In addition to refilling the oral contraceptive prescription, which of the following is the most appropriate screening for cervical cancer? A) High-risk human papillomavirus (HPV) genotyping only B) Pap smear and high-risk HPV genotyping C) Pap smear with reflex HPV testing D) Pap smear only E) No cervical cancer screening is indicated at this time
Correct Answer: E) No cervical cancer screening is indicated at this time
The correct answer is E. No cervical cancer screening is indicated at this time given this patient's age of 20 years. The United States Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) guidelines recommend screening for cervical carcinoma with Pap smear testing every 3 years between ages 21 and 29 years, and Pap smear screening every 5 years with co-testing for human papillomavirus (HPV) or every 3 years with Pap smear testing alone between ages 30 and 65 years. Screening is not recommended before age 21 years because the risks of screening and additional testing typically outweigh the benefits in this population. However, this patient should undergo appropriate screening for sexually transmitted infections, such as Chlamydia trachomatis and Neisseria gonorrhoeae, given her history of unprotected intercourse with multiple prior partners. Choice A (High-risk HPV genotyping only) is not recommended as a screening modality for sexually transmitted infections or cervical cancer. HPV strains 6 and 11 are most commonly implicated in condyloma acuminata, whereas strains 16, 18, 31, and 33 confer the highest risk for progression to cervical cancer. Screening in asymptomatic patients is not recommended as infections are typically transient and may be cleared by the patient's immune system. Choice B (Pap smear and high-risk HPV genotyping) is not recommended for this patient. This combination is typically reserved as a cervical cancer screening modality for patients between ages 30 and 65 years, not for patients under age 21 years. Choice C (Pap smear with reflex HPV testing) is not recommended for this patient. While clinicians may choose to perform reflex HPV testing if the result of cytology is abnormal to further delineate the risk for cervical cancer, neither of these tests are recommended in patients under age 21 years. Choice D (Pap smear only) is not recommended by the USPSTF for patients under age 21 years. Educational Objective: Pap smear testing is an important screening tool for the detection of cervical carcinoma. The USPSTF guidelines recommend screening for cervical carcinoma with Pap smear testing every 3 years between ages 21 and 29 years, and Pap smear screening every 5 years with co-testing for human papillomavirus or every 3 years with Pap smear testing alone between ages 30 and 65 years. Screening is not recommended for patients under age 21 years.
▶ Blood Lymphoreticular System Hereditary spherocytosis 1 question
Source: 5A #37
A 5-year-old boy is brought to the office by his mother because of a 6-month history of moderate right upper abdominal pain that occurs 15 to 60 minutes after he eats. The episodes occur once or twice weekly and resolve in less than 1 hour. He otherwise is asymptomatic. He has no history of serious illness and receives no medications. His mother says he eats three fast-food meals weekly. Growth and development are appropriate for age. Vital signs are within normal limits. The abdomen is nontender. The spleen tip is palpated 2 cm below the left costal margin. Ultrasonography of the abdomen shows 20 gallstones measuring 3 mm to 10 mm. His hemoglobin concentration is 7.7 g/dL (N=11.5–14.5). A blood smear shows spherocytes. Which of the following is the most likely cause of this patient's condition? A) Change in the shape of red blood cells B) A fat-rich diet C) Presence of antiplatelet IgG antibodies D) Production of autoantibodies directed against the red blood cell membrane E) Switch from glutamic acid to valine in the globin chain
Correct Answer: A) Change in the shape of red blood cells
Hereditary spherocytosis (HS) is a disorder of red blood cell membrane proteins caused by autosomal dominant mutations in ankyrin, spectrin, and band erythrocyte membrane proteins. These mutations result in loss of the normal connection between the inner membrane skeleton and the outer lipid bilayer, limiting the ability of red blood cells to conform when passing through narrow capillary beds. This leads to early removal of red blood cells by the spleen and hemolysis, causing normocytic hemolytic anemia. The clinical presentation includes fatigue, weakness, pallor, splenomegaly, and jaundice. Laboratory findings show anemia, indirect hyperbilirubinemia, and spherocytes on blood smear. Patients are prone to bilirubin gallstone formation due to high levels of indirect bilirubin, explaining this patient's cholelithiasis. The splenomegaly (spleen tip 2 cm below costal margin) and hemoglobin of 7.7 g/dL confirm hemolytic anemia. Splenectomy is the definitive treatment to reduce premature red blood cell consumption. Choice B (fat-rich diet) does not explain the spherocytes on blood smear or splenomegaly, though it may precipitate pain after fatty meals in patients with cholelithiasis. Choice C (antiplatelet IgG antibodies) is seen in immune thrombocytopenia, which presents with thrombocytopenia, mucosal bleeding, and petechiae or purpura. Platelets and erythrocytes appear normal on peripheral smear, not spherocytes. Choice D (autoantibodies against red blood cell membrane) is characteristic of autoimmune hemolytic anemia, a Type II hypersensitivity reaction where antibody binding causes complement-mediated destruction of erythrocytes. Spherocytes would not be expected in autoimmune hemolytic anemia. Choice E (glutamic acid to valine substitution in globin chain) is characteristic of sickle cell disease, which results in abnormal hemoglobin S polymerization and sickling of erythrocytes, not spherocytes.
▶ Multisystem Processes Disorders Folic Acid Deficiency 1 question
Source: 5A #40
A previously healthy 82-year-old woman comes to the physician because of generalized weakness and a sore tongue for 1 month. She lives by herself. Her diet has consisted mainly of tea and homemade bread since her husband died 6 months ago. Examination shows pallor, mild jaundice, and a smooth, red tongue. There is no lymphadenopathy or organomegaly. Neurologic examination shows no focal findings. Laboratory studies show: Hematocrit 26%, Mean corpuscular volume 120 μm³, Leukocyte count 2500/mm³, Reticulocyte count 0.2%, Platelet count 20,000/mm³. This patient most likely has which of the following nutritional deficiencies? A) Folic acid B) Niacin C) Vitamin B₁ (thiamine) D) Vitamin B₆ E) Vitamin B₁₂ (cobalamin)
Correct Answer: A) Folic acid
Folic acid (vitamin B₉) is converted to tetrahydrofolic acid and serves as a coenzyme in nucleotide and nucleoside synthesis. Folate is found in leafy vegetables and absorbed in the jejunum. Deficiency occurs with malnutrition, alcohol use disorder, and anti-folate medications (phenytoin, methotrexate). Megaloblastic anemia results from impaired DNA synthesis and is characterized by macrocytosis and hypersegmented neutrophils. This patient's presentation—malnutrition for 6 months, macrocytic anemia (MCV 120), pancytopenia, glossitis, and jaundice—is consistent with folic acid deficiency. Folate stores deplete rapidly with malnutrition, whereas vitamin B₁₂ reserves are extensive and take longer to deplete, making folic acid deficiency more likely in this acute malnutrition scenario. Niacin (Choice B) causes pellagra with the classic triad of dementia, diarrhea, and dermatitis (often in a necklace pattern around the neck). This patient lacks diarrhea and dermatitis. Vitamin B₁/thiamine (Choice C) is a cofactor in glucose metabolism and ATP production. Deficiency causes Wernicke encephalopathy (confusion, ophthalmoplegia, ataxia) progressing to Korsakoff syndrome (dementia, confabulation, hallucinations). This patient has no neuropsychiatric findings. Vitamin B₆ (Choice D) deficiency is uncommon from dietary insufficiency alone but presents with glossitis, stomatitis, and seborrheic dermatitis. It typically occurs with chronic isoniazid therapy for tuberculosis and can cause peripheral neuropathy. This patient has no evidence of these conditions. Vitamin B₁₂/cobalamin (Choice E) deficiency also causes macrocytic anemia with hypersegmented neutrophils but additionally causes posterior column and spinocerebellar tract demyelination. It is seen in malnutrition, alcoholism, pernicious anemia, and Crohn disease. However, with only 6 months of malnutrition and extensive body stores of B₁₂, depletion is unlikely, and this patient lacks neurologic findings. Educational Objective: Megaloblastic anemia from impaired DNA synthesis is most commonly related to folate or vitamin B₁₂ deficiency. Folic acid deficiency develops more rapidly than B₁₂ deficiency in malnutrition because folate body reserves are less extensive than B₁₂ reserves.
▶ Human Development Driving assessment in older adulthood 1 question
Source: 5A #41
An 82-year-old woman with dementia, Alzheimer type, is brought to the physician by her daughter for recommendations about driving restrictions. Since the patient moved in with her daughter 3 weeks ago, they have had several arguments because her daughter has not allowed the patient to drive. The patient last drove a car 2 years ago and has no history of motor vehicle collisions. The patient's husband provided her daily care until he died of a myocardial infarction 3 weeks ago. The patient's only medication is galantamine, which she has taken for 4 years. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. Mental status examination shows a sad mood and reactive affect. She reports no suicidal thoughts. She is able to correctly repeat six digits forward and recalls one of three objects after 5 minutes; she is unable to correctly copy a rectangle or two intersecting pentagons. Which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation regarding this patient's driving? A) The patient should be allowed to drive only with supervision B) The patient should be allowed to drive short distances only C) The patient should be allowed to drive until her driving skills are formally assessed D) The patient should not have access to car keys until her driving skills are formally assessed E) The patient's driver license should be revoked
Correct Answer: D) The patient should not have access to car keys until her driving skills are formally assessed
Alzheimer dementia is the most prevalent dementia in elderly patients and is characterized by progressive cognitive decline affecting instrumental activities of daily living (IADLs) before basic activities of daily living. Driving is an IADL requiring higher cognitive abilities. This patient demonstrates significant cognitive impairment: inability to copy geometric figures (visuospatial dysfunction), poor recall (one of three objects), and recent major life stressor (husband's death 3 weeks ago). A patient's ability to perform IADLs should be assessed through self-reporting, caregiver reporting, and direct observation (driving test, occupational therapy evaluation). For potentially unsafe activities like driving, the activity must be restricted until formal assessment occurs. Choices A, B, and E are premature actions without formal assessment. Choice C is dangerous as it allows continued driving by a potentially unsafe driver. Choice D is correct because it restricts access to car keys (preventing unsafe driving) while allowing time for formal driving assessment to determine the need for driving restrictions and other supportive measures. Formal assessments can reveal the need for in-home support or assisted living placement.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Nausea and vomiting 1 question
Source: 5A #42
A 4-week-old male infant is brought to the office by his parents because of a 1-week history of 'increasing spit-up.' His parents report that the patient will forcefully spit up within a few minutes after every feeding. He has a history of mild breast-feeding jaundice which resolved spontaneously. The patient was born at term following an uncomplicated spontaneous vaginal delivery to a primigravid mother. Vital signs are within normal limits. Palpation over the right upper abdominal quadrant discloses a 1.5-cm mass. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step to determine the cause of this patient's symptoms? A) Anteroposterior and decubitus x-rays of the abdomen B) Barium enema with air contrast C) Complete blood count and comprehensive metabolic panel D) Ultrasonography of the abdomen E) Observation of the infant while feeding and spitting up
Correct Answer: D) Ultrasonography of the abdomen
Pyloric stenosis is caused by hypertrophy of the pyloric sphincter, leading to gastric outlet obstruction and vomiting. It commonly presents with nonbloody, nonbilious emesis after feedings beginning around age 3 to 6 weeks, as in this 4-week-old infant. It is more common in male infants. Symptoms may begin with occasional vomiting, then progress to projectile emesis after every feed, dehydration, and malnutrition as the stenosis worsens. Repeated bouts of vomiting can cause metabolic and electrolyte derangements, classically hypochloremic, hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis due to losses of hydrochloric acid, a major component of gastric secretions. Infants can have severe electrolyte derangements that should be treated urgently. Dehydration can be shown on examination by decreased activity, listlessness, dry mucous membranes, and poor skin turgor. Pyloric stenosis on abdominal examination presents as a small, nontender mass in the epigastric region. Diagnosis of pyloric stenosis is by ultrasonography of the abdomen showing thickened pyloric musculature. Management includes correcting fluid and electrolyte abnormalities followed by surgical pyloromyotomy. Anteroposterior and decubitus x-rays of the abdomen (Choice A) is a nonspecific study that would not necessarily show pyloric stenosis. An x-ray can help with the diagnosis of constipation and bowel obstruction but is neither sensitive nor specific and would not clearly outline hypertrophy of the pylorus. Barium enema with air contrast (Choice B) is used to examine for abnormalities in the large intestine such as colon cancer and can be used in children to evaluate for congenital megacolon (Hirschsprung disease) and intestinal malrotation, but is not useful in the diagnosis of pyloric stenosis. Complete blood count and comprehensive metabolic panel (Choice C) can be helpful in the full evaluation of pyloric stenosis and repeated vomiting, which can cause metabolic and electrolyte derangements, classically hypochloremic, hypokalemic metabolic alkalosis. However, it would only be diagnostic of metabolic derangements and dehydration that can be addressed with supportive measures. Diagnosis and treatment of the underlying cause, pyloric stenosis, should be done using ultrasonography of the abdomen and surgical pyloromyotomy. Observation of the infant while feeding and spitting up (Choice E) can be helpful in determining between physiologic regurgitation in infants versus projectile vomiting in pyloric stenosis. However, this patient has a 1.5-cm mass on abdominal palpation, making physiologic regurgitation less likely. Due to palpation of an abdominal mass, further evaluation for pyloric stenosis should be done using ultrasonography of the abdomen. Educational Objective: Pyloric stenosis is caused by hypertrophy of the pyloric sphincter, leading to gastric outlet obstruction and vomiting in infants. Diagnosis of pyloric stenosis is by ultrasonography of the abdomen showing thickened pyloric musculature.
▶ Skin Subcutaneous Tissue Scabies 1 question
Source: 5A #45
A 63-year-old man comes to the clinic because of a 4-day history of a severely itchy rash that started on his right hand and forearm but has since spread to his left hand. This morning, he noticed some "spots" around his waistline. Most of the spots are red bumps, but some of the spots now have turned into scabs. The patient's new roommate, who moved into his apartment a few weeks ago, has had a similar itchy rash. The patient has no other history of serious illness and takes no medications. Vital signs are within normal limits. A photograph of the patient's right hand is shown; similar findings are noted over the waistline. There is no crusting or spreading erythema. He has no rash elsewhere. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacotherapy at this time? A) Oral acyclovir B) Oral cephalexin C) Oral prednisone D) Topical permethrin E) Topical triamcinolone
Correct Answer: D) Topical permethrin
Scabies is a cutaneous infection caused by the Sarcoptes scabiei mite, transmitted through direct skin-to-skin contact. The clinical presentation is classic: pruritic, superficial burrows and papules in the interweb spaces of digits, flexural wrist, axillary folds, periumbilical area, waist, and genitalia. The patient's presentation with progressive itchy rash starting on hands and spreading to waistline, combined with similar symptoms in a close contact (roommate), is pathognomonic for scabies. Diagnosis can be clinical or confirmed by demonstration of mites on skin scrapings using mineral oil preparation. Treatment requires topical permethrin applied from the neck down in two applications 1 week apart. Close contacts must also be treated to prevent recurrence. Patients should wash all previously worn clothing and bedding in hot water and dry on hot settings. Residual pruritus following treatment may persist for several weeks and can be managed with oral antihistamines and topical steroids. Oral acyclovir (Choice A) treats viral infections like herpes simplex virus and varicella zoster virus, which present with painful vesicles, not the burrows and papules seen here. Oral cephalexin (Choice B) is a first-generation cephalosporin targeting gram-positive bacteria and is used for bacterial skin infections, not parasitic infestations. Oral prednisone (Choice C) and topical triamcinolone (Choice E) are glucocorticoids used for inflammatory and autoimmune dermatologic conditions like eczema or psoriasis, not for acute scabies management. While topical steroids may be used after successful treatment to manage residual itching, they are not the primary treatment for scabies. Educational Objective: Scabies is caused by Sarcoptes scabiei mite infestation transmitted through direct skin-to-skin contact. Patients present with pruritic papules and burrows in interweb spaces, intertriginous folds, and flexural surfaces. Both the patient and close contacts must be treated with topical permethrin to ensure successful treatment and prevent recurrence.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Backache, including low back pain 1 question
Source: 5A #46
A previously healthy 47-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 1-week history of low back pain that radiates to the left buttock and along the lateral side of the left leg. Her symptoms began 1 day after moving furniture in her home. She has not had fever, chills, constipation, or urinary incontinence. Examination shows diffuse tenderness over the paraspinal muscles of the lumbosacral area and no tenderness over the spine. Range of motion of the left lower extremity is limited by pain. Deep tendon reflexes are normal, muscle strength is intact, and sensory examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Bed rest for 1 week B) CT scan of the lumbosacral spine C) Epidural corticosteroid injection D) Oral ibuprofen therapy E) Oral prednisone therapy
Correct Answer: D) Oral ibuprofen therapy
This patient presents with acute low back pain (1-week duration) with referred pain to the lower extremity following a clear mechanical trigger (moving furniture). Importantly, the neurologic examination is completely reassuring: normal deep tendon reflexes, intact muscle strength, and no sensory abnormalities. This indicates referred pain rather than true radiculopathy. Nonspecific acute low back pain is very common in primary care. Most patients with acute low back pain (less than 4 weeks duration) resolve without further intervention. Additional imaging and laboratory studies are not necessary in the absence of neurologic deficits. Red-flag symptoms that would warrant emergent imaging include new fecal incontinence, urinary retention, saddle anesthesia, or rapidly progressive neurologic deficits—none of which are present here. Oral ibuprofen therapy is the most appropriate next step because it provides symptomatic relief for acute nonspecific low back pain without neurologic compromise. Incorrect answers: - Bed rest (Choice A): Bed rest is not recommended for acute low back pain; patients who rest experience increasingly severe pain and slower recovery. Early mobilization is preferred. - CT scan (Choice B): Imaging is not indicated without red-flag symptoms or neurologic deficits. MRI would be preferred if imaging were needed, but it is not necessary here. - Epidural corticosteroid injection (Choice C): This is appropriate for lumbar radiculopathy, which presents with motor or sensory deficits or reflex changes. This patient has referred pain without neurologic changes. - Oral prednisone (Choice E): While prednisone may be used in acute radiculopathy, evidence shows limited-to-no benefit from short prednisone tapers. It is not indicated for nonspecific low back pain without radiculopathy. Educational Objective: Acute low back pain is common and often self-limited. Thorough neurologic examination is essential to differentiate referred pain from true radiculopathy. In the absence of red-flag signs or neurologic deficits in the first 4 weeks, further imaging is not required. NSAIDs provide appropriate symptomatic management for acute nonspecific low back pain.
▶ Immune System Type 1 Hypersensitivity Reactions 1 question
Source: 5A #47
A 55-year-old man comes to the office because of a 2-week history of a recurrent and severely itchy diffuse rash. The patient reports that the rash typically resolves within 3 to 4 hours and is most severe over his back. He has not had fever, shortness of breath, abdominal pain, or any other symptoms. Use of diphenhydramine during the episodes provides moderate relief. He has no history of serious illness and takes no routine medications. Vital signs are within normal limits. The patient does not currently have a rash but shows a previously taken photograph of his back. Which of the following is the most appropriate diagnostic study at this time? A) C1-esterase inhibitor testing B) Complete blood count with leukocyte differential C) Radioallergosorbent testing D) Serum C3 and C4 concentrations E) No further diagnostic studies are indicated
Correct Answer: E) No further diagnostic studies are indicated
Urticaria (hives) is characterized by transient skin swelling with pink, pruritic wheals that resolve within 24 hours. It results from mast-cell degranulation and release of vasoactive substances, particularly histamine. Triggers include allergens, medications (aspirin, NSAIDs), foods, and upper respiratory infections, though most cases are idiopathic. Treatment includes oral antihistamines as first-line therapy, with epinephrine for anaphylaxis and omalizumab for chronic refractory cases. Importantly, no additional laboratory workup is required for acute, uncomplicated urticaria in the absence of other symptoms or concerning features. C1-esterase inhibitor testing (Choice A) is indicated for hereditary or acquired angioedema, which presents with deeper dermal and submucosal swelling lasting 2-3 days, commonly affecting the larynx, lips, eyelids, and abdominal wall—not consistent with this patient's presentation. Complete blood count with leukocyte differential (Choice B) may be used in refractory urticaria when concerned about lymphoproliferative disorders or hypereosinophilic syndrome, which can present with eczema, hives, or angioedema plus end-organ dysfunction. This patient's presentation does not suggest hypereosinophilic syndrome. Radioallergosorbent testing (Choice C) detects specific IgE antibodies associated with type I hypersensitivity and may identify food or environmental allergens. However, it is not recommended in the workup of uncomplicated, acute urticaria. Serum C3 and C4 concentrations (Choice D) are tested in angioedema cases, where decreased C4 is common in hereditary and acquired angioedema. Normal C4 may be seen in idiopathic angioedema, hypereosinophilic-associated angioedema, and ACE-inhibitor-associated angioedema. These tests are not indicated for urticaria. Educational Objective: Acute, uncomplicated urticaria requires no laboratory monitoring or additional diagnostic workup. Diagnosis is clinical, and treatment with antihistamines is sufficient. Extensive testing should be reserved for refractory cases or when clinical features suggest alternative diagnoses such as angioedema or systemic conditions.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Pseudogout 1 question
Source: 2A #3
A 42-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 1-day history of severe pain, swelling, and warmth in her left knee. Her pain is moderately relieved with ibuprofen use. She notes that she awoke with the pain 1 day after she did an aerobic kickboxing workout. Her temperature is 37.2°C (99°F), pulse is 86/min, and blood pressure is 120/70 mm Hg. The upper aspect of the left knee is erythematous and warm to the touch with an effusion. Range of motion is limited by pain. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Reassurance B) Application of a knee brace and ice and elevation C) MRI of the knees D) Intravenous antibiotic therapy E) Arthrocentesis
Correct Answer: E) Arthrocentesis
Pseudogout results from calcium pyrophosphate crystalline deposition within the joint space and may manifest acutely following trauma, surgery, or extreme exertion. Crystalline deposition provokes an inflammatory response, leading to the classic presentation of joint pain, erythema, effusion, calor, and reduced range of motion. Pseudogout most commonly affects the knee. Arthrocentesis is both therapeutic and diagnostic, as it allows for sampling of synovial fluid that may show the presence of calcium pyrophosphate crystals and an increased concentration of leukocytes. Additionally, arthrocentesis is necessary to rule out competing diagnoses such as septic arthritis or hemarthrosis given the patient's presentation of monoarticular erythema and swelling. These competing diagnoses cannot be excluded on clinical features alone. Plain film x-rays may show chondrocalcinosis and degenerative joint changes. First-line medical management includes nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drugs and colchicine. Reassurance alone (Choice A) will lead to prolonged pain and joint disability. Application of a knee brace, ice, and elevation (Choice B) are useful supportive measures but are less effective at reducing pain in pseudogout than arthrocentesis and do not facilitate diagnosis or exclusion of alternate diagnoses; this would be appropriate for knee sprain, ligamentous or meniscal injury, or traumatic hemarthrosis. MRI of the knees (Choice C) is less sensitive for detection of crystals within the joint space than plain film x-rays and is not commonly used for diagnosis of pseudogout; it would be appropriate if a meniscal tear were suspected. Intravenous antibiotic therapy (Choice D) is appropriate for septic arthritis, which is less likely in this patient as it typically presents with fever.
▶ Endocrine System Papillary thyroid cancer 1 question
Source: 2A #5
A healthy 52-year-old woman comes to the physician because she is concerned about her risk for developing thyroid cancer. Her father was just diagnosed with papillary carcinoma of the thyroid gland. She takes no medications. Examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate screening test for this patient? A) Palpation of the thyroid gland B) Measurement of serum thyroid antibodies C) I-131 scintigraphy of the thyroid gland D) Ultrasonography of the thyroid gland E) CT scan of the thyroid gland
Correct Answer: A) Palpation of the thyroid gland
The most appropriate initial screening for thyroid cancer includes a thorough history and physical examination directed toward identification of palpable thyroid nodules, regional lymphadenopathy, or vocal hoarseness. This clinical assessment is the first-line approach before any imaging or laboratory studies. Choice B (Serum thyroid antibodies) is useful for diagnosing autoimmune thyroiditis (e.g., Hashimoto disease) but thyroid cancers do not typically generate antithyroid antibodies. Choice C (I-131 scintigraphy) is useful for evaluating thyroid nodules with suppressed TSH or multiple nodules to assess functionality, but it is less appropriate as a screening test due to radiation exposure and should only be used after physical examination findings warrant further investigation. Choice D (Ultrasonography) is highly useful for evaluating detected thyroid nodules to assess echogenicity, location, size, and characteristics prior to biopsy, but should not be performed prior to physical examination. It is an appropriate next step only if nodules are detected on examination. Choice E (CT scan) may detect nodules incidentally but is not an optimal imaging modality for thyroid nodule evaluation; ultrasonography is superior for this purpose. Educational Objective: The most appropriate initial screening for thyroid cancer includes a thorough history and physical examination directed toward identification of palpable thyroid nodules, regional lymphadenopathy, or vocal hoarseness.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Alzheimer Disease 1 question
Source: 2A #7
An 82-year-old man is brought to the physician by his daughter because of a 6-month history of memory problems and strange behavior. He lives alone. His daughter says that four times during this period, she found him wandering around his apartment naked in the middle of the day. She took his car and car keys 2 months ago to prevent him from driving because he repeatedly became lost while driving home from the grocery store. He has a history of head injury at the age of 14 years. He has hypercholesterolemia and hypertension. Current medications include simvastatin and diltiazem. His paternal grandmother had dementia, Alzheimer type. The patient has an eighth-grade education. Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows a slow but normal gait. On mental status examination, he describes his mood as "okay." He appears mildly irritated at having an appointment but is friendly. His Mini-Mental State Examination score is 22/30. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in pharmacotherapy? A) Decrease the dosage of diltiazem B) Decrease the dosage of simvastatin C) Add clopidogrel to the current regimen D) Add donepezil to the current regimen E) Add selegiline to the current regimen F) Add sertraline to the current regimen
Correct Answer: D) Add donepezil to the current regimen
Dementia, Alzheimer type, is associated with decreased cerebral cholinergic signaling. Treatment with cholinesterase inhibitors, such as donepezil or rivastigmine, is helpful for improving cholinergic signaling and results in symptomatic benefits including improved cognition and reduced neuropsychiatric symptoms. Cholinesterase inhibitors do not result in neuroprotection and do not alter the underlying disease course of dementia, Alzheimer type. They enhance vagal tone and are contraindicated in patients with atrioventricular block or sinus bradycardia due to increased risk for syncope. They are often used in conjunction with N-methyl-D-aspartate (NMDA) receptor antagonists, such as memantine, which provides neuroprotective effects by limiting glutamate-induced excitotoxicity. Incorrect choices: Decreasing diltiazem (A) is not recommended as calcium channel blockers have not been shown to worsen cognitive dysfunction in dementia. Decreasing simvastatin (B) is not recommended as statins have not been shown to worsen cognitive function and may have limited beneficial effects. Clopidogrel (C) is an antiplatelet medication useful for secondary prophylaxis in coronary and peripheral artery disease but does not manage dementia and may increase bleeding risk. Selegiline (E), a monoamine oxidase inhibitor, has only small evidence for delaying cognitive decline and is associated with significant adverse effects including hypertensive crisis and serotonin syndrome. Sertraline (F), an SSRI, is not indicated as this patient does not display depressive symptoms; while SSRIs may be appropriate for depression-related pseudodementia, they have not shown direct benefits for altering dementia progression. Educational Objective: Cholinesterase inhibitors such as donepezil or rivastigmine improve cholinergic signaling in patients with dementia, Alzheimer type, resulting in symptomatic improvement including improved cognition and decreased neuropsychiatric symptoms. These medications are often used in conjunction with NMDA receptor antagonists such as memantine.
▶ Multisystem Processes Disorders Intimate Partner Abuse 1 question
Source: 2A #9
A previously healthy 27-year-old woman, gravida 2, para 1, at 26 weeks' gestation comes to the physician for a routine prenatal visit. Her temperature is 36.7°C (98°F), pulse is 82/min, respirations are 20/min, and blood pressure is 120/70 mm Hg. Physical examination shows no abnormalities except for ecchymoses in various stages of healing over the forearms and lower abdomen. The fundal height is 27 cm. The cervix is long and closed, and the uterus is nontender and firm. Fetal heart tones are normal. Her hemoglobin concentration is 12 g/dl, platelet count is 280,000/mm³, and bleeding time is 6 minutes. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Domestic abuse B) HELLP syndrome C) Immune thrombocytopenic purpura D) Preeclampsia E) von Willebrand disease
Correct Answer: A) Domestic abuse
Domestic abuse is the most likely explanation for this patient's distribution of bruising. Domestic abuse should always be considered in patients with a suspicious pattern of bruising and/or explanations for the mechanism of injury that do not match the physical examination findings. Areas of bruising that are particularly worrying include the lower abdomen, posterior rib cage, and head. The forearms may also become bruised while employing defensive movements to prevent injury to the face or torso during an attack. All patients with such physical examination findings should be asked in a tactful and nonthreatening manner if they feel safe at home. If possible, all patients should be assessed alone to prevent intimidation that may occur when the abusing partner is present. If patients express that they are being abused, it is the provider's role to assess whether or not they are prepared to act. Often, patients are not yet willing or able to leave a violent relationship and understanding this requires empathy on the part of the provider. However, all patients should receive assistance with devising a safety plan, which includes identifying a place to flee if necessary, signaling to children or neighbors who can call for help, and preparing an emergency kit stored outside of the home with necessary items that might be required if the patient should need to flee. Written materials such as pamphlets should generally be avoided as these can be discovered by the abuser. Many states require mandatory reporting for the abuse of children, the elderly, and disabled persons, but requirements for reporting the abuse of adults varies by state. HELLP syndrome (Choice B) presents with evidence of hemolysis, increased liver enzymes, and low platelets. This patient has a normal hemoglobin for pregnancy, and her platelet count is within the reference ranges. Immune thrombocytopenic purpura (Choice C) requires the presence of thrombocytopenia, which this patient does not demonstrate. Preeclampsia (Choice D) presents with hypertension and proteinuria. This patient's blood pressure is within normal limits. Von Willebrand disease (Choice E) is a bleeding disorder in which patients are deficient in von Willebrand factor, which is a necessary component for platelet aggregation and clot formation.
