Patients typically present with mucocutaneous bleeding and mild thrombocytopenia with a prolonged partial thromboplastin time. Educational Objective: Domestic abuse should be suspected in patients who present with a suspicious distribution of bruises on the lower abdomen, back, or forearms. Bruises on the shins are not typically suspicious. Pregnant women, children, and the elderly are at a particularly high risk for abuse, although anyone may be a victim. Patients who do not wish to report their abuser should be encouraged to develop a safety plan should the need to flee arise.
▶ Multisystem Processes Disorders Polymyalgia Rheumatica 1 question
Source: 2A #12
A 77-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 4-week history of moderately severe aching and morning stiffness in his shoulders and hips. He has difficulty getting out of bed in the morning and reaching over his head because of the pain. During this period, he has had low-grade fever and generalized fatigue. Examination shows decreased range of motion of the shoulders and hips. There is no joint or muscle tenderness. His erythrocyte sedimentation rate is 70 mm/h. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Allopurinol therapy B) Amitriptyline therapy C) Colchicine therapy D) Cyclophosphamide therapy E) Ibuprofen therapy F) Methotrexate therapy G) Prednisone therapy H) Plasmapheresis
Correct Answer: G) Prednisone therapy
Polymyalgia rheumatica (PMR) is an inflammatory rheumatic disorder occurring in elderly patients characterized by stiffness and aching of proximal muscles and joints of the neck, shoulders, torso, and hips, more prominent in the morning. Patients display systemic inflammation signs including fever, chills, malaise, fatigue, and elevated C-reactive protein and erythrocyte sedimentation rate. PMR patients are at increased risk for giant cell arteritis, which often coexists; thorough examination should exclude giant cell arteritis and counsel patients about symptoms such as headache, jaw claudication, temporal tenderness, and amaurosis fugax. Initial treatment requires oral corticosteroids such as prednisone, typically for a prolonged course of 1-2 years. Allopurinol (A) decreases uric acid production and treats gout, which affects the first metatarsophalangeal joint, not shoulder and hip girdles. Amitriptyline (B) is a tricyclic antidepressant useful for fibromyalgia, distinguished from PMR by normal inflammatory markers and pain distant from proximal limb girdles. Colchicine (C) treats inflammatory arthritis and gout by inhibiting microtubule formation to prevent white blood cell translocation into joints. Cyclophosphamide (D) is an alkylating agent for severe rheumatic conditions like systemic lupus erythematosus and rheumatoid arthritis but has significant adverse effects (hemorrhagic cystitis, myelosuppression) and is rarely required for PMR. Ibuprofen (E) is a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug not generally effective for PMR, though may provide mild symptomatic relief. Methotrexate (F) treats rheumatoid arthritis but PMR typically lacks joint erosions and does not involve interphalangeal joints. Plasmapheresis (H) treats autoantibody-mediated diseases like myasthenia gravis and Goodpasture syndrome, but PMR is not primarily autoantibody-mediated and plasmapheresis is not useful for its treatment.
▶ Social Sciences Patient education and counseling 1 question
Source: 2A #13
A 56-year-old woman comes to the physician for a routine examination in October. She has type 2 diabetes mellitus well-controlled with metformin. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows no abnormalities. The patient says that she does not want to get the influenza vaccine this year because she knows people who have "come down with the flu because of the shot." Which of the following is the most appropriate physician response? A) "As your physician, you should trust my recommendation. Please take the influenza vaccine." B) "If you are not going to accept the vaccine, I'd like you sign a waiver of liability form." C) "Influenza can cause pneumonia and death, and type 2 diabetes mellitus puts you at risk. How will your family feel if anything happens to you?" D) "Patients who are incubating the influenza virus when they receive the vaccine mistakenly attribute their symptoms to the vaccine. You should get the vaccine." E) "Why people still believe this myth I'll never know because scientific studies have proven that it is impossible for patients to get the flu from the vaccine."
Correct Answer: D) "Patients who are incubating the influenza virus when they receive the vaccine mistakenly attribute their symptoms to the vaccine. You should get the vaccine."
The physician should respond to this patient by addressing her concern in an accurate, yet polite and nonjudgmental way followed by a concise recommendation. Choice D accomplishes this by explaining the actual mechanism behind her misconception (patients incubating influenza attributing symptoms to the vaccine) while providing a clear recommendation. Choice A is paternalistic and does not build rapport. The patient should trust the physician's recommendation because they know the physician has their best interests at heart, not solely because of their position. Choice B is inappropriate because the physician should not become defensive or bring up legal concerns without first discussing the matter further with the patient. The physician should understand how strongly the patient holds this belief and how she came to this conclusion before declaring the patient's decision final. Choice C uses intimidation and guilt to coerce the patient into receiving treatment, which is not appropriate. The physician's role is to explain treatment options and their respective risks and benefits to help the patient make an informed decision. Choice E responds with emotion and frustration, which is not productive. This approach will likely result in the patient leaving the office holding even more strongly to her views and being less likely to receive vaccination. Furthermore, the patient may not ask the physician questions in the future and instead turn to less reliable sources of information. Educational Objective: When confronted with a patient who holds an incorrect belief, physicians should respond accurately, yet politely, and in a nonjudgmental way. Responding with intimidation, emotion, or defensiveness will not likely induce the patient to change their opinion.
▶ Human Development Falls and prevention in older adulthood 1 question
Source: 2A #14
An 82-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 6-month history of frequent falls. Prior to each fall, she had a feeling of imbalance when walking or turning. She has never lost consciousness during these episodes. She has a 5-year history of stable coronary artery disease and a 10-year history of osteoarthritis of the knees. Current medications include 81-mg aspirin and lovastatin. She resides in an assisted living facility and has a bathroom with fixtures for elderly residents. Her pulse is 80/min, respirations are 14/min, and blood pressure is 140/85 mm Hg with no orthostatic changes. On examination, she has difficulty rising from her chair. Her gait while using her cane is slow with instability on turns. Muscle strength is 4/5 in the lower extremities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Prescription for a powered wheelchair B) 24-Hour ambulatory ECG monitoring C) Duplex ultrasonography of the carotid arteries D) Physical therapy E) Meclizine therapy
Correct Answer: D) Physical therapy
This patient demonstrates physical changes of normal aging, including decreased muscle strength (4/5 in lower extremities) and impaired balance, which are the primary causes of her mechanical falls. Physical therapy is the most appropriate intervention to strengthen muscles and improve coordination and balance, thereby reducing fall risk. The patient has no loss of consciousness, no orthostatic hypotension, and no prodromal symptoms of dizziness or vertigo, making cardiac and neurologic workup unnecessary at this time. A powered wheelchair (Choice A) would accelerate muscle atrophy and is not indicated. 24-Hour ambulatory ECG monitoring (Choice B) and duplex ultrasonography of carotid arteries (Choice C) would only be warranted if the patient had experienced loss of consciousness or transient ischemic attack symptoms. Meclizine therapy (Choice E) is used for vertigo treatment, but this patient reports imbalance rather than dizziness or room spinning. When evaluating falls in elderly patients, it is important to assess whether loss of consciousness occurred, as this would prompt investigation of underlying cardiac disease, orthostatic hypotension, and neurologic disease. Other mechanical fall risk factors to evaluate include declining vision, cognitive impairment, medication adverse effects, arthritis, and orthostatic hypotension. Objective measurements such as the Timed Up and Go test (rising from a chair, walking across the room, turning around, and sitting back down) can help risk stratify patients for fall likelihood. Educational Objective: Features of normal aging, including decreased muscle strength and balance, put elderly patients at risk for mechanical falls. Loss of consciousness at the time of a fall should prompt cardiac and neurologic evaluation. Physical therapy can be used to improve balance and strengthen muscles in elderly patients to decrease fall risk.
▶ Behavioral Health Panic Disorder Without Agoraphobia 1 question
Source: 2A #15
A 32-year-old woman is brought to the emergency department by her husband because of the onset of shortness of breath, profuse sweating, and chest discomfort while at a concert 30 minutes ago. She had a similar episode 1 month ago while at her daughter's school play. She has felt anxious since that time and is concerned that she is going crazy. She has monitored her pulse during the past month, and it has ranged between 70-80/min. Examination shows a pulse of 110/min and blood pressure of 140/76 mm Hg. An opening systolic click is heard over the left fourth intercostal space. Which of the following is the most likely cause of her symptoms? A) Generalized anxiety disorder B) Hyperthyroidism C) Mitral valve prolapse D) Panic disorder E) Paroxysmal atrial tachycardia
Correct Answer: D) Panic disorder
Panic disorder is characterized by recurrent, unexpected panic attacks associated with worry about future panic attacks or avoidance of triggers. This patient presents with classic features of panic disorder: acute onset of fear/anxiety peaking within minutes, accompanied by multiple sympathetic hyperactivity symptoms including tachycardia (pulse 110/min), shortness of breath, chest discomfort, and profuse sweating. The episodic nature of her symptoms (similar episode 1 month ago), normal resting pulse (70-80/min), and subsequent anxiety about having another attack are hallmark features. Treatment includes psychotherapy, long-term SSRIs, and short-term benzodiazepines. Generalized anxiety disorder (Choice A) features excessive persistent worrying with more constant symptoms rather than the episodic panic attacks seen here. Hyperthyroidism (Choice B) presents with anxiety but includes additional characteristic findings such as heat intolerance, weight loss, hair loss, diarrhea, amenorrhea/oligomenorrhea, and thyroid gland abnormalities on examination—none of which are present in this patient. Mitral valve prolapse (Choice C) typically presents with a holosystolic murmur (not an opening systolic click) and would cause persistent rather than episodic shortness of breath from pulmonary edema. While an opening systolic click may be heard with mitral valve prolapse, the episodic nature and symptom pattern are more consistent with panic disorder. Paroxysmal atrial tachycardia (Choice E) would present with palpitations and may cause lightheadedness, pallor, and diaphoresis, but the episodic panic attack presentation with anxiety and fear of losing control is more characteristic of panic disorder. Educational Objective: Panic attacks feature acute fear or anxiety peaking within minutes with four or more symptoms of sympathetic hyperactivity (tachycardia, palpitations, sweating, trembling, shortness of breath, chest pain, dizziness, choking sensation, gastrointestinal distress, paresthesias, chills, derealization, fear of dying, or fear of losing control). Panic disorder involves recurrent unexpected panic attacks with subsequent worry about future attacks or avoidance behaviors.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Cerebral Infarction 1 question
Source: 2A #16
A 42-year-old man comes to the physician for a routine follow-up examination. He feels well. He has a 2-year history of mild hypertension treated with sodium restriction and a reduced-calorie diet. He has had a 2-kg (4.4-lb) weight loss as a result of the diet. His father sustained a cerebral infarction at the age of 55 years, and the patient is concerned about his own risk for cerebral infarction. He does not smoke cigarettes and drinks two glasses of wine each night with dinner. He is an accountant and does not exercise regularly. He is 178 cm (5 ft 10 in) tall and weighs 123 kg (272 lb); BMI is 39 kg/m². His blood pressure is 160/98 mm Hg. Examination shows no other abnormalities. Serum lipid studies show a total cholesterol concentration of 228 mg/dL, an HDL-cholesterol concentration of 45 mg/dL, and an LDL-cholesterol concentration of 148 mg/dL. Which of the following is the strongest predisposing factor for cerebral infarction in this patient? A) Alcohol consumption B) Family history C) Hypercholesterolemia D) Hypertension E) Obesity F) Sedentary lifestyle
Correct Answer: D) Hypertension
Cerebral infarction occurs due to ischemic or hemorrhagic loss of blood supply to the brain, with approximately 80% being ischemic events caused by thromboembolic disease. Unmodifiable risk factors include advanced age, African American race, biologic sex, and family history. Modifiable risk factors include hypertension, lack of physical activity, hyperlipidemia, smoking, and diabetes mellitus. Of all risk factors, hypertension is the single most important and strongest predisposing factor for cerebral infarction. In this patient, his blood pressure of 160/98 mm Hg represents stage 2 hypertension despite dietary modifications. While the patient has multiple other risk factors (family history, hypercholesterolemia, obesity, sedentary lifestyle, and moderate alcohol consumption), hypertension remains the strongest independent predictor. Alcohol consumption in small amounts is actually associated with decreased stroke risk. Family history, hypercholesterolemia, and sedentary lifestyle are associated with increased stroke risk but are weaker than hypertension. Obesity alone is not an independent risk factor but is often associated with hypertension, dyslipidemia, lack of physical activity, and diabetes mellitus—all of which increase cerebral infarction risk.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Carpal Tunnel Syndrome 1 question
Source: 2A #17
A 47-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 6-month history of mild hand pain that occurs primarily over the palmar surfaces. The pain is associated with numbness and tingling, especially over her thumbs and middle and index fingers. Her symptoms are exacerbated by prolonged typing at work and sometimes awaken her from sleep. She has not had any difficulty writing by longhand or grasping small objects. She has type 2 diabetes mellitus well controlled with diet. She takes no medications. Vital signs are within normal limits. Tapping the palmar aspect of the wrist produces pain and tingling in both hands. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Use of wrist splints B) Oral nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug therapy C) Corticosteroid injections D) Endoscopic decompression of the median nerve E) Open decompression of the median nerve
Correct Answer: A) Use of wrist splints
Carpal tunnel syndrome results from compression of the median nerve within the carpal tunnel and presents with signs and symptoms including pain, weakness, paresthesia of the first three digits, and positive provocative test maneuvers such as the Tinel or Phalen tests. This patient demonstrates classic features: pain and paresthesias in the distribution of the median nerve (thumb, index, and middle fingers), symptoms exacerbated by repetitive activity (typing), nocturnal symptoms, and a positive Tinel sign (tapping the palmar aspect of the wrist). Patients with mild symptoms can be treated conservatively with wrist splinting. Wrist splints are typically worn at night but may also be used during the day. Splinting reduces the range of motion of the wrist and limits activities that increase pressure within the carpal tunnel. Oral nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory drug therapy (Choice B) has not been shown to be effective for carpal tunnel syndrome. Corticosteroid injections (Choice C) are a reasonable option for mild carpal tunnel syndrome but are invasive and associated with greater risks compared to wrist splinting; they may be considered as an interval therapeutic option for patients with persistent symptoms prior to surgery. Endoscopic decompression (Choice D) and open decompression (Choice E) of the median nerve may be indicated for patients with persistent or severe symptoms, or weakness in the distribution of the median nerve. Both techniques are similarly effective and have similar complication rates, but they are not appropriate in this patient with mild symptoms prior to a trial of less invasive therapy. The initial management for patients with mild carpal tunnel syndrome includes conservative treatment with wrist splinting.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Fibromyalgia 1 question
Source: 2A #18
A 52-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 14-month history of generalized muscle pain and difficulty sleeping. The pain is most severe during periods of stress but is always present to some degree. She feels exhausted after awakening in the morning despite 8 to 9 hours of sleep nightly. Vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows no abnormalities except for 12 areas of muscle point tenderness. In addition to recommending an aerobic exercise program, which of the following is the most appropriate treatment for this patient? A) Amantadine therapy B) Antidepressant therapy C) Benzodiazepine therapy D) Glucocorticoid therapy E) Opiate analgesic therapy F) Psychostimulant therapy
Correct Answer: B) Antidepressant therapy
Fibromyalgia is a common cause of fatigue and generalized muscle pain believed to be caused by central sensitization of the pain response. It commonly presents in women between ages 20 and 55 years with at least 3 months of fatigue, headache, musculoskeletal pain, and sleep and cognitive disturbances. Concurrent depression and anxiety are common, with symptoms often worsened during stress. Physical examination discloses muscle point tenderness. Previous diagnostic criteria required identification of at least 11 of 18 predefined tender points, though this is no longer used clinically. Laboratory studies including C-reactive protein and erythrocyte sedimentation rate are unremarkable. Initial treatment includes patient education, aerobic exercise regimen, and antidepressants (tricyclic antidepressants or selective serotonin reuptake inhibitors). Amantadine (Choice A) is used for Parkinson disease and occasionally for fatigue in multiple sclerosis, not fibromyalgia. Benzodiazepines (Choice C) treat anxiety and seizure disorders but are not recommended for fibromyalgia despite associated anxiety. Glucocorticoids (Choice D) treat inflammatory, autoimmune, and allergic conditions, but fibromyalgia lacks inflammation and glucocorticoids play no role; this may be confused with polymyalgia rheumatica. Opiate analgesics (Choice E) carry high risk for substance use disorder and should not be started prior to trying alternative therapies like exercise and antidepressants. Psychostimulants (Choice F) treat attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder and narcolepsy but have no role in fibromyalgia treatment.
▶ Respiratory System Croup 1 question
Source: 2A #20
A previously healthy 2-year-old boy is brought to the physician by his mother because of hoarse cough and difficulty breathing that began yesterday evening and became progressively worse during the night. All appropriate immunizations are up-to-date. He appears tired but responds normally to stimuli. His temperature is 38°C (100.4°F), pulse is 164/min, respirations are 40/min, and blood pressure is 102/68 mm Hg. Pulse oximetry on room air shows an oxygen saturation of 86%. Examination shows labored respirations and inspiratory stridor that can be heard without a stethoscope. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Bacterial tracheitis B) Bronchiolitis C) Epiglottitis D) Laryngotracheobronchitis (croup) E) Retropharyngeal abscess
Correct Answer: D) Laryngotracheobronchitis (croup)
Laryngotracheobronchitis (croup) is an infection that presents most commonly in children with fever, rhinorrhea, a barking cough, and inspiratory stridor. Stridor refers to a high-pitched sound caused by turbulent airflow through obstructed or narrowed upper airway structures, such as the pharynx, larynx, or trachea. Croup is typically mild and self-limited, but severe inflammation of the upper airway can result in life-threatening airway obstruction. It is most commonly caused by parainfluenza virus. Physical examination may demonstrate signs of respiratory distress, such as substernal or subcostal retractions. Laboratory studies commonly show leukocytosis, and plain x-rays may show subglottic narrowing of the airway (steeple sign). Treatment includes supplemental oxygen, nebulized racemic epinephrine, and systemic corticosteroids, especially when airway obstruction is severe. When airway obstruction is persistent and progressive or if the patient develops signs of respiratory failure and cyanosis, endotracheal intubation and mechanical ventilation may be required. Care should be taken to keep children calm to prevent laryngospasm. Bacterial tracheitis (Choice A) presents with cough, respiratory distress, and stridor, but odynophagia or dysphagia is commonly present. Children often appear acutely ill with high fevers. This patient has only a low-grade fever, making croup more likely. Bronchiolitis (Choice B) causes inflammation of the bronchioles with respiratory distress, fever, and cough. Wheezing is present on physical examination, whereas this patient has stridor rather than wheezing, making croup more likely. Epiglottitis (Choice C) has historically been caused by Haemophilus influenzae type B and presents with fever, anxiety, sore throat, respiratory distress, dysphagia, muffled voice, and inability to manage oral secretions. This patient has received all immunizations and is not experiencing dysphagia or drooling, making croup more likely. Retropharyngeal abscess (Choice E) presents with fever, sore throat, muffled voice, neck pain or stiffness, lymphadenopathy, and dysphagia. This patient has inspiratory stridor, which is more common with croup.
▶ Renal Urinary System Hypertensive Renal Disease 1 question
Source: 2A #22
An asymptomatic 42-year-old man with polycystic kidney disease comes for a routine follow-up examination. He has hypertension treated with hydrochlorothiazide. His blood pressure has been 156/94 mm Hg on two previous visits. His temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 80/min, and blood pressure is 160/96 mm Hg. The lungs are clear to auscultation. Cardiac examination shows a normal S1 and S2 with no murmurs, rubs, or gallops. His serum creatinine concentration is 1 mg/dL. Urinalysis shows 2+ protein and 5-10 RBC/hpf. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Echocardiography B) Intravenous pyelography C) Lisinopril therapy D) Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole therapy E) Cerebral arteriography
Correct Answer: C) Lisinopril therapy
The most appropriate next step in management is to add lisinopril therapy. The patient has suboptimally controlled hypertension and is at risk for cardiovascular disease, cerebral infarctions, and chronic kidney disease. In patients with underlying chronic kidney disease (e.g., polycystic kidney disease), predictors of disease progression include increased blood pressure and proteinuria. Renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system inhibitors, such as lisinopril and other ACE inhibitors, are effective first-line agents for blood pressure control and are more effective than other antihypertensive agents in decreasing proteinuria and slowing chronic kidney disease progression. Echocardiography (Choice A) is unlikely to provide further diagnostic or therapeutic information at this time. The patient has a reassuring cardiac examination with no symptoms concerning for congestive heart failure. Optimal blood pressure control is the priority to reduce cardiovascular disease risk. Intravenous pyelography (Choice B) is not indicated in this patient with known kidney disease and suboptimally controlled hypertension. While it can evaluate ureteral course, anatomical variants, and renal collecting system abnormalities, it is not the next management step. Trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole therapy (Choice D) is not needed as the patient displays no signs or symptoms of infection. Cerebral arteriography (Choice E) should be considered in patients with polycystic kidney disease to screen for cerebral aneurysms, but adequate hypertension control must be addressed first to slow renal disease progression and reduce aneurysm rupture risk. Educational Objective: Uncontrolled hypertension is a risk factor for cardiovascular disease, cerebral infarction, and chronic kidney disease progression. Antihypertensive agents that inhibit the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system (e.g., lisinopril and ACE inhibitors) are recommended for patients with proteinuria or chronic kidney disease to slow disease progression.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Pill esophagitis 1 question
Source: 2A #25
A 77-year-old woman who is a resident of a skilled nursing care facility is brought to the physician because of a 1-week history of heartburn and difficulty swallowing. She has had no weight loss. She sustained a cerebral infarction 2 years ago. She has hypertension and hyperlipidemia. Current medications include chlorthalidone, potassium chloride, alendronate, aspirin, and lovastatin. Examination shows no abnormalities except for residual right-sided weakness. Upper endoscopy shows two shallow ulcers in the distal esophagus. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Barrett esophagus B) Drug-induced esophagitis C) Esophageal cancer D) Herpes simplex esophagitis E) Reflux esophagitis
Correct Answer: B) Drug-induced esophagitis
Drug-induced esophagitis can result from a variety of medications, including aspirin, bisphosphonates (e.g., alendronate), and tetracycline antibiotics. It is thought to be primarily a result of direct irritation of the esophageal mucosa. Drug-induced esophagitis is more common in elderly patients because of a variety of factors including polypharmacy, increased gastrointestinal transit time, and decreased salivary production. Endoscopy may reveal localized ulcerations. Treatment includes discontinuation of the offending medication, and most patients experience rapid improvement in symptoms after the offending medication is stopped. In this case, the patient is taking both aspirin and alendronate, both known culprits for drug-induced esophagitis. Barrett esophagus (Choice A) is a state of premalignancy marked by intestinal metaplasia of the distal esophagus. Patients typically report a longstanding history of heartburn and may experience odynophagia. If untreated, Barrett esophagus may progress to esophageal cancer (Choice C), which presents with progressive dysphagia, odynophagia, and weight loss. This patient has no weight loss, making malignancy less likely. Herpes simplex esophagitis (Choice D) typically occurs in patients with a history of immunosuppression, such as patients with HIV, patients undergoing chemotherapy, or those who have undergone an organ transplantation. This patient has no immunosuppression history. Reflux esophagitis (Choice E) presents with chronic heartburn that is worsened after meals and with lying down. Patients often report a history of heartburn and may develop an associated cough, sinus pain, or halitosis. Prolonged reflux esophagitis increases the risk for developing Barrett esophagus. However, the acute presentation with shallow ulcers on endoscopy in the setting of specific medications is more consistent with drug-induced esophagitis.
▶ Skin Subcutaneous Tissue Acne Vulgaris 1 question
Source: 2A #27
A 16-year-old boy is brought to the physician because of a 1-year history of severe acne that has not responded to treatment with over-the-counter topical medications. He has no history of serious illness and takes no other medications. He works in a garage on weekends and participates on the track team at school. He occasionally drinks coffee in the morning and sodas after work. He is 178 cm (5 ft 10 in) tall and weighs 64 kg (142 lb); BMI is 20 kg/m². A photograph of the forehead shows scattered open comedones, pustules, and inflamed papules consistent with moderate inflammatory acne. Genital and pubic hair development are Tanner stage 5. In addition to oral antibiotic and topical retinoid therapy, which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation for this patient? A) Avoidance of caffeine B) Avoidance of sun exposure C) Cleansing with topical alcohol twice daily D) Low-fat diet E) Use of moisturizers three times daily
Correct Answer: B) Avoidance of sun exposure
When starting a tetracycline antibiotic for the treatment of acne, it is important to counsel patients to avoid sun exposure as even small amounts of sun exposure can cause severe phototoxicity. This patient's physical examination shows scattered open comedones, pustules, and inflamed papules consistent with moderate inflammatory acne. Oral tetracyclines, including tetracycline, doxycycline, and minocycline, may be used for moderate to severe inflammatory acne. While there is a component of bacterial overgrowth to the pathogenesis of acne, these antibiotics are used primarily for their anti-inflammatory effects. To prevent bacterial resistance, they may be used at lower doses than would be required when treating an infection. All patients prescribed oral tetracyclines should be counseled on the possibility of gastrointestinal upset, phototoxicity, and pill esophagitis. Minocycline has an additional risk for causing drug-induced lupus and pigmentary changes to the skin. Incorrect Answers: A) Neither avoidance of caffeine nor a low-fat diet have been demonstrated to improve acne vulgaris. The only dietary interventions for acne vulgaris that are supported by data are decreasing the intake of high glycemic index foods and skim milk. In a patient with moderate to severe inflammatory acne, these dietary interventions are not likely to make a clinically significant difference. C) Cleansing with topical alcohol twice daily is not advised in the care of any patient's skin. Alcohol causes drying and irritation and will worsen acne vulgaris. D) See explanation for Choice A. E) Use of moisturizers three times daily is unnecessary and may worsen this patient's acne by causing further occlusion of the pilosebaceous units. Patients can continue daily moisturizer with a noncomedogenic product, if desired, but use of such a product three times daily is excessive. Educational Objective: Oral tetracyclines, including tetracycline, doxycycline, and minocycline, may be used for moderate to severe inflammatory acne. When starting a tetracycline antibiotic, patients should be counseled to avoid sun exposure or use sunscreen if exposure cannot be avoided to prevent phototoxicity.
▶ Male Reproductive Genital Herpes 1 question
Source: 2A #28
A 32-year-old man comes to the physician because of painful blisters on his penis for 2 days. During the past 2 years, he has had similar blisters that occur approximately four times annually and last 10 days. Six months ago, a viral culture of fluid from a blister was negative. He currently takes no medications. He does not smoke or drink alcohol. He has had six lifetime female sexual partners and does not use condoms. His current sexual partner is asymptomatic. His temperature is 38.1°C (100.6°F), pulse is 80/min, respirations are 12/min, and blood pressure is 130/80 mm Hg. There is bilateral inguinal lymphadenopathy. Examination of the penis shows multiple 3-mm vesicular lesions on the shaft. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacotherapy? A) Acyclovir B) Ceftriaxone C) Doxycycline D) Ibuprofen E) Penicillin F) Prednisone
Correct Answer: A) Acyclovir
Acyclovir is the most appropriate pharmacotherapy for this patient's genital herpes infection caused by herpes simplex virus (HSV). The clinical presentation is classic for genital herpes: recurrent painful vesicles on the genitalia occurring multiple times per year, lasting approximately 10 days, with associated bilateral inguinal lymphadenopathy and fever. Herpes simplex typically begins with vesicle formation, which lyse and progress to shallow, painful ulcers with an erythematous border. Genital herpes is most often caused by HSV-2 but can also be caused by HSV-1. Following initial infection, the virus remains latent in the sacral ganglia until reactivation causes recurrent painful vesicles and punched-out erosions on the genitalia. The patient's recent false-negative viral culture result is explained by improper sampling technique—viral culture should be taken from the base of a vesicle that has just been deroofed, not from blister fluid. Treatment for herpetic infections involves drugs that inhibit viral DNA polymerase, classically guanosine analogs such as acyclovir, valacyclovir, and famciclovir. Incorrect answers: Ceftriaxone (B) and doxycycline (C) are used to treat Neisseria gonorrhoeae and Chlamydia trachomatis infections, not genital herpes. While L1-L3 serotypes of C. trachomatis cause lymphogranuloma venereum (presenting as a painless papule or ulcer), vesicles are not typical of this condition. Ibuprofen (D), a nonsteroidal anti-inflammatory medication, may help decrease pain associated with genital herpes but does not target the underlying viral infection. Penicillin (E) is the treatment of choice for syphilis, which presents as a painless chancre, not painful vesicles and ulcers. Prednisone (F) is not indicated in the treatment of uncomplicated genital herpes infections. Educational Objective: Genital herpes infections are most often caused by HSV-2 and are characterized by recurrent painful vesicles and ulcers with associated inguinal lymphadenopathy. The treatment of choice is oral acyclovir or other guanosine analogs that inhibit viral DNA polymerase.
▶ Female Reproductive Breast Bacterial Vaginosis 1 question
Source: 2A #29
A previously healthy 22-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 3-day history of malodorous vaginal discharge. She has not had fever, chills, or pain with urination. Menses occur at regular 28-day intervals. Her last menstrual period was 2 weeks ago. She uses an oral contraceptive and has not missed a dose. Her temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 78/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 130/76 mm Hg. On abdominal examination, bowel sounds are normal. Pelvic examination shows gray, thin, watery vaginal discharge but no other abnormalities. Urinalysis and a pregnancy test are negative. A photomicrograph of a Gram stain of the discharge shows clue cells. Results of cervical testing for Chlamydia trachomatis and Neisseria gonorrhoeae are pending. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Ceftriaxone therapy B) Doxycycline therapy C) Fluconazole therapy D) Metronidazole therapy E) No treatment is indicated until cervical testing results are received
Correct Answer: D) Metronidazole therapy
Bacterial vaginosis is a common gynecologic condition characterized by a shift of vaginal flora and overgrowth of Gardnerella vaginalis, a gram-variable, facultative anaerobe. It presents with gray, thin, malodorous vaginal discharge, vaginal pH greater than 4.5, and a fishy odor upon KOH testing. The identification of clue cells on wet mount preparation is diagnostic, as seen in this patient. Metronidazole is the most appropriate pharmacotherapy. Bacterial vaginosis is not considered a sexually transmitted infection, and treatment of asymptomatic sexual partners is not recommended. Choice A (Ceftriaxone) is used to treat Neisseria gonorrhoeae, a sexually transmitted infection presenting with vaginal pruritus and mucopurulent discharge, not the gray watery discharge seen here. Choice B (Doxycycline) treats Chlamydia trachomatis, a sexually transmitted infection, which is unlikely given the clinical presentation and diagnostic findings of bacterial vaginosis. Choice C (Fluconazole) treats vaginal candidiasis, which presents with erythema, pruritus, and thick white discharge with hyphae on wet mount, not clue cells. Choice E is incorrect because this patient meets diagnostic criteria for bacterial vaginosis and should be treated immediately rather than waiting for pending test results.
▶ Endocrine System Diabetic neuropathy 1 question
Source: 2A #30
An 82-year-old man comes to the physician for a follow-up examination. He has a 2-year history of a painful tingling sensation in his feet that did not respond to trials of gabapentin and carbamazepine. He has a 20-year history of type 2 diabetes mellitus treated with glyburide. Examination today shows normal muscle strength. Sensation to light touch is decreased to the midcalves bilaterally. Which of the following is the most appropriate alternative pharmacotherapy for this patient? A) Fluoxetine B) Morphine C) Nortriptyline D) Phenelzine E) Trazodone
Correct Answer: C) Nortriptyline
Nortriptyline, a tricyclic antidepressant (TCA) medication, is the best alternative therapy for this patient with diabetic neuropathy that is refractory to gabapentin and carbamazepine. Diabetic neuropathy typically develops in patients with long-standing and poorly controlled diabetes mellitus. The location of neuropathy tends to be in a stocking-glove distribution and gradually ascends from the feet to more proximal portions of the leg. Symptoms can range from loss of sensation to dysesthesia. Early detection is essential as improved glycemic control can halt or slow disease progression. Testing is completed in an ambulatory setting using a monofilament applied with light pressure to five separate points on the plantar surface of the foot. Loss of ability to detect one or more pressure points should prompt further evaluation and tighter glycemic control. First-line treatment for painful neuropathy includes gabapentin, as it is generally well tolerated, but some patients require additional or alternative medications for refractory symptoms. In conjunction with neuropathy treatment, patients should undergo frequent foot examinations to detect ulcers, cuts, or fungal growth that may predispose to diabetic foot infection. Fluoxetine (Choice A) is a selective serotonin reuptake inhibitor with no role in treating diabetic neuropathy; it is used primarily for major depressive disorder and psychiatric disorders. Morphine (Choice B) is an opiate medication less likely to be effective for long-term diabetic neuropathy treatment and is associated with undesirable adverse effects including dependence risk. It does not generally modulate neuropathic pain though may provide temporary relief. Additionally, in elderly patients, opiate analgesics increase the risk for falls and delirium, making them medications to avoid in geriatric populations. Phenelzine (Choice D) is a monoamine oxidase inhibitor now rarely used to treat depression with no role in diabetic neuropathy treatment. It has several undesirable adverse effects and drug interactions. Trazodone (Choice E) is a serotonin reuptake inhibitor used on-label for depression and off-label for anxiety and insomnia, but is not used to treat diabetic neuropathy. Educational Objective: Diabetic neuropathy occurs in patients with long-standing, poorly controlled diabetes mellitus and frequently presents with loss of sensation or dysesthesia in a stocking-glove distribution. Screening for neuropathy is performed yearly using a monofilament test. First-line treatment is usually with gabapentin, although refractory cases may require TCA medications such as nortriptyline.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Migraine Headache 1 question
Source: 2A #31
A 42-year-old woman comes to the physician because of recent high blood pressure readings. During the past month, her blood pressure was 150/94 mm Hg on two occasions at health fairs. She has a history of migraines that have occurred twice weekly during the past 6 months. She has been taking ibuprofen four to six times weekly for her migraines. She has no other history of serious illness and takes no other medications. Her pulse is 88/min, and blood pressure is 152/92 mm Hg. The remainder of the examination shows no abnormalities. In addition to discontinuing ibuprofen therapy, administration of which of the following is the most appropriate next step in pharmacotherapy? A) ACE inhibitor B) α-Adrenergic blocking agent C) β-Adrenergic blocking agent D) Angiotensin-receptor blocking agent E) Daily aspirin
Correct Answer: C) β-Adrenergic blocking agent
Migraine is a common condition presenting with recurrent primary headaches that are typically severe, unilateral, throbbing, and associated with nausea, vomiting, photophobia, and phonophobia. Migraines are postulated to arise from deficient serotonin inhibition of descending pain pathways. For patients with disabling or frequent migraines, prophylactic treatment is recommended. β-Adrenergic blockers are among the commonly used, first-line agents for migraine prophylaxis. The mechanism by which they impart migraine prevention is poorly understood and is not believed to be directly related to their effect on blood pressure. Other options for migraine prophylaxis include antidepressants (tricyclic antidepressants and serotonin-norepinephrine reuptake inhibitors) and anticonvulsants. In this patient, blood pressure is potentially worsened as a result of the renovascular effect of ibuprofen, and a β-adrenergic blocker is a suitable choice to both treat hypertension and prevent migraines. ACE inhibitors and angiotensin-receptor blocking agents are antihypertensive agents that lack robust data demonstrating effectiveness for migraine prophylaxis and are not commonly used for migraine prevention. α-Adrenergic blocking agents are used for hypertension and benign prostatic hyperplasia but do not play a significant role in migraine prophylaxis. Daily aspirin is used for primary or secondary prophylaxis of coronary artery disease, cerebrovascular disease, and peripheral arterial disease but does not have a recognized role in migraine prevention. Educational Objective: β-Adrenergic blockers are among the commonly used agents for migraine prophylaxis. In a patient with concomitant hypertension, they may be an appropriate choice in treating both conditions.
▶ Respiratory System Mycoplasma pneumoniae Pneumonia 1 question
Source: 2A #32
A previously healthy 30-year-old woman comes to the physician because of fever, nonproductive cough, and left-sided chest pain for 5 days; her symptoms started during a hiking trip with several friends in late August. One of these friends has similar symptoms. Mild diffuse crackles are heard. Which of the following is the most likely causal organism? A) Borrelia burgdorferi B) Coxsackievirus C) Influenza virus D) Mycoplasma pneumoniae E) Streptococcus pneumoniae
Correct Answer: D) Mycoplasma pneumoniae
Mycoplasma pneumoniae is the most likely causal organism in this case. The patient presents with fever, nonproductive cough, and chest pain with diffuse crackles on examination, consistent with pneumonia. Pneumonia can be caused by viral pathogens (influenza, SARS-CoV-2) or bacterial pathogens. Common bacterial causes include Streptococcus pneumoniae, Haemophilus influenzae, Chlamydophila pneumoniae, and Mycoplasma pneumoniae. Infections with M. pneumoniae are typically less severe than S. pneumoniae infections and are colloquially referred to as 'walking pneumonia.' A chest x-ray should be obtained to evaluate for pulmonary infiltrates, and empiric antibiotic therapy is typically initiated. Borrelia burgdorferi (Choice A) causes Lyme disease, which presents with erythema migrans rash, not pneumonia. While the patient was hiking, Lyme disease is endemic only to certain parts of the United States. Coxsackievirus (Choice B) is an enterovirus that can cause various manifestations including myocarditis, encephalitis, and hand-foot-and-mouth disease. While pneumonia may occur, it is more common in children. Influenza virus (Choice C) is a common cause of viral pneumonia, but typically during winter months. Summer presentation would be atypical for influenza infection. Streptococcus pneumoniae (Choice E) is a common bacterial cause of pneumonia but typically presents with focal lung findings indicating lobar pneumonia and more severe symptoms than M. pneumoniae infection. Educational Objective: Pneumonia is most commonly viral or bacterial in origin. Common bacterial pathogens include S. pneumoniae, C. pneumoniae, H. influenzae, and M. pneumoniae. Patients with Mycoplasma infections tend to be less ill than those with S. pneumoniae, leading to the term 'walking pneumonia.'
▶ Respiratory System Coxsackievirus Infection 1 question
Source: 2A #34
A 37-year-old man comes to the physician because of intermittent chest pain over the past 2 days. Four days ago, he had a sore throat, headache, and malaise. Two days ago, he developed fever and a sharp stabbing pain in the right lateral chest. The pain resolved spontaneously after 1 hour but has recurred six times since then. There is no pain with exertion. He appears well. His temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 80/min, and blood pressure is 120/70 mm Hg. The lungs are clear to auscultation. Cardiac examination shows a normal S1 and S2 with no murmurs, rubs, or gallops. Laboratory studies show: Hematocrit 45%, Leukocyte count 3700/mm³, Platelet count 250,000/mm³, Serum creatine kinase 500 U/L. An x-ray of the chest and an ECG show no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Empyema B) Pericarditis C) Pleurodynia D) Pneumothorax E) Pulmonary embolism F) Systemic lupus erythematosus
Correct Answer: C) Pleurodynia
Pleurodynia, also known as Bornholm disease, is an acute viral illness often caused by coxsackievirus group B, among other viruses. It presents with fever, headache, and characteristic paroxysmal spasms of the muscles of the chest wall with pleuritic chest pain. Coxsackievirus is an RNA enterovirus transmitted via oral secretions or feces. Pleurodynia tends to occur in epidemic outbreaks during summer months. Patients may present with leukocytosis or increased serum creatine kinase concentrations caused by muscle inflammation. The disease is usually self-limiting and resolves over several days to a week. This patient's presentation is classic: prodromal viral symptoms (sore throat, headache, malaise) followed by fever and characteristic paroxysmal, episodic sharp chest pain that resolves spontaneously and recurs multiple times, with elevated creatine kinase reflecting muscle inflammation. Empyema (Choice A) is an accumulation of pus within the pleural space presenting with fever, progressive cough, and shortness of breath, often as a complication of pneumonia. It presents with pleuritic chest pain that is not paroxysmal or episodic. Pericarditis (Choice B) presents with substernal pleuritic chest pain that worsens when lying down and improves with leaning forward, along with a diastolic friction rub and increased inflammatory markers such as erythrocyte sedimentation rate and C-reactive protein. It can occur in patients with inflammatory, infectious, or malignant conditions such as viral infection, systemic lupus erythematosus, tuberculosis, and lymphoma, and can present as a complication of myocardial infarction or cardiac surgery. This patient lacks the positional nature of pain and friction rub. Pneumothorax (Choice D) presents with pleuritic chest pain and tachypnea with diminished breath sounds and hyperresonance to percussion on the affected side. This patient has clear lungs and normal vital signs. Pulmonary embolism (Choice E) presents with pleuritic chest pain, dyspnea, and hemoptysis if infarcted, often in the setting of immobilization, hypercoagulability, and possible known deep venous thrombosis. Vital signs may show tachycardia, tachypnea, hypoxia, and hypotension if sufficiently large. This patient has normal vital signs and no risk factors. Systemic lupus erythematosus (Choice F) is an autoimmune disease that may cause pleuritic chest pain but is typically accompanied by other suggestive signs and symptoms such as fever, rash, photosensitivity, kidney disease, and nonerosive arthritis. This patient lacks these systemic features. Educational Objective: Pleurodynia, also known as Bornholm disease, is an acute viral illness that presents with fever, headache, and characteristic paroxysmal spasms of the muscles of the chest wall with pleuritic chest pain, elevated creatine kinase, and is self-limiting.
▶ Multisystem Processes Disorders Syncope 1 question
Source: 2A #36
An 11-year-old boy is brought to the camp infirmary by his cabinmates 1 hour after collapsing. The boys had been playing a game that involved standing, breathing deeply and rapidly for 1 minute, and then expiring against a closed mouth. The patient collapsed and struck his head on the edge of his bed. He did not open his eyes or move for several minutes but is now alert and fully oriented. There is a 1 x 1-cm ecchymosis on the occipital scalp. His pupils react to light. Reflexes are symmetric; gait and muscle strength are normal. Which of the following is the most likely cause of the loss of consciousness? A) Akinetic seizure B) Cardiac arrhythmia C) Cerebral hypoperfusion D) Epidural hematoma E) Hypercapnia
Correct Answer: C) Cerebral hypoperfusion
The patient likely experienced a syncopal episode secondary to cerebral hypoperfusion. Serum pH and pCO2 are primary regulators of cerebral blood flow. Acute hypocapnia induced by breathing deeply and rapidly causes respiratory alkalosis, resulting in cerebral vasoconstriction and decreased cerebral blood flow. Additionally, forced expiration against a closed glottis (Valsalva maneuver) rapidly increases intrathoracic pressure, decreasing venous return, preload, and cardiac output, which results in an abrupt drop in mean arterial pressure. The combination of these two factors significantly reduces cerebral blood flow and causes syncope. In the absence of underlying pathology, resumption of normal respiratory drive and a supine position quickly correct the perfusion deficit and restore consciousness. Akinetic seizure (Choice A) is unlikely without a postictal state and clear precipitating activity. Absence seizures in children are characterized by staring spells without obvious loss of consciousness or postural tone. Cardiac arrhythmia (Choice B) is unlikely in an otherwise healthy 11-year-old. Further cardiac monitoring should be considered only with a family history of arrhythmias, sudden cardiac death, or familial cardiomyopathy. The proximate cause is much more likely related to induced cerebral hypoperfusion from respiratory alkalosis and Valsalva maneuver. Epidural hematoma (Choice D) did not cause the loss of consciousness. The occipital ecchymosis resulted from trauma sustained during the syncopal episode, not before. Hypercapnia (Choice E) would not be expected in a patient engaging in voluntary hyperventilation. Acute hypercapnia typically presents with dyspnea, fatigue, confusion, and somnolence. Educational Objective: Cerebral blood flow is primarily regulated by blood pH and pCO2 concentrations. Increased pH and/or decreased pCO2 results in cerebral vasoconstriction and decreased cerebral blood flow. Combining Valsalva maneuvers with respiratory alkalosis can result in acute decreases in cerebral blood flow leading to syncope.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Hepatitis C 1 question
Source: 2A #37
A 27-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 1-week history of yellow eyes and dark urine. He has a history of intravenous drug use. Laboratory studies 6 months ago showed a positive hepatitis A antibody assay, a negative hepatitis B surface antigen assay, and a positive hepatitis B surface antibody assay. Examination today shows jaundice and hepatomegaly. Laboratory studies are consistent with hepatitis. Which of the following is the most likely cause of his hepatitis? A) Delta virus infection B) Hepatitis C infection C) Reactivation of hepatitis A D) Reinfection with hepatitis A E) Reinfection with hepatitis B
Correct Answer: B) Hepatitis C infection
Hepatitis C virus (HCV) infection is the most likely cause of this patient's hepatitis. HCV is transmitted through exposure to infected blood and can be acquired through intravenous drug use, sexual intercourse (although less common), and before universal screening, from blood transfusions. HCV is an RNA virus that establishes residence in hepatocytes. Acute infection typically causes an increase in liver-associated enzymes and occasionally jaundice. The acute phase is self-limited and may lead to chronic infection, which can progress to cirrhosis in some patients. The diagnosis should be suspected in individuals with laboratory and physical examination evidence of hepatitis and a history of intravenous drug use. Diagnosis is made using a two-step process: HCV antibody is tested first, and if positive, HCV viral load is measured. Patients with HCV infection have both positive antibody and high viral load. Most instances of HCV infection are now curable with newer medications such as ledipasvir and sofosbuvir for 8 to 24 weeks. Delta virus infection (Choice A) requires concurrent HBV infection for full viral assembly and secretion. This patient does not have HBV infection (negative surface antigen), making hepatitis D unlikely. Reactivation of hepatitis A (Choice C) does not occur. Most patients with hepatitis A recover spontaneously with lifelong immunity. Reinfection with hepatitis A (Choice D) is possible but unlikely. The antibody assay remains positive for long periods following initial infection, making it unhelpful for diagnosis. Reinfection with hepatitis B (Choice E) is incorrect. The patient's positive hepatitis B surface antibody and negative surface antigen indicate prior immunization, not active infection. Educational Objective: HCV is an RNA virus acquired through exposure to contaminated blood, particularly in intravenous drug users who utilize nonsterile needles or share needles. HCV can cause self-limited acute infection but may lead to chronic infection, often asymptomatic. HCV can usually be cured with combination antiretroviral therapy.
▶ Respiratory System Pulmonary Tuberculosis 1 question
Source: 2A #39
A previously healthy 37-year-old woman who works as a prison guard comes to the physician because of a 2-month history of low-grade fever, fatigue, and a cough productive of scanty white sputum that is occasionally blood-streaked. She has had no shortness of breath. She has smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 20 years and drinks four alcoholic beverages daily. Her temperature is 37.6°C (99.7°F), pulse is 88/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 95/68 mm Hg. Examination shows rhonchi that clear with coughing; no crackles are heard. Laboratory studies show hemoglobin 9.5 g/dL, serum bilirubin 1.2 mg/dL, alkaline phosphatase 80 U/L, AST 75 U/L, and ALT 60 U/L. A chest x-ray shows a normal cardiac silhouette and a right upper lung field infiltrate; there are no pleural effusions. Which of the following is the most likely cause of the cough? A) Bronchitis B) Environmental irritants C) Lung cancer D) Pneumonia E) Sarcoidosis F) Tuberculosis
Correct Answer: F) Tuberculosis
This patient's presentation with fever, fatigue, productive cough with hemoptysis, and upper lung field infiltrate is classic for pulmonary tuberculosis. She has multiple risk factors including alcohol use disorder and occupational exposure to crowded conditions in a prison setting. Secondary reactivation tuberculosis typically results in apical pulmonary infiltrates with fibrocaseous cavitary lesions and classically presents with fever, night sweats, weight loss, and hemoptysis. The patient has developed anemia of chronic disease (hemoglobin 9.5 g/dL), which is consistent with tuberculosis. Diagnosis is confirmed with culture samples from sputum or bronchoalveolar lavage. Purified protein derivative testing and serum interferon gamma release assays are screening methods but are not adequate for confirming diagnosis. Treatment requires combination antimicrobial therapy including rifampin, isoniazid, pyrazinamide, and ethambutol, along with initial respiratory isolation. Incorrect answers: Bronchitis (Choice A) presents with productive cough and obstructive lung disease signs but lacks fever and anemia of chronic disease. Environmental irritants (Choice B) exacerbate asthma, which does not typically present with fever or anemia. Lung cancer (Choice C) presents as chronic consolidation or nodular opacity and may cause anemia of chronic disease but not fever. Pneumonia (Choice D) presents acutely with crackles and patchy or lobar infiltrates, whereas this patient's chronic course and prison exposure history are more concerning for tuberculosis. Sarcoidosis (Choice E) is a noncaseating granulomatous disease presenting with bilateral hilar lymphadenopathy and upper-lobe-predominant reticular opacities, not upper lobe infiltrates with hemoptysis. Educational Objective: Reactivation tuberculosis presents with fever, night sweats, weight loss, hemoptysis, and apical infiltrates or cavitary lesions visible on chest x-ray. Patients may develop anemia of chronic disease. Risk factors include alcohol use disorder, homelessness, endemic area origin, and living or working in close quarters such as prisons or nursing facilities.
▶ Female Reproductive Breast Breast Cyst, Solitary 1 question
Source: 2A #40
An otherwise healthy 9-year-old girl is brought to the physician by her mother because she noticed a lump in her daughter's left breast 10 days ago. The mother has a history of breast cancer. The patient is at the 70th percentile for height and 40th percentile for weight. Breast development is Tanner stage 2, and pubic hair development is Tanner stage 1. There is no axillary lymphadenopathy. Examination shows a 2 x 2.5-cm slightly raised mass just beneath the left areola. The mass is tender and slightly granular; there is no erythema. The right nipple is slightly tender but otherwise normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Follow-up examination in 6 months B) Mammography C) Ultrasound-directed biopsy of the mass D) Excision of the mass E) Antibiotic therapy
Correct Answer: A) Follow-up examination in 6 months
The correct answer is A. Follow-up examination in 6 months is appropriate for this young girl who has just begun thelarche (breast development). Puberty in girls typically follows a predictable course of thelarche to pubarche (pubic hair) to a growth spurt and finally menarche. Mild unilateral or bilateral breast swelling can be normal in early puberty because of increased estrogen production and growth of mammary tissue. The patient's mother can be reassured that this is likely a normal finding of beginning puberty rather than a sign of malignancy. However, the patient should be examined again in 6 months to ensure that no concerning features such as hardening of the mass or lymphadenopathy have developed. Mammography (Choice B) is the first step in evaluating breast masses in patients over age 35. In younger patients, the typical density of breast tissue makes mammography difficult to interpret accurately. Imaging studies are not necessary at this time because this patient is undergoing thelarche; observation is more appropriate. Ultrasound-directed biopsy of the mass (Choice C) is not necessary given that this patient's breast mass is consistent with a normal variant of thelarche and can be monitored. Ultrasound-directed biopsy would be appropriate for a suspicious breast mass to obtain a definitive pathologic diagnosis. Excision of the mass (Choice D) is not necessary in this case. This invasive procedure carries potential for cosmetic disfigurement and should be reserved for breast masses with a confirmed pathologic diagnosis and indication for removal. Antibiotic therapy (Choice E) is unnecessary as the patient's mass is not caused by bacterial infection. Mastitis, which occurs in breastfeeding women when a milk duct becomes clogged and subsequently infected, requires antibiotic therapy. Findings of infection including warmth, erythema, and fluctuance are absent. Educational Objective: In girls, puberty follows a predictable course of thelarche, pubarche, a growth spurt, and menarche. During thelarche, enlargement of breast tissue occurs, which can sometimes initially present as a subareoler mass.
▶ Pregnancy Childbirth Puerperium Asymptomatic urinary tract infection in pregnancy 1 question
Source: 2A #42
A 16-year-old girl comes to the physician because of a 2-day history of urinary frequency and urgency and pain with urination. Over the past 2 months, she has had two urinary tract infections treated with trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole. Menses occur at regular intervals, and she frequently uses tampons. She became sexually active with one partner 3 months ago. She uses depot medroxyprogesterone for contraception. She and her partner use condoms consistently, and they both have tested negative for sexually transmitted diseases. Examination shows bilateral pelvic tenderness to palpation and no costovertebral angle tenderness. After treatment for the current infection, which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation to prevent future urinary tract infections in this patient? A) Delay postcoital urination B) Discontinue tampon use C) Postcoital single-dose nitrofurantoin therapy D) Switch from depot medroxyprogesterone to a diaphragm E) Switch from depot medroxyprogesterone to an oral contraceptive
Correct Answer: C) Postcoital single-dose nitrofurantoin therapy
Lower urinary tract infections (UTIs) present with dysuria, frequency, urgency, and suprapubic discomfort. They occur more frequently in women than in men because of a shorter urethra and favorable regional environment for bacterial growth. Risk factors include the presence of a urinary catheter, anatomic genitourinary defects, diabetes mellitus, pregnancy, and frequent or recent sexual intercourse. Urinalysis typically shows bacteriuria, white blood cells, positive leukocyte esterase, and positive nitrites depending on the infectious organism. Treatment of uncomplicated UTIs typically consists of outpatient antibiotic management with trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole, nitrofurantoin, or cephalexin. Preventive measures for recurrent UTIs include voiding immediately after coitus, oral hydration, and improvement of feminine hygiene practices to reduce the introduction of bacteria to the urethra. If these preventive measures fail, antibiotic prophylaxis with daily trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole or postcoital single-dose nitrofurantoin is appropriate. Choice A is incorrect because delaying postcoital urination could allow bacteria to flourish in the genitourinary tract and increase the risk for infection; immediately voiding after coitus decreases the risk for developing a UTI. Choice B is incorrect because discontinuing tampon use would not decrease this patient's risk for developing a UTI; excessive tampon use is more likely to lead to toxic shock syndrome. Choices D and E are incorrect because switching contraceptive methods would not decrease this patient's risk for developing a UTI and may lead to unwanted adverse effects such as breakthrough bleeding. The educational objective is that UTIs present commonly in women with dysuria, frequency, urgency, and suprapubic discomfort, and preventive measures include voiding immediately after coitus, oral hydration, and improvement in hygiene practices, along with potential prophylactic antibiotics if these more conservative measures are ineffective.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Lactose intolerance 1 question
Source: 2A #45
A 9-month-old girl is brought to the physician for a well-child examination. Two weeks ago, she had viral gastroenteritis; her symptoms lasted for 10 days and included low-grade fever, vomiting, and diarrhea. Her mother recently resumed feeding her daughter the cow milk-based formula she had been drinking before the illness. The infant's stools have continued to be watery and she has had "a lot of gas." She is otherwise healthy, and her immunizations are up-to-date. Vital signs are within normal limits. Physical examination shows no abnormalities except for mild abdominal distention with increased bowel sounds. There are no palpable masses or organomegaly. Which of the following is the most likely explanation for this patient's current gastrointestinal symptoms? A) Bacterial enteritis B) Celiac disease C) Cow milk allergy D) Lactose intolerance E) Persistent viral gastroenteritis
Correct Answer: D) Lactose intolerance
Lactase catalyzes the breakdown of lactose (disaccharide) to galactose and glucose (monosaccharides) at the brush border of enterocytes. Deficiency of lactase may be induced by several mechanisms. Primary lactase deficiency can occur after early childhood in some genetically predisposed children. Additionally, gastroenteritis or autoimmune disease may damage the intestinal brush border and lead to effective lactase deficiency. In this case, the recent viral gastroenteritis damaged the brush border, resulting in secondary lactase deficiency. Individuals with lactose intolerance typically present with abdominal cramping, bloating, flatulence, and diarrhea after the ingestion of lactose-containing foods—exactly matching this patient's presentation of watery stools and gas following resumption of cow milk-based formula. Bacterial enteritis (Choice A) such as Salmonella classically presents with mucosal inflammation and invasion, leading to nausea, vomiting, cramping, and diarrhea with associated fever and bloody stools if severe. Symptoms are typically self-limited with resolution within 48-72 hours, which does not fit this patient's ongoing symptoms two weeks after the initial illness. Celiac disease (Choice B) is an immunologic intolerance to the protein gliadin that presents with abdominal discomfort, bloating, nausea, and diarrhea following gluten exposure, often with weight loss and dermatitis herpetiformis. This patient has no history of gluten exposure and no systemic symptoms. Cow milk allergy (Choice C) involves acute IgE-mediated allergic responses to milk allergens, typically occurring within minutes to hours of ingestion. Signs and symptoms include abdominal pain, nausea, vomiting, diarrhea, dysphagia, shortness of breath, wheezing, urticaria, skin flushing, and palpitations. This patient's isolated diarrhea and bloating specifically after milk ingestion is more consistent with lactose intolerance than acute allergic reaction. Additionally, milk protein allergy commonly causes bloody stools, which this patient does not have. Persistent viral gastroenteritis (Choice E) is unlikely as it would present with nausea, vomiting, and diarrhea but would not occur specifically following milk ingestion as in this patient.
▶ Multisystem Processes Disorders Obesity 1 question
Source: 2A #46
A 47-year-old woman comes to the physician to discuss weight loss. She has a 5-year history of hypertension and was recently diagnosed with type 2 diabetes mellitus. She has been overweight her entire life and gained approximately 9 kg (20 lb) after the birth of each of her three children. She has tried over-the-counter medications for weight loss and lost 18 kg (40 lb) on commercial diets but has been unable to keep the weight off. She is not following any particular diet at this time but she walks for 30 minutes three times weekly. Current medications are hydrochlorothiazide and metformin. Her father died of a myocardial infarction at the age of 52 years. She does not smoke cigarettes. She is 165 cm (5 ft 5 in) tall and weighs 104 kg (230 lb); BMI is 38 kg/m². Her temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), pulse is 84/min, respirations are 12/min, and blood pressure is 140/80 mm Hg. Examination shows no other abnormalities. Serum studies show: Glucose 140 mg/dL, Cholesterol total 225 mg/dL, HDL-cholesterol 50 mg/dL, LDL-cholesterol 120 mg/dL, Triglycerides 280 mg/dL. A complete blood count and serum electrolyte concentrations are within the reference range. Liver and thyroid function tests show no abnormalities. Which of the following is most likely to be effective in achieving weight loss in this patient? A) Exercise program B) Gastric bypass C) Low-carbohydrate diet D) Low-fat diet E) Orlistat therapy
Correct Answer: B) Gastric bypass
Gastric bypass is the most effective intervention for this patient's weight loss. She has a BMI of 38 kg/m² with three obesity-related complications (hypertension, type 2 diabetes mellitus, and hyperlipidemia), meeting criteria for bariatric surgery: BMI >35 kg/m² with at least one obesity-related health complication, or BMI >30 kg/m² with uncontrolled diabetes mellitus or metabolic syndrome. Roux-en-Y gastric bypass is commonly used and results in dramatic weight reduction and improvement of health status. All patients require psychological and nutritional counseling prior to surgery and must adhere to a strict diet before and after surgery. Yearly assessment for nutritional deficiencies (especially vitamin B12) is necessary following surgery due to altered anatomy and abnormal absorption. Exercise (Choice A) helps reduce cardiovascular risk and maintain weight loss but infrequently produces meaningful weight loss alone. Low-carbohydrate (Choice C) and low-fat (Choice D) diets can be effective, but this patient has previously tried strict dieting and regained all weight, indicating she requires a more definitive intervention. Orlistat (Choice E) interferes with dietary fat absorption but is poorly tolerated due to gastrointestinal adverse effects and is less effective for patients with obesity-related complications who meet bariatric surgery criteria. Educational Objective: Patients with BMI >40 kg/m², BMI >35 kg/m² with at least one obesity-related complication, or BMI >30 kg/m² with uncontrolled diabetes mellitus or metabolic syndrome are candidates for gastric bypass, which often results in dramatic weight reduction and health improvement with strict dietary adherence and yearly monitoring for nutritional deficiencies.
▶ Cardiovascular System Calcium Channel Blockers Cardiovascular Adverse Effects 1 question
Source: 2A #47
An asymptomatic 67-year-old woman comes to the physician for a follow-up visit. She has a 10-year history of type 2 diabetes mellitus and a 3-year history of hypertension. Her medications are glyburide, metoprolol, amlodipine, and fluoxetine. Her blood pressure is 136/86 mm Hg. The lungs are clear to auscultation. S1 and S2 are normal. The point of maximal impulse is at the fifth intercostal space at the midclavicular line. Examination of the lower extremities shows 2+ edema from the mid calf to the foot bilaterally. Sensation to pinprick and vibration is decreased over the feet. Laboratory studies show serum urea nitrogen 8 mg/dL, creatinine 0.8 mg/dL, urine glucose 1+, and urine protein trace. Which of the following is the most likely cause of this patient's edema? A) Adverse effect of medication B) Congestive heart failure C) Deep venous thrombosis D) Lymphatic obstruction E) Nephrotic syndrome
Correct Answer: A) Adverse effect of medication
Amlodipine is a dihydropyridine calcium channel blocker that acts as an arterial vasodilator to decrease mean arterial pressure and is used in the management of hypertension. A common adverse effect of calcium channel blockers includes peripheral edema caused by the redistribution of intravascular fluid to the interstitial space. Typically, patients are euvolemic, and bilateral lower extremity edema is not caused by increased fluid retention or cardiac, hepatic, renal, or lymphatic dysfunction. Diuretics are unlikely to help patients with calcium channel blocker-induced lower extremity edema. Patients with this adverse effect are managed with a reduction in dose or switching to a different antihypertensive medication. Congestive heart failure (Choice B) can cause lower extremity edema from decreased cardiac output, but this patient has no history of congestive heart failure, myocardial infarction, myocarditis, or other causes of decreased cardiac output. Deep venous thrombosis (Choice C) can cause lower extremity swelling, erythema, and pain, but is typically unilateral. Bilateral deep venous thrombosis is less common, and this patient's painless bilateral lower extremity edema is more likely caused by a medication adverse effect. Lymphatic obstruction (Choice D) causes lymphedema as a result of obstruction or excision of lymphatic channels, such as in cases of lymphoma, radiation, invasive parasitiasis, or after a lymph node dissection. It is typically localized to a single upper, single lower, or bilateral lower extremities and is an unlikely cause of this patient's lower extremity edema. Nephrotic syndrome (Choice E) can cause fluid retention and lower extremity edema. However, nephrotic syndrome is characterized by significant proteinuria, which is not seen in this patient (only trace protein). Increased creatinine would also generally be expected. Educational Objective: A common adverse effect of calcium channel blockers is bilateral lower extremity edema caused by the redistribution of fluid into the interstitial space. Patients with this adverse effect are managed with a reduction in dose or switching to a different antihypertensive medication.
▶ Social Sciences Patient confidentiality 1 question
Source: 2A #48
The mother of a 16-year-old boy telephones the physician to request drug screening during his annual examination. She suspects that he has been using drugs because his grades recently dropped, and she has heard rumors that some of his friends are using drugs. She says that she has not discussed her request with her son and asks the physician to perform the drug screening without her son's knowledge. Which of the following is the most appropriate course of action? A) Agree to testing to abide by the mother's wishes B) Agree to testing after she faxes written documentation of her request to the office C) Agree to testing but inform her that telling her the results would violate the physician-patient relationship D) Do not agree to testing because her son is an emancipated minor and she cannot legally make such a request E) Do not agree to testing unless her son agrees to the drug testing
Correct Answer: E) Do not agree to testing unless her son agrees to the drug testing
It is unethical to test any patient for drugs unknowingly, particularly an adolescent at the request of a parent. The physician should not agree to perform testing unless the patient assents to it. The patient must be informed if drug screening will be performed. Instead, the physician should encourage the patient's mother to discuss her son's school performance directly with him and may open a discussion on substance abuse with the patient during their upcoming visit by inquiring about his attitudes toward drug use. If the patient does endorse substance abuse, disclosing this to his mother is still not appropriate as the physician is obligated to protect the patient's confidentiality, even though he is an adolescent. Exceptions to adolescent confidentiality are permitted only in situations of child abuse, suicidality, homicidal ideation, certain cases related to sexually transmitted infections, mental health care, and in some states when a patient consents to an abortion. Discussing the patient's substance use habits with his parents without the patient's knowledge is not appropriate. Each state has varying statutes regarding confidentiality; physicians should be familiar with the statutes in their areas. Choice A is incorrect because the physician should not agree to testing simply to abide by the mother's wishes. Choice B is incorrect because written documentation does not change the ethical obligation to obtain patient assent. Choice C is incorrect because the physician should not agree to testing at all without the patient's knowledge, regardless of whether results would be disclosed. Choice D is incorrect because there is no indication this patient is an emancipated minor (one who has legally attained adulthood through marriage, military service, living independently, or managing their own finances). Whether emancipated or not, if drug screening is warranted, the patient should be made aware and allowed to assent.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Cubital Tunnel Syndrome 1 question
Source: 4A #1
A 16-year-old boy presents with sensory deficits over the ring and small fingers. He works part-time bagging groceries and is a pitcher on his high school baseball team. Physical examination reveals sensory loss over the volar aspects of the ring and small fingers, with otherwise normal findings. What is the most likely cause of this patient's symptoms? A) Occupational tasks involving repetitive grip B) Osteoarthritis C) Repetitive pitching D) Synovial crystal deposition E) Idiopathic cause
Correct Answer: C) Repetitive pitching
Ulnar tunnel syndrome (cubital tunnel syndrome) is a neuropathy of the ulnar nerve occurring at the elbow level. The ulnar nerve innervates intrinsic hand muscles (lumbricals, interossei, hypothenar, palmaris brevis) and forearm muscles (flexor carpi ulnaris, ulnar portion of flexor digitorum profundus), and provides sensation to the volar aspects of the ring and small fingers. Common risk factors include repetitive motions such as pronation/supination of the forearm, tasks requiring repetitive grip, and repetitive throwing. Patients most commonly present with sensory symptoms; more severe cases present with intrinsic hand muscle weakness or claw hand deformity. In this 16-year-old pitcher, repetitive pitching is the most likely cause rather than his part-time grocery bagging job. Diagnosis is confirmed with electromyography and nerve conduction studies. Treatment is usually conservative, with surgery reserved for refractory cases. Choice A (occupational tasks) is less likely given the part-time nature of his job compared to his regular pitching activities. Choice B (osteoarthritis) is a degenerative condition more common in adults and less likely in a healthy 16-year-old.
