Choice D (synovial crystal deposition/gout) typically presents with inflammatory arthropathy following purine-rich meals or alcohol and lacks the localized sensory findings seen here. Choice E (idiopathic/juvenile idiopathic arthritis) would present with joint pain, inflammation, and extra-articular manifestations like uveitis, lymphadenopathy, and rash, which are absent in this patient.
▶ Social Sciences Medication Errors 1 question
Source: 4A #2
A boy undergoes operative repair of a humerus fracture. Postoperatively, his parents notice that he is unresponsive and barely breathing and notify the nursing staff. The patient has no significant past medical history. His only medication is intravenous morphine. On examination, he is unconscious. His temperature is 37°C (98.6°F), heart rate is 120/min, respirations are 10/min, and blood pressure is 90/50 mm Hg. After naloxone administration, his consciousness improves and his respirations increase to 18/min. It is most appropriate for the hospital to investigate this incident as which of the following? A) Act of negligence B) Improper hand-off C) Perioperative risk D) Quality indicator E) Sentinel event
Correct Answer: E) Sentinel event
This patient received an excessive dose of opioid medication, resulting in decreased consciousness and life-threatening respiratory suppression that required naloxone reversal. This incident exemplifies a sentinel event—a patient safety event resulting in death, permanent harm, or temporary serious harm that may require life-sustaining treatment. Sentinel events typically prompt a root cause analysis (RCA) to determine the systems-level factors contributing to the adverse event. RCA involves identifying core systems-level issues that lead to the chain of events culminating in the adverse event, using strategies such as creating a timeline, surveying failed controls, and completing risk tree analysis. A multidisciplinary team works to define the problem, analyze contributing factors, and design corrective actions. Choice A (Act of negligence) refers to individual failure to act as a prudent physician would. However, no individual is identified as culpable in this case. Per RCA principles, systems-level causes (such as lack of standardized opioid administration procedures) rather than individual-level causes are the root of most adverse events. Choice B (Improper hand-off) refers to inadequate communication between healthcare professionals. While RCA may ultimately identify communication difficulties, inadequate communication is not mentioned in the case description. Choice C (Perioperative risk) refers to the hypothetical risk for perioperative adverse events. This case describes an actual event that occurred, not a hypothetical risk. Opioid-related adverse events are relatively rare when medications are titrated appropriately. Choice D (Quality indicator) refers to measures of healthcare quality such as staffing ratios or board certification rates. While sentinel events are commonly related to poor quality indicator scores, sentinel events themselves are not defined as quality indicators. Educational Objective: A sentinel event is a patient safety event resulting in death, permanent harm, or temporary serious harm that may require life-sustaining treatment. Sentinel events prompt root cause analysis to determine systems-level factors contributing to the event, using strategies including timeline creation, control failure assessment, and risk tree analysis.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Otitis Media 1 question
Source: 4A #8
A 15-month-old girl is brought to the physician by her mother because of a 5-day history of fever, difficulty sleeping, decreased appetite, and fussiness. She has no history of serious illness or known allergies. She receives no medications. She appears mildly ill but is interactive. She is at the 75th percentile for length and 50th percentile for weight. Her temperature is 38.3°C (101°F). Examination shows purulent nasal drainage. The left tympanic membrane is edematous and erythematous. The right tympanic membrane and oropharynx are normal. Which of the following is the most appropriate pharmacotherapy? A) Amoxicillin B) Azithromycin C) Cefuroxime D) Clindamycin E) Tetracycline
Correct Answer: A) Amoxicillin
Acute otitis media is an acute infection of the middle ear that spreads via the Eustachian tubes from the nasopharynx. It classically occurs in infants and children from viral or bacterial pathogens, commonly Streptococcus pneumoniae or Haemophilus influenzae. The condition presents with ear pain, fever, and an erythematous tympanic membrane with retrotympanic pus or middle ear effusion. Infants may present with nonspecific signs including fussiness, decreased appetite, poor sleeping, and ear tugging. Recurrent episodes may lead to mastoiditis or scarring of the tympanic membrane with conductive hearing loss. First-line therapy is amoxicillin with or without clavulanate, plus symptomatic treatment. Amoxicillin is a β-lactam antibiotic with broad activity against Gram-positive and Gram-negative bacteria, including S. pneumoniae and H. influenzae, and is commonly used for pediatric upper respiratory tract, sinus, and ear infections. Azithromycin (Choice B) is a macrolide antibiotic with limited activity against S. pneumoniae and H. influenzae, making it less effective than amoxicillin for acute otitis media. Cefuroxime (Choice C) is a second-generation cephalosporin with comparable but slightly reduced efficacy against S. pneumoniae and H. influenzae compared with amoxicillin. It is an acceptable alternative for penicillin-allergic patients but is not preferred for this patient with no known allergies. Clindamycin (Choice D) binds the bacterial ribosomal 50S subunit and has limited activity against H. influenzae. It is also highly unpalatable and difficult for infants to tolerate, making it a poor choice for this patient. Tetracycline (Choice E) binds the bacterial ribosomal 30S subunit and is not appropriate for patients under 8 years of age due to the risk of irreversible discoloration of tooth enamel.
▶ Male Reproductive Epididymitis 1 question
Source: 4A #12
A 17-year-old boy comes to the physician because of a 3-day history of increasingly severe pain of his right testicle. He also has had sweating, chills, and burning pain with urination during this time. He has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. His temperature is 38.5°C (101.3°F); other vital signs are within normal limits. Examination shows edema and tenderness to palpation behind the right testicle. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Antibiotic therapy B) Scrotal elevation and warm baths C) Ultrasonography of the scrotum D) Referral to a urologist E) Bed rest and reassurance
Correct Answer: A) Antibiotic therapy
This patient has acute epididymitis, an infection of the epididymis, and the most appropriate next step is antibiotic therapy. Acute epididymitis classically presents with unilateral scrotal pain associated with a tender, swollen mass over the posterior aspect of the testis. In severe cases like this patient's, it presents with localized erythema, dysuria, and systemic symptoms such as fever and rigors. Physical examination shows an intact cremasteric reflex, which helps differentiate acute epididymitis from testicular torsion (where the cremasteric reflex is often absent). In men under age 35, the underlying cause is most commonly Chlamydia trachomatis or Neisseria gonorrhoeae infection, whereas E. coli and Pseudomonas species are more common in older men. Evaluation includes urine nucleic acid amplification testing for both organisms, urinalysis, and urine culture. Treatment includes empiric ceftriaxone to cover gonorrheal infection and doxycycline or azithromycin to cover chlamydial infection in patients at risk for sexually transmitted infections. Levofloxacin or trimethoprim-sulfamethoxazole are recommended for low-risk patients. Symptomatic management includes scrotal elevation, ice application, and NSAIDs. Scrotal elevation and warm baths (B) are not the most appropriate next step. While scrotal elevation aids symptomatic management, warm baths would worsen erythema and edema; ice should be used instead. Failure to treat with antibiotics could lead to abscess formation or spread to surrounding structures. Ultrasonography of the scrotum (C) may aid diagnosis, with Doppler showing increased blood flow to the inflamed epididymis. However, acute epididymitis is typically diagnosed clinically. Delaying antibiotic treatment for imaging increases complication risk. Ultrasonography would be appropriate if testicular torsion were suspected. Referral to a urologist (D) on an emergency basis is appropriate for testicular torsion, which presents with severe testicular pain, tenderness, scrotal swelling, and a negative cremasteric reflex, requiring emergent surgical detorsion and orchiopexy. Urologic referral is not required for uncomplicated acute epididymitis. Bed rest and reassurance (E) are inappropriate. This patient has a severe epididymal infection with systemic symptoms. Failure to treat the underlying infection could lead to abscess formation or spread to nearby structures, so antibiotic therapy is essential.
▶ Biostatistics Epidemiology Population Health Internal Validity 1 question
Source: 4A #13
A randomized, double-blind trial is performed to assess the efficacy of a new drug for the treatment of streptococcal pharyngitis. A total of 590 women between the ages of 20 and 34 years, who have culture-confirmed streptococcal pharyngitis, are enrolled; 295 women receive treatment with penicillin V potassium, and 295 receive treatment with the new drug. Results show bacteria eradication of 94.2% in the group treated with penicillin V potassium compared with 99.4% in the group treated with the new drug (P=0.04). Which of the following is the most accurate conclusion based on these findings? A) The new drug should be the gold standard for treatment of streptococcal pharyngitis B) The results are not statistically significant C) The study does not have a control group D) The study has good internal validity E) The study uses a case-control study design
Correct Answer: D) The study has good internal validity
Internal validity describes the degree to which a study establishes or supports a faithful conclusion about cause and effect—whether the design of the study was sufficient to support the conclusion drawn. A randomized, double-blind trial is a stringent type of prospective study wherein patients are randomly assigned to receive a particular intervention, forming the gold standard of medical research. This study demonstrates good internal validity because: (1) it uses randomization to minimize bias and confounding; (2) it employs double-blinding to prevent bias; (3) the restricted demographic characteristics (women aged 20-34 with culture-confirmed streptococcal pharyngitis) decrease the likelihood of confounding variables; and (4) the study design allows for a faithful conclusion about cause and effect regarding the drug's efficacy. Choice A is incorrect because concluding the new drug should be the gold standard is premature. The study lacks external validity (generalizability) since it only includes women aged 20-34, and findings should be replicated before translation into routine clinical practice. Choice B is incorrect because the P-value of 0.04 is statistically significant. A P-value of 0.05 or less is generally considered statistically significant, meaning there is only a 5% chance of erroneously rejecting the null hypothesis if it is true. Choice C is incorrect because the study does have a control group. In randomized trials, the control group may be a placebo or standard drug group. In this study, penicillin V potassium serves as the control group. Choice E is incorrect because this is a randomized trial, not a case-control study. Case-control studies group patients according to disease outcome status and analyze odds of exposure. In this study, patients are grouped by treatment assignment, not disease outcome status, and the investigators control the exposures and outcomes. External validity determines whether an experiment can be generalized and its conclusions applied to groups beyond those in the study population. While this study has good internal validity, its external validity is decreased due to the narrow demographic characteristics of the study population.
▶ Human Development Weight changes in infancy and childhood 1 question
Source: 4A #14
A 4-month-old male infant born at 3500 g (7 lb 11 oz) now weighs 7000 g (15 lb 7 oz). Birth weight falls near the 50th percentile, and current weight falls near the 65th percentile for age. The parents are concerned about the infant's weight gain. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step? A) He should be switched from breast-feeding to bottle-feeding B) He should be taken to a nutritionist for further evaluation C) His thyroid-stimulating hormone concentration should be checked D) The parents should be reassured that this weight gain is appropriate E) His weight gain is too rapid for his age
Correct Answer: D) The parents should be reassured that this weight gain is appropriate
Growth rates are a useful way of assessing a child's growth over a given interval of time and can detect abnormal growth patterns that may indicate underlying medical conditions. Growth charts plot the expected length and weight of a child of a particular sex at a given age, with percentiles also plotted for length and weight at a particular age. In this scenario, the patient was born at 3500 g, which falls near the 50th percentile for birth weight. At age 4 months, he weighs 7000 g, which falls near the 65th percentile of weight for his age. Over the first four months of life, this patient has gained 3500 g, which is normal. The parents should be reassured that this weight gain is appropriate. Children at extreme ends of the growth curves are often healthy. However, persistent accelerated or decelerated growth rates usually warrant further investigation. Common causes of accelerated weight gain include endocrine abnormalities (e.g., hypercortisolism) or genetic abnormalities (e.g., Down syndrome), while decelerated weight gain may occur because of malnutrition, immunodeficiency, or metabolic or organ system abnormalities. Breast-feeding is the optimal source of nutrition for the growth and healthy development of infants and should be continued whenever possible. This patient's rate of weight gain is appropriate and requires no dietary modifications, making switching to bottle-feeding unnecessary. An evaluation with a nutritionist is beneficial when there is an observed deceleration of the rate of weight gain with a preserved rate of gain of length, which often occurs as a result of malnutrition. This patient's rate of weight gain is appropriate and does not require evaluation by a nutritionist at this time. Congenital hypothyroidism commonly presents in the first few months of life with feeding difficulties, lethargy, constipation, myxedematous facies, macroglossia, large fontanelles, hypotonia, and hypothermia. Infants with hypothyroidism are often in the lower percentiles for height and weight and display decelerated rates of weight gain. This patient is growing normally and does not display other findings suggestive of hypothyroidism, rendering testing unnecessary. The weight gain is not too rapid for his age. If further observation yields a persistent acceleration of the rate of weight gain, then further medical workup should be performed. Both height and weight percentiles and growth rates should be considered when any abnormality is suspected.
▶ Human Development Preventive and travel medicine in adulthood 1 question
Source: 4A #20
A 65-year-old man comes to the physician to request prostate cancer screening. He has not had urinary symptoms. He has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. His father had prostate cancer. Physical examination shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step regarding prostate cancer screening for this patient? A) Digital rectal examination and measurement of serum prostate-specific antigen (PSA) concentration B) Digital rectal examination only C) Shared decision-making discussion of risks and benefits of screening D) Measurement of serum PSA concentration only E) Prostate cancer screening is not indicated for this patient
Correct Answer: C) Shared decision-making discussion of risks and benefits of screening
The most appropriate next step is discussing the risks and benefits of prostate cancer screening options and allowing the patient to decide through shared decision-making. Prostate cancer is the second most common cancer in men and can be asymptomatic or present with urinary symptoms (hesitancy, hematuria) or bone pain if metastatic. Risk factors include advancing age, certain ethnicities, family history, and genetic factors (BRCA mutations, Lynch syndrome). While screening patients over age 55 with serum PSA measurement may decrease prostate cancer mortality, significant harms exist including false-positive results leading to unnecessary biopsies. Many prostate cancers remain indolent and clinically insignificant during a patient's lifetime. Treatment of screen-detected cancers carries risks from surgical resection and chemoradiotherapy. The United States Preventive Services Task Force (USPSTF) recommends that the decision to screen for prostate cancer between ages 55-69 years be individualized based on patient values after shared decision-making with the physician. The USPSTF does not recommend screening for patients over age 70. Choice A (DRE and PSA) is premature without first discussing risks/benefits and obtaining informed consent. Choice B (DRE only) is not recommended as initial screening due to low sensitivity and specificity. Choice D (PSA only) may be appropriate after shared decision-making but the discussion must occur first. Choice E is incorrect because the patient desires screening and the decision should be individualized based on his preferences after informed discussion, not denied outright.
▶ Nervous System Special Senses Presbyopia 1 question
Source: 4A #21
A 55-year-old man comes to the office for a routine examination. Eighteen months ago, he had progressive difficulty reading and "seeing small things." His symptoms initially improved with the use of weak reading glasses; he then required stronger reading glasses. He reports no other current problems with his vision and is otherwise asymptomatic. He has hypertension treated with hydrochlorothiazide. Vital signs are within normal limits. Visual acuity is 20/30 in the left eye and 20/20 in the right eye. No other abnormalities are noted. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Glaucoma screening B) Measurement of fasting serum glucose concentration C) Referral to an ophthalmologist D) Ultrasonography of the carotid arteries E) No further management is indicated
Correct Answer: E) No further management is indicated
This patient presents with presbyopia, an age-related loss of the eye's accommodative ability. Accommodation is the ability of the eye to alter and increase its refractive power to focus on near visual targets. The Helmholtz theory of accommodation explains that contraction of the ciliary muscle relaxes tension on the zonular fibers of the crystalline lens, allowing the lens to become rounder and increase the curvature of its anterior surface, thereby increasing its dioptric power for focusing on close objects. As the lens stiffens with normal aging, this accommodative ability is lost. Presbyopia is a normal age-related change presenting with blurry vision at near distances, requiring objects to be held farther away to be seen clearly. Normal pupillary reflexes to accommodation are preserved. Treatment is with reading glasses with positive dioptric power. This patient maintains good visual acuity (20/20 in right eye, 20/30 in left eye) and is doing well with stronger reading glasses, so no further management is indicated. Glaucoma screening (Choice A) is incorrect because glaucoma is a progressive optic neuropathy causing loss of retinal ganglion cell axons and peripheral vision with optic disc cupping. It does not present with loss of accommodative ability or difficulties with near vision while sparing distance vision. Measurement of fasting serum glucose concentration (Choice B) is useful for diagnosing diabetes, which can cause blurry vision, visual distortion, or vision loss from diabetic retinopathy or macular edema. However, diabetes does not selectively cause loss of near vision while sparing distance vision. Referral to an ophthalmologist (Choice C) is not necessary at this time. The patient is seeing well with reading glasses and maintaining good visual acuity. Ophthalmology referral would be warranted if the patient were unhappy with vision or if visual acuity fell below 20/40, warranting evaluation for cataract, refractive error, or other causes of vision loss. Ultrasonography of the carotid arteries (Choice D) is useful for diagnosing amaurosis fugax or branch retinal artery occlusion from cholesterol embolization. Amaurosis fugax presents with brief episodes of transient, unilateral, painless vision loss from microembolization of the ophthalmic artery. Branch retinal artery occlusion presents with sudden, often sectoral vision loss that does not fluctuate. Neither condition presents with isolated loss of near vision as in this scenario.
▶ Respiratory System Asbestosis 1 question
Source: 4A #22
A 72-year-old man has had increasing shortness of breath for 6 months. He has lost 1.4 kg (3 lb) in the last 3 months, but his appetite has not decreased. He has had a nonproductive cough for more than 40 years. He has smoked two packs of cigarettes daily for 50 years and worked from age 30 to 40 years in a factory making automobile brake linings. He is afebrile and not cyanotic. There is dullness to percussion; on auscultation, diminished breath sounds and egophony over the right lower chest are noted. X-rays of the chest show a right pleural effusion and a mass contiguous with the pleura in the right lateral chest. There are diffuse irregular linear opacities that are most dense in the lower lung fields. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Anthracosis B) Asbestosis C) Berylliosis D) Byssinosis E) Silicosis
Correct Answer: B) Asbestosis
Asbestosis is a type of pneumoconiosis resulting from inhalation of asbestos fibers, leading to progressive inflammation and fibrosis of the alveoli and pulmonary interstitium. This patient's occupational history of working in an automobile brake lining factory from age 30-40 is a classic exposure risk, as brake linings historically contained asbestos. Asbestosis presents with chronic cough and dyspnea associated with progressive pulmonary fibrosis. Physical examination may reveal pulmonary crackles, digital clubbing, and signs of pulmonary hypertension. Characteristic imaging findings include bilateral infiltrates with lower lung predominance, calcified pleural plaques, rounded atelectasis, and pleural effusions—all present in this case. The pleural effusion and mass contiguous with the pleura are particularly suggestive of asbestos-related disease, which carries an increased risk for both bronchogenic carcinoma and malignant mesothelioma. Anthracosis (Choice A) is caused by coal dust inhalation affecting coal miners and industrial workers, not brake lining manufacturers. Berylliosis (Choice C) is associated with aerospace and nuclear engineering work and presents as a noncaseating granulomatous disease with lymphadenopathy resembling sarcoidosis, lacking the pleural involvement seen here. Byssinosis (Choice D) results from cotton, flax, or hemp dust exposure in textile workers and presents with episodic respiratory distress and wheezing, not the chronic progressive fibrosis pattern shown. Silicosis (Choice E) from silica dust exposure typically presents with perihilar and upper lobe predominant opacities and would not characteristically involve pleural disease. The pleural involvement and occupational history of asbestos exposure make asbestosis the most likely diagnosis.
▶ Pregnancy Childbirth Puerperium Hyponatremia 1 question
Source: 4A #24
A 42-year-old man comes to the emergency department because of a 2-hour history of headache, dizziness, nausea, and confusion. He completed a marathon 3 hours ago. During the race, he drank approximately 4 liters of water but consumed no electrolytes or food. He takes no medications. He appears pale and is diaphoretic. His temperature is 37.4°C (99.3°F), pulse is 104/min, respirations are 20/min, and blood pressure is 108/62 mm Hg. Examination shows no other abnormalities. Which of the following laboratory findings is most likely to be noted in this patient? A) Hyperkalemia B) Hypernatremia C) Hypoglycemia D) Hypokalemia E) Hyponatremia
Correct Answer: E) Hyponatremia
Hyponatremia is the most likely laboratory finding in this patient. While running a marathon and sweating profusely, the patient replenished his fluids with free water only, which is hypotonic to the sweat that was lost. This resulted in a progressive and relatively rapid decline in overall sodium concentration, causing symptomatic hyponatremia. Symptoms include nausea, headache, vomiting, confusion, and in severe cases, seizures or coma—all of which this patient exhibits. Treatment for symptomatic acute hyponatremia includes stopping free water intake or administering saline solution. In chronic hyponatremia, correction must be done slowly to avoid central pontine myelinolysis. Hyperkalemia (Choice A) is unlikely; it causes muscle weakness, cardiac arrhythmias, and gastrointestinal symptoms, typically from kidney disease, metabolic acidosis, or certain medications. Hypernatremia (Choice B) would not occur given the patient's excess intake of hypotonic water, which dilutes serum sodium rather than concentrating it. Hypoglycemia (Choice C) is possible from marathon exertion consuming significant calories, but the patient's excess water consumption makes hyponatremia the more likely primary cause of his symptoms. Hypokalemia (Choice D) is unlikely; while potassium may be lost through sweat, it would not explain this patient's acute neurological symptoms. Hypokalemia typically causes muscle weakness, cramping, and cardiac arrhythmias. Educational Objective: Hyponatremia can be precipitated by excess free water intake in the setting of excess diaphoresis without appropriate electrolyte replenishment. Patients with hyponatremia related to increased free water intake versus sweat loss should be closely monitored and have their electrolytes replenished appropriately.
▶ Blood Lymphoreticular System Quantitative thrombocytopenia 1 question
Source: 4A #25
A 46-year-old man with thrombocytopenia comes to the office for a preoperative evaluation prior to the extraction of four wisdom teeth. The patient says he feels well. He takes no medications. Complete blood count discloses a platelet count of 110,000/mm³. Which of the following is the most appropriate recommendation for this patient? A) Begin desmopressin therapy B) Cancel the dental extraction C) [Implied correct answer - proceed with extraction] D) Reschedule another appointment to establish platelet count prior to the dental extraction E) Transfuse platelets prior to dental extraction
Correct Answer: C) Proceed with dental extraction as scheduled
Thrombocytopenia is characterized by a low platelet count, which can result in excessive bleeding and bruising. The most common causes include immune-mediated destruction of platelets (such as immune thrombocytopenic purpura and drug-induced thrombocytopenia), decreased platelet production (aplastic anemia, leukemia), and increased platelet consumption (disseminated intravascular coagulation, thrombotic thrombocytopenic purpura). In this patient with no medical history and no medications, ITP is most likely. For patients with ITP and stable platelet counts above 50,000/mm³, no interventions for thrombocytopenia are required as these patients generally do not have issues with hemostasis during dental procedures. This patient has a platelet count of 110,000/mm³, which is well above the threshold for safe dental procedures. Desmopressin therapy (Choice A) is not appropriate for this patient. While desmopressin could be considered if the platelet count was less than 30,000/mm³ or if there were signs of active bleeding, it is not first-line therapy for thrombocytopenia in ITP. Desmopressin is more commonly used for von Willebrand disease, and corticosteroids would be more appropriate if treatment were needed. Canceling the dental extraction (Choice B) is not appropriate. Given this patient's stable thrombocytopenia and platelet count greater than 100,000/mm³, he is at low risk for bleeding complications from dental procedures. The extraction should proceed as scheduled. Rescheduling to establish platelet count (Choice D) is unnecessary. This patient has known thrombocytopenia with platelet counts greater than 100,000/mm³, indicating stable disease. The extraction can proceed as scheduled. Platelet transfusion (Choice E) is not appropriate. Transfusion would only be considered if the platelet count was less than 30,000/mm³ with signs of active bleeding, or less than 10,000/mm³. This patient has a stable platelet count greater than 100,000/mm³, making transfusion unnecessary. Educational Objective: Patients with stable thrombocytopenia and platelet counts greater than 50,000/mm³ do not require additional treatments or therapies for thrombocytopenia prior to dental surgeries.
▶ Behavioral Health Attention-Deficit/Hyperactivity Disorder 1 question
Source: 4A #26
A boy is brought to the office by his mother for a well-child examination. She reports that he has been more fidgety lately and has not been able to focus on his school work for more than a few minutes at a time. He also does not multitask well. The boy eats well, sleeps well, and has a happy demeanor. He has no history of serious illness and receives no medications. Vaccinations are up-to-date. Vital signs are within normal limits, and physical examination discloses no abnormalities. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Administer behavioral rating scales to the patient and obtain rating scales from his teacher B) Initiate methylphenidate therapy C) Obtain genetic testing D) Recommend decreasing dietary sugar intake E) Reassure the patient's mother this is normal behavior at this age
Correct Answer: A) Administer behavioral rating scales to the patient and obtain rating scales from his teacher
Attention-deficit/hyperactivity disorder (ADHD) presents with chronic symptoms of hyperactivity, impulsivity, and/or inattention that occur in more than one setting and impair academic, social, and/or emotional function. Children with predominant hyperactivity symptoms are fidgety and may have difficulty taking turns (postulated to result from a dysregulated dopamine reward pathway), whereas children with predominant inattentive symptoms tend to daydream, process information slowly, and have difficulty completing tasks (related to norepinephrine dysregulation). For diagnosis, physicians must establish that the patient is functionally impaired in several settings, so parents and teachers must fill out behavioral rating scales. This patient presents with symptoms consistent with ADHD (fidgetiness and inattention affecting school work), but formal diagnosis requires behavioral rating scales from multiple sources before treatment can be initiated. Initiate methylphenidate therapy (Choice B) would not yet be indicated. First, this patient should be formally diagnosed with ADHD with rating scales. If he meets ADHD criteria, parent training in behavior management should be initiated before considering stimulant therapy. Stimulants, which include amphetamine salts and methylphenidate, are first-line treatments for children aged 6 years and older, but only after formal diagnosis. Obtain genetic testing (Choice C) is not needed for ADHD diagnosis. Though ADHD is heritable with multiple involved genetic variants, it is a clinical diagnosis. This patient can be diagnosed with ADHD based on presentation and rating scales. Recommend decreasing dietary sugar intake (Choice D) is not indicated. Though some studies have shown the effectiveness of elimination diets for some children, the effect of diet on ADHD symptoms is not fully known and controversial. National guidelines do not recommend routine dietary interventions. Reassure the patient's mother this is normal behavior at this age (Choice E) is incorrect. This patient has impairing inattention and hyperactivity symptoms that require diagnostic evaluation for ADHD. Children should be able to pay attention and sit still to a reasonable extent. Educational Objective: ADHD is a clinical diagnosis requiring evidence of chronic symptoms of hyperactivity, impulsivity, and/or inattention occurring in multiple settings with functional impairment. Formal diagnosis requires behavioral rating scales from parents and teachers before initiating treatment.
▶ Skin Subcutaneous Tissue Psoriasis 1 question
Source: 4A #28
A 22-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 3-month history of thick, flaky fingernails. During this time, he also has had dry, scaly, red patches of skin on his forearms and chest. He has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. During the past 2 years, he has worked as a restaurant dishwasher. A photograph of the left hand and right forearm is shown; similar findings are seen over the left forearm and trunk. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis? A) Atopic dermatitis B) Hydradenitis suppurativa C) Nummular eczema D) Psoriasis E) Tinea corporis
Correct Answer: D) Psoriasis
Psoriasis is a chronic inflammatory skin condition often localized to sites of trauma, including the elbows and knees. It is associated with production of tumor necrosis factor (TNF)-α, interleukin (IL)-12, IL-17, and IL-23 produced by keratinocytes, dendritic cells, and T-cells. The Th17 lymphocyte is thought to be central to the pathophysiology of psoriasis, and newer biologic therapies target the cytokines released by these cells. Clinically, plaque psoriasis presents with thick, salmon-colored plaques on the extensor extremities with overlying silvery, white scale. Psoriasis may involve the nails, is associated with underlying psoriatic arthritis in a subset of patients, and has been associated with the development of atherosclerotic disease. This patient's history of nail involvement and rash is clinically consistent with psoriasis. Atopic dermatitis and nummular eczema are inflammatory skin conditions characterized by itching followed by rash, with lichenified plaques commonly found on flexural surfaces. Nummular eczema presents with pruritic, coin-shaped lesions. This patient's condition is more consistent with psoriasis given the clinical morphology of the lesions and nail involvement. Hydradenitis suppurativa is an inflammatory condition affecting apocrine gland-bearing areas (axillary, inguinal, anogenital, inframammary), presenting with sterile abscesses in intertriginous areas. The absence of abscesses makes this diagnosis unlikely. Tinea corporis presents with annular, pink plaques with peripheral scale, often caused by Trichophyton rubrum. While fungal infections may present with peripheral scale, this patient's presentation is more consistent with psoriasis. Treatments for psoriasis include topical steroids, topical calcipotriene (vitamin D analogue), narrowband UVB light therapy, and newer systemic biologic agents including adalimumab, ixekizumab, secukinumab, and ustekinumab that specifically target different inflammatory pathways involved in psoriasis.
▶ Respiratory System Acute Upper Respiratory Infection 1 question
Source: 4A #29
A child presents with symptoms of nasal congestion, cough, sneezing, and nonexudative pharyngitis consistent with a viral upper respiratory tract infection. The patient has normal mobile tympanic membranes, no fever, and appears well. Which of the following is the most appropriate management? A) Supportive care with symptomatic treatment B) Sinus lavage in the clinic C) Culture of the nasal discharge D) X-ray of the sinuses E) Antiviral therapy F) Broad spectrum antibiotic therapy
Correct Answer: A) Supportive care with symptomatic treatment
Upper respiratory tract infections are common in children, with most cases caused by viruses and resolving with supportive care. This patient's presentation of nasal congestion, cough, sneezing, and nonexudative pharyngitis is consistent with viral upper respiratory tract infection. The absence of fever, normal mobile tympanic membranes, and nonexudative pharyngitis make acute otitis media, bacterial sinusitis, and group A streptococcal pharyngitis unlikely. Therefore, antibiotics are not indicated. Supportive care measures such as humidifier, warm water with honey, and acetaminophen or ibuprofen are appropriate for symptomatic treatment of this self-limited illness. Sinus lavage (Choice B) is unnecessary at this stage and can be done at home if needed; it does not address all symptoms. Nasal discharge culture (Choice C) may identify viral pathogens but typically does not change management in well-appearing, afebrile patients with self-limited infections and increases costs. Sinus X-rays (Choice D) have poor sensitivity and specificity compared to CT; imaging is not typically needed for uncomplicated upper respiratory infections. Antiviral therapy (Choice E) is reserved for influenza and other specific viral infections; this patient likely has a self-limited minor respiratory infection not requiring antivirals. Broad spectrum antibiotics (Choice F) are inappropriate without evidence of bacterial infection and would promote antibiotic resistance, increase costs, and risk side effects. Educational Objective: Viral upper respiratory tract infections are typically self-limited and should be managed with symptomatic supportive care rather than unnecessary testing, imaging, or antimicrobial therapy.
▶ Renal Urinary System Renal Artery Stenosis Due to Atherosclerosis 1 question
Source: 4A #30
A 65-year-old man with a history of coronary artery disease comes for a follow-up examination. He underwent coronary artery bypass grafting 4 years ago. He has a 3-year history of intermittent claudication. His blood pressure measurements from his last eight visits show: 24 months ago 130/80, 21 months ago 130/60, 15 months ago 140/70, 12 months ago 130/80, 8 months ago 160/90, 6 months ago 170/96, 4 months ago 180/110, and 2 months ago 180/110 mm Hg. Current medications include aspirin and a blood pressure medication. Current blood pressure is 180/110 mm Hg. Heart sounds are normal. Dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses are not palpable bilaterally. Serum studies show: K+ 3.1 mEq/L, HCO3- 28 mEq/L, urea nitrogen 20 mg/dL, glucose 90 mg/dL, creatinine 1.1 mg/dL. Which of the following is the most likely cause of this patient's increased blood pressure? A) Dissecting abdominal aortic aneurysm B) Essential hypertension C) Renal artery stenosis D) Renal failure E) White-coat hypertension
Correct Answer: C) Renal artery stenosis
Renal artery stenosis is a cause of secondary hypertension related to abnormal stimulation of the juxtaglomerular apparatus secondary to stenosis of the renal artery leading to insufficient afferent blood flow. This results in excessive production of renin and angiotensin. Secondary hypertension should be considered in new-onset or treatment-resistant hypertension. There are two main causes of renal artery stenosis: fibromuscular dysplasia and atherosclerosis. In younger patients, fibromuscular dysplasia is the most common cause, while older patients develop renal artery stenosis more commonly from atherosclerosis. This older patient with a history of coronary artery disease and peripheral artery disease (evidenced by absent dorsalis pedis and posterior tibial pulses bilaterally and intermittent claudication) secondary to atherosclerosis likely has renal artery stenosis also secondary to atherosclerosis. The gradual worsening of hypertension over several months in the setting of established atherosclerotic disease supports a chronic secondary cause rather than essential hypertension. Diagnosis is established with renal artery Doppler ultrasonography or magnetic resonance angiography. Treatment involves angioplasty or stenting of the stenosed renal artery to improve flow. ACE inhibitors can be considered for unilateral stenosis but can lead to acute renal failure in the setting of bilateral renal artery stenosis. Incorrect answers: A dissecting abdominal aortic aneurysm would present with acute severe abdominal or back pain and acute vital sign abnormalities, not a chronic progressive course. Essential hypertension is primary hypertension without identifiable secondary cause, but this patient has strong evidence of atherosclerotic disease suggesting a secondary cause. Renal failure is defined by elevated creatinine and decreased glomerular filtration rate; this patient's creatinine is normal at 1.1 mg/dL. White-coat hypertension presents with elevated readings only in clinical settings with normal home readings, but this patient has gradually developed sustained hypertension over months, indicating a chronic pathologic process. Educational Objective: Renal artery stenosis from atherosclerosis can cause secondary hypertension in older patients, especially with evidence of other atherosclerotic diseases such as coronary artery disease and peripheral artery disease.
▶ Skin Subcutaneous Tissue Actinic Keratoses 1 question
Source: 4A #31
A 76-year-old man comes to the physician because of a 5-year history of progressive redness and scaling over bald areas of his scalp. He has hypertension, hypercholesterolemia, and psoriasis over his arms. His medications are lisinopril, hydrochlorothiazide, lovastatin, clobetasol, and aspirin. He is a retired high school sports coach. Vital signs are within normal limits. There are large areas of scaly, dry rash over the upper extremities. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis of this patient's scalp findings? A) Actinic keratosis B) Basal cell carcinoma C) Melanoma D) Psoriasis E) Seborrheic dermatitis
Correct Answer: A) Actinic keratosis
Actinic keratoses are atypical proliferations of keratinocytes caused by chronic UV damage. Over time, UV damage leads to thymidine dimers in DNA and p53 mutations, inhibiting normal tumor suppressor gene function. This causes partial thickness atypia of keratinocytes within the epidermis, which can evolve into invasive squamous cell carcinoma. Actinic keratoses classically present as ill-defined, rough, scaly papules found on sun-exposed areas including the face, ears, neck, and distal extremities. The patient's history as a retired high school sports coach with significant sun exposure, combined with the presentation of scaly lesions on bald scalp areas (sun-exposed), strongly supports this diagnosis. Treatment includes cryotherapy, topical 5-fluorouracil, or topical imiquimod. Lesions unresponsive to conventional treatment warrant skin biopsy to rule out progression to squamous cell carcinoma. Baral cell carcinoma presents as a pearly papule with rolled borders, telangiectasia, and possible erosion/ulceration—not the rough, scaly appearance described. Melanoma presents as an evolving pigmented growth with asymmetry, irregular borders, and variable coloration, which is not consistent with this presentation. Psoriasis is a chronic inflammatory condition presenting with salmon-colored plaques with thick, micaceous scales, commonly affecting the occipital scalp and not associated with UV damage. Seborrheic dermatitis presents with ill-defined erythematous patches with greasy scale on sebaceous areas but does not present as localized scaly papules in sun-damaged skin.
▶ Gastrointestinal System Oral cancer 1 question
Source: 4A #32
A 56-year-old man comes to the office because of a 1-month history of a nonhealing lesion on his tongue. He says he thinks the lesion may have appeared when he bit his tongue while sleeping. He has a 2-year history of type 2 diabetes mellitus well controlled with metformin and diet. He works as an electrician at a shipyard. He has smoked one pack of cigarettes daily for 17 years. He drinks three 12-oz beers weekly. Vital signs are within normal limits. There is a 2-cm, ulcerated lesion over the left aspect of the tongue. The remainder of the examination, including that of the head and neck, shows no abnormalities. Which of the following is most likely to have prevented this patient's lesion? A) Alcohol abstinence B) Human papillomavirus vaccination C) Smoking cessation D) Use of a mask at work E) Use of a mouth guard at night
Correct Answer: C) Smoking cessation
Squamous cell carcinoma is the most common type of oropharyngeal carcinoma and is strongly associated with tobacco use and infection with human papillomavirus (HPV). Patients may present with an oropharyngeal lump or nodule, nonhealing ulcers, dysphagia, odynophagia, and weight loss. Smoking is the strongest risk factor in this patient's history for oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma. Establishing the diagnosis with a biopsy is essential to guiding further management. Staging can be aided by cervical sentinel lymph node biopsy and imaging with CT scan or MRI. Early-stage disease may be managed successfully with surgical excision, although more advanced lesions usually require a combined approach with surgery, radiotherapy, and chemotherapy. Alcohol abstinence (Choice A) is incorrect because while alcohol consumption is a risk factor for developing oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma, it is not as strong a risk factor as smoking and has been primarily shown in patients with heavy consumption. Additionally, alcohol may act synergistically with smoking to increase risk. Human papillomavirus vaccination (Choice B) is incorrect because HPV vaccine first became available in 2006, at which time this 56-year-old patient was too old to have received it. The HPV vaccine is recommended for children ages 9-12 years or adults not previously vaccinated prior to age 26 years. Vaccination in adults older than age 26 is controversial and of possibly limited benefit. Additionally, smoking confers greater risk for oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma than HPV exposure. Use of a mask at work (Choice D) is incorrect because shipyard workers' exposure to asbestos is strongly associated with mesothelioma, not oropharyngeal squamous cell carcinoma. This patient's history of smoking is a much more likely cause of his cancer than his history of working in a shipyard. Use of a mouth guard at night (Choice E) is incorrect because mechanical trauma to the tongue while sleeping would be expected to heal over several days or weeks. The nonhealing nature of the lesion is suggestive of oropharyngeal carcinoma, not simple trauma. Wearing a mouth guard would not have been likely to prevent this condition.
▶ Renal Urinary System Renal Artery Stenosis Due to Fibromuscular Dysplasia 1 question
Source: 4A #33
A 37-year-old woman comes to the physician because of a 3-month history of frequent headaches. She has no history of serious illness and takes no medications. Her last health maintenance examination 3 years ago showed no abnormalities. There is no family history of serious illness. BMI is 24 kg/m². Her temperature is 37.1°C (98.8°F), pulse is 76/min, respirations are 16/min, and blood pressure is 168/102 mmHg. Physical examination shows no abnormalities except for an epigastric bruit. Arteriography shows fibromuscular dysplasia of the renal artery. The underlying mechanism of this patient's condition was most likely initiated by the release of which of the following substances? A) Aldosterone B) Angiotensin I C) Angiotensin II D) Angiotensin III E) Renin
Correct Answer: E) Renin
Renal artery stenosis is a cause of secondary hypertension related to activation of the renin-angiotensin-aldosterone system. Low afferent blood flow to the affected kidney leads to abnormal stimulation of the juxtaglomerular apparatus and excessive production of renin. Renin is the primary initiating substance that converts angiotensinogen to angiotensin I, which is then converted to angiotensin II by angiotensin-converting enzyme (ACE) in the pulmonary vasculature. Angiotensin II is a potent vasoconstrictor that directly mediates systemic hypertension. Angiotensin II also leads to increased Na+ reabsorption in the renal tubules, aldosterone release by the adrenal cortex with subsequent increased Na+ and fluid retention and hypokalemia, vasopressin release from the posterior pituitary leading to increased fluid retention, enhanced adrenergic function through inhibition of norepinephrine reuptake in sympathetic nerve endings, and stimulation of hypothalamic thirst centers. While aldosterone, angiotensin I, angiotensin II, and angiotensin III would all be increased in this patient, their increase occurs secondary to the primary release of renin, which is the chief driver of hypertension in renal artery stenosis. In younger patients, fibromuscular dysplasia is the most common cause of renal artery stenosis, while in older patients it is typically caused by atherosclerosis. An abdominal bruit may be disclosed in the region of the stenosis. Diagnosis is established with renal artery doppler ultrasonography or MR angiography, which are able to quantify the degree of renal artery stenosis and associated renal atrophy. Treatment involves angioplasty or stenting of the stenosed renal artery to improve flow. ACE inhibitors can be considered for unilateral stenosis but can lead to acute renal failure in the setting of bilateral renal artery stenosis.
▶ Musculoskeletal System Fractures 1 question
Source: 4A #34
A 47-year-old woman comes to the physician because of right wrist pain that began 2 days ago when she fell on her outstretched arm while playing tennis. She has had limited use of her hand and wrist since the injury occurred. She is otherwise healthy. There is tenderness to palpation over the radial and dorsal aspect of the right anatomic snuffbox. Muscle strength of the right fingers is 5/5, and sensation throughout the hand is intact. Plain x-rays of the hand and wrist show mild degenerative changes at the first carpometacarpal joint. Which of the following is the most appropriate next step in management? A) Reassurance that the pain should subside within 1 week B) Arthrography C) Cast immobilization D) Arthroscopy E) Surgical exploration and repair
Correct Answer: C) Cast immobilization
The scaphoid is the most common carpal bone to be fractured, typically from a fall onto an outstretched hand (FOOSH injury). Clinical presentation includes wrist pain with tenderness in the anatomic snuffbox—the area between the extensor pollicis longus and extensor pollicis brevis tendons. This patient's mechanism of injury, localized pain over the snuffbox, and limited hand/wrist function are classic for scaphoid fracture. Plain x-rays are the initial imaging of choice; however, initial radiographs may not show the fracture. With high clinical suspicion and negative initial x-rays, the appropriate management is cast immobilization to prevent complications (nonunion and avascular necrosis, particularly of the proximal pole due to distal blood supply from the radial artery) while awaiting repeat x-rays in 2-3 weeks. Choice A is inappropriate because reassurance without immobilization risks serious complications. Choice B (arthrography) is used for soft tissue injuries in larger joints (shoulder, hip, knee) and is not indicated for suspected scaphoid fracture. Choice D (arthroscopy) is a surgical visualization technique for larger joints and is not the appropriate next step for suspected scaphoid fracture. Choice E (surgical exploration and repair) may be indicated for displaced fractures but is premature before confirming the diagnosis with repeat imaging and before attempting conservative management.
